# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 13

## Page 6644

Volume Thirteen

Book: Introduction to Quranic Exegesis

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah bless his resting place—said in a work he composed late in his life at the Citadel of Damascus:

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help, guidance, and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides is rightly guided, and whoever He leads astray has no guide. I bear witness that none is worthy of worship except Allah alone, with no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah send His blessings and abundant peace upon him and his family.

---

## Page 6645

Section: Distinguishing Truth from Falsehood

Allah has made this distinction clear through His Book and His Prophet. The more closely a person follows the Book He revealed and the Prophet He sent, the greater that person’s discernment. The further a person strays from following the Book and the Messenger, the further they are from such discernment, and truth becomes confused with falsehood for them. Such are those who confuse worship of the Most Merciful with worship of Satan, a truthful prophet with a lying pretender to prophethood, and the signs of the prophets with the deceptions of liars—until they even confuse the Creator with the created.

Indeed, Allah, glorified and exalted is He, sent Muhammad with guidance and the religion of truth to bring people out of darkness into light. Through him, He distinguished between truth and falsehood, guidance and misguidance, right conduct and waywardness, truthfulness and lying, knowledge and ignorance, right and wrong, and the path of Allah’s blessed allies and that of His wretched enemies;

---

## Page 6646

and clarified the matters over which people differed. The prophets before him did likewise. Allah Most High says:

“Humankind was one community. Then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of good tidings and warners, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth to judge between people concerning their differences. None differed over it except those who had been given it, after clear proofs had come to them, out of jealous rivalry among themselves. Then Allah, by His permission, guided those who believed to the truth concerning which they had differed. Allah guides whom He wills to a straight path.”

He Most High also says:

“By Allah, We certainly sent messengers to nations before you, but Satan made their deeds seem attractive to them. So he is their ally today, and they will have a painful punishment.”

“And We have not sent down the Book to you except for you to clarify for them that over which they differed, and as guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

He, glorified and exalted is He, says:

“Blessed is He who sent down the Criterion to His servant, that he might be a warner to all peoples.”

He Most High also says:

“Alif Lām Mīm.”

“Allah—there is no deity except Him, the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all.”

“He has sent down to you the Book in truth, confirming what came before it, and He sent down the Torah and the Gospel,”

“previously, as guidance for humankind; and He sent down the Criterion.”

The great majority of Quran commentators said that it is the Quran. Ibn Abī Hātim related through his chain of transmission that al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas said: “It is the Criterion: it distinguished between truth and falsehood.” He added: “Similar statements have been related from ʿAtāʾ, Mujāhid, Muqsim, Qatādah and Muqātil ibn Hayyān.”

It was also related through his chain of transmission, from Shaybān, from Qatādah, concerning His words, “and He sent down the Criterion,” that he said: “It is the Quran, which Allah sent down to Muhammad. Through it He distinguished between truth and falsehood, clarified His religion, prescribed His laws, made lawful what He permitted and unlawful what He forbade, and laid down

---

## Page 6647

His limits, commanded obedience to Him, and forbade disobedience to Him.”

ʿAbbād ibn Mansūr reported: “I asked al-Hasan about the words of the Most High, ‘And He sent down the Criterion.’ He said: ‘It is a Book of truth.’”

Furqān (“distinction”) is a verbal noun of faraqa (“to distinguish”), like rujhān (“preponderance”), kufrān (“ingratitude”), and khusrān (“loss”). Likewise, Quran is originally a verbal noun of qaraʾa (“to recite”). This is its meaning in His words:

“Indeed, it is for Us to collect it and recite it. So when We have recited it, follow its recitation. Then it is for Us to explain it.”

The recited words themselves are also called “Quran,” a frequent usage, as in His words:

“So when you recite the Quran, seek refuge in Allah from Satan, the accursed.”

Likewise, kalām (“speech”) is a noun denoting the verbal action expressed by kallama and takallama (“to speak”), whose verbal nouns are taklīm and takallum; it also denotes the speech itself. When a person speaks, his speech involves an action and movement on his part, which is what the verbal noun denotes. That movement produces a sound articulated into letters, which constitutes speaking itself. Thus, “speech,” “utterance,” and similar terms encompass both senses. Accordingly, speech is sometimes classified as a kind of action, when the act of speaking is intended, and sometimes as a separate category alongside action, when the words spoken are intended. The term encompasses both. This is discussed at length elsewhere.

The point here is that when furqān is used as a verbal noun, it means that He sent down the separation and distinction between truth and falsehood. This is sent down in the Book, for the Book contains that separation. Sending down the distinction means sending down that which distinguishes. If furqān means that which distinguishes, it likewise denotes the distinguishing criterion. The two meanings therefore amount to the same thing. And if what is meant

---

## Page 6648

by furqān is the act itself denoted by the verbal noun, then sending it down is like sending down faith and justice. Through the Quran, He has placed in hearts the ability to distinguish between truth and falsehood, just as He has placed faith and justice within them. He, glorified and exalted, sent down the Book and the Balance. The Balance has been interpreted as justice, and also as that by which things are weighed so that justice may be known. It is thus like furqān, which is interpreted both as distinction itself and as that by which distinction is achieved. The two necessarily go together.

If distinction itself is intended, it is the result, fruit, and consequence of the Book. If that which distinguishes is intended, then the Book itself is what distinguishes. It therefore has two names, each indicating an attribute different from the other. It is called a Book because it is a written collection whose letters are preserved, read, and written. It is called a Criterion because it distinguishes between truth and falsehood, as explained above. Likewise, it is called guidance because it guides to the truth, and healing because it heals hearts of the diseases of doubts and desires. The same applies to its other names.

The same is true of the Messenger’s names, such as al-Muqaffī (the successor to the prophets), al-Māhī (the effacer), and al-Hāshir (the gatherer). Likewise, Allah’s Most Beautiful Names include the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful, the Sovereign, the All-Wise, and others.

Names and attributes may be joined by a conjunction because they differ, even when they refer to one and the same entity, as in His words:

“Exalt the name of your Lord, the Most High, who created and proportioned, and who destined and then guided.”

And His words:

“He is the First and the Last, the Ascendant and the Intimate.”

There are other similar examples.

---

## Page 6649

Here He mentions that He revealed the Book—for He revealed it in separate portions—and that He sent down the Torah and the Gospel. He also mentions that He sent down the Criterion. He, glorified and exalted, has sent down faith into hearts, and has sent down the Balance and faith. The “Balance” is also a means by which distinction is achieved, just as it is achieved through the Quran. When He sends down the Quran, faith and distinction result from it. A parallel is His statement:

“We certainly gave Moses and Aaron the Criterion, a light, and a reminder.”

It has been said that the Criterion is the Torah, and it has also been said that it is His judgment in granting Moses victory over Pharaoh, as in His statement:

“If you have believed in Allah and in what We sent down to Our servant on the Day of Criterion.”

Likewise, He says:

“There has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book.”

It has been said that the “light” is Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, and it has also been said that it is Islam.

And He says:

“There has come to you a conclusive proof from your Lord, and We have sent down to you a clear light.”

It has been said that the “conclusive proof” is Muhammad, and it has also been said that it means evidence and proof. Another interpretation is that it is the Quran. Evidence and proof encompass the signs with which Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent. There, however, He uses the expressions “We gave” and “there has come to you,” whereas here He says:

“And He sent down the Criterion.”

He uses the expression “sending down.” For this reason, it has become widely accepted among them that the Quran and conclusive proof are attained through knowledge and clear exposition, as has been achieved through the Quran, and also through reflection and differentiation between the people of truth and the people of falsehood: He delivers the former and grants them victory, while punishing the latter. He thus distinguishes between the two groups, just as one who distinguishes between Allah’s allies and His enemies does so by showing kindness to the former and punishing the latter.

---

## Page 6650

This is like His statement in the Quran:

“If you have believed in Allah and in what We sent down to Our servant on the Day of Criterion—the day the two armies met. And Allah has power over all things.”

Al-Wālibī reported from Ibn ʿAbbās concerning “the Day of Criterion”: “The day of Badr, on which Allah distinguished truth from falsehood.” Ibn Abī Hātim said: “Similar explanations were reported from Mujāhid, Muqsim, ʿUbayd Allah ibn ʿAbd Allah, al-Dahhāk, Qatāda and Muqātil ibn Hayyān.”

Most of them also understood His statement in this sense:

“If you fear Allah, He will grant you a criterion.”

This is like His statement:

“And whoever fears Allah, He will make for him a way out.”

That is, out of every difficulty that constrains people. Al-Wālibī reported from Ibn ʿAbbās concerning His statement, “If you fear Allah, He will grant you a criterion,” that it means “a way out.” Ibn Abī Hātim said: “The same was reported from Mujāhid, ʿIkrima, al-Dahhāk, Qatāda, al-Suddī and Muqātil ibn Hayyān, except that Mujāhid said: ‘A way out in this world and the Hereafter.’” It was also reported through al-Dahhāk that Ibn ʿAbbās said: “Victory.” He added: “The concluding wording from Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Suddī is ‘deliverance.’”

Concerning “He will grant you a criterion,” ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr said: “A separation between truth and falsehood, through which Allah makes your truth manifest and extinguishes the falsehood of those who oppose you.”

Al-Baghawī related that Muqātil ibn Hayyān said: “A way out of doubts in this world.” This, however, may be an explanation of what Muqātil ibn Hayyān meant, just as Abū al-Faraj ibn al-Jawzī related from Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, ʿIkrima, al-Dahhāk and Ibn

---

## Page 6651

Qutayba that they said: “It means a way out.” He then said: “The meaning is that He will grant you a way out of error in this world.” But this is not what they meant. Rather, they meant the way out mentioned in His words:

“And whoever fears Allah, He will make for him a way out,”

and the criterion mentioned in His words:

“And what We sent down to Our servant on the Day of Criterion.”

It has been related that Ibn Zayd said: “Guidance in their hearts by which they distinguish truth from falsehood.” The two kinds of criterion—guidance and clarification, and victory and deliverance—are the two kinds of “prevailing” in His statement, Exalted is He:

“It is He who sent His Messenger with guidance and the religion of truth to make it prevail over all religion.”

He makes it prevail through clarification, argument and proof, and through might, power and the spear.

Likewise, “authority” occurs in His words:

“And grant me, from Yourself, a supporting authority.”

This kind consists of proof and knowledge, as in His words:

“Or have We sent down to them an authority that speaks in support of what they associate with Him?”

And His words:

“Those who dispute Allah’s signs without any authority having come to them have nothing in their hearts but pride.”

And His words:

“They are nothing but names you and your forefathers have named, for which Allah has sent down no authority.”

“Authority” has been explained as the authority of power and might, and also as proof and clarification. One aspect of the criterion is reflected in Allah’s description of him in His words:

“My mercy encompasses all things. I shall decree it for those who fear Me and give zakat, and those who believe in Our signs.”

“Those who follow the Messenger, the unlettered Prophet, whom they find mentioned in the Torah they have

---

## Page 6652

and the Gospel. He enjoins upon them what is right and forbids them what is wrong, makes good things lawful for them and forbids them foul things, and relieves them of their burden and the shackles that bound them.”

Thus, a distinction is drawn between right and wrong: the former is commanded and the latter forbidden; and between good and foul things: the former are made lawful and the latter prohibited. Another aspect of the “criterion” is that He distinguishes between the people of truth—the rightly guided believers who set things right and perform good deeds—and the people of falsehood—the disbelievers who go astray, spread corruption and commit evil deeds. He, Exalted is He, says:

“Do those who commit evil deeds think that We will make them like those who believe and do righteous deeds, equal in their life and death? How evil is their judgment!”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“Or shall We treat those who believe and do righteous deeds like those who spread corruption on earth? Or shall We treat the God-fearing like the wicked?”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“Shall We then treat those who submit to Us like the criminals? What is the matter with you? How do you judge?”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“The two groups are like one who is blind and deaf and one who sees and hears. Are the two alike? Will you not then take heed?”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“What of one who devoutly worships during the hours of the night, prostrating and standing, fearing the Hereafter and hoping for his Lord’s mercy? Say, ‘Are those who know equal to those who do not know?’ Only people of understanding take heed.”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“The blind and the seeing are not equal, nor are darkness and light, nor shade and scorching heat. Nor are the living and the dead equal. Indeed, Allah makes whomever He wills hear, but you cannot make those in the graves hear. You are only a warner. Indeed, We have sent you with the truth as a bearer of good tidings and a warner.”

And He says:

---

## Page 6653

Exalted is He:

“Is one who was dead, then We gave him life and a light by which to walk among people, like one who is in darkness from which he cannot emerge?”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“Is one who is a believer like one who is defiantly disobedient? They are not equal.”

Thus, He—glory be to Him—has made clear the distinction between those who obey Allah and the Messenger and those who disobey Allah and the Messenger, just as He has made clear the distinction between what He has commanded and what He has forbidden.

More importantly still, He has made clear the distinction between the Creator and creation, and that no created being may equate the Creator with creation in any respect, thereby making a created being a rival to the Creator. He, Exalted is He, says:

“Among people are those who take others besides Allah as equals, loving them as they love Allah. But those who believe are stronger in their love for Allah.”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“Do you know of any equal to Him?”

“And there is none comparable to Him.”

“There is nothing like Him.”

He has set forth parables in the Quran concerning those who fail to draw this distinction, but instead set up equals to their Lord and equate Him with His creation. As they will say while screaming in the Fire:

“By Allah, we were clearly astray when we equated you with the Lord of all worlds!”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“Is He who creates like one who does not create? Will you not then take heed? If you tried to count Allah’s blessings, you could not enumerate them. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful. Allah knows what you conceal and what you reveal. Those whom they invoke besides Allah create nothing, but are themselves created. They are dead, not living, and do not know when they will be resurrected.”

He—glory be to Him—is the Creator, the All-Knowing, the Truth, the Ever-Living who never dies. Those besides Him create nothing, as He says:

“Indeed, those whom you invoke besides Allah will never create

---

## Page 6654

a fly, even if they all gathered for that purpose. And if a fly were to snatch something from them, they could not retrieve it from it. Weak are both the seeker and the sought!”

“They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves.”

This is a parable Allah has set forth. The fly is among the smallest creatures, yet all those invoked besides Allah cannot create a fly, even if they gather for that purpose. And if a fly snatches something from them, they cannot retrieve it from it.

Once it is clear that they cannot create a fly or wrest back what it snatches from them, they are all the more incapable of creating anything else or overpowering it.

An “example” is the original case and the counterpart to which something is compared, as He says:

“When the son of Mary was cited as an example, your people immediately raised a clamour over him.”

That is, when they made him a counterpart, they drew an analogy between him and their gods, saying, “If he was worshipped yet will not be punished, then the same applies to our gods.” Thus, they cited him as an example for their gods and began raising a clamour and expressing amazement, using him as an argument against the Messenger. Yet the distinction between him and their gods is plain, as He, Exalted is He, explained in His words:

“Indeed, those for whom We have already decreed the finest reward will be kept far away from it.”

And He said concerning Pharaoh:

“We made them a precedent and an example for those who came after.”

That is, an example from which lessons are drawn and against which others are measured: whoever acts as he did will receive the same recompense, so that people may take warning from him and not act as he did.

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“We have certainly sent down to you clarifying verses and an example from those who passed away before you.”

This refers to what He has recounted of the circumstances of past nations, from which lessons are drawn

---

## Page 6655

and against which the circumstances of later nations are measured, as He says:

“There is certainly a lesson in their stories for people of understanding.”

Whoever is a believer is compared with the believers of the past and knows that Allah will grant him happiness in this world and the Hereafter. Whoever is an unbeliever is compared with the unbelievers of the past and knows that Allah will bring him misery in this world and the Hereafter. As He says concerning the latter:

“Are your unbelievers better than those, or have you been granted immunity in the Scriptures?”

He also says:

“Similar ways have passed before you, so travel through the land and see what was the fate of those who denied the truth.”

And He says concerning the believers:

“Allah has promised those of you who believe and do righteous deeds that He will surely make them successors in the land, just as He made those before them successors.”

And He says:

“And remember Jonah, the man of the whale, when he went away in anger, thinking that We would not constrain him. Then he called out in the darkness, ‘There is no god but You. Glory be to You! Indeed, I have been among the wrongdoers.’”

“So We answered him and delivered him from distress. And thus do We deliver the believers.”

And He says in the account of Job:

“As a mercy from Us and a reminder for those who worship.”

“As a mercy from Us and a reminder for people of understanding.”

And He says:

“Those are the ones whom Allah has guided, so follow their guidance.”

And He says:

“Or do you think that you will enter Paradise without experiencing the like of what befell those who passed away before you? They were afflicted with poverty and hardship and were so shaken that even the Messenger and those who believed with him said, ‘When will Allah’s help come?’ Indeed, Allah’s help is near.”

And He says:

“And We relate to you accounts of the messengers through which We strengthen your heart.”

Thus, the term “example” means the counterpart against which something is measured and from which lessons are drawn; it is also used to mean

---

## Page 6656

the argument as a whole. He, glorified be He, says:

“He sets forth an example for Us, forgetting his own creation. He says, ‘Who will give life to bones when they have crumbled to dust?’”

He means that no one can revive them once they have crumbled to dust. In this denial, he likens the Creator to the created being, treating the former like the latter as incapable of reviving them. This holds whether he formulates his argument as an analogy or a categorical syllogism. This has been discussed at length elsewhere, where it has been explained that these two forms of reasoning—categorical and analogical—and the example set forth in the Quran convey the same meaning.

Thus, if you say, “Nabīdh is intoxicating, and every intoxicant is forbidden,” and establish the major premise through his saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—

“Every intoxicant is forbidden,”

this is equivalent to his saying—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—by analogy with wine; for wine was prohibited only because it intoxicates, and this quality is also present in nabīdh.

In His saying:

“An example has been set forth, so listen to it,”

He makes one of the smallest created beings an example and a counterpart from which a lesson may be drawn. If they cannot create even the lowliest of Allah’s creatures or contend with it, then they cannot create anything else. Through this, something of the Creator’s greatness becomes known, as does the fact that everything they worship besides Allah, in heaven and on earth, is powerless even before the smallest of His creatures.

It has also been said: “They made their gods counterparts to Allah, so listen to the mention of them.” This interpretation arose because its proponents did not understand the example Allah had set forth: they made the polytheists the ones who had set forth this example.

Allah has set forth examples like this in the Quran to show that a created being must not be compared to the Creator and made His rival and equal, as in His saying:

“Say, ‘Who provides for you from heaven

---

## Page 6657

and earth? Or who controls hearing and sight? Who brings the living out of the dead and the dead out of the living? Who governs all affairs?’ They will say, ‘Allah.’ Say, ‘Will you not then fear Him?’”

“That is Allah, your true Lord. What is there beyond the truth except error? How, then, are you turned away?”

“Thus your Lord’s decree has come into effect against those who defiantly disobey: they will not believe.”

“Say, ‘Can any of those you associate with Allah originate creation and then bring it back?’ Say, ‘Allah originates creation and then brings it back. How, then, are you deluded?’”

“Say, ‘Can any of those you associate with Allah guide to the truth?’ Say, ‘Allah guides to the truth. Is the One who guides to the truth more worthy of being followed, or one who cannot find the way unless guided? What is the matter with you? How do you judge?’”

“Most of them follow nothing but conjecture. Conjecture is no substitute whatsoever for the truth. Surely Allah knows all that they do.”

Having established His oneness, He likewise established prophethood, saying:

“This Quran could not have been fabricated by anyone other than Allah. Rather, it confirms what came before it and explains the Scripture in detail. There is no doubt about it: it is from the Lord of all worlds.”

“Or do they say, ‘He fabricated it’? Say, ‘Then produce a surah like it, and call upon whomever you can besides Allah, if you are truthful.’”

“Rather, they denied what they had not fully comprehended and whose fulfillment had not yet come to them.”

These people likened the created being to the Creator, and this was part of their denial. The polytheists did not regard their gods as equal to Allah in every respect. Rather, they believed that Allah was their Creator and Owner, and that they were His creatures and belonged to Him. Yet they treated Him and their gods as equals in love, reverence, supplication, worship, vows made to them, and other such matters that belong exclusively to the Lord.

---

## Page 6658

Thus, whoever sets another equal to Allah in anything that belongs exclusively to Him, glorified and exalted is He, is a polytheist. This is unlike someone who does not set anything equal to Him but commits sins while acknowledging that Allah alone is his Lord and submitting to Him out of fear of punishment for sin. Such a person must be distinguished from someone who does not acknowledge that this is forbidden.

Section

Just as He, glorified and exalted, distinguishes between things that differ, He groups together and treats equally things that are alike. In creation and command, He judges a thing as He judges its like. He neither distinguishes between two things that are alike nor treats two things that are not alike as equal. Rather, if they differ and are opposed, He does not treat them as equal.

The term “difference” in the Quran means opposition and contradiction, not merely the absence of likeness—as it does in the terminology of many theorists. Examples include His words:

“Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have found in it much contradiction.”

And His words:

“Indeed, you hold conflicting views.”

“Whoever is turned away from it is turned away.”

And His words:

“But they differed: some of them believed, while others disbelieved.”

He, glorified and exalted, has made clear that His established way neither changes nor shifts, in more than one

---

## Page 6659

place. The “established way” is a customary pattern whereby He acts in a later case as He did in its earlier counterpart. For this reason, He, glorified and exalted, commands us to draw lessons by comparison, saying:

“Indeed, in their stories there is a lesson for people of understanding.”

Drawing such a lesson means comparing something with its like and recognizing that the same judgment applies to both. As Ibn ʿAbbās said: “Why did you not compare the fingers to the teeth?” Thus, when He says:

“So take heed, O people of insight,”

and:

“Indeed, in their stories there is a lesson for people of understanding,”

this conveys that whoever acts as they did will receive the same recompense. This is so that one may beware of acting like the disbelievers and aspire to act like the believers who followed the prophets. The Most High says:

“There have been precedents before you, so travel through the land and see what was the end of those who denied the truth.”

The Most High also says:

“They almost drove you from the land to expel you from it; and then they would have remained after you only a little while.”

“Such was Our established way with the messengers We sent before you, and you will find no change in Our established way.”

The Most High also says:

“If the hypocrites, those in whose hearts is disease, and those who spread alarming rumours in Medina do not desist, We will surely give you power over them; then they will remain your neighbours there only a little while.”

“Accursed, they will be seized wherever they are found and utterly slain.”

“Such was Allah’s established way with those who passed away before, and you will find no change in Allah’s established way.”

Allah revealed this verse before the Battle of the Confederates, the ascendancy of Islam, and the humiliation of the hypocrites. Thereafter, they could no longer openly display what they had displayed previously—before and after Badr, and before and after Uhud. So they concealed their hypocrisy and kept it hidden; for this reason, he did not

---

## Page 6660

kill them, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. This is the answer offered by those who do not put heretics to death. They say: If they conceal their heresy, they cannot be put to death; but if they openly display it, they are put to death on the basis of this verse, in which He says:

“Accursed, they will be seized wherever they are found and utterly slain.”

“Such was Allah’s established way with those who passed away before, and you will find no change in Allah’s established way.”

Qatāda said: It was related to us that the hypocrites used to display the hypocrisy within themselves. Allah threatened them with this verse, and when He did so, they concealed it and kept it hidden. “Such was Allah’s established way with those who passed away before”—meaning: this is Allah’s established way with them when they openly display hypocrisy.

Muqātil ibn Hayyān said: His words, “Such was Allah’s established way with those who passed away before,” mean that just as those at Badr were killed and taken captive, so it would be with them. This is the meaning of His words: “Such was Allah’s established way with those who passed away before.”

Al-Suddī said: Hypocrisy took “three forms”: “hypocrisy” like that of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Nufayl, and Mālik ibn Dāʿis. These were leading figures among the Ansār, and they were ashamed to engage in unlawful sexual intercourse, thus preserving their honour. Regarding “those in whose hearts is disease,” he said: These were those who engaged in unlawful sexual intercourse. If they found an opportunity, they indulged in it; if they did not, they did not seek it out. And there was a form of “hypocrisy” in which men overpowered women by force. These were the ones who would sit

---

## Page 6661

by the roadside. Then come the words:

“Accursed.”

The verse then specifies:

“Wherever they are found”—committing this act of forcing themselves upon women.

Al-Suddī said: This is a ruling in the Quran that is not being applied. If one man or more were to follow a woman, overpower her and rape her, the ruling concerning them would be neither flogging nor stoning, but that they be seized and beheaded.

Al-Suddī said: His words, “Established way,” mean that this was how the preceding nations were treated. He said: Whoever forces himself upon a woman and is killed, no blood money is due from his killer, because he is a violent aggressor.

I say: This may take two forms. The first is that he is killed to repel his assault upon her, as when he overpowers her. This falls under the statement:

“Whoever is killed defending his honour is a martyr.”

She is entitled to repel him by killing him. If, however, she consented, the matter involves disagreement and distinctions; there are two well-known rulings concerning this from ʿUmar and ʿAlī. But if he has intercourse with her against her will, and she finds no one to help her against him, such offenders are of two types:

The first is one who has force at his disposal, like brigands who seize property. These are brigands whose purpose is sexual outrage, and they are to be put to death. Al-Suddī said this, and others have said it too. Abū al-Lūbī mentioned that such a case had arisen before him, and considered these offenders even more deserving of being classed as brigands.

The second is that they have no such force at their disposal, but instead do this through treachery

---

## Page 6662

and deception until, once a woman is in their hands, they rape her. Such a person is a brigand who acts by treachery, as al-Suddī said, and he too is to be put to death. If they are a group operating within a town, they are like brigands operating within a town. These questions are discussed elsewhere.

The point is that Allah has declared that His established way will neither be altered nor changed. His established way is His customary practice, whereby He treats a thing as He treated its like in the past. This entails that He, glorified is He, passes similar judgments on similar matters. Hence He says:

“Are your disbelievers better than those?”

And He says:

“Gather those who did wrong and their counterparts”—that is, those who resemble them and are their equals.

And He says:

“And when the souls are paired”—He pairs each with its counterpart.

Allah Most High also says:

“Or do you think you will enter Paradise without experiencing the like of what befell those who passed away before you?”

And He says:

“You have a good example in Abraham and those with him, when they said to their people, ‘We are free of you and whatever you worship besides Allah. We reject you, and enmity and hatred have arisen between us and you forever.’”

And He says:

“The foremost, the first of the Emigrants and the Helpers, and those who followed them in goodness—Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him. He has prepared for them Gardens beneath which rivers flow, where they will abide forever. That is the supreme triumph.”

Thus He made those who follow them in goodness share with them in the divine pleasure and Paradise mentioned here. Allah Most High has also said:

“And those who believed afterwards, emigrated, and strove alongside you—they are of you.”

And Allah Most High says:

“And those who came after them say,

---

## Page 6663

‘Our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and place no resentment in our hearts toward those who believe. Our Lord, You are indeed Most Kind, Most Merciful.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“And others among them who have not yet joined them. He is the Almighty, the All-Wise.”

Whoever follows the foremost, the first believers, is one of them. They are the best of humanity after the prophets, for Muhammad’s community is the best community ever brought forth for humanity, and they are the best of Muhammad’s community. As established through several routes of transmission in the authentic hadith collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best generation is the generation among whom I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them.”

Thus, knowing their statements concerning religious knowledge and faith, and knowing their deeds, is better and more beneficial than knowing the statements and deeds of later generations. This applies to all branches of religious knowledge and practice, including Quranic interpretation, the foundations of religion and its subsidiary matters, asceticism, worship, moral character, jihad, and other matters. They are superior to those who came after them, as the Quran and Sunnah indicate. Following their example is therefore better than following the example of later generations, and knowing their consensus and disagreements concerning religious knowledge and faith is better and more beneficial than knowing what is reported of the consensus and disagreements of others.

This is because their consensus is necessarily protected from error, and when they disagree, the truth cannot lie outside their views. The truth can therefore be sought among their positions. None of their statements may be judged erroneous until it is known that the Quran and Sunnah indicate otherwise. Allah Most High says:

“Obey Allah and obey the Messenger and those in authority among you. Then if you disagree

---

## Page 6664

over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is best and fairest in outcome.”

As for those of later generations who have not taken care to follow them and tread their path, nor have any familiarity with their words and deeds—among the practitioners of speculative theology, reasoned opinion, asceticism, and Sufism—they do not know the way of the Companions and their successors in much of what they discuss concerning religious knowledge or put into practice. You find that, in many important religious matters, these people rely chiefly on what they suppose to be consensus, although they know nothing whatsoever of the early generations’ views on those matters, or know some of their views but not the rest. Sometimes they report a consensus while knowing only their own position and that of their opponents among later groups—one, two, or three groups. At other times, they know the views of some of the early generations.

The former case is common in “questions concerning the foundations of religion and its subsidiary matters.” Thus, you find the books of speculative theologians filled with such claims: they report consensus and disagreement while knowing nothing whatsoever of what the early generations said on the matter. Indeed, the position of the early generations may lie entirely outside the positions they mention. You find this in questions concerning Allah’s speech, actions, and attributes, such as the questions of the Quran, seeing Allah, divine decree, and other matters.

When they speak of the consensus of the Muslims, they have no knowledge of that consensus. Even if it were possible to know the consensus of the Muslims, these people would not be among those who possess such knowledge, because they do not know the views of the early generations. How much more so, then, when, in the case of the Muslims, it is impossible to establish with certainty

---

## Page 6665

their consensus on disputed matters, unlike the early generations, whose consensus can often be known. When they mention disagreements among later generations, that alone does not justify treating these as matters open to scholarly judgment, in which each view is admissible and does not contravene consensus. For many of the foundational principles advanced by later generations are innovations introduced into Islam, preceded by a consensus of the early generations to the contrary. Dissent arising after the consensus of the early generations is unquestionably erroneous, as with the dissent of the Khawārij, Rāfidah, Qadariyyah, and Murjiʾah, whose positions contradicting the widely transmitted, well-known scriptural texts and the consensus of the Companions have become notorious.

This differs from known disagreements among the early generations: a view held in such a disagreement cannot be said to contravene consensus; it can only be refuted by a scriptural text. If it is said, “The Successors reached consensus on one of their two views, thereby ending the disagreement,” such a claim rests on two premises:

First: knowing that no one in the Muslim community continued to uphold the other view. This is impossible to establish.

Second: whether something of this kind ends the disagreement … (1) is well known.

Thus, a disputed view among the early generations

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Blank in the original.

---

## Page 6666

may be upheld if supported by evidence; for … (1) to the contrary, whereas a disputed view among later generations cannot … (2), because many such views were preceded by a consensus to the contrary, just as the scriptural texts indicate the contrary. Contradicting the consensus of the early generations is unquestionably erroneous.

Moreover, there is no question of religion that the early generations did not address. They must therefore have held a view that either contradicts or agrees with the view in question. We have explained at length elsewhere that their correct views are more numerous and better, and that their errors are less serious than those of later generations, whose errors are more numerous and more egregious. This applies to all the religious disciplines. There are many examples of this, more than can be exhaustively discussed here. Allah, glorified is He, knows best.

Section

It should also be understood that when the interpretation of the Quran and hadith is known from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there is no need to consult the statements of language scholars on the matter. For once their interpretation and intended meaning are known from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, there is no need to adduce the statements of language scholars or anyone else as evidence. Hence

> **Footnotes:**
> (1, 2) Blank in the original.

---

## Page 6667

the jurists said: “Terms are of three kinds”: those whose definitions are known through revelation, such as prayer and zakat; those whose definitions are known through language, such as the sun and the moon; and those whose definitions are known through custom, such as taking possession and the word “kindness” in His words: “Live with them in kindness.”

Among the greatest blessings Allah bestowed upon them was their firm adherence to the Book and the Sunnah. One of the principles agreed upon by the Companions and those who followed them in righteousness was that no one could ever be allowed to oppose the Quran with his own opinion, spiritual taste, rational judgment, analogy, or ecstatic experience. For it is established from them, through conclusive proofs and clear signs, that the Messenger brought guidance and the religion of truth, and that the Quran guides to what is most upright.

It contains accounts of those before them, news of what comes after them, and judgment concerning their affairs. It is decisive, not frivolous. Whatever tyrant abandons it, Allah will crush him; whoever seeks guidance elsewhere, Allah will lead him astray. It is Allah’s firm rope, the wise reminder, and the straight path. Desires cannot bend it, nor tongues confound it: no one can bend it to his desires or distort it with his tongue. It does not wear out through frequent repetition; when repeated time after time, it neither wears out nor becomes tiresome like other speech. Its wonders never cease, and scholars never have their fill of it. Whoever speaks by it speaks the truth; whoever acts upon it is rewarded; whoever judges by it is just; and whoever calls to it is guided to a straight path.

Thus, the Quran was the guide to be followed. Hence, there is nothing in the statements of

---

## Page 6668

any of the early generations to indicate that he opposed the Quran with reason, opinion, or analogy, or with spiritual taste, ecstatic experience, or unveiling. Nor did any of them ever say, “Reason and transmitted revelation conflict on this matter,” let alone say, “Reason must therefore take precedence, while transmitted revelation—meaning the Quran, hadith, and statements of the Companions and their successors—must either be consigned to Allah or reinterpreted.”

Nor did any of them claim to have a spiritual taste, ecstatic experience, inward address, or unveiling that contradicted the Quran and hadith. Still less did any of them claim that he received knowledge from the same source as the angel who comes to the Messenger, that he drew knowledge of Allah’s oneness from that source, and that all the prophets drew from his niche of light. Nor did any of them say, “A friend of Allah is superior to a prophet,” or make similar statements uttered by heretics. Such claims had not yet arisen among the Muslims.

Claims of this kind were known only among heretics from among the Jews or Christians. Some of them allow that someone who is not a prophet may be superior to a prophet, as they may say concerning the disciples. They regard them as messengers and say that they are superior to David and Solomon—indeed, even to Abraham and Moses—although they call them prophets. They make other such claims as well.

The early generations would not accept anything set against a verse except another verse that explained and abrogated it, or the Sunnah of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that explained it. For the Sunnah of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, clarifies the Quran, points to its meaning, and expresses it.

They would call whatever countered a verse an abrogating text. Thus, for them, abrogation was a general term for anything that removed a verse’s apparent indication of a false meaning, even if that meaning was not

---

## Page 6669

intended by it, and even if the verse’s apparent wording did not indicate it. Indeed, that meaning might not be understood from the verse at all, yet some people had understood it that way. They would therefore call whatever removed that ambiguity and misapprehension “abrogation.” This terminology, however, is not reported from every one of them.

This originates in Satan’s insinuations, after which Allah makes His verses firm. When Satan cast into people’s minds the supposition that a verse indicated a meaning it did not indicate, these scholars called whatever removed that supposition “abrogation.” Thus, they described His words:

“Fear Allah as much as you are able”

as abrogating His words:

“Fear Allah as He should be feared.”

Likewise, they described His words:

“Allah does not burden a soul beyond what it can bear”

as abrogating His words:

“Whether you reveal what is within yourselves or conceal it, Allah will call you to account for it. Then He forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills.”

There are other examples whose detailed discussion does not belong here. The point is that they agreed that nothing could be set against the Quran except the Quran itself—not opinion, rational argument, or analogy, nor spiritual taste, ecstatic experience, inspiration, or unveiling.

The earliest innovations, such as “the innovation of the Khawārij,” arose only from their misunderstanding of the Quran. They did not intend to oppose it, but understood it to mean something it did not indicate. They thought it required declaring those who committed sins to be disbelievers, on the grounds that a believer is a righteous, Allah-fearing person.

They said: “Whoever is not righteous and Allah-fearing is therefore a disbeliever and will remain in the Fire forever.”

Then they said: “And ‘Uthmān

---

## Page 6670

and ‘Alī and those who sided with them are not believers, because they ruled by other than what Allah has revealed.” Thus, their innovation rested on two premises.

The first was that anyone who contravenes the Quran through an action or a mistaken opinion is a disbeliever.

The second was that ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, and those who sided with them had done so. For this reason, one must guard against declaring Muslims disbelievers on account of sins and errors. This was the first innovation to appear in Islam: its adherents declared Muslims disbelievers and deemed their blood and property lawful. Authentic hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, condemn them and command that they be fought. Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal, may Allah be pleased with him, said: “The hadiths concerning them have been authentically transmitted through ten routes.” Muslim therefore included them in his Sahīh, while al-Bukhārī recorded a selection of them separately.

Despite this condemnation, they sought only to follow the Quran. How much worse, then, is someone whose innovation consists in opposing the Quran and turning away from it, while also declaring Muslims disbelievers—as the Jahmiyyah do?

Then, when the Shī‘ah emerged, the person who introduced Shiism was not motivated by religion; rather, his purpose was corrupt. It has even been said that he was a hypocrite and a heretic. Their innovation is therefore founded upon fabricating lies against the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and rejecting authentic hadiths as false. Hence, lying is more prevalent among them than among any other sect of the Muslim community. This is unlike the Khawārij, among whom no one is known to lie.

---

## Page 6671

As for the Shī‘ah, the reports transmitted by any of their shaykhs can scarcely be trusted, so prevalent is lying among them. For this reason, the compilers of authentic hadith collections avoided them. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim therefore transmit ‘Alī’s hadiths only through members of his household—such as his sons al-Hasan, al-Husayn, and Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyyah, and his scribe ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn Abī Rāfi‘—or through the companions of Ibn Mas‘ūd and others, such as ‘Abīdah al-Salmānī, al-Hārith al-Taymī, Qays ibn ‘Abbād, and their like. These men were truthful in what they narrated from ‘Alī, which is why the compilers of authentic hadith collections included their reports.

These two groups—the Khawārij and the Shī‘ah—emerged after the killing of ‘Uthmān. During the caliphates of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, and the early part of ‘Uthmān’s caliphate, in the first year of his rule, the Muslims were united, with no disputes among them. Then, towards the end of ‘Uthmān’s caliphate, events occurred that brought about a measure of division. A group of troublemakers and wrongdoers rose up and killed ‘Uthmān, and the Muslims became divided after his killing.

When the Muslims fought one another at Siffīn and agreed to appoint two arbitrators, the Khawārij rebelled against the Commander of the Faithful, ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib. They broke away from him and the Muslim community, withdrawing to a place called Harūrā’. The Commander of the Faithful left them alone, saying: “You are entitled to this from us: we will not withhold your rightful share of the public revenues, nor will we bar you from the mosques.” He continued to leave them alone until they deemed the Muslims’ blood and property lawful: they killed ‘Abd Allāh ibn Khabbāb and raided the Muslims’ grazing herds. ‘Alī then knew that they were the group mentioned by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace

---

## Page 6672

when he said:

“Any one of you would consider his prayer insignificant compared with theirs, his fasting compared with theirs, and his recitation compared with theirs. They recite the Quran, but it does not go beyond their throats. They pass out of the religion as an arrow passes through its quarry. Their distinguishing sign is that among them is a man with a malformed hand, on which is a piece of flesh bearing a few hairs.”

Another narration states:

“They kill the Muslims and leave the idolaters alone.”

‘Alī addressed the people and told them what he had heard from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said: “These are the people: they have shed blood that is forbidden to shed and raided people’s grazing herds.” He then fought them and found the distinguishing sign, though it had nearly gone undiscovered, and prostrated to Allah in gratitude.

The Shī‘ah also emerged during his time, but they concealed their beliefs, not disclosing them to ‘Alī or his followers. They were, in fact, three groups:

“One group” said that he was a god. When he discovered them, he burned them with fire and dug trenches for them by the entrance to the mosque of Banū Kindah. It is said that he recited:

When I saw that the matter was abhorrent,
I stoked my fire and summoned Qanbar.

Al-Bukhārī narrated in his Sahīh that Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“Some heretics were brought to ‘Alī, and he burned them with fire. Had it been me, I would not have burned them, because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade punishing with Allah’s punishment. Instead, I would have struck off their heads, because he said:

‘Whoever changes his religion, kill him.’”

---

## Page 6673

The position stated by Ibn ‘Abbās is that of most jurists. It has also been reported that he granted them three days’ respite.

The second group were “the revilers.” ‘Alī had received word that Ibn al-Sawdā’ was reviling Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, so he sought him out. It is said that he sought him out to kill him, but Ibn al-Sawdā’ fled from him.

The third group were “those who gave him precedence,” who regarded him as superior to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. It is reported from him through numerous independent chains of transmission that he said:

“The best of this community after its Prophet is Abū Bakr, then ‘Umar.”

Al-Bukhārī narrated this in his Sahīh from Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyyah, who asked his father: “Who is the best of people after the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?”

He replied: “Abū Bakr.”

He asked: “Then who?”

He replied: “‘Umar.”

The early Shī‘ah did not dispute the superiority of Abū Bakr and ‘Umar; their disagreement concerned only ‘Alī and ‘Uthmān. Thus, Sharīk ibn ‘Abd Allāh said:

“The best of people after the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, are Abū Bakr and ‘Umar.”

He was asked: “You say this, although you are one of the Shī‘ah?”

He replied: “All the Shī‘ah used to hold this view. ‘Alī himself said this from his pulpit—are we then to disbelieve what he said?”

For this reason, Sufyān al-Thawrī said:

“Whoever regards ‘Alī as superior to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar has disparaged the Emigrants and the Helpers. I do not believe that any deed of his ascends to Allah, Mighty and Majestic, while he holds this view.”

Abū Dāwūd narrated this in his Sunan. He appears to have been alluding critically to al-Hasan ibn Sālih ibn Hayy, for the Sālihiyyah Zaydīs, the most upright of the Zaydī groups, are named after him.

---

## Page 6674

But at that time the Shī‘ah had no organized community, leader, territory, or military force with which to fight the Muslims. These belonged only to the Khārijites, who set themselves apart with their own leader, community, and territory. They called their territory the “abode of migration” and regarded Muslim territory as an abode of unbelief and war.

Both groups disparage the Muslim rulers—indeed, they declare them disbelievers. Most Khārijites declare ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, and those loyal to them disbelievers, while the Rāfidah curse Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, and those loyal to them. However, it was among the Khārijites that overt wrongdoing appeared: bloodshed, seizure of property, and armed rebellion. For this reason, authentic hadiths enjoined fighting them. The hadiths condemning them and commanding that they be fought are exceedingly numerous. Hadith scholars regard them as transmitted through numerous independent chains, like the hadiths concerning the vision of Allah, the punishment and trial of the grave, intercession, and the Cistern.

Hadiths have also been narrated condemning the Qadariyyah and the Murji’ah. Some were narrated by compilers of the Sunan, such as Abū Dāwūd and Ibn Mājah. Some accept these reports and support their reliability, while other scholars have criticized them and judged them weak. What is reliably established in condemnation of the Qadariyyah and similar groups, however, consists of reports from the Companions, such as Ibn ‘Umar and Ibn ‘Abbās.

As for the term “Rāfidah,” it first appeared in Islam when Zayd ibn ‘Alī ibn al-Husayn rose in revolt early in the second century, during the caliphate of Hishām ibn

---

## Page 6675

‘Abd al-Malik, and the Shī‘ah followed him. When he was asked about Abū Bakr and ‘Umar, he affirmed his allegiance to them and prayed for Allah’s mercy upon them. Some people then rejected him, so he said: “You have rejected me! You have rejected me!” Thus they came to be called the Rāfidah. The Rāfidah give their allegiance to his brother, Abū Ja‘far Muhammad ibn ‘Alī, while the Zaydiyyah give their allegiance to Zayd and take their name from him. From that time onward, the Shī‘ah were divided into the Zaydiyyah and the Imāmī Rāfidah.

Then, toward the end of the Companions’ era, the Qadariyyah emerged. Their innovation arose from their minds’ inability to reconcile belief in Allah’s decree with belief in His commands and prohibitions, His promises and warnings. They thought it impossible to believe in both. They already believed in Allah’s religion, His commands and prohibitions, and His promises and warnings, but supposed that, if these were true, He could not have known before issuing His commands who would obey and who would disobey. For they thought that someone who knew what would happen could not rightly issue a command while knowing that the person commanded would disobey rather than obey him. They also thought that, if He knew people would cause corruption, it would not be right for Him to create those whom He knew would do so.

When their denial of the prior divine decree reached the Companions, they condemned it vehemently and disavowed them. ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar even said:

“Tell those people that I disavow them and they have nothing to do with me. By the One in whose name ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar swears, if any one of them possessed gold equal to Mount Uhud and spent it in charity, Allah would not accept it from him until he believed in divine decree.”

He then related the hadith of Gabriel from his father. This is the first hadith in Sahīh Muslim. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim also narrated an abridged version through Abū Hurayrah.

Debate over divine decree then became widespread, with most of it taking place in Basra and Syria

---

## Page 6676

and some of it in Medina. Their more moderate members and the majority of them came to affirm the prior divine decree and the previously written record. People’s disagreement then came to concern “the will” and “the creation of human actions.” They divided into two parties over this. The “negators” said: “Will means nothing other than volition. He willed only what He commanded, and He did not create any human actions.”

Opposing them were those who debated divine decree among the “advocates of compulsion,” such as al-Jahm ibn Safwān and others like him. They said: “Will means nothing other than volition, and commands and prohibitions do not entail willing.” They also said: “The servant has no action whatsoever and no power; rather, Allah alone acts and possesses power.” Alongside this, Jahm denied the divine names and attributes. It is reported that he said: “Allah is not to be called a ‘thing,’ nor by any other name applied to human beings, except ‘the All-Powerful,’ since the servant possesses no power.”

The Khawārij had already spoken about declaring sinners among those who face the qiblah to be unbelievers. They said: “They are unbelievers who will remain in the Fire forever.” People then debated this matter, and the Qadariyyah entered the debate after the death of al-Hasan al-Basrī. ‘Amr ibn ‘Ubayd and his followers said: “They are neither Muslims nor unbelievers; rather, they occupy a position between the two, and they will remain in the Fire forever.” Thus, they agreed with the Khawārij that these people would remain there forever and that they possessed nothing of Islam or faith. However, they did not call them unbelievers, and they withdrew from

---

## Page 6677

the circle of al-Hasan al-Basrī’s companions, such as Qatādah, Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī, and others like them. From that time, after al-Hasan’s death, they were called the Mu‘tazilah. It is reported that Qatādah used to say: “Those are the Mu‘tazilah.”

People disputed “religious designations and rulings”: that is, religious designations such as Muslim, believer, unbeliever, and sinner, and the rulings concerning such people in this world and the Hereafter. The Mu‘tazilah agreed with the Khawārij regarding their fate in the Hereafter, but not their treatment in this world. Thus, they did not deem it lawful to shed their blood and seize their property as the Khawārij did. As for religious designations, they introduced “the position between the two positions.” This was the distinctive doctrine of the Mu‘tazilah alone; others shared all their other views.

The Murji’ah also emerged, most of them from Kūfah. Neither the companions of ‘Abd Allāh nor Ibrāhīm al-Nakha‘ī and others like him belonged to the Murji’ah. They took the opposite position to the Khawārij and the Mu‘tazilah, saying: “Actions are not part of faith.” This innovation was the least serious of innovations, since much of the dispute over it concerned names and wording rather than substantive rulings. The jurists to whom this view is attributed, such as Hammād ibn Abī Sulaymān, Abū Hanīfah, and others, agreed with the rest of Ahl al-Sunnah that Allah punishes in the Fire those perpetrators of major sins whom He chooses to punish, then brings them out through intercession, as established in authentic hadiths, and that faith necessarily requires

---

## Page 6678

that one profess it verbally. They also agreed that the prescribed deeds are obligatory and that whoever neglects them deserves blame and punishment. Thus, their disagreement over whether deeds form part of faith, over qualifying one’s profession of faith, and over similar matters was largely a dispute over wording. For when faith is mentioned without qualification, it includes deeds, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Faith has sixty-odd branches—or seventy-odd branches—the highest of which is saying, ‘There is no god but Allah,’ and the lowest is removing something harmful from the road. Modesty is a branch of faith.”

When deeds are conjoined to faith, as in His words:

“Indeed, those who believe and do righteous deeds,”

faith is then mentioned with its scope qualified by that conjunction. Here, it may be said that deeds are included in faith and are conjoined to it as the particular is conjoined to the general. Alternatively, it may be said that they are not included in it, but that this applies when they are conjoined. The same applies to the terms “the poor” and “the needy”: when either is mentioned alone, it encompasses the other; when they are conjoined, they denote two distinct categories, as in the verse concerning alms:

“Alms are only for the poor and the needy…”

And in the verse concerning expiation, He says:

“Its expiation is to feed ten needy people.”

He also says:

“But if you conceal them and give them to the poor, that is better for you.”

Here, “the poor” and “the needy” mean the same thing. This distinction concerning faith likewise applies to the terms righteousness, piety, and right conduct, and to sin, transgression, and wrongdoing. Their meanings differ depending on whether they occur separately or together, as is apparent to anyone who reflects on the Quran.

This has been discussed at great length in the treatment of faith and the commentary on the hadith of Gabriel, which explains that faith is rooted in the heart: namely, belief in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers, as in

---

## Page 6679

the Musnad, where the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, is reported to have said:

“Islam is outwardly manifest, while faith is in the heart.”

He also said in an authentic hadith:

“Indeed, there is a piece of flesh in the body: if it is sound, the rest of the body is sound; and if it is corrupt, the rest of the body is corrupt. It is the heart.”

Thus, when faith is present in the heart, the heart is sound, and the rest of the body must therefore be sound. This soundness is the fruit of what is in the heart. Hence some have said, “Deeds are the fruit of faith.” Since the soundness of the body necessarily follows from the soundness of the heart, it is included in the term “faith,” as the Quran and Sunnah state in numerous places.

In sum, the eminent figures accused of irjā’, such as Talq ibn Habīb, Ibrāhīm al-Taymī, and others like them, held the form of irjā’ just described. They also did not qualify their profession of faith, saying, “Faith is the faith present within us, and we know with certainty that we believe.” They regarded such qualification as doubt. By contrast, ‘Abd Allah ibn Mas‘ūd and his companions would qualify their profession of faith. A report relates that he abandoned this practice after some of Mu‘ādh’s companions said to him what they said. Ahmad, however, rejected this and judged the report weak.

People came to hold three positions regarding qualifying one’s profession of faith. One holds that such qualification is obligatory, and that whoever does not qualify it is an innovator. Another holds that such qualification is prohibited, since it implies doubt concerning faith.

---

## Page 6680

And the third position—the most balanced and just—is that qualifying one’s profession of faith is permissible in one respect, while omitting that qualification is permissible in another. If a person means, “I do not know whether I am fulfilling everything Allah has made obligatory upon me or whether He accepts my deeds,” without intending any doubt about what is in his heart, then his qualification is commendable. His intention is to avoid declaring himself pure or asserting with certainty that he has performed a deed as commanded and that it has been accepted from him. Sins are many, and people in general have reason to fear hypocrisy.

Ibn Abī Mulaykah said:

“I met thirty of Muhammad’s Companions, all of whom feared hypocrisy for themselves. Not one of them said that his faith was like the faith of Gabriel and Michael.”

At the beginning of his Sahīh, al-Bukhārī devoted chapters to “Faith and the Refutation of the Murji’ah.”

One of Abū Hanīfah’s followers who wrote on this subject stated:

“Abū Hanīfah, Abū Yūsuf, and Muhammad disapproved of a man saying, ‘My faith is like the faith of Gabriel and Michael’—Muhammad said, ‘Because their certainty is greater’—or, ‘My faith is like Gabriel’s faith,’ or, ‘My faith is like Abū Bakr’s faith,’ or, ‘Like this person’s faith.’ Rather, he should say, ‘I believe in what Gabriel and Abū Bakr believed in.’”

Abū Hanīfah and his followers do not permit qualifying one’s profession of faith on the grounds that deeds are part of faith. They condemn the Murji’ah, by whom they mean those who do not consider it obligatory to fulfil religious duties or avoid prohibited acts, but regard faith alone as sufficient.

The prohibition of qualifying one’s profession of faith has also been justified on the grounds that faith cannot validly be made conditional, since something made conditional does not come into existence until its condition is fulfilled. This is what they said concerning a man’s statement to his wife:

“You are divorced if Allah wills.”

Thus, if faith is made contingent

---

## Page 6681

on a condition, then, like anything else made conditional, it comes into being only when that condition is fulfilled.

They said:

“The fulfilment of the condition of divine willing, for which the speaker hopes, cannot be ascertained until the Day of Resurrection. If he makes his resolve to act contingent upon assent and affirmation, then the divine will has become manifest and the commitment is valid, so there is no point in qualifying it. Moreover, a qualification appended to a statement nullifies it, so the profession of faith and the commitment no longer leave one a believer. The person who attaches this qualification to his faith may imagine that his assent remains intact, whereas the qualification removes it.”

I say:

Their reasoning in this matter applies only to someone who makes his initial profession of faith conditional upon Allah’s will. An example is someone who wishes to enter Islam and is told, “Believe,” but replies, “I shall believe if Allah wills,” or, “I have believed if He wills,” or, “I have embraced Islam if Allah wills,” or, “I bear witness, if Allah wills, that there is no god but Allah, and I bear witness, if Allah wills, that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.”

Those among the early and later generations who qualified their profession of faith did not intend to qualify the act of professing faith itself. Rather, their qualification concerned their report of the faith they already possessed. They qualified it either because faith in its unrestricted sense entails entry into Paradise, while they did not know how their lives would end—as though asking a man, “Are you a believer?” meant asking him, “Are you a believer in Allah’s sight, destined for Paradise?” He would then reply, “I am, if Allah wills”—or because they did not know whether they had fulfilled all that obligatory faith requires.

For this reason, when some of them were asked, “Are you a believer?” they would answer, “I believe in Allah, His angels, and His Books,” affirming this categorically without making it conditional. Or one would say, “If you mean

---

## Page 6682

the faith that safeguards my life and property, then I am a believer. But if you mean His words:

‘The believers are only those whose hearts tremble when Allah is mentioned, whose faith increases when His verses are recited to them, and who put their trust in their Lord.’

‘Those who establish prayer and spend out of what We have provided for them.’

‘It is they who are truly believers.’

And His words:

‘The believers are only those who believe in Allah and His Messenger, then never doubt, and strive with their wealth and their lives in Allah’s cause. It is they who are truthful.’

Then I am a believer, if Allah wills.”

As for the act of professing faith itself, no one attached this qualification to it, nor was such a qualification prescribed. Rather, everyone who came to faith and embraced Islam did so categorically, without making it conditional.

It thus becomes clear that the disagreement on this issue may be merely verbal: what the former prohibited is not what the latter approved and enjoined. Whoever spoke categorically was affirming the present state of his heart. This is valid and does not contradict making the completeness of faith and one’s final outcome conditional upon Allah’s will. However, the former do not regard deeds as part of faith, so what they call faith amounts to what the latter call Islam.

The well-known position among the scholars of hadith is that one does not qualify one’s profession of Islam. This is also the well-known position reported from Ahmad, may Allah be pleased with him, although permission to qualify it has also been reported from him. This has been discussed at length in the commentary on the hadith of Gabriel and other texts concerning faith in the Quran and Sunnah.

---

## Page 6683

If a man says to his wife, “You are divorced, if Allah wills,” there is a well-known disagreement concerning this. We have favoured distinguishing between two cases: these words may be intended either to effect a divorce or to prevent it from taking effect. If he means, “You are divorced by this pronouncement,” then saying “if Allah wills” is like saying “by Allah’s will.” Allah has willed the divorce when the man pronounces it, so it takes effect. But if he makes it conditional to prevent it from taking effect, or makes it conditional upon Allah willing it at some later time, these words do not effect a divorce until he subsequently pronounces divorce, for it is then that Allah has willed that she be divorced.

The claim that Allah’s will makes it immediately effective is incorrect. Rather, we know with certainty that divorce takes effect only when the woman is divorced through a pronouncement by her husband or someone acting in his place, whether a guardian or an agent. Without a pronouncement of divorce, no divorce ever takes effect. Thus, if he says, “You are divorced, if Allah wills,” intending a genuine condition, the divorce takes effect only through a subsequent pronouncement. The same applies if he intends the condition to prevent it from taking effect immediately. But if he intends to effect the divorce immediately and invokes Allah’s will to affirm and confirm it, then these words do effect a divorce.

I know of no one who, in making the profession by which he enters into faith, has made it conditional upon Allah’s will. If someone does make it conditional, and by “I am a believer, if Allah wills” he means, “I shall believe later,” then he has not become a believer. He is like someone who is asked, “Will you embrace the religion of Islam?” and answers, “I will, if Allah wills.” Such a person has not embraced Islam but remains in disbelief. If, however, he means, “I have already believed, and my faith is by Allah’s will,” then he has become a believer. Yet the wording, left unqualified, admits of

---

## Page 6684

both meanings. It is therefore impermissible to use such wording without qualification when making the profession by which one enters the faith. Moreover, the basic rule is that only future matters are made conditional upon Allah’s will; past and present matters are not. Those who added this qualification did not do so when entering the faith, as explained above. How could they, when they were commanded to say:

“We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us and what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, and the Tribes.”

Allah Most High also says:

“The Messenger has believed in what was revealed to him from his Lord, and so have the believers. All have believed in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers.”

Thus, He states that they believed: their faith had certainly come into being, without any qualification.

Everyone must say, “We believe in Allah and what has been revealed to us,” as Allah commanded, without adding any qualification. This is agreed upon among Muslims. None of the early generations ever added such a qualification in a case like this. The discussion concerns only a person describing himself as a believer, just as he might describe himself as righteous and God-fearing. In their understanding, saying to someone, “You are a believer,” is like asking him, “Are you righteous and God-fearing?” If he says, “I am righteous and God-fearing,” he has praised his own purity. Instead, he says, “If Allah wills; I hope to be so.”

This is because complete faith is followed by Allah’s acceptance of it, His rewarding it, and the angel’s recording it. The qualification refers to these matters, not to what the person knows about himself and what has already come into being and become established. It is not valid to make that conditional upon Allah’s will. Rather, one says, “This has come about through Allah’s will, bounty, and kindness.” In this context, saying “if Allah wills” means “since Allah has willed it”: it is an affirmation, not a condition.

---

## Page 6685

A man may also say, “By Allah, such-and-such will certainly happen, if Allah wills,” while being certain that it will happen. What is made conditional is the action itself, as in His words:

“You will surely enter the Sacred Mosque, if Allah wills.”

Allah knows that they will enter it. A person may also say, “I will certainly do such-and-such, if Allah wills,” without being certain that it will happen, but hoping that it will. Thus he says, “It will happen, if Allah wills.” His resolve to do it may be firm, although he is not certain that what he has resolved upon will occur. His resolve may also be hesitant and itself conditional upon Allah’s will. Yet whenever the intended action is conditional, the continuation of the resolve must also be conditional. For, on this assumption, the resolve is conditional in such a case, whether at its inception or in its continuation. Hence, a man who makes his pronouncement of divorce conditional does not break his oath. The particle in (“if”) does not preserve the resolve; thus, when it precedes a past-tense verb, that verb necessarily takes on a future meaning. You say, “If Zayd comes, it will be so.”

“If they believe in the same as you believe in, then they are rightly guided; but if they turn away, they are only in dissension.”

When the past is intended, the particle in (“if”) is used, as in His words:

“If you love Allah, then follow me.”

A distinction is therefore made between saying, “I am a believer, if Allah wills,” and saying, “If Allah has willed my faith.”

The same applies if what he means is, “I do not know in what state my life will end.” It was said to Ibn Mas'ūd, “So-and-so testifies that he is a believer.” He replied, “Then let him testify that he is among the people of Paradise.” What he meant was this: to testify that one is a believer in Allah’s sight is to testify that one will die in faith. The same applies if he means, “My faith has come about through Allah’s will.” As for someone who does not add this qualification and says, “I do not doubt the faith in my heart,” there is no blame upon him if

---

## Page 6686

he does not commend himself or assert with certainty that he acts as commanded and that Allah has accepted his deeds—even if he does not claim that his faith is like the faith of Gabriel, Abū Bakr, and ‘Umar, or make similar statements of the Murji’ah. Thus Mis‘ar ibn Kidām used to say, “I do not doubt my faith.” Ahmad said:

“He was not one of the Murji’ah, for the Murji’ah are those who say that deeds are not part of faith, whereas he used to say, ‘They are part of faith, but I do not doubt my faith.’”

Al-Thawrī used to say to Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah, “Will you not tell him to stop saying this?” They belonged to the same tribe. This matter has been discussed at length elsewhere. The point here is that this disagreement among people of knowledge and religious devotion was like their disagreements over many rulings. All of them were people of faith and the Quran.

As for Jahm, he maintained that faith consists solely in the heart’s assent, even if one does not profess it verbally. This view is not known to have been held by any of the scholars or leading authorities of the Muslim community. Indeed, Ahmad, Wakī‘, and others declared those who held this view to be unbelievers. Yet it was the view defended by al-Ash‘arī and most of his followers. Nevertheless, they also said, “Anyone whom the revealed law judges to be an unbeliever, we likewise judge to be an unbeliever; and we take the Lawgiver’s declaration of his unbelief as evidence that his heart is devoid of knowledge.” Their views and those of others concerning faith have been discussed at length.

---

## Page 6687

The fundamental premise from which this dispute arose was the belief that a believer could have no unbelief or hypocrisy within him. Some imagined that there was consensus on this, as al-Ash‘arī claimed. This was the root of the Murji’ah’s doctrine, just as the root of the Qadarī doctrine was their inability to believe in both revealed law and divine decree. Having adopted this premise, they split into two camps.

The Khawārij and Mu‘tazilah said: “We know with certainty that deeds are part of faith. Whoever abandons them has therefore abandoned part of faith, and when part of it disappears, all of it disappears, because faith is indivisible, and faith and hypocrisy cannot coexist within a person. Thus, those who commit sins will remain in the Fire forever, since they possess no faith at all.”
