The Murji’ah—both their moderates and their extremists, such as the Jahmiyyah—said: “We know that sinners among those who face the qiblah will not remain in the Fire forever; rather, they will emerge from it, as established by hadiths transmitted through numerous independent chains. We also know from the Book, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the leading scholars that they are not unbelievers who have apostatized. For the Book commands that the thief’s hand be cut off, not that he be killed, and the Sunnah prescribes flogging the drinker, not killing him. If these people were unbelievers who had apostatized, they would have to be put to death.”

This made the weakness of the Khawārij’s position apparent to the Mu‘tazilah, who consequently differed with them over the rulings applicable to such people in this world.

The Khawārij accept from the Sunnah only what clarifies statements left unelaborated, not what, in their view, contradicts the apparent meaning of the Quran. Thus, they do not stone the adulterer, nor do they recognize, for theft,

---

## Page 6688

a minimum threshold. Accordingly, they may say: “The Quran does not prescribe putting an apostate to death.” Thus, in their view, apostates may fall into two categories.

We know the “views of the Khawārij” only through what others have transmitted from them. We have not encountered any book they authored, as we have the works of the Mu‘tazilah, the Rāfidah, the Zaydiyyah, the Karrāmiyyah, the Ash‘ariyyah, the Sālimiyyah, the adherents of the four schools of law, the Zāhiriyyah, the schools of the hadith scholars, the philosophers, the Sufis, and others of their kind. Their views have been discussed in detail elsewhere.

People follow “different approaches” in arranging the followers of deviant doctrines. Some arrange them according to when they first appeared, beginning with the Khawārij. Others arrange them according to the relative mildness or severity of their doctrines, beginning with the Murji’ah and ending with the Jahmiyyah. This was the approach of many followers of Ahmad—may Allah be pleased with him—such as his son ‘Abd Allāh and others, al-Khallāl, Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Battah and their peers, and Abū al-Faraj al-Maqdisī. Both groups end with the Jahmiyyah, because their innovations are the gravest.

Al-Bukhārī likewise followed this approach in his Sahīh: he began with the “Book of Faith and Refutation of the Murji’ah” and concluded it with the “Book of Divine Oneness and Refutation of the Heretics and the Jahmiyyah.”

---

## Page 6689

When writers began composing works on speculative theology, they placed divine oneness and the attributes first, so that their discussion began with the Jahmiyyah. Abū al-Qāsim al-Tabarī likewise arranged his book on the foundations of the Sunnah in this way. Al-Bayhaqī devoted a separate work to each subject: he wrote works on the divine attributes, divine decree, the branches of faith, the proofs of prophethood, and resurrection and rising from the dead. A detailed treatment of these matters belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that the dispute over “religious designations and rulings” concerning faith and Islam arose from their assumption that faith was indivisible. The former group therefore said: “When someone commits a sin, part of his faith disappears; consequently, all of it disappears, and he will remain in the Fire forever.” The Jahmiyyah and the Murji’ah replied: “We know that he will not remain in the Fire forever and that he is not an unbeliever who has apostatized. Rather, he is one of the Muslims. Since he is a Muslim, he must be a believer possessing complete faith, not merely a portion of it.” This was because, in their view, faith was indivisible.

They therefore had to make faith a single thing shared by all who face the Kaaba in prayer. The jurists of the Murji’ah said: “It is affirmation in the heart and profession with the tongue.” The Jahmiyyah subsequently said: “Verbal affirmation may not be obligatory if a man is unable to speak or is under compulsion. What is indispensable, therefore, is affirmation in the heart.” The Murji’ah said: “When a man embraces Islam, he is a believer before any deeds become obligatory upon him.”

All these groups denied that faith “decreases.” Yet it has been reliably reported from the Companions

---

## Page 6690

that faith increases and decreases. This is also the position of the leading scholars of the Sunnah. Ibn al-Mubārak used to say, “It varies in degree and increases,” but refrained from using the word “decreases.” Regarding the view that it does not decrease, two positions are reported from Mālik. The Quran explicitly mentions its increase in several places, and the scriptural texts indicate its decrease, as in the Prophet’s statement:

“The adulterer does not commit adultery while being a believer.”

There are other similar texts. However, this wording is known only from his description of women as:

“Deficient in intellect and religion.”

He counted a woman’s not fasting or praying during menstruation as a deficiency in her religion. More than one scholar has cited this as evidence that faith decreases.

The underlying principle of Ahl al-Sunnah is that faith varies in degree in two respects: what the Lord commands and what the servant does.

As for the first, the faith required of a particular believer is not identical to that required of every believer. Initially, the Muslims were commanded to observe a certain measure of faith. Later, they were given further commands and were commanded to abandon things they had previously been commanded to do, as in the case of the direction of prayer. Initially, faith included believing that it was obligatory to face Jerusalem. Later, faith included the prohibition on facing it and the obligation to face the Kaaba. Thus, the requirements of faith varied within a single revealed law.

Moreover, whoever is obliged to perform Hajj, pay zakat, or engage in jihad is required, as part of faith, to know what he has been commanded to do and to believe that Allah has imposed upon him what is not obligatory

---

## Page 6691

upon others except in a general sense, whereas he is required to believe in it in detail. Likewise, when a man first embraces Islam, he is required only to make a general affirmation. Then, when the time for prayer comes, he must believe that it is obligatory and perform it. Thus, people are not equal in the faith they have been commanded to have. This is one of the fundamental errors of the Murji’ah: they assume that faith is a single, uniform thing and that all those accountable before Allah are equal in it. They therefore say that the faith of the angels, the prophets, and the most sinful of people is equal, just as, when a sinful person utters the two testimonies of faith or recites the Opening Chapter of the Quran, his words are the same as those of other people.

The response to them is as follows: It has become clear that the faith Allah has made obligatory upon His servants varies in kind and degree, and that they differ greatly in this regard. The angels are required to have faith that is not required of human beings; the prophets are required to have faith that is not required of others; scholars are required to have what is not required of others; and rulers are required to have what is not required of others. This does not mean merely that they are required to perform certain deeds, but also that they are required to give assent and affirmation.

Although people are required to affirm in general everything the Messenger brought, most of them do not know the details of everything he conveyed. How, then, can they be commanded to affirm in detail what they do not know? Nor is a servant obliged to know about deeds he has not been commanded to perform, or to know the commands concerning them. Whoever is commanded to perform Hajj must know the rites of Hajj he has been commanded to perform and believe in them. He is therefore required to have a measure of faith

---

## Page 6692

and deeds beyond what is required of others. Likewise, whoever is commanded to pay zakah must know what Allah has commanded concerning it, believe in it, and act upon it in ways not required of others. Thus, he is required to possess knowledge and faith and perform deeds that are not required of others—if knowledge and deeds are regarded as outside faith. If all of these are included within the meaning of faith, the point is stronger still. In either case, he is required to have faith that is not required of others.

For this reason, some people may believe in the Messenger in general terms, but when further matters arise, they do not believe in them and thus become hypocrites. This happened to a group who became hypocrites when the direction of prayer was changed to the Kaaba, and another group who became hypocrites when the Muslims were defeated at Uhud, and in similar cases.

This is why Allah describes the hypocrites in the Quran as having believed and then disbelieved, as mentioned in Surah al-Munāfiqūn. Something similar is mentioned in Surah al-Baqarah, where He says:

“Their example is that of one who kindled a fire, but when it illuminated what was around him, Allah took away their light and left them in darkness, unable to see.

Deaf, mute and blind—so they will not return.”

Some of the early generations said: “They knew, then denied; they saw, then became blind.” Among these were those who initially believed in general terms, but then matters arose that they did not believe

---

## Page 6693

in, and so became hypocrites inwardly. Unable to declare their apostasy openly, they instead voiced their hypocrisy among their close associates. This is as Allah described them concerning jihad, saying:

“But when a clear, decisive surah is revealed and fighting is mentioned in it, you see those in whose hearts is disease looking at you like someone overcome by death. So woe to them!

Obedience and fitting words would have been better. Then, when the matter was settled, had they been true to Allah, it would have been better for them.”

In short, it is beyond dispute that people’s circumstances differ with respect to the faith Allah has enjoined, and their faith and religious practice differ in degree accordingly. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, described women as “deficient in intellect and religion.” Explaining the deficiency in their religion, he said: “When a woman menstruates, she neither fasts nor prays.”

This is something Allah has commanded. Thus, this is not a deficiency in her religion for which she is punished. Rather, it is a deficiency insofar as she has not been commanded to perform these acts of worship in this condition, whereas the man’s religious practice is complete in this respect, since he is commanded to worship in every condition. This shows that someone who is commanded to perform an act of obedience and performs it is superior to someone who is not commanded to perform it, even though the latter is not disobedient. The former is superior in religion and faith, while the latter is neither deserving of punishment nor blameworthy.

This increase resembles the increase in faith attained through voluntary acts of worship. Here, however, the increase comes through something obligatory for one person but not obligatory for another. If he were to forgo this additional measure on this basis, he would not deserve punishment for forgoing it; the other person likewise would not deserve punishment for forgoing it. Yet the former’s faith is more complete. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “The believers most complete in faith are those best in character.”

---

## Page 6694

This makes clear that faith varies in degree both in what is commanded of it and in the reports that must be believed.

The second type concerns differences in how people fulfil the requirements of faith, even when their obligations are the same. This is thought to be the point of dispute, although both types are disputed. People differ in this respect too: the faith of a thief, an adulterer or a drinker is not like that of others, nor is the faith of someone who fulfils the obligations like that of someone who neglects some of them. Just as the religious commitment, righteousness and piety of one are not like those of the other—rather, one surpasses the other in religious commitment, righteousness and piety—so too does he surpass him in faith. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The believers most complete in faith are those best in character.”

Faith and hypocrisy may coexist in a person, as related in the two Sahīh collections from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Whoever possesses four traits is a pure hypocrite, and whoever possesses one of them has a trait of hypocrisy until he abandons it: when he speaks, he lies; when entrusted with something, he betrays that trust; when he makes a covenant, he breaks it; and when he disputes, he transgresses all bounds.”

The fundamental premise of these people is that faith is neither divisible nor admits of degrees. Rather, it is a single thing in which all people are equal, both with respect to the faith the Lord has made obligatory and with respect to the deeds a person performs. They erred in both respects, then split into different groups, as explained earlier. The Murji’ah came to hold three positions. Their scholars and imams are the best among them

---

## Page 6695

in their teaching, namely that faith consists of assent in the heart and profession by the tongue. The Jahmiyyah said that it consists solely of assent in the heart. The Karrāmiyyah said that it consists solely of verbal profession. Thus, whoever makes this profession is a believer whose faith is complete. However, if he affirms it in his heart, he is among the people of Paradise; if he denies it in his heart, he is a hypocrite and a believer who belongs among the people of Hell.

This position is peculiar to the Karrāmiyyah, who invented it. No one before them held it, and it is the most recently introduced position concerning faith. Some people report that they hold that whoever professes faith with his tongue but not in his heart is among the people of Paradise. This misrepresents their position. Rather, they say that he is a believer whose faith is complete, yet belongs among the people of Hell. Their position therefore entails that a believer whose faith is complete will be punished in Hell—indeed, will remain there forever. Yet it is established through mass transmission from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that:

“Whoever has an atom’s weight of faith in his heart will come out of it.”

If they say that he will not remain there forever, despite being a hypocrite, their position entails that hypocrites will come out of Hell. Yet Allah has said of the hypocrites:

“Indeed, the hypocrites will be in the lowest depths of the Fire, and you will never find any helper for them.”

Allah also forbade His Prophet to offer funeral prayers for them or seek forgiveness for them, saying to him:

“Whether you seek forgiveness for them or do not seek forgiveness for them—even if you seek forgiveness for them seventy times—Allah will never forgive them.”

And He said: “And do not

---

## Page 6696

ever offer funeral prayer for any of them who dies, nor stand at his grave. Indeed, they disbelieved in Allah and His Messenger and died defiantly disobedient.” He has thus declared that they disbelieved in Allah and His Messenger.

If they say:
“These people voiced their disbelief in secret and thereby became disbelievers. A person is a believer only if he professes faith with his tongue and says nothing that contradicts it, for that would constitute apostasy from faith.”

They are told:
“Even if they harboured hypocrisy without voicing it, they would still be hypocrites.” Allah Most High says:

“The hypocrites fear lest a surah be revealed about them, informing them of what is in their hearts. Say, ‘Mock on; Allah will surely bring to light what you fear.’”

Moreover, Allah has stated that they say with their tongues what is not in their hearts, and that they are liars. Allah Most High says:

“Among the people are those who say, ‘We believe in Allah and the Last Day,’ yet they are not believers.”

And He says:

“When the hypocrites come to you, they say, ‘We bear witness that you are indeed the Messenger of Allah.’ Allah knows that you are indeed His Messenger, and Allah bears witness that the hypocrites are indeed liars.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Islam is outward, while faith is in the heart.”

Allah Most High says:

“The Bedouins say, ‘We believe.’ Say, ‘You have not believed; rather, say, “We have submitted,” for faith has not yet entered your hearts.’”

In the two Sahīh collections, Sa‘d reports:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave to some men but did not give to another. I said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, you gave to so-and-so and so-and-so, but left out so-and-so, even though he is a believer?’ He replied, ‘Or rather, a Muslim,’ repeating this twice or three times.”

A fuller discussion

---

## Page 6697

of this belongs elsewhere. I have devoted a volume to it, besides my other writings on the subject. People have discussed this term and what it denotes at great length, for it is the pivot around which religion revolves. No terms have greater bearing on felicity and misery, praise and blame, reward and punishment than the terms “faith” and “disbelief.” Hence this foundational subject is called “questions of designations and rulings.”

I have seen a work on this subject by Ibn al-Haysam arguing that faith consists solely of verbal profession, and another by Ibn al-Bāqillānī arguing that it consists solely of the heart’s assent. Both lived in the same age, and both refuted the Mu‘tazilah and the Rāfidah.

“The point here” is that the early generations held fast to the Quran and faith. When division and disagreement arose within the Muslim community, those involved split into factions. Their underlying reliance was not on the Quran and faith, but on principles introduced by their teachers, upon which they relied concerning Allah’s oneness, His attributes, divine decree, faith in the Messenger, and other matters. They then cited as evidence whatever they thought agreed with those principles in the Quran, while reinterpreting whatever contradicted them.

Thus, when they cite the Quran and hadith as evidence, you find that they take no care to establish precisely what they signify, nor do they examine exhaustively what the Quran contains concerning the matter in question, since their actual reliance is on something else. As for the verses that contradict their positions, they set about reinterpreting them like someone intent on rejecting them by any means possible;

---

## Page 6698

his aim is not to understand what the Messenger intended, but to prevent his opponent from citing them as evidence. Hence many of them—including Abū al-Husayn al-Basrī and his followers, such as al-Rāzī, al-Āmidī, and Ibn al-Hājib—said that if the Muslim community differed over the interpretation of a verse, holding two views, later generations could introduce a third view, unlike the case in which they differed over legal rulings, holding two views. They thus allowed that the entire community might be united upon error in interpreting the Quran and hadith, and that Allah might have revealed a verse intending a meaning that the Companions and their successors did not understand, while they said instead that Allah intended another meaning.

Had they grasped what this “position” entailed, they would not have said this. For their foundational principle is that the community does not unite upon error, nor does it hold two views that are both mistaken while the correct view is a third that it has never expressed. Yet they have become accustomed to reinterpreting whatever contradicts their views. For them, the purpose of interpretation is to identify a possible meaning in the wording of a verse: to show that the wording could bear that meaning. They have failed to realize that, when an interpreter construes a verse in a particular sense, he is explaining its intended meaning and reporting that Allah Most High intended that meaning.

Likewise, if they say that a verse may mean this, whereas the community before them said only that it meant either this or that, they have allowed that Allah had not informed the community of His intended meaning, and that the community had reported His intention to be something other than what He actually intended. Yet their claim could hold if interpretation consisted in saying that this meaning may be intended, without

---

## Page 6699

asserting that it is the intended meaning. The entire community before them would then have been ignorant of Allah’s intended meaning, having gone astray from understanding it, and the age of the Companions and their successors would have passed without their knowing the meaning of the verse. Rather, one group would have said, “He may intend this meaning,” and another group, “He may intend this meaning,” while none of them knew what was intended. Then a third party would have come and said, “Here is a meaning that may be the intended one.” If the community’s ignorance of the meanings of the Quran and its failure to understand the Lord’s intent were such, their claim would have some validity. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The “point” is that many of the later scholars came to rely, in their religion, on neither the Quran nor the faith brought by the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, unlike the early generations. Hence the early generations were more complete in knowledge and faith, their errors were less serious, and their correct judgments more numerous, as we have explained earlier.

The foundation upon which they built was what Allah commanded them in His words:

“O you who believe, do not put yourselves before Allah and His Messenger, and fear Allah. Indeed, Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

This commands the believers to observe the conduct with which He described the angels, as He, Most High, said:

“They say, ‘The Most Merciful has taken a son.’ Glory be to Him! Rather, they are honoured servants.”

“They do not precede Him in speech, and they act at His command.”

“He knows what is before them and what is behind them. They intercede only for those whom He approves, and they stand in awe of Him.”

“Should any of them say, ‘I am a god besides Him,’ We would recompense him with Hell. Thus do We recompense the wrongdoers.”

Thus He, glorified be He, described them as those who

---

## Page 6700

do not precede Him in speech and act at His command. Thus they convey nothing concerning His attributes or anything else unless He, glorified be He, has first conveyed it. Their reports and statements therefore follow His reports and statements, as He said:

“They do not precede Him in speech.”

Their deeds likewise follow His command: they do only what He has commanded them to do, and so they obey His command, glorified be He.

He, glorified be He, also described the angels of Hell in these terms, saying:

“Protect yourselves and your families from a Fire whose fuel is people and stones, over which are stern and mighty angels who do not disobey Allah in what He commands them, and do what they are commanded.”

Some have thought that this is repetition for emphasis. Others have said: rather, they did not disobey Him in the past and will do what they are commanded in the future.

A better explanation than either is that a disobedient person is one who refuses to obey a command despite being capable of complying. If someone failed to do what he was commanded because he was incapable, he would not be disobedient. Thus, His statement:

“They do not disobey Allah in what He commands them”

does not in itself establish that they do what they are commanded, for someone who is incapable is neither disobedient nor performing what he was commanded. He therefore said:

“And do what they are commanded”

to make clear that they are capable of doing what they have been commanded. They therefore do not leave it undone, whether through incapacity or disobedience. Anyone who leaves a command unfulfilled does so for one of these two reasons: either he is incapable, or he is disobedient and unwilling to obey. If, however, he is obedient, willing to obey the one commanding him, and capable, then his performance of what he has been commanded necessarily follows. Such are the angels mentioned here: they do not disobey Allah in what He commands them, and they do what they are commanded.

---

## Page 6701

He has also described the angels as:

“Honored servants. They do not precede Him in speech and act at His command.”

“He knows what is before them and what is behind them. They do not intercede except for those whom He approves, and they are apprehensive out of fear of Him.”

“Whoever among them says, ‘I am a god besides Him,’ We will recompense with Hell. Thus do We recompense the wrongdoers.”

The angels therefore believe what their Lord conveys and obey His commands. They convey nothing until He has conveyed it, and do nothing until He commands it, as He, Most High, said:

“They do not precede Him in speech and act at His command.”

Allah has commanded the believers to conduct themselves in the same way toward Allah and His Messenger. Human beings have not heard Allah’s speech directly from Him; rather, a human messenger stands between them and Him. They must therefore not speak until the Messenger has stated what he conveys to them from Allah, and they must act only according to what he has commanded them, as He, Most High, said:

“O you who believe, do not go ahead of Allah and His Messenger, and fear Allah. Indeed, Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

Mujāhid said: “Do not preempt him in any matter until Allah rules on it through his words.”

(Tuqaddimū means tataqaddamū, “to go ahead,” and is an intransitive verb. It has also been recited yuqaddimū. One says qaddama and taqaddama, just as one says bayyana and tabayyana, “to become clear.” Qaddama may also be used transitively, meaning to put someone else forward, but here it is intransitive. Thus lā tuqaddimū means lā tataqaddamū: “Do not go ahead of Allah and His Messenger.”)

Every believer must therefore speak on any matter of religion only in accordance with what has been brought by

---

## Page 6702

the Messenger, and must not go ahead of him. Rather, he must consider what the Messenger has said, so that his words follow the Messenger’s words and his deeds follow his commands. This was the way of the Companions, those who followed their path in righteousness, and the leading scholars of the Muslims. Thus, none of them would oppose the revealed texts with his own reasoning or establish a religion other than what the Messenger brought. Whenever one of them wished to understand or discuss any religious matter, he would examine what Allah and the Messenger had said: from it he would learn, on its basis he would speak, upon it he would reflect and deliberate, and by it he would establish his proofs. This is the foundation of Ahl al-Sunnah.

The people of innovation, however, do not, at heart and in reality, rely on what they have received from the Messenger. Rather, they rely on their own views or spiritual experiences. If they then find the Sunnah in agreement with these, well and good; otherwise, they are unconcerned. When they find that it contradicts them, they turn away from it, consigning the matter to Allah, or distort it through reinterpretation.

This, then, is the distinction between the people of faith and Sunnah and the people of hypocrisy and innovation. Although the latter may possess a substantial share of faith through following the Sunnah, they have hypocrisy and innovation in proportion to the extent to which they have gone ahead of Allah and His Messenger and opposed Allah and His Messenger. If, however, they do not know that this contradicts the Messenger, and would not have said it had they known, then they are not hypocrites, but innovators deficient in faith. Their error is forgiven, and they are not punished for it, even though it diminishes their faith.

---

## Page 6703

Section

Whoever opposes what the Messenger brought has neither knowledge nor justice in doing so. Rather, he has nothing but ignorance, injustice, conjecture, “and what their souls desire, although guidance has already come to them from their Lord.”

This is because whatever the Messenger has reported is true, both inwardly and outwardly. It is therefore inconceivable that the truth should lie in its opposite. Thus, whoever believes the opposite holds a false belief, and a false belief cannot constitute knowledge. Whatever the Messenger has commanded is just, containing no injustice. Whoever forbids it has therefore forbidden justice, and whoever commands its opposite has commanded injustice, for the opposite of justice is injustice. Thus, whatever contradicts what he brought can only be ignorance and injustice, conjecture and what souls desire.

Such opposition falls into one of two categories: at best, it was a law prescribed for one of the prophets and subsequently abrogated; at worst, it was never prescribed at all, but is a substituted law. Everything that contradicts the judgment of Allah and His Messenger is therefore either an abrogated law or a substituted law that Allah never prescribed. Rather, someone legislated it without Allah’s permission, as He says:

“Or do they have partners who have ordained for them in religion what Allah has not permitted?”

Yet either of these may occur in subtle and intricate matters through independent reasoning on the part of their proponents, who, in doing so, have exerted

---

## Page 6704

their utmost in seeking the truth. Their sound judgments and faithful adherence may far outweigh such errors, as occurred with some of the Companions in matters of divorce, inheritance, and the like. Nothing of this kind occurred among them in clear and weighty matters, because the Messenger’s explanation of these matters was evident among them; none would contradict it unless he opposed the Messenger. They held fast to the rope of Allah, referred their disputes to the Messenger for judgment, and did not presume to act ahead of Allah and His Messenger, let alone deliberately oppose Allah and His Messenger.

As time passed, much that had been evident to them became obscure to many people, and much that had been clear to them became difficult for many to discern. Thus, departures from the Quran and Sunnah became far more common among later generations than they had been among the early Muslims, even though those responsible may have been exercising independent reasoning and been excused, with Allah forgiving their errors and rewarding their efforts.

They may also perform good deeds for which one of them receives the reward of fifty men performing those deeds in that earlier time. This is because the earlier Muslims found others to help them, whereas these later Muslims found no one to help them. However, receiving multiplied rewards for certain deeds, when the Companions did not receive the same multiplication, does not necessarily make them superior to the Companions, nor does a distinguished person among them equal a distinguished Companion. For the Companions’ precedence in faith and jihad, in opposing the people of the earth out of loyalty to the Messenger, and in believing him

---

## Page 6705

and obeying him in what he conveyed and enjoined, before his call had spread, his message had prevailed, his helpers and supporters had grown numerous, and the proofs of his prophethood had become widely known—indeed, when believers were few and disbelievers and hypocrites were many—and in spending their wealth in Allah’s cause, seeking His Countenance under such circumstances, is something no one can attain the like of any longer. As reported from him, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the two authentic hadith collections:

“Do not revile my Companions. By Him in whose Hand is my soul, if one of you were to spend gold equal to Mount Uhud, it would not equal a mudd (a small measure) given by one of them, nor even half of it.”

Numerous authentic reports relate that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best generation is my generation, those among whom I was sent, then those who follow them, then those who follow them.”

Thus, the first generation as a whole is better than the second, the second better than the third, and the third better than the fourth. Yet someone in the fourth generation may be better than some members of the third, and likewise someone in the third may be better than some members of the second.

Can someone who came after the Companions be better than some of the less distinguished Companions—not the most distinguished? This is disputed, and al-Qādī ‘Iyād and others have reported two views on it. Some frame the comparison in terms of figures such as Mu‘āwiyah and ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-‘Azīz: Mu‘āwiyah has the distinction of companionship and jihad alongside the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, while ‘Umar has the distinction of his virtues of justice, asceticism, and fear of Allah Most High. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

And “the point here” is that whoever opposes the Messenger must necessarily follow conjecture

---

## Page 6706

and what their souls desire, as the Most High says concerning the polytheists who worship al-Lāt and al-‘Uzzā:

“They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire, although guidance has surely come to them from their Lord.”

He says concerning those who claim that the angels are female:

“Indeed, those who do not believe in the Hereafter give the angels female names. They have no knowledge of this; they follow nothing but conjecture, and conjecture is of no avail against the truth. So turn away from whoever turns away from Our remembrance and desires nothing but the life of this world. That is the extent of their knowledge. Indeed, your Lord knows best who has strayed from His way, and He knows best who is rightly guided.”

They have declared them female, as He says:

“And they have made the angels, who are servants of the Most Merciful, female.”

The other reading has “in the presence of the Most Merciful,” followed by:

“Female. Did they witness their creation? Their testimony will be recorded, and they will be questioned.”

Of these people He says, “They follow nothing but conjecture,” because this is merely a claim about a matter of fact, involving no action. In the other passage, however, He says, “and what their souls desire,” because they used to worship them and call upon them. There, worship and action followed their own desires, so He said:

“They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire.”

As for what the Messenger brought, it is as He says:

“By the star when it descends, your companion has neither gone astray nor erred. Nor does he speak from desire. It is nothing but revelation revealed, taught to him by one mighty in power.”

Everyone who opposes the Messenger remains bound by conjecture and what their souls desire. If he believes what he says and has an argument with which he seeks to substantiate it, the utmost he attains is conjecture, which is of no avail against the truth

---

## Page 6707

, as when they appeal to a faulty analogy, a false report, or words addressed to them which they believe to be from Allah, though they were cast into their minds by Satan. These three are the mainstay of those who oppose the Sunnah on the strength of what they regard as proof and evidence.

A person may appeal to rational arguments, believing them to be demonstrations and conclusive proofs, when they are in fact fallacious objections composed of ambiguous expressions and equivocal meanings, whose truth he has not distinguished from their falsehood. This is found in all the arguments advanced by those who oppose the Quran and Sunnah: they construct their arguments from equivocal expressions, but once clarification and distinctions are sought, truth becomes distinguishable from falsehood. Such are the arguments based on reason.

If someone defending falsehood relies on evidence from transmitted sources, it will either be falsely attributed to the Messenger or fail to support the claim for which the advocates of falsehood invoke it. The objection therefore concerns either the chain of transmission or the text and its bearing on the claim made. Such is the evidence from transmitted sources; this is the evidence used by those versed in outward knowledge.

As for the evidence invoked by those who experience spiritual tasting, ecstasy, unveiling, and spiritual communications, those among them who follow the truth do receive sound inspirations that accord with reality. Thus, in the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Among the nations before you there were men who were inspired. If there is anyone like that in my community, it is ‘Umar.”

‘Umar used to say, “Draw near to those who are obedient and listen to what comes from their lips, for true matters are disclosed to them.” In al-Tirmidhī, it is reported from Abū Sa‘īd that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 6708

said:

“Beware the believer’s discernment, for he sees by the light of Allah.” He then recited His words: “Indeed, in that are signs for those who discern.”

One of the Companions said, “By Allah, I believe it is indeed the truth that Allah casts into their hearts and hearing.” In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, Abū Hurayrah reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“My servant continues to draw near to Me through voluntary acts of worship until I love him. When I love him, I become his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he grasps, and his foot with which he walks.”

Another version reads:

“Through Me he hears, through Me he sees, through Me he grasps, and through Me he walks.”

He thus states that the servant hears and sees through the Truth. They used to say that tranquillity spoke through the tongue of ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever asks to be appointed a judge and seeks help in obtaining the position will be left to his own resources. But whoever neither asks for it nor seeks help in obtaining it, Allah sends down to him an angel to guide him aright.”

Allah Most High says:

“Light upon light.”

This is the light of faith together with the light of the Quran. He Most High also says:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord, followed by a witness from Him…?”

This is the believer who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord and is followed by a witness from Allah: the Quran. In the Quran, Allah bears witness to the same truth established by the believer’s clear evidence of faith.

Even the most accomplished rational thinkers acknowledged this much when discussing the obligation of rational inquiry and its yielding knowledge. They were told that those devoted to purification, spiritual discipline, worship, and devotion to Allah attain understanding and certain knowledge without rational inquiry. Such was the exchange between the shaykh known as al-Kabīrī and al-Rāzī and his companion. They said to him, “Shaykh, we have heard that you possess certain knowledge.”

He replied, “Yes.”

They asked, “How do you know, when we have been debating

---

## Page 6709

for a long time, with me refuting whatever he puts forward and him refuting whatever I put forward?”

He replied:

“They are inward impressions that come upon souls, which the souls are powerless to repel.”

The two men marvelled at this and kept returning to the subject. One of them asked to receive these inward impressions himself, so the shaykh instructed and trained him until he experienced them. He had been one of the Mu‘tazilite advocates of negation. It then became clear to him that the truth lay with the advocates of affirmation, and that Allah, glorified be He, is above His heavens. He knew this with immediate certainty.

I saw this account in the handwriting of Judge Najm al-Dīn Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Khalaf al-Maqdisī, who stated that Shaykh al-Kabīrī had related it to him. More than one person had already related it to me on his authority before I saw it in his handwriting. The shaykhs have much to say on matters of this kind.

The description the shaykh gave answered them in terms they understood. They divide knowledge into immediate and inferential knowledge, with inferential knowledge resting upon immediate knowledge. Immediate knowledge is knowledge that takes such an inescapable hold upon a created being’s soul that he cannot free himself from it. This is the definition given by Judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Tayyib and others.

Its distinctive feature, then, is that it takes hold of the soul in a manner that makes it impossible to repel. Thus, he told them:

“The certain knowledge we possess is of this kind: knowledge that takes such an inescapable hold upon the soul that one cannot free oneself from it.”

He called them “inward impressions” because knowledge is accompanied by reassurance and tranquillity that impel one to act upon it. These inward impressions encompass both. Many accomplished rational thinkers have acknowledged this, including such earlier figures as al-Kiyā al-Harrāsī and al-Ghazālī

---

## Page 6710

and others, as well as later figures such as al-Rāzī and al-Āmidī. They said:

“We do not deny that some people may acquire immediate knowledge of what we come to know through rational inquiry. We do not dispute this. But if it is not immediate knowledge, it must have evidence, and that evidence must entail what it demonstrates, such that the absence of the evidence entails the absence of what it demonstrates.”

They said:

“If rejecting the belief he has acquired would entail rejecting something known with immediate certainty, then this is the evidence. Otherwise, it is mere delusion that deserves no consideration.”

A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere. The point is that this kind of experience does occur. Yet many things also occur that are thought to belong to this category, or many of these people fail to distinguish truth from falsehood, just as happens with rational and scriptural evidence.

Some of these people hear a voice addressing them or see someone instructing them to do something, when that address comes from Satan and the one addressing them is Satan, though they imagine him to be one of Allah’s allies, one of the “men of the unseen.”

These “men of the unseen” are jinn, though the person imagines the one before him to be human. He may say to him:

“I am al-Khadir, or Elijah; indeed, I am Muhammad, or Abraham, the close friend of Allah, or the Messiah, or Abū Bakr, or ‘Umar; or I am Shaykh So-and-so, or Shaykh So-and-so”—naming people of whom he thinks well.

He may carry him through the air or bring him food, drink, or money for his expenses. The person then imagines this to be a miraculous favour—indeed, a sign and a miracle indicating that this being is one of the men of the unseen or one of the angels—when in fact it is a devil who has deceived him. Thus, this

---

## Page 6711

and similar things occur frequently. I know of many such incidents, just as I know of many errors in scriptural and rational matters. These people follow conjecture, which is of no avail against the truth. Had they not presumed to act ahead of Allah and His Messenger, but instead held fast to the Quran and Sunnah, it would have become clear to them that this comes from Satan. Many of them follow their spiritual taste, their inward experience, and whatever they find pleasing, without knowledge, guidance, or insight. Thus, they follow their desires without even conjecture; the best among them follow conjecture and what their souls desire.

When any of these people is asked for proof, he cites his imitation of those forefathers and predecessors whom he loves, like the polytheists who said:

“Indeed, we found our forefathers following a religion, and we are following in their footsteps.”

If they take the opposite approach, they invoke divine decree, arguing that Allah willed this and gave them power over it. Thus, they act upon their desires and their own wishes to the extent of their ability, like rulers given power over others. What they ought to have done was to act as Allah commanded, following His command and what He loves and is pleased with, rather than following their own will and what they themselves love and are pleased with. They ought also to have sought Allah’s help, saying:

“You alone we worship, and You alone we ask for help.”

There is no might or power except through Allah. They should not rely on the power, influence, and spiritual state they have been granted, for these are all part of worldly fortune. After prayer, and when standing upright after bowing, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would say:

“O Allah, none can withhold what You give, and none can give what You withhold; nor can worldly fortune avail its possessor against You.”

---

## Page 6712

Spiritual taste and inward experience thus relate to a person’s love and the sweetness, savor, and flavor he finds in it. Everyone who loves has such a taste and experience in relation to what he loves. If this is not supported by authority from Allah—namely, what He revealed to His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—then that person is following his desires without guidance. Allah Most High has said:

“And who is more astray than one who follows his desires without guidance from Allah?”

He also said:

“Why should you not eat of that over which Allah’s name has been mentioned, when He has already explained to you in detail what He has forbidden you, except what you are compelled to eat? Indeed, many lead others astray through their desires without knowledge. Surely your Lord knows best those who transgress.”

Likewise, whoever follows the communications that come to him, or the lights and unseen figures he sees, without testing them against the Quran and Sunnah, is merely following conjecture, which is of no avail against the truth. Among those granted inward communication and inspiration, none is better than Umar, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Among the nations before you were people who were inspired. If there is anyone among my nation who is one of them, it is Umar.”

Umar’s views agreed with his Lord’s revelation on several matters. Nevertheless, he was required to hold fast to what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had brought, and not to accept whatever came to him until he had submitted it to the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He was not to put himself ahead of Allah and His Messenger. Rather, he was to assess whatever came to him accordingly; whenever matters became clear to him that contradicted his own impressions, he would return to the Sunnah. Abū Bakr would explain to him matters that had escaped him, and he would defer to the explanation of

---

## Page 6713

al-Siddīq and to his guidance and instruction, as happened on the day of al-Hudaybiyyah, on the day the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, died, and when he debated with him concerning those who withheld zakat, among other occasions. A woman would challenge what he said and cite evidence from the Quran, and he would accept her correction, as happened concerning women’s dowries. There are many such examples.

Anyone granted inspiration, inward communication, and spiritual unveiling is no better than Umar. He must therefore follow Umar’s path in holding fast to the Quran and Sunnah, following what the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, brought—not making what the Messenger brought subordinate to whatever comes to him inwardly. Those who erred and went astray abandoned this course, contenting themselves with what came to them inwardly and supposing that this freed them from the need to follow transmitted knowledge.

One of them would say:

“They received their knowledge from one dead man after another, while we received ours from the Ever-Living, who never dies.”

The reply to him is: What trustworthy narrators have transmitted from the infallible Messenger is true. Were it not for transmission safeguarded from error, you and those like you would be either polytheists, Jews, or Christians. As for what comes to you inwardly, how do you know that it is inspiration from Allah? How do you know that it is not inspiration from Satan?

“Inspiration” is of two kinds: inspiration from the Most Merciful and inspiration from Satan. Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, the devils inspire their allies to dispute with you.”

And Allah Most High said:

“Thus We have appointed for every prophet an enemy—devils from humankind and jinn—who inspire

---

## Page 6714

one another with alluring speech in order to deceive.” And Allah Most High said:

“Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend?”

Al-Mukhtār ibn Abī Ubayd was of this sort. Thus, when Ibn Umar and Ibn Abbās were told about him, one of them was told, “He claims to receive revelation.” He replied:

“Indeed, the devils inspire their allies to dispute with you.”

The other was told, “He says that it descends upon him.” He replied:

“Shall I inform you upon whom the devils descend?”

Such people need the criterion grounded in faith, the Quran, Prophetic guidance, and revealed law more than others do. They have sensory perceptions—things they see and hear—and a person experiences sensory perceptions involuntarily, just as someone may see and hear things without choosing to do so. Likewise, those engaged in rational inquiry have their reasoning and rational knowledge, while those who rely on transmitted knowledge have reports passed down to them. These three are the means of acquiring knowledge: sensory perception, reports, and rational inquiry.

Everyone draws on these three to establish some things, but certain means predominate among certain people, whether in religious or nonreligious matters. Medicine, for example, involves experience and reasoning. Some practitioners rely predominantly on experience, while others rely predominantly on reasoning. Reasoning is rooted in experience, and experience necessarily involves reasoning, though of a kind resembling reasoning from customary regularities, in which the underlying cause and its relevance are not known. The practitioner of reasoning identifies the relevant cause and bases the judgment upon it. Reason is particularly concerned with reasoning, drawing comparisons, and universal propositions; it therefore requires sensory perceptions that

---

## Page 6715

provide the foundation for its reasoning. Sensory perception, in turn, may err if the person perceiving lacks reason.

People say, “The senses were mistaken.” Yet the error sometimes lies in the senses and sometimes in the perceiver. The senses perceive a particular thing, but the perceiver supposes it to be something else, so the error arises from that supposition. Reason is therefore indispensable.

Thus, a sleeping person sees things that do have existence and reality, but they are images and representations. When reason is absent, the dreamer takes them to be the realities themselves. For example, someone sees himself in another place, speaking to the dead and being spoken to by them, and doing many things. While asleep, he is certain that it is he himself who is speaking and acting, because his reason has departed from him. The form he sees is a representation and image of his own form, but his reason is absent, so he supposes that this representation is his very self. When his reason returns, he knows that these were images and representations. Some people, however, retain their reason and know during a dream that they are dreaming.

This resembles someone who sees his own image or another person’s image in a mirror. If his reasoning is weak, he supposes that the image is the person himself, even treating it as he would treat that person. This happens to children and the simple-minded. Likewise, a figure may appear to one of them in the light, moving, rising and descending. They suppose it to be an actual person and do not realize that it is an image.

When the senses perceive correctly, they do not err; rather, the accompanying reason has failed to distinguish between the actual entity and its representation. For reason has previously understood

---

## Page 6716

that something of this kind may be a representation. It has also understood the necessary properties of the particular person himself: that he cannot be in the air or in a mirror, that his body cannot be somewhere other than where he is, and that a single body cannot be in two places.

Those who experience unveilings and communications see and hear both things that exist externally and things that exist only within themselves, as happens to a sleeper. Everyone knows this. Yet they may see figures externally, before their very eyes—whereas what exists in someone’s imagination cannot be seen by anyone else. Those figures address them, carry them, and take them to ʿArafāt, where they stand, or to places other than ʿArafāt. They bring them gold, silver, food, clothing, weapons, and other things, and go out among people. They also bring them whomever they seek. For example, someone may desire a woman or a boy, and they bring that person to him, either carried through the air or by making that person hasten there. The person brought reports having felt such a powerful urge that remaining where they were was impossible, or reports having heard a voice addressing them. They may also kill an enemy whom he wishes to have killed, or make that enemy ill.

All of this occurs frequently. Some people, however, know that it comes from Satan, that it is sorcery, and that it has come about through the words and deeds of sorcery they have performed.

Others know that it comes from the jinn and say, “This is a miraculous gift: He has honored us by placing the jinn at our service.” Still others suppose those figures to be nothing but human beings or

---

## Page 6717

angels. If they are unknown to him, he says, “These are men of the unseen.” If they identify themselves, saying, “This is al-Khidr, this is Elijah, this is Abū Bakr and this is ‘Umar, and this is Shaykh ‘Abd al-Qādir, Shaykh ‘Adī, or Shaykh Ahmad al-Rifā‘ī,” or someone else, he assumes that this is indeed the case. Here, the error is not in what he saw, but in his judgment: he failed to recognize that these were devils appearing in the forms of those people.

Many such people suppose that the Prophet himself—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or another prophet or righteous person comes to them while they are awake. Some see such a figure at the grave of the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—or of a shaykh. They truthfully report that the figure claimed to be the Prophet or the shaykh, or that someone identified it to them as such; their error, however, lies in believing those claims.

A person endowed with reason and knowledge knows that this is not the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Sometimes he recognizes this from the conduct of these figures that contradicts the revealed Law, such as their ordering him to do something contrary to the commands of Allah and His Messenger. Sometimes he knows that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—never came to any of his Companions while they were awake after his death, nor did he address them from his grave, and asks, “How, then, could this happen to me?” At other times, he knows that the deceased has not risen from his grave, and that his soul is in Paradise and does not come into this world in this manner.

This happens frequently to many of these people, and they call that form the raqīqa of

---

## Page 6718

so-and-so. They may say, “It is his essence taking form,” or call it “his spiritual being.”

Some of these people say, “When I die, do not let anyone wash me, nor let so-and-so attend me, for I shall wash myself.” When he dies, they see him come and wash that body. This is actually a jinn who had told the deceased, “You will return after death,” and he believed this to be true, for it had told him things during his lifetime. The devil’s purpose is to mislead his followers.

In lands inhabited by polytheists, such as India, people very often see a deceased person return after his death, open his shop, return things entrusted to him, settle debts, enter his home and then leave. They have no doubt that it is the very same person, whereas it is only a devil that has assumed his appearance.

Some of these people attend the funeral of their father or someone else. While the deceased lies on his bier, the person sees him walking among the people, holding his son and his father by the hand, having been appointed shaykh in succession to his father. His son has no doubt that it was his father himself who walked with him—the figure that he alone, and no one else, saw. Yet it was only a devil. Such a devil may call himself Khālid, or some other name, and tell them that he is one of the men of the unseen. They believe him to be a righteous human being and call him Khālid al-Ghaybī. They associate the shaykh with him, calling him Muhammad al-Khālidī, or something similar.

The jinn, like human beings, are subject to commands and prohibitions. Allah sent human messengers to both them and humankind, and commanded them all to obey the messengers, as He says:

---

## Page 6719

exalted is He:

“O company of jinn and humankind, did there not come to you messengers from among yourselves, relating My verses to you and warning you of the meeting of this Day of yours? They will say, ‘We testify against ourselves.’ The worldly life deluded them, and they will testify against themselves that they were disbelievers.”

This comes after His words:

“And on the Day He gathers them all together: ‘O company of jinn, you have led many humans astray.’ Their allies among humankind will say, ‘Our Lord, we benefited from one another, and we have reached the term You appointed for us.’ He will say, ‘The Fire is your abode, where you will remain forever, except as Allah wills.’”

More than one of the early generations explained: “That is, you have seduced and led astray many humans.”

Al-Baghawī said:

Some said, “Humans benefited from the jinn through the false rumours, magic and soothsaying that the jinn conveyed to them, and through their making attractive to them the things they arranged and made readily accessible to them. The jinn benefited from humans through their obedience to them in the misguidance and sins they made attractive to them.” Muhammad ibn Kaʿb said, “It means their obeying one another and agreeing with one another.”

Ibn Abī Hātim related that al-Hasan al-Basrī said, “Their benefiting from one another consisted only in the jinn giving commands and humans carrying them out.” He also related that Muhammad ibn Kaʿb said, “It means companionship in this world.”

Ibn al-Sāʾib said, “Humans benefited from the jinn by seeking refuge with them. The jinn benefited from humans by saying, ‘We have taken humans captive along with the jinn, so that they have come to seek refuge with us.’ Thus they grew in their own estimation of their honour and greatness.” This is like His words:

“And there were men among humankind who sought refuge with men among the jinn, so they increased them in transgression.”

---

## Page 6720

I say: “Benefiting from something” means enjoying it and thereby obtaining what one seeks, wants and desires. This includes the mutual enjoyment of men and women, as Allah says:

“For whatever enjoyment you have had with them, give them their bridal dues as an obligation.”

It also includes indecencies, such as males taking pleasure in males and females in females.

This includes benefiting from employing others and from leaders in positions of authority, just as kings and masters benefit from their soldiers and slaves. It also includes benefiting from possessions, such as clothing. Among the examples of this are His words:

“Provide for them—the wealthy according to his means and the poor according to his means.”

Some of the early generations would give a woman a servant as a parting gift, so that she could benefit from his service. Others would give clothing or financial support. Hence the jurists said: “The highest parting gift is a servant, and the lowest is clothing sufficient for prayer to be valid.”

In sum, humans benefiting from jinn and jinn benefiting from humans resembles humans benefiting from one another. Allah Most High says:

“Close friends, that Day, will be enemies to one another, except the righteous.”

He also says:

“And all ties between them will be severed.”

Mujāhid said: “These are the bonds of affection that were not for Allah’s sake.” Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, said:

“You have taken idols besides Allah only as a bond of affection among yourselves in this worldly life. Then, on the Day of Resurrection, you will disown one another and curse one another.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Have you seen the one who takes his own desire as his god?”

The polytheist worships whatever he desires. Following one’s desires means deriving enjoyment from what one desires. All of this has occurred among humans and jinn.

---

## Page 6721

At times, each serves the purposes of the other: a jinn brings a human what he wants—an image, wealth, or the killing of his enemy—and the human obeys the jinn. Sometimes he prostrates to him; sometimes he prostrates to whatever the jinn commands him to prostrate to; and sometimes he gives the jinn access to his body, allowing him to commit an indecent sexual act with him. Likewise, some female jinn desire from the humans they serve what human women desire from men. This is common among male and female jinn. Many male jinn obtain from human women what human men obtain from them, and they may also do this with males.

Jinn possess humans for three reasons:

Sometimes a jinn loves the person he possesses and possesses him to enjoy him. This kind of possession is gentler and less severe than others. Sometimes the human has harmed them by urinating on them, pouring hot water on them, killing some of them, or causing some other kind of harm. This is the most severe kind of possession, and they often kill the possessed person. Sometimes they do it merely to torment him, just as foolish humans torment wayfarers.

One way humans benefit from jinn is by employing them to report hidden matters, just as soothsayers receive such reports. Some humans seek this because of the authority, wealth, and other benefits it brings. If the people are disbelievers, as the Arabs were, they do not mind his being called a soothsayer, just as some Arabs were soothsayers. When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, arrived in Medina, there were soothsayers there, and the hypocrites sought to have their disputes adjudicated by

---

## Page 6722

the soothsayers. Abū Abraq al-Aslamī was one of the soothsayers before he embraced Islam. If the people are Muslims, however, such a man does not openly present himself as a soothsayer; instead, he portrays this as a miraculous gift, though he is of the same kind as the soothsayers. For the jinn serves the human with these reports only in return for what he gains from him: the human obeys him in something he desires, whether associating partners with Allah, committing sexual indecency, consuming what is unlawful, or killing someone unjustly.

The devils pursue what Allah has forbidden—disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience. They delight in evil and turmoil, loving these things even when they derive no benefit from them. They tell a thief to steal, then go to the owners of the property and say, “So-and-so stole your belongings.”

Hence people speak of the angelic, animal, predatory, and satanic faculties. The angelic faculty comprises beneficial knowledge and righteous deeds; the animal faculty comprises appetites, such as eating and drinking; and the predatory faculty comprises anger, which serves to repel what causes harm. The satanic faculty, however, is pure evil: it neither secures benefit nor wards off harm.

The philosophers and others like them who have no knowledge of jinn and devils do not recognize this faculty. They recognize only appetite and anger. Appetite and anger were created to serve a beneficial purpose; what is blameworthy is transgressing the bounds in either. Satan, however, commands evil that brings no benefit, and he loves it, as Iblīs did to Adam when he whispered to him, and just as

---

## Page 6723

he refused to prostrate before him. Satan commands envy, although the envious person gains nothing when the one he envies loses a blessing; he simply resents that blessing. His resentment may arise because his own aims have been frustrated, or it may not.

One way humans benefit from the jinn is by employing them to bring things they seek, such as wealth, food, clothing, and money for expenses. The jinn may bring some of these things or direct a person to buried treasure or something else. The jinn, in turn, benefit from humans by using them to carry out what Satan desires of disbelief, wickedness, and disobedience.

Another way humans benefit from the jinn is by employing them to carry out what they seek of associating partners with Allah, killing, and sexual indecency. Sometimes a jinnī assumes the human’s appearance. When one of the human’s followers calls upon him for help, the jinnī comes, and the follower thinks it is the shaykh himself. At other times, a follower calls out to his shaykh, crying, “O my master so-and-so!” The jinnī conveys those words to the shaykh in a voice resembling the human’s, until the shaykh thinks it is that very person’s voice. The shaykh then says, “Yes,” and makes a gesture to ward off the harm. The jinnī reproduces that voice and action, so the person thinks it is his shaykh himself who answered him and performed that act.

A follower of the shaykh may even have his hand in a dish, eating, when the jinnī puts his own hand into it in the form of the shaykh’s hand and takes some food. The follower then thinks that his shaykh is present with him. Meanwhile, the jinnī makes a similar dish appear before the shaykh himself, who puts his hand into it, until the shaykh believes that his hand is

---

## Page 6724

in that dish. When the disciple visits him, the shaykh tells him, “My hand was in the dish,” and he believes him, although they had been a month’s journey apart. The shaykh had remained where he was, and his hand had not grown longer. Rather, the jinnī had presented an image to both the shaykh and the disciple, until each thought that one of them was with the other. What was actually present with each was only what the jinnī had depicted and made him imagine.

When a shaykh served by a jinnī is asked about a matter hidden from him—whether a theft, someone who has died and whose condition he is asked to report, an illness affecting women, or something else—the jinnī may depict it for him. He shows him an image of the stolen property, and the shaykh says, “You have lost such-and-such.” Then, if the owner of the property is held in high regard and the shaykh wishes to direct him to his stolen property, the shaykh depicts for him the person who took it or the place where it is. They go there and find it just as he said.

Most of them, however, display an image of the property without having it at hand, because the person who stole it also has a jinnī serving him. The jinn fear one another just as humans do. If the jinnī identifies the thief, the thief’s allies come and harm him. Sometimes he does not identify him because the thief and his accomplices serve and bribe him. The same happens among humans to someone who knows thieves: he may know the thief but not identify him, either because of some benefit he hopes to obtain from him or because he fears him. But if the stolen property belongs to a powerful man whom he fears and whose favour he seeks, he identifies the thief. This and similar things are instances of their benefiting from one another.

The jinn are bound by religious obligations just as humans are, and Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent

---

## Page 6725

to both communities, jinn and humans. The disbelieving jinn will enter the Fire, according to the scriptural texts and the consensus of the Muslims.

As for their believers, there are two views concerning them. Most scholars hold that they too will be rewarded and enter Paradise. It has been reported that they will dwell on its outskirts, where humans will see them while they cannot see humans—the reverse of the situation in this world. This is a hadith related by al-Tabarānī in his al-Mu‘jam al-Saghīr; its chain of transmission requires examination.

Ibn Abī Laylā and Abū Yūsuf cited as evidence for this the words of the Most High:

“And for all are ranks according to what they have done.”

Both jinn and humans, righteous and wicked alike, have been mentioned in al-Ahqāf and al-An‘ām.

Al-Awzā‘ī and others cited as evidence the words of the Most High:

“Whom neither human nor jinn has touched before them.”

The Most High says in al-Ahqāf:

“Those are the ones against whom the sentence has become due, along with nations of jinn and humans who passed away before them. Indeed, they were losers.” “And for all are ranks according to what they have done.”

Before this, the people of Paradise had already been mentioned in His words:

“Those are the ones from whom We accept the best of what they have done and overlook their misdeeds, among the people of Paradise.”

Then He says:

“And for all are ranks according to what they have done, so that He may repay them fully for their deeds, and they will not be wronged.”

‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam said: “The ranks of the people of Paradise ascend, while the ranks of the people of the Fire descend.”

The Most High also relates the words of the jinn:

“Among us are the righteous, and among us are those otherwise; we have followed divergent paths.”

And they said: “Among us are Muslims, and among us are the unjust. So whoever

---

## Page 6726

submits to Allah has sought out right guidance.”

“As for the unjust, they will be fuel for Hell.”

Thus, among them are disbelievers, the defiantly sinful and the disobedient. Among them, too, are those who are devout and religious but whose knowledge is somewhat limited, just as among humans. Each kind of jinn gravitates towards its human counterpart: Jews towards Jews, Christians towards Christians, Muslims towards Muslims, the defiantly sinful towards the defiantly sinful, and people of ignorance and religious innovation towards their like.

Humans employing jinn for some purpose is like humans employing other humans. Some employ them for forbidden acts, such as obscenities, injustice, associating partners with Allah, and speaking about Allah without knowledge. They may imagine these to be miraculous gifts bestowed upon the righteous, whereas they are merely the deeds of devils. Others employ them in permissible matters, such as fetching their property, pointing out a place containing wealth that has no owner whose rights are protected, warding off someone who harms them, and similar things. This is like humans seeking one another’s assistance in such matters.

And “the third category” is employing them in obedience to Allah and His Messenger, just as humans are employed for such purposes: commanding them to do what Allah and His Messenger have commanded, and forbidding them from what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden, just as one commands and forbids humans. This was the way of our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and it is the way of those of his community who follow him and take him as their example. They are the best of creation, for they command humans and jinn to do what Allah and His Messenger have commanded them

---

## Page 6727

and forbid humans and jinn from what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden them; for our Prophet Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent with this mission to both humans and jinn. Allah said to him:

“Say, ‘This is my way: I and those who follow me call to Allah with clear insight. Glory be to Allah! And I am not one of those who associate partners with Him.’”

And He said:

“Say, ‘If you love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive you your sins. And Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.’”

When Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, called out, “Sāriya, to the mountain!” he said, “Allah has troops who convey my voice.” Allah’s troops include angels and righteous jinn. Thus, Allah’s troops conveyed Umar’s voice to Sāriya, meaning that they called out to him in a voice like Umar’s. Otherwise, Umar’s own voice could not itself travel such a great distance.

This is like a man calling to someone far away, saying, “So-and-so!” He is helped by an intermediary between them, who calls out, “So-and-so!” He might say to someone far away, “So-and-so, hold back the water! Come to us!” Although that person cannot hear his voice, the intermediary calls out similar instructions to him: “So-and-so, hold back the water! Release the water!” He does so in a voice like the first man’s if the recipient will respond only to that voice. Otherwise, it makes no difference which voice is used, provided he knows that his companion has called him.

Here is an account: Umar once sent out an army. Someone came and told the people of Medina that the army had been victorious. The news spread, so Umar said, “Where did you get

---

## Page 6728

this news?” They replied, “A man with such-and-such characteristics told us.” Umar said, “That was Abū al-Haytham, the courier of the jinn. The human courier will arrive several days later.”

A king may give a man an order and require him to keep it secret, yet when the man goes out, he finds people discussing it: the jinn hear it and tell people about it. Those who employ the jinn for permissible purposes resemble Solomon in his employment of them. He, however, was granted a dominion that no one after him was to have, and humans and jinn were subjected to him. This was granted to no one else. When a powerful jinn lunged at the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to interrupt his prayer, he said:

“I seized him and throttled him until his saliva flowed onto my hand. I wanted to tie him to one of the mosque’s pillars, but then I remembered the supplication of my brother Solomon, so I released him.”

He did not employ the jinn at all. Rather, he called them to faith in Allah, recited the Quran to them, conveyed the message to them, and received their pledge of allegiance, just as he did with humans. What he was granted, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was greater than what Solomon was granted: he directed jinn and humans to worship Allah alone and to attain happiness in this world and the Hereafter, seeking no personal benefit other than Allah’s Face and His pleasure.

He chose to be a servant-messenger rather than a prophet-king. David, Solomon, and Joseph were prophet-kings, while Abraham, Moses, Jesus, and Muhammad were servant-messengers. The latter station is superior, just as the foremost, those brought near to Allah, are superior to the righteous, the people of the right hand.

Many who witness these extraordinary wonders believe them to be miracles granted to the friends of Allah, and many of the people of

---

## Page 6729

speculative theology and religious learning have failed to distinguish the extraordinary signs granted to prophets and the righteous from those possessed by Satan’s allies—sorcerers, soothsayers, unbelievers among the polytheists and the People of the Book, and adherents of innovation and misguidance among those who have entered Islam. They have treated extraordinary phenomena as a single category, saying:

“Any of them can constitute a prophetic miracle if accompanied by a claim to prophethood, its presentation as proof, and a challenge to produce its like. If an unbeliever or sorcerer who is not a prophet claims prophethood, Allah must deprive him of whatever such powers he possessed and appoint someone to match his feat. Were one of these people to challenge a prophet, Allah would render that challenger incapable.”

Thus, in their view, the defining feature of prophetic miracles is simply that those to whom the prophet is sent cannot produce anything like the extraordinary things he brings forth. They say, “People’s inability to match them is itself a departure from the customary course of events.” This, then, is what constitutes prophetic miracles according to them.

In this they resembled their Muʿtazilite predecessors, who said that prophetic miracles were departures from the customary course of events. The latter, however, denied the miracles granted to the righteous and denied that sorcery and soothsaying were anything more than sleight of hand and trickery. They did not know that devils assist in such things.

The former affirmed the miracles granted to the righteous, but then claimed that Muslims had unanimously agreed that such phenomena occur only through a righteous man or a prophet. They said, “If they appear at a man’s hands, then, by virtue of this consensus, he is righteous.” Yet these very people have themselves stated that sorcerers can produce their like. They thus contradicted themselves on this matter, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 6730

As a result, many people do not know what powers sorcerers and soothsayers possess or what wonders devils perform, and they suppose that such things occur only through a righteous man. Someone through whom these phenomena appear therefore imagines them to be miracles granted to the righteous, reinforcing his conviction that his path is the path of Allah’s friends. Others likewise think this of him. They then say, “Once someone becomes a friend of Allah, no objection may be raised against him.”

Some of them see such a man violating what is known beyond doubt to be part of the Messenger’s religion: abandoning the obligatory prayers; consuming vile substances such as wine, hashish, carrion and the like; committing indecent acts; engaging in obscenity and vulgarity in speech; wronging people; killing without right; and associating partners with Allah. Yet they still believe him to be one of Allah’s friends, to whom Allah Most High has graciously granted these miracles without any deeds on his part. They do not know that these are the works of devils and come from the devils’ allies, and that devils use them to mislead people and lead them astray.

Devils have become involved in various forms of this. Sometimes they come to a person in a dream, and one of them says, “I am Abū Bakr al-Siddīq. I shall lead you in repentance to me and become your shaykh, and you shall lead others in repentance to me.” He puts a head covering on the man, who wakes with it on his head.
