The man has no doubt that it was al-Siddīq who came to him, not knowing that it was a devil. Things like this have happened to several shaykhs in Iraq, al-Jazīra and Syria.

Sometimes a devil cuts a man’s hair while he is asleep, and he wakes to find his hair cut. At other times, he says, “I am Shaykh so-and-so,” and the man has no doubt that the shaykh himself came to him and cut his hair.

---

## Page 6731

Often a man calls upon his shaykh, living or dead, for help, and the devils come to him in that shaykh’s form. They may deliver him from what he fears, leaving him in no doubt that the shaykh himself came to him, or that an angel assumed his form and came to him. He does not know that the one who appeared in that form was merely a devil. Because he associated partners with Allah, the devils led him astray; angels do not answer someone who associates partners with Allah.

At other times, they come to someone who is alone in the wilderness. He may be a king or a high-ranking emir, an unbeliever who has become separated from his companions, grown thirsty and feared death. A jinn comes to him in human form, gives him water, calls him to Islam and leads him in repentance. The man embraces Islam at his hands, and the jinn leads him in repentance, feeds him and shows him the way. The man asks, “Who are you?” He replies, “I am so-and-so.” He is one of the believing jinn.

Something of this kind happened in connection with me. I was in Egypt, in the citadel, and many Turks from the eastern lands experienced something like this. That figure told one of them, “I am Ibn Taymiyya.” The emir had no doubt that it was indeed me, and informed the king of Mārdīn. The king of Mārdīn then sent a messenger about this to the king of Egypt. I was in prison, so they found this astonishing, although I had never left prison. Rather, this was a jinn who loved us and was doing for the Tatar Turks what I used to do for them when they came to Damascus: I would call them to Islam, and whenever one of them pronounced the two testimonies of faith, I would feed them

---

## Page 6732

whatever was available. He did for them what I used to do, intending thereby to honor me, so that the man would think I was the one who had done it.

Some people asked me, “Why could it not have been an angel?” I replied, “No. An angel does not lie, yet this being said, ‘I am Ibn Taymiyya,’ knowing that he was lying.”

Many people have seen someone who said, “I am al-Khidr,” when he was actually a jinn. Some people then came to reject these accounts in denial of al-Khidr’s death, while those who knew the accounts to be true became certain that al-Khidr was alive. Both groups are mistaken. Those who saw someone saying, “I am al-Khidr,” are numerous and truthful, and the accounts have been transmitted through numerous independent channels. Yet they were mistaken in thinking that he was al-Khidr; he was merely a jinn.

Similar things therefore happen to Jews and Christians. Someone claiming to be al-Khidr often comes to them in their churches. Likewise, someone claiming to be al-Khidr comes to the Jews in their synagogues. There are more true accounts of this than can be included here. They show that those who saw someone and thought he was al-Khidr were telling the truth, but were mistaken in thinking he was al-Khidr: he was merely a jinn.

He may also claim to be the Messiah, Moses, Muhammad, Abū Bakr, Umar, or such-and-such a shaykh. All of this has happened. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever sees me in a dream has truly seen me

---

## Page 6733

, for Satan cannot assume my form.” Ibn Abbās said: “In the form he had during his lifetime.”

This concerns seeing him in a dream. As for wakefulness, anyone who thinks that one of the dead appears in person before people’s eyes before the Day of Resurrection has been misled by his own ignorance. This is where the Christians went astray: they believed that the Messiah, after being crucified—as they suppose—came to the disciples, spoke to them, and gave them instructions. This is mentioned in their Gospels, all of which attest to it. The one who came was a devil who said, “I am the Messiah”; he was not the Messiah himself. It is possible that the disciples were deceived by something like this, just as many Muslim shaykhs have been deceived. However, what the Messiah instructed them to convey before he was raised up is the truth that they were obliged to convey. He was not raised up until he had conveyed the messages of his Lord, so there was no need for him to return after being raised to heaven.

After al-Hallāj was killed, someone would come to his followers saying, “I am al-Hallāj,” and they would see him before their very eyes in al-Hallāj’s form. Likewise, after the death of a shaykh in Egypt called al-Dasūqī, written messages and letters would reach his followers in his name. A truthful man among his followers showed me the letter he had sent. I saw that it was in the handwriting of the jinn—and I have seen the handwriting of the jinn more than once—and it contained statements of the sort made by jinn. That devotee believed that the shaykh was alive and used to say, “He has departed.” Then he died.

Likewise, there was another shaykh in the East who performed extraordinary feats through the jinn. It was said that, after this, he would visit the closest of

---

## Page 6734

his followers in his own form, and they would believe it was him. Likewise, among those who believed that ʿAlī or Muhammad ibn al-Hanafiyyah was still alive, a jinn would come to some of their companions in his likeness. Similarly, one of the Rāfidah may sometimes see the one they await, when what he actually sees is a jinn.

This is a broad subject, and such occurrences are frequent. The more ignorant a people are, the more common these things are among them. They occur more among polytheists than among Christians, and they occur among Christians just as they do among those who have entered Islam. Such things lead some people to embrace Islam and others to repent—people who are more astray than those involved in these occurrences. Through them, they move to something better than their former condition. This is like a human shaykh given to lying and wickedness: some unbelievers may come to him, and he calls them to Islam. They embrace it and become better than they were, even if that man’s intention is corrupt. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, Allah supports this religion through a wicked man and through people who have no share of good.”

This is also like the arguments and proofs advanced by many proponents of speculative theology and reasoned opinion. These silence many adherents of falsehood and strengthen the hearts of many adherents of truth, even if they are themselves false, for other arguments are more false still. Good and evil have degrees. Thus, some people benefit from these arguments, moving from their former position to something better.

---

## Page 6735

Many Muslims who adhere to religious innovations—including the Rāfidah, the Jahmiyyah, and others—have travelled to the lands of the unbelievers, where great numbers embraced Islam through them and benefited thereby. They became Muslims who adhered to religious innovations, which was better than remaining unbelievers. Likewise, a ruler may undertake a military campaign in which he wrongs both Muslims and unbelievers, thereby incurring sin. Nevertheless, it may benefit many people who were unbelievers and then became Muslims. Compared with the conduct of someone who fulfils his duty, this is an evil; for those unbelievers, however, it is a good.

Likewise, people may hear weak hadiths concerning encouragement and warning, virtues, legal rulings, and stories, and thereby move to something better than their former condition, even when those reports are false. This resembles a man who embraces Islam out of desire for worldly gain and fear of the sword. Once he has embraced Islam and spent a long time among Muslims, faith enters his heart. The very humbling of the unbelief he had followed, his subjugation, and his coming under Muslim rule are better than his remaining an unbeliever. He has thus moved to something better than his former condition, and the evil in which he lived has diminished. Then, if Allah wills to guide him, He places faith in his heart.

Allah Most High sent the messengers to secure benefits and perfect them, and to eliminate harms and reduce them. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called people to faith to the fullest extent possible

---

## Page 6736

and brought each person, as far as possible, to a better state than before.

“For all there are ranks according to what they have done, so that He may repay them in full for their deeds, and they will not be wronged.”

Most speculative theologians refute falsehood with falsehood and religious innovation with religious innovation. Yet they may refute the falsehood of unbelievers—polytheists and People of the Book—with falsehood held by Muslims, so that an unbeliever becomes a Muslim who adheres to religious innovation. More specifically, some of them refute manifest innovations, such as that of the Rāfidah, with a lesser innovation—namely, that of adherents of the Sunnah. We have already discussed the various categories of religious innovation.

There is no doubt that the Mu‘tazilah are better than the Rāfidah and the Khawārij. The Mu‘tazilah acknowledge the caliphate of all four caliphs, and all of them profess allegiance to Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, and ‘Uthmān. They are likewise known to profess allegiance to ‘Alī, and some prefer him to Abū Bakr and ‘Umar. However, one of their early figures is reported to have said:

“One of the two parties at the Battle of the Camel was guilty of grave sin, but I do not know which.”

They also reported that he said:

“If ‘Alī and al-Zubayr were to testify, I would not accept their testimony, because one of them was guilty of grave sin, though it is not known which.”

If ‘Alī were to testify alongside another man, there are two views concerning the acceptance of his testimony. This position is exceptional among them; the overwhelming majority hold ‘Alī in great esteem. They are well known for censuring Mu‘āwiyah, Abū Mūsā, and ‘Amr ibn al-‘Ās on account of ‘Alī. Some of them declare these men unbelievers and evildoers, unlike Talhah and al-Zubayr

---

## Page 6737

and ‘Ā’ishah, for they say that these three repented of fighting him. All of them profess allegiance to ‘Uthmān and hold Abū Bakr and ‘Umar in great esteem. They regard sins as grave matters and therefore strive to be truthful, like the Khawārij; they do not fabricate lies as the Rāfidah do. Nor do they advocate establishing an abode distinct from the abode of Islam, as the Khawārij do. They have written books explaining the Quran and defending the Messenger, and they possess many merits that give them an advantage over the Khawārij and the Rāfidah. Their aim is to affirm Allah’s oneness, mercy, wisdom, and truthfulness, and obedience to Him. Their five principles derive from these five qualities. Yet they erred in some of what they said concerning each of their five principles.

Thus, under “divine oneness,” they included negating Allah’s attributes, denying that He will be seen, and asserting that the Quran is created, thereby agreeing with the Jahmiyyah. Under “justice,” they included the claim that He does not will what occurs, that what He does not will occurs, and that He did not create His servants’ actions. They thus denied His power, will, and creation in order to affirm justice. Under mercy, they included denying things He created because they did not recognize the wisdom in them.

Likewise, they and the Khawārij upheld “the fulfillment of threats of punishment” to affirm that the Lord is truthful and does not lie. For, in their understanding, He had announced a general threat of punishment, and unless this position were upheld, it would follow that He had lied. They erred in their understanding of the threat.

Likewise, by “enjoining right and forbidding wrong by the sword,” they intended obedience to Allah and His Messenger, just as the Khawārij and the Zaydiyyah do, but they erred in this. Likewise, by denying extraordinary occurrences other than prophetic miracles, they intended to affirm prophethood

---

## Page 6738

and defend it. Yet they erred in their approach, for the truth cannot be defended by denying what is true. This was because they had not properly understood what distinguishes the signs of the prophets.

The Ash‘ariyyah refuted innovations of the Mu‘tazilah, the Rāfidah, the Jahmiyyah, and others, exposed contradictions in their doctrines, and upheld hadith, the Sunnah, and the way of the community. Their explanations of the contradictions of the leading proponents of innovation, and their refutations of them, benefited many people. Al-Ash‘arī had himself belonged to the Mu‘tazilah and remained upon their doctrine for forty years, studying under Abū ‘Alī al-Jubbā’ī. When he abandoned their doctrine, he was thoroughly familiar with their principles, how to refute them, and how to expose their contradictions.

[As for what he continued to uphold of the Sunnah] (*), it was not peculiar to the Mu‘tazilah, but belonged to what they shared with the Jahmiyyah. As for the doctrines peculiar to the Mu‘tazilah, al-Ash‘arī did not side with them on any of these; rather, he opposed them in all their principles. In “questions of divine justice, religious designations, and rulings,” he inclined toward the doctrine of Jahm and others like him. Many groups, such as “the Najjāriyyah,” the followers of Husayn al-Najjār, and “the Dirāriyyah,” the followers of Dirār ibn ‘Amr, differ with the Mu‘tazilah concerning divine decree, religious designations and rulings, and the fulfillment of threats of punishment.

The Mu‘tazilah are among those furthest removed from the path of those who experience spiritual unveiling and extraordinary occurrences, and from the path of the Sufis; they denounce and disparage it.

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 111):
> Regarding the words, “As for what he retained of the Sunnah,” it appears that an omission occurred here. The correct wording is: “As for what he retained of [opposition to] the Sunnah.” Allah knows best.

---

## Page 6739

Likewise, they denounce the Christians more vehemently than they denounce the Jews, although they are closer to the Jews, just as the Sufis and those like them are closer to the Christians. The Christians have worship, asceticism, and moral conduct, but lack knowledge and insight, so they are astray. The Jews have knowledge and rational inquiry, but lack righteous intention, worship, asceticism, and noble character, so they have incurred Allah’s anger, while the Christians are astray.

Abū Muhammad ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Abī Hātim said:
“I know of no disagreement among the Quran commentators concerning this expression.”

He narrated through his chain of transmission, from Abū Rawq, from Ibn ‘Abbās:
“‘Nor the way of those who have gone astray’: these are the Christians, whom Allah led astray because of the lies they fabricated against Him. This means: ‘Inspire us with Your true religion—which is that there is no god but Allah alone, without partner—so that You do not become angry with us as You became angry with the Jews, nor lead us astray as You led the Christians astray, and thus punish us as You punish them.’ That is: ‘Protect us from that through Your gentleness, mercy, compassion, and power.’”

Ibn Abī Hātim said:
“I know of no disagreement among the Quran commentators concerning this expression.”

Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah said: “They used to say: ‘Whoever among our scholars becomes corrupt bears a resemblance to the Jews, and whoever among our worshippers becomes corrupt bears a resemblance to the Christians.’”

The speculative theologians base their approach on examining knowledge and its evidence. They therefore exalt knowledge and the means of attaining it—namely, evidence—and following the path to it—namely, rational inquiry. Those devoted to asceticism exalt spiritual aspiration, the aspirant, and the path of those who pursue such aspiration.

---

## Page 6740

Thus, the ascetics base their approach on spiritual aspiration, while the speculative theologians base theirs on rational inquiry—the faculty of knowledge. Those who follow the straight path need both, and both must accord with what the Messenger brought. For faith consists of words, deeds, and conformity to the Sunnah.

The speculative theologians exalted rational inquiry and turned away from spiritual aspiration. They exalted rational inquiry in general without adhering to inquiry prescribed by revelation. They therefore erred in two respects. First, they failed to give spiritual aspiration its due importance: although they regard outward deeds as obligatory, they do not understand the deeds of the heart or their true nature. Second, in their inquiry they failed to distinguish between true inquiry prescribed by revelation, which the Lawgiver commanded and spoke of, and false, innovated inquiry that is forbidden.

Likewise, the “Sufis” exalted spiritual aspiration in general—the aspiration of the heart—condemned caprice, and went to great lengths in this regard. Yet many of them failed to distinguish between aspiration prescribed by revelation, which accords with the command of Allah and His Messenger, and innovated aspiration. Rather, they devoted themselves to the path of spiritual aspiration instead of the path of rational inquiry, and many of them turned away. Error thus crept in through these two avenues.

For this reason, Christians are drawn to the Sufis, and the Sufis to them, while Jews are drawn to the speculative theologians, and the speculative theologians to them. Between Jews and Christians there is the utmost mutual aversion and hatred. Likewise, between those devoted to speculative theology and opinion-based reasoning and those devoted to Sufism and asceticism

---

## Page 6741

there is mutual aversion and hatred. Both are departures from the straight path—the path of those whom Allah has blessed: the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous. What excellent companions they are! We ask Allah the Magnificent to guide us and all our brethren to the straight path, the path of those whom He has blessed, not those who have incurred His wrath, nor those who have gone astray. Amen.

Section

If it is objected:

If the books of the Gospels in their possession state that the Messiah was crucified and that, some days after the crucifixion, he came to them and told them, “I am the Messiah”—and they do not say that Satan assumed his form, for Satan is not flesh and bone—“and these are the marks of the nails,” or words to that effect, then where is the Gospel of which Allah, Mighty and Majestic, said:

“Let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed therein”?

Before this, He said:

“And We sent Jesus, son of Mary, following in their footsteps, confirming the Torah that came before him. We gave him the Gospel, containing guidance and light, confirming the Torah that came before it, and as guidance and admonition for the God-fearing.”

“Let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed therein. And whoever does not judge by what Allah has revealed—it is they who are defiantly disobedient.”

And indeed,

---

## Page 6742

He had said before this:

“How do they come to you for judgment when they have the Torah, which contains Allah’s judgment, and then turn away after that? Such people are not believers.”

“Indeed, We sent down the Torah, containing guidance and light. By it, the prophets who submitted to Allah judged for the Jews, as did the godly scholars and rabbis, for they were entrusted with safeguarding Allah’s Book and were witnesses to it.”

He also said:

“Had they upheld the Torah and the Gospel and what was revealed to them from their Lord, they would have received provision from above them and from beneath their feet.”

He also said:

“Say, ‘O People of the Scripture, you have no ground to stand on unless you uphold the Torah and the Gospel and what has been revealed to you from your Lord.’ What has been revealed to you from your Lord will surely increase many of them in transgression and disbelief, so do not grieve over the disbelieving people.”

This is a command to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to say this to the People of the Scripture to whom he was sent—those living in his time and those who would come after them until the Day of Resurrection. He was not commanded to say it to those among them who had already repented. Likewise, His statement:

“How do they come to you for judgment when they have the Torah, which contains Allah’s judgment?”

speaks of the Jews then living and states that they possessed the Torah, containing Allah’s judgment. Likewise, His statement:

“Let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed therein”

is a command from Allah, conveyed through Muhammad, to the people of the Gospel. And who is not subject to commands conveyed through Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace?

Before addressing this, the reply is:

It has been said that nowhere in the world is there a copy containing exactly what Allah revealed in the Torah and the Gospel; rather, that has been altered. For the Torah’s transmission through numerous independent transmitters was interrupted, and the Gospel

---

## Page 6743

was received from only four individuals.

Some of those who hold this view claim that much of what is in the Torah or the Gospel is false and is not Allah’s speech; others say that this concerns only a small portion. It has also been said that no one altered any of the wording of the Scriptures; rather, they distorted their meanings through interpretation. Each of these two views has been held by many Muslims.

The correct view is the third: sound copies existed on earth and survived until the time of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, alongside many altered copies. Anyone who says that nothing in the copies was altered is denying something he cannot rule out. Anyone who says that all the copies after the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, were altered has said something he knows to be mistaken. The Quran commands them to judge by what Allah revealed in the Torah and the Gospel and states that they contain His judgment. Nowhere does the Quran state that they changed all the copies.

That being so, we say: He, glorified is He, said:

“Let the people of the Gospel judge by what Allah has revealed therein.”

What Allah revealed is what they received from the Messiah. As for the Gospel’s account of his circumstances after he was raised up, this is like the Torah’s mention of the death of Moses, peace be upon him. It is well known that these accounts in the Torah and the Gospel concerning Moses and Jesus after their departure are not part of what Allah revealed or what people received from Moses and Jesus. Rather, they are material that people wrote alongside that revelation to explain the circumstances of their departure. This is merely an account from

---

## Page 6744

those who lived after them about their circumstances. It is not part of what Allah revealed to them, nor of what they commanded during their lifetimes or told people.

The same applies to His words:

“You have no ground to stand upon until you uphold the Torah, the Gospel, and what has been revealed to you from your Lord.”

And His words:

“Had they upheld the Torah, the Gospel, and what was revealed to them from their Lord, they would have enjoyed provision from above them and from beneath their feet.”

Upholding the Scripture means acting upon what Allah commanded in it, including believing what He communicated through the Messenger. What its copyists wrote after the Messenger’s death—his lifespan and similar details—is not part of what Allah revealed to the Messenger, nor of the commands or accounts conveyed. Something similar may occur in authored books: a person writes a book, and its copyist mentions at the end the author’s lifespan, lineage, age, and other details that are not the author’s own words.

For this reason, the Companions and the scholars instructed that the Quran be kept free of additions and that nothing other than the Quran be written in its written copies. Thus, neither surah names, markings for every five or ten verses, “Amen,” nor anything else should be written in them. The early copies were written in this manner by people of knowledge. In some copies, however, the copyists wrote the surah names, markings for every five or ten verses, and indications of where to pause and resume recitation. At the end of the copy, a copyist might also write an affirmation of its truth, a supplication, his name, and similar things. None of this is part of the Quran.

---

## Page 6745

So too, the accounts in the Gospel of Christ’s crucifixion and death, and of his coming to the disciples after being raised up, are not statements made by Christ. Rather, they concern what those who came after him saw. What Allah revealed is what was heard from Christ as he conveyed Allah’s message.

If it is said:

“If the disciples believed that Christ was crucified and that he came to them several days later, yet they were the ones who transmitted the Gospel and the religion from Christ, then this casts doubt on their transmission.”

The answer is:

What must be accepted from the disciples, and from everyone who transmitted from the prophets, is only what they transmitted from the prophets, for authoritative proof lies in the prophets’ words. Anything beyond that depends upon evidence: if it is true, it is accepted; otherwise, it is rejected.

Thus, what the Companions transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, of the Quran and hadith must be accepted, especially what was transmitted through numerous independent channels, such as the Quran and many Prophetic traditions. As for their own statements, whatever they unanimously agreed upon is protected from error by their consensus. Whatever they disputed must be referred back to Allah and the Messenger.

Umar initially denied the death of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, until Abū Bakr corrected him. They also disagreed about his burial until Abū Bakr settled the matter with the hadith he narrated. They disagreed about dispatching Usāmah’s army, and they disagreed about fighting those who withheld zakat. None of this undermined what they transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

The Christians are not unanimous concerning Christ’s crucifixion, and none of them witnessed

---

## Page 6746

his crucifixion; for it was the Jews who crucified the person in question, and none of Christ’s companions was present. Those Jews who crucified him mistook the crucified man for Christ. It has also been said that they knew he was not Christ, but lied and misled the people. The first account, however, is the well-known one and is accepted by most people.

Thus, the Christians have no report from anyone they consider trustworthy establishing that Christ was crucified. Rather, their principal basis for this belief is the person who came several days later and said, “I am Christ.” That was a devil. They themselves acknowledge that devils often appear, with one claiming to be a prophet or a righteous man, saying, “I am such-and-such a prophet or righteous man,” when he is actually a devil. There are numerous accounts of this, such as the story of the monk to whom someone appeared and said, “I am Christ. I have come to guide you.” Recognizing that it was the devil, the monk replied, “You have already conveyed the message, and we act upon it. If you now bring anything that contradicts it, we will not accept it from you.”

Neither the Christians nor the Jews, then, have knowledge that Christ was crucified, as Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, those who differ concerning it are in doubt about it. They have no knowledge of it, following only conjecture.”

He attributed the claim of having killed him to the Jews, saying:

“And their saying, ‘We killed the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, the Messenger of Allah.’”

They deserve punishment for these words, since they believed it permissible to kill Christ. Whoever considers killing him permissible is like one who has killed him. Thus, in making this statement, they are both lying and sinning. If they say it as a boast, they gain no glory, because they did not kill him; yet they incur sin for considering it lawful and striving to carry it out. The Prophet said

---

## Page 6747

—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—:

“When two Muslims confront each other with their swords, both the killer and the one killed will be in the Fire.” They asked, “O Messenger of Allah, that accounts for the killer, but what about the one killed?” He replied, “He was intent on killing his companion.”

Regarding His words:

“Indeed, those who differ concerning it are in doubt about it,”

some say that this refers to the Jews, while others say it refers to the Christians. The verse encompasses both groups. Regarding His words, “are in doubt about it,” some say this means about his being killed. Others say it means about him—that is, they are in doubt whether he was crucified or not, just as they differed concerning him: the Jews said he was a sorcerer, while the Christians said he was God. Thus, the Jews and Christians differed over whether he was crucified or not, and they are in doubt about it:

“They have no knowledge of it.”

If this is their position concerning the crucifixion, what, then, of the one who came after he was raised up and claimed to be Christ?

If it is asked: If the disciples who knew him held such a belief, where, then, are those believers in him of whom He said:

“And I will place those who follow you above those who disbelieve,”

and:

“So We supported those who believed against their enemy, and they became victorious”?

The answer is: The supposition of those among them who thought that he was crucified does not undermine their faith, provided they did not distort what Christ brought, but acknowledged that he was Allah’s servant and Messenger, His word which He conveyed to Mary, and a spirit from Him. Given this, their belief that he was crucified does not detract from

---

## Page 6748

his faith, for this is a belief that he died in a particular manner. Crucifixion would, at most, mean that he was killed, and a prophet’s being killed does not detract from his prophethood. The Children of Israel killed many prophets. Allah Most High said:

“How many a prophet fought with many devoted followers at his side,”

to the end of the verse. Allah Most High also said:

“Muhammad is no more than a messenger; messengers have passed away before him. If he dies or is killed, will you turn back on your heels?”

Likewise, the belief of those among them who thought that he came and spoke to them after being raised up resembles the belief of many Muslim shaykhs that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—came to them while they were awake. They do not become disbelievers on that account. Indeed, this belief was held by someone who was among the most devoted to following the Sunnah and the Prophet, and who surpassed others in asceticism and worship. He would be visited by someone whom he thought to be the Messenger of Allah. This was an error on his part that did not render him a disbeliever. Likewise, the supposition of those disciples who thought that the one who appeared to them was Christ does not take them outside faith in Christ, nor does it undermine what they transmitted from him.

When ʿUmar believed that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had not died, but had gone to his Lord as Moses had gone, and that he would not die until his Companions had died, this did not detract from his faith. It was merely an error, which he retracted.

---

## Page 6749

Section

Allah Most High’s words concerning this matter:

“They have no knowledge of it, but follow only conjecture,”

reproach them for following conjecture without knowledge. Likewise, He says:

“These are nothing but names you and your forefathers have given them, for which Allah has sent down no authority. They follow nothing but conjecture and what their souls desire, although guidance has already come to them from their Lord.”

Likewise, He says:

“They have no knowledge of it. They follow nothing but conjecture, and conjecture is no substitute for the truth.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Those who call upon others besides Allah as partners follow nothing but conjecture; they do nothing but guess.”

And He says:

“Is one who guides to the truth more worthy of being followed, or one who cannot find the way unless he is guided? What is the matter with you? How do you judge?”

“Most of them follow nothing but conjecture. Conjecture is no substitute for the truth. Surely Allah knows all that they do.”

These are several passages in which Allah condemns those who follow nothing but conjecture. Likewise, His words:

“Say: ‘Do you have any knowledge that you can produce for us? You follow nothing but conjecture and do nothing but guess.’”

“Say: ‘To Allah belongs the conclusive argument,’”

demand knowledge and condemn those who follow conjecture without possessing knowledge. Likewise, He says: “Tell me on the basis of knowledge, if

---

## Page 6750

you are truthful.” His words:

“Indeed, many lead others astray through their desires, without knowledge,”

and similar passages condemn those who act without knowledge and follow conjecture. Yet the mass-transmitted Sunnah and the consensus of the Muslim community establish that a judge rules on the testimony of two witnesses and, if there are no witnesses, requires the opposing litigant to swear an oath.

In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) is reported to have said:

“You bring your disputes before me, and some of you may present their arguments more eloquently than others. I judge only according to what I hear. If I award anyone something that rightfully belongs to his brother, he must not take it, for I am only assigning him a piece of the Fire.”

The Muslims agree that exercising independent judgment in “ascertaining whether the grounds for applying a ruling are present” is indispensable. Examples include two just persons determining the equivalent compensation for game killed, or using indications to determine the direction of the Kaaba when it is uncertain, and similar matters. A person cannot be certain of the result: the direction of prayer may lie somewhere other than the direction indicated by his judgment. Likewise, when a judge rules, he may have awarded one litigant something that rightfully belongs to the other.

This is unavoidable in the evidence for legal rulings. The apparent general applicability of texts may admit of a contrary interpretation; the same applies to solitary reports and analogical reasoning. Although some have disputed analogical reasoning, the jurists among them, such as the Zāhirīs, have not disputed solitary reports. Those who have disputed both have not disputed general applicability, such as the Baghdad Muʿtazilites. Even those who dispute general applicability and analogical reasoning, such as some of the Rāfidīs—including al-Mūsawī and others like him—have not disputed transmitted reports. The Imāmīs rely principally on what has been transmitted from the Twelve Imams, so they cannot dispense with transmission, and there is no one who

---

## Page 6751

can dispense with apparent textual meanings, transmitted reports, and analogical reasoning. Rather, everyone must act on some of these while acknowledging that the contrary is possible. This is acting on conjecture, yet the Quran has forbidden following conjecture. People have taken different approaches to explaining why this is permissible.

One group said:

“Only knowledge is ever to be followed; conjecture is not to be acted upon at all.”

They maintain that a solitary report yields knowledge, and say the same of apparent textual meanings. Indeed, they say, “We are certain that whoever disagrees with us is mistaken, and we overturn his ruling,” as Dāwūd and his followers maintain. Their principal basis, however, is merely what they suppose to be the apparent meaning—as for the presumption of continuity, it is in many cases among the weakest forms of evidence. Moreover, much of what they adduce as evidence may not represent the apparent meaning of the wording at all; rather, its apparent meaning is the opposite.

Another group said:

“Since evidence has established the obligation to act on what is most probable, we are following knowledge. Thus, when knowledge is present, we act on knowledge, not conjecture.”

This is the approach of the judge Abū Bakr and his followers. Here arises the familiar question concerning the “definition of jurisprudence” as knowledge of the practical rulings of the Sharia. Al-Rāzī defined it as “knowledge of the practical rulings of the Sharia, established individually through inference, such that their being part of the religion is not known self-evidently.” He said:

“If you ask, ‘Jurisprudence concerns probable judgments, so how have you defined it as knowledge?’ I reply: When a jurist exercising independent judgment considers it most likely that one case shares with another the operative basis of

---

## Page 6752

the ruling, he is certain that he must act on the conclusion to which his probable judgment has led him. Knowledge is thus certainly attained, while conjecture figures in the process leading to it.”

The substance of this answer is that there are two premises here:

First: I have formed a conjectural judgment.

Second: Conclusive evidence has established the obligation to follow this conjectural judgment.

The “first premise” is known through introspection, while the “second” is practical and established through inference. Thus, conjecture itself is not a premise in the proof, as some have imagined. Nevertheless, it may be objected:

The obligation to act on this conjecture is a ruling belonging to legal theory, not jurisprudence itself. Rather, jurisprudence is the conjectural judgment derived from apparent textual meanings, solitary reports, and analogy; legal theory establishes that acting on this conjecture is obligatory. Jurists, as such, do not address this question. Hence, this practical ruling of legal theory is not jurisprudence itself.

This answer is that of the judge Abū Bakr, who based it on his own foundational position: according to him, every jurist exercising independent judgment is correct, there is no objectively correct ruling to be sought, and there is no evidence requiring one conjectural judgment to be preferred over another. Rather, in his view, conjectural judgments arise by chance.

Al-Ghazālī and others who defended his position said:

“A conjectural judgment may arise from a person’s inclination towards one of two opinions rather than the other, as when someone of stern disposition inclines towards one opinion, and someone of gentle disposition towards another.”

Accordingly, in their view, whenever a jurist exercising independent judgment finds a conjectural judgment within himself, Allah’s ruling for him is

---

## Page 6753

to follow this conjectural judgment. Abū al-Maʿālī and others vehemently rejected this view of his, and they were justified in doing so. First, it amounts to an obstinate denial of the obvious: conjectural judgments have supporting indications and evidence whose presence requires one judgment to be preferred over another. This is self-evident, and the revealed Law affirms it and gives some things precedence over others. The discussion concerns two questions: following conjecture, and whether jurisprudence consists of conjectural judgments.

As for the first question, the correct answer is the third answer: in everything Allah Most High has commanded, He has commanded only that one act on knowledge. In questions whose answers are unclear, a person must examine the evidence and act upon the stronger view. That this is the stronger view is known when there is something conclusively established. If, however, he is uncertain whether one view outweighs the other, he does not act upon it. When he judges it likely that one is stronger, he does so only because he has evidence indicating its greater strength.

There is a difference between believing that one view is stronger and one belief being stronger than another. Believing that a view is stronger may amount to knowledge, and a person may refrain from acting until he knows which view is stronger. Even when he merely judges it likely that one is stronger, he must base that judgment on evidence which he considers stronger than the evidence supporting the other side. The greater strength of this evidence is not known, so the process must ultimately reach a point at which its greater strength is known to him. He is then following what he knows to be stronger. This is following knowledge, not conjecture; it is following what is best, as Allah says:

“Hold to them firmly, and command your people to follow the best of them.”

And He says:

“Those who listen to what is said and follow the best of it.”

And He says:

“Follow the best of what has been sent down to you from your Lord.”

Thus, if

---

## Page 6754

one of the two pieces of evidence is stronger, following it is best, and this is known. The jurist exercising independent judgment must therefore act upon what he knows to be stronger than the alternative—that is, upon the stronger of two conflicting pieces of evidence. In doing so, he acts only upon knowledge. This is the answer given by al-Hasan al-Basrī, Abū, and others. The Quran condemns those who follow nothing but conjecture, whose conjecture is not grounded in knowledge that this is stronger than the alternative. As Allah says:

“They have no knowledge of it; they follow only conjecture.”

And He says:

“Do you have any knowledge that you can produce for us? You follow nothing but conjecture.”

Likewise, elsewhere He condemns those who follow nothing but conjecture: they have mere conjecture, unaccompanied by knowledge, and they follow it. What the revealed Law prescribes, and what reasonable people practice, is to act only with knowledge that one alternative is stronger than the other. They thus hold its greater strength as a conviction on which to act. Yet its being stronger does not necessarily mean that the weaker alternative cannot be what is actually true.

This is as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained when he said:

“Some of you may be more skillful in presenting their case than others, and I judge only according to what I hear.”

If one litigant presents evidence, such as testimony in his favour, while the other produces no witness of his own, the judge knows that the former’s evidence is stronger. He therefore judges only on the basis of knowledge. Yet the other litigant may have evidence of which he is unaware, or which he cannot present effectively. For instance, he may have paid the other party or released him from liability, and have testimony supporting this of which he is unaware, or does not

---

## Page 6755

remember it, or does not dare to speak of it. He himself is then responsible for losing his right, since he failed to present his evidence. The judge ruled only with knowledge and justice; this man’s loss resulted from his own inability and negligence, not from the judge.

The same applies to the evidence for legal rulings. If two reports conflict, one reliably established with a connected chain of transmission and the other with a gap in its chain, the established, connected report is stronger than the disconnected one. This is known, because the narrator of the former is known to be upright and accurate, whereas the uprightness and accuracy of the other are unknown. It is like two witnesses, one whose uprightness has been attested and another whose uprightness has not: the accredited witness carries greater weight, although the other’s statement may in fact be true. Nevertheless, the jurist exercising independent judgment has acted only upon knowledge—his knowledge that one is stronger than the other. He is not someone who follows nothing but conjecture. This became clear to him only after exhaustive investigation into the person who transmitted that hadith with a gap in its chain and into the accreditation of this witness.

The narrator in the disconnected report may indeed be upright and accurate in his retention, just as this witness may be upright. We do not know that the narrator lacks uprightness; rather, we lack knowledge of the uprightness of either him or the witness. Their uprightness may remain unknown despite its actual strength and preponderance. This is where error arises in independent judgment. Yet no scholar can require the jurist to abandon what he knows in favour of something he does not know merely because it may actually be established.

If one of the two positions must be preferred, then what is known to be established must be preferred over what is not known to be established, even though the latter is not independently known to be absent. For if the two

---

## Page 6756

conflict and contradict one another, affirming one means negating the other. This known evidence is known to affirm the one and negate the other, while the unknown evidence does the reverse. If one must be preferred, then what is known to be established must certainly be preferred over what is not known to be established.

But it may be said: “It is not established with certainty.” We have already explained that there is a difference between believing that one view carries greater weight and a belief itself carrying greater weight. Believing that one view carries greater weight is knowledge, and the jurist exercising independent judgment has acted only upon that knowledge: his belief that this view outweighs the other. By contrast, one belief outweighing another is conjecture.

Yet he is not among those of whom Allah says:

“They follow nothing but conjecture.”

Rather, there is a conjecture that this view carries greater weight and a conjecture that the other does. The former conjecture is the stronger, and its greater weight is known. His ruling is therefore based on his knowledge of the stronger conjecture and its stronger evidence. This is something he knows, not merely conjectures. The same occurs in all branches of knowledge and practical pursuits, such as medicine, commerce, and so forth.

As for their claim that jurisprudence belongs to the realm of conjecture, a number of scholars, including Abū al-Khattāb, have offered another answer: the knowledge intended here is knowledge based on outward appearances, even though one allows that the reality may be otherwise, as in His words:

“If you know them to be believers.”

The precise answer, however, is twofold:

---

## Page 6757

The first is to say that most questions of jurisprudence that people need answered and on which fatwas are given are established by a scriptural text or consensus. Conjecture and disagreement concern only a small proportion of what people need, and this occurs in all other disciplines as well. Many disputed questions concern matters that rarely arise or are hypothetical. As for the indispensable knowledge people need concerning what is obligatory, forbidden, and permissible for them, it is known with certainty. What is necessarily known to be part of the religion is itself part of jurisprudence. No early scholar is known to have excluded it from jurisprudence, nor did anyone introduce this restrictive qualification except al-Rāzī and those like him. All jurists mention in their books of jurisprudence the obligations of prayer, zakah, pilgrimage, facing the direction of prayer, ablution, and ritual bathing to remove sexual impurity; the prohibition of intoxicating drink and indecent acts; and other matters necessarily known to be part of the religion.

Furthermore, whether something is necessarily known to be part of the religion is relative. Someone who has recently embraced Islam, or who grew up in a remote desert region, may not know it at all, let alone know it as a matter of necessity. Many scholars know as a matter of necessity that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, performed the prostrations for forgetfulness, ruled that blood money was to be borne by the ‘āqila (those collectively liable for its payment), and ruled that the child is attributed to the marital bed. There are other such matters that specialists know as a matter of necessity, while most people do not know them at all.

The second answer is to say that jurisprudence is only truly jurisprudence when it comes from a jurist who exercises independent judgment and reasons from evidence. Such a jurist knows that this evidence carries greater weight and that this conjecture carries greater weight.

---

## Page 6758

so jurisprudence consists in his knowledge that this evidence and this conjecture carry greater weight. Jurisprudence is not his certainty that he must act in accordance with the conclusion reached through his independent judgment. Rather, that certainty belongs to the principles of jurisprudence. The scholar of legal theory discusses evidence by category and speaks in general terms. He says: “When two pieces of evidence conflict, judgment must follow the stronger of the two.” He also says: “When a general text conflicts with a specific text, the specific text carries greater weight; and when a report with a connected chain of transmission conflicts with one whose chain is incomplete, the connected report carries greater weight.” He further says: “Where a general text is unaccompanied by indications restricting its scope, its inclusion of all individual instances is more probable than their exclusion, and one must act accordingly.”

The jurist, by contrast, discusses particular evidence concerning a particular ruling. For example, he may say that Allah’s words:

“The food of those who were given the Scripture is lawful for you, and your food is lawful for them, as are chaste believing women and chaste women from among those who were given the Scripture before you”

apply specifically to the People of the Book and were revealed after His words:

“Do not marry polytheist women.”

That latter verse does not encompass the People of the Book. Even if it does encompass them, this is a later, specific text, and thus abrogates and restricts the scope of the earlier text. The jurist therefore knows that this text’s indication of permissibility carries greater weight than that text’s indication of prohibition. He knows this preponderance with certainty. This jurisprudence, which is the jurist’s distinctive province, is certain knowledge, not conjecture. Whoever does not possess this knowledge is merely following the four imams and the majority of scholars who permitted marriage to women of the People of the Book. The belief of someone who merely follows others is not jurisprudence.

Hence his words, “who reasons from evidence concerning their particular instances”: the jurist has reasoned from evidence concerning the particular

---

## Page 6759

ruling being sought and asked about. Where he does not know which carries greater weight, he suspends judgment and expresses no opinion. If he is told, “Yet He has said:

‘Do not hold on to marriage bonds with disbelieving women,’”

he replies: “This was revealed in the year of al-Hudaybiyyah, and it refers to polytheist women, for the circumstances of its revelation establish with certainty that they are intended. Sūrat al-Mā’idah was revealed afterward. Its text is therefore specific and later, whereas the other is general and earlier; a later, specific text carries greater weight than an earlier, general text.”

Thus, when His words were revealed:

“Do not hold on to marriage bonds with disbelieving women,”

ʿUmar parted from a polytheist wife, as did others. This shows that they remained married to polytheist women until this verse was revealed. Had the verse in Sūrat al-Baqarah been revealed before this one, that would not have been the case. This therefore shows that the verse in Sūrat al-Baqarah came after the verse in Sūrat al-Mumtahanah, and that the verse in Sūrat al-Mā’idah came after the verse in Sūrat al-Baqarah. This reasoning, and others like it, is the reasoning of the jurist who knows that a particular piece of evidence, and the conjecture it yields, carry greater weight than opposing evidence. This is knowledge, not conjecture.

It has thus become clear that conjecture rests on evidence that gives rise to it, and that the scholar derives his knowledge only from what establishes knowledge of preponderance, not from conjecture itself—unless he knows that it carries greater weight. As for conjecture whose preponderance is not known, it is impermissible to follow it. This is what Allah condemned in those of whom He said:

“They follow nothing but conjecture.”

They follow nothing but conjecture and possess no knowledge. Had they known that their conjecture carried greater weight, they would have been following knowledge and would not have been among those who follow nothing but conjecture. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 6760

Section

There are three things to distinguish here.

First: the preponderant conjecture in the mind of the jurist exercising independent judgment.

Second: the conflicting pieces of evidence—which some speculative theologians call “indicators”—where the jurist knows that the evidence giving rise to that conjecture is stronger than the opposing evidence.

Third: there may, in fact, be other evidence supporting the alternative view of which the jurist is unaware. This is what happens in most matters involving independent juristic judgment. A person may hear a text of general application, just as Ibn ʿUmar and others heard that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade cutting leather socks and commanded that no one leave until bidding farewell to the House; or that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade wearing silk. Its apparent wording is general, and this outweighs the presumption that no prohibition applies. They therefore acted upon this stronger evidence, knowing with certainty that the prohibition takes precedence over that presumption.

Yet there may be specific evidence supporting the presumption. Since they were unaware of it, however, they could not abandon what they knew in favour of what they did not know. Thus, they would issue fatwas stating that a menstruating woman must perform the farewell circumambulation and cut her leather socks, and that silk is forbidden in both small and large quantities.

---

## Page 6761

And Ibn al-Zubayr considered it forbidden for both men and women, because of the general wording of the Prophet’s saying:

“Whoever wears silk in this world will not wear it in the Hereafter.”

Yet there were, in fact, specific texts establishing that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted a menstruating woman to depart without performing the farewell circumambulation; that she could wear leather socks and other garments forbidden to a man in ihrām, though she must avoid the face veil and gloves; and that he permitted an area of silk two, three, or four fingers wide. This was made clear in the Sahīh, in the report narrated by ʿUmar, of which his son ʿAbd Allāh was unaware. He also had a robe edged with silk.

When Ibn ʿUmar and others heard these specific texts, they revised their views. They then knew that there had, in fact, been evidence stronger than the evidence upon which they had continued to rely, but of which they had been unaware. In both situations, their judgments rested on knowledge; they were not among those who follow mere conjecture. Initially, they gave precedence to the general wording over the presumption of freedom from obligation or prohibition. This preference was based on knowledge, for the former unquestionably takes precedence, and Islamic law abounds in examples of this.

The texts in His Book establishing what Allah has made obligatory or forbidden—such as ablution, prayer, pilgrimage, and other matters—are texts of general application. Likewise, He prohibited such things as carrion, blood, and pork through texts of general application. These outweigh and take precedence over the presumption of freedom from obligation or prohibition, which would negate both obligation and prohibition.

Whoever gives these texts precedence has therefore judged on the basis of knowledge, applying the stronger of two pieces of evidence whose relative strength is known. He is not among those who follow mere conjecture. However, because he allows for the possibility that the text’s general scope has been qualified, his conclusion amounts to preponderant conjecture. If he knew that there was no such qualification, he would be certain of its general application. Likewise,

---

## Page 6762

if he knew that a category was intended, he would be certain that no restriction to particular instances applied. The same reasoning applies to all other evidence. For example, someone may rely on texts that have been abrogated without the abrogating text having reached him. Such was the case with those who prohibited preparing fruit infusions in vessels and visiting graves because the abrogating text had not reached them. Likewise, some continued to pray towards Jerusalem before learning of the abrogation, including Muslims living in the desert regions, Mecca, Abyssinia, and elsewhere.

These were distinct from those in Medina, some of whom offered a single prayer facing both directions: part towards one direction and part towards the other. When they learned of the abrogation during the prayer, they turned from facing Jerusalem to facing the Kaaba—from the direction of Syria to that of Yemen.

Al-Qādī Abū Bakr and others deny that there is an actual ruling to be sought through independent legal reasoning, or evidence pointing to it. They say that there is nothing beyond the conjecture in the mind of the jurist exercising independent judgment, that evidentiary indications have no fixed standard, and that no indication is stronger than another. Yet if they say this, it follows that someone who acts on the weaker rather than the stronger evidence is mistaken, whereas, on their view, there is no error in objective reality.

The early generations, the four imams, and the majority, however, maintain that some evidentiary indications are objectively stronger than others. A person must therefore exercise independent judgment and seek the strongest evidence. If he finds one piece of evidence stronger than another and finds nothing that opposes it, he acts upon it. Allah does not burden any soul beyond its capacity. If, in reality, there is evidence stronger than this,

---

## Page 6763

he is mistaken but excused, and is rewarded for his independent judgment and for acting upon what appeared to him to be the stronger evidence. His error is forgiven. The ruling that holds in reality is binding, but only if one is able to ascertain it; whoever is unable to ascertain it is not held accountable for failing to follow it.

If “error” means sin, then the jurist exercising independent judgment does not err. Rather, every such jurist is correct, obedient to Allah, and doing what Allah has commanded him to do. If it means failing to know the truth as it actually is, then only one is correct, and he receives two rewards. This is like those who independently determine the direction of the Kaaba and then pray in four different directions: the one who correctly faces the Kaaba—only one does so, and he receives two rewards for his independent judgment and his action—attains greater excellence than the others. The strong believer is more beloved to Allah than the weak believer. Whoever Allah grants greater knowledge and deeds, He grants greater reward in proportion to that increase in knowledge and deeds. The Most High says:

“That was Our argument which We gave Abraham against his people. We raise in degrees whom We will.”

Mālik reported from Zayd ibn Aslam that this means: “Through knowledge.” Likewise, He says in the account of Joseph:

“He could not have taken his brother under the king’s law unless Allah had willed it. We raise in degrees whom We will, and above everyone endowed with knowledge is one more knowing.”

It has thus become clear that all jurists exercising independent judgment spoke on the basis of knowledge and followed knowledge, that “jurisprudence” is among the noblest branches of knowledge, and that they are not among those who follow nothing but conjecture. Some of them, however, may possess knowledge that others lack, either because they heard something the others did not hear, or because they understood something the others did not understand. As the Most High says:

“And remember David and Solomon, when they judged concerning the field into which the people’s sheep had strayed by night. We were witnesses to their judgment.”

---

## Page 6764

“So We gave Solomon understanding of the case, and to each We gave judgment and knowledge.”

Such is the case with those who exercise independent judgment, inquiry, and reasoning from evidence in fundamental and subsidiary matters. None of the early Muslims or the imams distinguished between fundamental and subsidiary matters. Indeed, dividing religion into “two categories”—fundamentals and subsidiary matters—was unknown among the Companions and their successors. None of the early Muslims, the Companions, or their successors said that a scholar who exhausts his efforts in seeking the truth incurs sin, whether in fundamental or subsidiary matters. Rather, this distinction originated with the Muʿtazilah and was introduced into the principles of jurisprudence by those who transmitted it from them.

They reported that ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Hasan al-ʿAnbarī said, “Everyone who exercises independent judgment is correct,” meaning that he incurs no sin. This is the position of the majority of the imams, such as Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfiʿī, and others. Hence, they accept the testimony of adherents of deviant doctrines and pray behind them. As for those who reject their testimony—such as Mālik and Ahmad—this does not necessarily imply their sinfulness. Rather, the purpose is to denounce wrongdoing and shun those who openly display religious innovation. When such a person is shunned, people refrain from praying behind him, and his testimony is not accepted, this serves to prevent him from openly displaying religious innovation.

For this reason, Ahmad and others distinguished between someone who advocates religious innovation and openly displays it, and someone who does not. Likewise, al-Khiraqī said:

“Whoever prays behind someone who openly displays religious innovation or wrongdoing must repeat the prayer.”

A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

---

## Page 6765

Those who distinguished between fundamentals and subsidiary matters did not provide a criterion separating the two categories. Sometimes they say, “This is certain, while that is probable.” Yet many questions of practical rulings are certain, while many questions of fundamentals are probable in some people’s estimation. Whether something is certain or probable is a relative matter. At other times they say, “Fundamentals concern knowledge and reported truths, while subsidiary matters concern practice.” Yet denying many practical obligations constitutes unbelief, such as denying the obligation of prayer, zakat, fasting, and Hajj. Sometimes they say, “These are matters known through reason, while those are known through revelation.” But if they are matters known through reason, it does not follow that someone who errs concerning them must be declared an unbeliever, for unbelief is a judgment of revealed law and depends upon that law. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

When a person reflects on people’s disagreements, he finds that each group possesses knowledge that the other lacks, just as happens in questions of practical rulings.

An example is the preceding discussion of the five fundamentals: divine unity, justice, the intermediate position between the two positions, questions of religious designations and rulings, and the execution of the threatened punishment. These are the principles on which the Muʿtazilah base their allegiance to those who agree with them and their disavowal of those who disagree. We have already explained that they intended to affirm the Lord’s oneness, His justice, wisdom, mercy, and truthfulness, and obedience to His commands. Yet they erred concerning each of these matters, as explained above.

The same applies to those who opposed them among the Jahmiyyah and those who followed their approach, such as Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī and his followers: they opposed them concerning the five fundamentals, and they possessed knowledge

---

## Page 6766

that the others lacked, while the others possessed knowledge that these lacked. Neither group fully comprehended what the Quran and Sunnah explain concerning these matters; rather, they knew some things and were ignorant of others. For these determinists do not, in reality, affirm justice, wisdom, mercy, or truthfulness for Allah, whereas the other group sought to affirm these matters. As for justice, the determinists regard every possible act as just, while injustice, in their view, is impossible. Thus, there is no justice that He intends to enact and no injustice that He intends to avoid.

For this reason, they hold that He may do anything, even if it is evil. They say, “Evil is whatever is forbidden, and there is none to forbid Him.” They also hold that He may command anything, even wrongdoing and associating partners with Him, and forbid anything, even worshipping Him alone and doing good. Thus, they recognize no criterion governing His actions.

For this reason, their opponents confronted them with the implication that He could cause miracles to appear at the hands of a liar, and they had no sound answer. They could offer neither a distinction between miracles and other phenomena nor a means of knowing the truthfulness of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, without contradicting their own principle. Allah Most High has said:

“Allah bears witness that there is no deity except Him, as do the angels and those endowed with knowledge; He upholds justice.”

Yet, according to their position, this conveys nothing, for among possible acts there is no distinction between justice and injustice such that He would uphold the one rather than the other. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

The same applies to “wisdom”: according to them, He does not act for a wise purpose. They have interpreted “wisdom” as either knowledge, power, or will. Yet it is well known that one who possesses power may be

---

## Page 6767

wise or unwise. Likewise, a person’s will may be wise or foolish, and knowledge corresponds to what is known, whether it is wise or foolish. Thus, their position does not, in reality, affirm that Allah is wise. The same applies to “mercy”: for them, it amounts to nothing more than a will that favors one of two equal alternatives without any determining factor, bearing equally upon benefiting His servants and harming them. Thus, their position does not, in reality, affirm either mercy or love.

This has been discussed at length elsewhere, where their inconsistency concerning the divine attributes and actions has been explained: they affirm will while denying love, pleasure, and wisdom. Their inconsistency, and that of everyone who affirms some attributes while denying others, has also been explained, as has the still greater inconsistency of the philosophers who deny will.

Al-Rāzī discussed “the question of will” in al-Matālib al-ʿĀliyah (The Lofty Aims) and favored denying it, because he could not answer the philosophers’ argument on the principles of his Jahmī and Muʿtazilī associates, so he took refuge in the philosophers’ position. He does likewise on other questions: sometimes he favors the philosophers’ view, sometimes that of the speculative theologians, and sometimes he is bewildered and suspends judgment. At the end of his life, he acknowledged that neither group’s approach heals the sick or quenches thirst.

He said:

“I have reflected upon the methods of speculative theology and the approaches of philosophy, but I have not found them to heal the sick or quench thirst. I have found the most direct path to be the way of the Quran. Concerning affirmation, read: ‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne’; ‘To Him ascend words

---

## Page 6768

that are good’; and concerning negation, read:

‘There is nothing like Him.’

‘They do not encompass Him in knowledge.’

Whoever has experienced what I have experienced will know what I have come to know.”

It has thus become clear that they do not affirm the Lord’s justice, wisdom, or mercy. The same applies to truthfulness: when they sought to establish proof that Allah is truthful, they were unable to do so. They therefore said:

“Truthfulness in internal speech is necessary, because He knows all things, and it is impossible for someone who knows to have within himself an assertion contrary to his knowledge.” Al-Ghazālī and others relied on this argument.

They were answered: This is a weak argument for two reasons.

First: Truthfulness in that meaning is of no avail unless truthfulness is established in the expressions that signify it; and, in their view, it distinguishes between actions.

Second: They affirmed the existence of an internal assertion on the grounds that a person may tell you a lie, so that a meaning subsists within him that is not knowledge: namely, the meaning of the assertion. This entails that they hold that someone who knows may have within himself an assertion contrary to his knowledge.

When al-Rāzī discussed the question of Allah’s being unable to utter speech without meaning anything by it, in opposition to the Hashwiyyah, he was asked: “Has any group within the Muslim community said that Allah means nothing by His speech? The dispute is only whether He speaks words whose meaning His servants do not understand.” It was also said

---

## Page 6769

to him: “Even supposing this is disputed, he has offered no proof that it is impossible. He has merely said, ‘This is a fault or deficiency, and Allah is free of it.’”

He was then told: “He must mean either the meaning subsisting in the divine essence or the created expressions. The first cannot be intended here, because the question concerns someone who utters an ordered sequence of letters without meaning anything by it, whereas what subsists in the essence is the meaning itself. If you mean the letters—which is what he intends—then, according to you, they are created. You hold that He may create anything and that no act is beneath Him. A fault, in your view, is what you do not will; so this is impossible.”

It thus becomes clear that they have no argument establishing either His truthfulness or His freedom from fault in His speech. Such an argument can only be made by someone who holds that certain acts are unbefitting Him. This also makes clear that they do not affirm His justice, wisdom, mercy, or truthfulness.

The Muʿtazilah, however, seek to affirm these matters. Hence they mention them in their introductory statements concerning the attributes, as Abū al-Husayn al-Basrī and others do. Thus, at the beginning of Suwar al-adillah, there is an introductory statement to the effect that Allah is One and just:

“He does not wrong people in the least, but it is people who wrong themselves.”

And:

“Indeed, Allah is Most Kind and Merciful to people.”

I believe it also affirms His truthfulness. Hence they declare anyone who attributes injustice, falsehood, or folly to Him, or likens Him to others, to be a disbeliever. Nevertheless, they have erred on many points, as has been pointed out elsewhere on several occasions. Each of the two groups therefore possesses both truth and falsehood. The truth in its entirety has been embraced only by those who followed the Emigrants and the Helpers and believed in everything the Messenger brought in accordance with

---

## Page 6770

its proper meaning, without believing in some of it while disbelieving in the rest. These are the recipients of mercy, who do not differ, unlike those who are divided. Allah Most High says:

“They will continue to differ,”

“except those on whom your Lord has mercy; and for that He created them.”

Section

The Jahmiyyah and the Muʿtazilah share in denying the divine attributes. Ibn Kullāb and his followers—such as al-Ashʿarī, Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Qalānisī, and their followers—affirmed the attributes. However, they did not affirm the attributes connected to His will: that He speaks when He wills; that His acts performed by choice subsist in His essence; that He loves the believers and is pleased with them after they believe, and is angry with the disbelievers and hates them after they disbelieve; and that He sees His servants’ deeds after they perform them. As Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘Act, for Allah will see your deeds, as will His Messenger and the believers.’”

He thus affirms a future seeing. Likewise, He Most High says:

“Then We made you successors in the land after them, to see how you would act.”

Another example is His calling to Moses when Moses came, with a call subsisting in His essence, whereas He had not called to him before that.

The Muʿtazilah and the Jahmiyyah say: “He created a call in the air.”

The Kullābiyyah and the Sālimiyyah say: “The call subsists in His essence and is beginningless, but Moses heard it.” Thus, they regard Moses’ hearing as newly occurring; otherwise, in their view, He has always been calling.

---

## Page 6771

Yet the Quran, the hadiths, and the statements of the early generations and the leading scholars all contradict both these views. It is clear that He called to Moses when Moses came, and that He speaks by His will, uttering particular speech at a particular time, as He says:

“We created you, then gave you form, then said to the angels, ‘Prostrate yourselves before Adam.’”

And He Most High says:

“Indeed, the likeness of Jesus in Allah’s sight is that of Adam: He created him from dust, then said to him, ‘Be,’ and he was.”

The Quran contains hundreds of verses indicating this principle, while the hadiths are too numerous to count. This is the position of the leading scholars of the Sunnah, the early generations, and the majority of rational people. Hence, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and others said: “He has always spoken whenever He wills and however He wills.” This is the position of the generality of Ahl al-Sunnah.

For this reason, they agreed that the Quran is the speech of Allah, sent down and uncreated. We know of no one among the early generations who said: “It is qadīm (beginningless) and has always existed.” Many of the later scholars who said, “It is qadīm,” did not understand what was meant. Some say: “It is qadīm in His knowledge.” Others say: “Qadīm means that it existed earlier, before the very time when the Prophet was sent—not that it is beginningless and has always existed.” Still others say: “Rather, what we mean by qadīm is that it is uncreated.” This matter has been discussed at length elsewhere.

“The point here” is that, according to this principle, when He created the creatures, He saw them and heard the voices of His servants, and this was by His will and power. For His creation of them was by His will and power, and thereby they became visible and their speech became audible. It is also stated in

---

## Page 6772

the Quran and Sunnah in numerous places that He singles out certain creatures for His looking and listening, as in the saying:

“There are three to whom Allah will not speak, at whom He will not look on the Day of Resurrection, and whom He will not purify, and they will have a painful punishment: a lying king, an elderly adulterer, and an arrogant pauper.”

The same applies to “listening.” He Most High says:

“And has listened to its Lord, as it must.”

That is, it listened. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah does not listen to anything as He listens to a prophet with a beautiful voice reciting the Quran melodiously and aloud.”

He also said:

“Allah listens more intently to a reciter of the Quran than the owner of a singing girl listens to her.”

This singles out certain voices, rather than others, for His listening. Likewise, (hearing) in the sense of responding—as in the saying, “Allah hears whoever praises Him,” the words of His close friend Abraham, “Indeed, You hear supplication,” and His words, “Indeed, He is All-Hearing, Near”—implies that this hearing is accorded to some in particular.

This singling out is thus established in the Book and Sunnah. It occurs through a quality subsisting in His essence by His will and power, as explained above. According to those who deny the attributes, however, it occurs through something created and separate from Him, not through a quality subsisting in His essence. His singling out those He loves for the looking and listening mentioned above entails that this particular kind is absent with respect to others.

Yet, despite this, should it be said that seeing and hearing themselves, understood simply as perception, are inseparable from His essence, so that nothing audible or visible can exist unless

---

## Page 6773

His perception is directed towards it, just as His knowledge is? Or should it be said that this too is subject to His will and power, so that He can refrain from looking at some created beings?

There are two views on this. The first is held by those who do not regard this as subject to His will and power. As for those who do regard it as subject to His will and power, they may nevertheless say that whenever something visible or audible exists, His perception must encompass it.

The second view is that hearing and seeing as such are subject to His will and power, so that He can refrain from looking at any created thing. This is what has been transmitted from a group of the early generations, as Ibn Abī Hātim narrated from Abū ʿImrān al-Jawnī, who said: “Allah has never looked upon anything in His creation without showing it mercy, but He has decreed not to look upon them.”

It may be said that this is like remembering and forgetting. Allah Most High says:

“Remember Me; I will remember you.”

In the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is reported to have said:

“Allah Most High says: ‘I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him. If he remembers Me within himself, I remember him within Myself. If he mentions Me in an assembly, I mention him in an assembly better than theirs. If he draws near to Me by a handspan, I draw near to him by a cubit. If he draws near to Me by a cubit, I draw near to him by an armspan. If he comes to Me walking, I come to him at a run.’”
