This remembrance is reserved for those who remember Him; those who do not remember Him do not receive this remembrance. Whoever believes in Him and obeys Him, He remembers with His mercy. Whoever turns away from the Reminder He has sent down, He turns away from him, as He says:

“But whoever turns away from My remembrance will have a life of hardship, and We will raise him up blind on the Day of Resurrection.”

“He will say, ‘My Lord, why have You raised me up blind when I used to see?’”

“He will say:

---

## Page 6774

‘Just as Our signs came to you and you forgot them, so today you will be forgotten.’” Similarly, He says:

“The hypocrites, men and women, are all alike: they enjoin what is wrong, forbid what is right, and are tight-fisted. They have forgotten Allah, so He has forgotten them.”

They have explained this forgetting as … (1) This forgetting is the opposite of the remembrance mentioned above. In the Sahīh, in the hadith concerning the disbeliever whom Allah calls to account, He says:

“‘Did you think that you would meet Me?’ He replies, ‘No.’ He says, ‘Then today I will forget you just as you forgot Me.’”

This implies that He does not remember him as He remembers those who obey Him. This too is subject to His will and power. He, glorified be He, created this servant and knew what he would do before he did it. When he acted, He knew what he had done and saw his deeds. Thus, this forgetting does not contradict what He, glorified be He, knew of this servant’s condition.

Section

The fundamental principle of “distinguishing” between truth and falsehood, guidance and misguidance, right direction and error, the path of happiness and salvation and the path of misery and destruction, is to regard what Allah sent His messengers with and revealed in His Books as the truth that must be followed, and through which

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 111):
> Perhaps the blank should read [not remembering him]. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 6775

discernment, guidance, knowledge, and faith are attained. One thus affirms that it is true and truthful, and measures all other human speech against it. Whatever agrees with it is true, and whatever contradicts it is false. If one does not know whether a statement agrees with it or contradicts it—because the statement is ambiguous and its speaker’s intent is unknown, or because the intent is known but it is unclear whether the Messenger brought anything confirming or refuting it—one withholds judgment and speaks only with knowledge.

Knowledge is what is established by evidence, and beneficial knowledge is what the Messenger brought. Knowledge may also be acquired from sources other than the Messenger, but in “worldly” matters such as medicine, arithmetic, agriculture, and trade. As for “divine matters and religious knowledge,” knowledge of these is derived from the Messenger. Of all creation, the Messenger knows these matters best, is most eager to teach them to people, and is most capable of explaining and making them known. He therefore surpasses everyone else in knowledge, ability, and will; through these three, the intended purpose is fully achieved.

Anyone other than the Messenger either has deficient or unsound knowledge of these matters; or lacks the will to convey what he knows of them and therefore does not explain it, whether out of desire, fear, or some other motive; or gives an inadequate explanation that fails to express what his heart knows.

The Messenger explains these matters in two ways:

---

## Page 6776

At times, he explains the “rational proofs” that establish these matters. The Quran is full of rational proofs and conclusive demonstrations concerning knowledge of divine matters and religious questions. At other times, he simply reports these matters, on the strength of the clear signs and conclusive proofs he has established that he is Allah’s Messenger, conveying His message; that he speaks nothing but the truth about Him; and that Allah has testified to this, making known to His servants and informing them that he is truthful and confirmed as such in what he conveys from Him.

The proofs by which we know that he is Allah’s Messenger are numerous and varied. They are rational proofs whose validity is known through reason. They are also scriptural, transmitted proofs, but the Messenger has explained them, pointed them out, and guided people to them. All schools of rational inquiry agree that the Quran contains rational proofs concerning religious questions, and they mention this in their works on religious foundations and Quranic commentary. Most rational thinkers also appeal to transmitted proofs consisting solely of revealed reports in religious questions, for once the Messenger’s truthfulness is established, he must be believed in whatever he reports.

“Knowledge falls into three categories.” Some things can be known only through rational proofs, and the best rational proofs are those explained by the Quran and pointed out by the Messenger. It should therefore be understood that the loftiest, most complete, and finest rational proofs are derived from the Messenger. Some people overlook this. Among them are those who disparage rational proofs altogether because they have come to think that these are the innovated speculative theology introduced by certain theologians. Others turn away from reflecting on the Quran and seeking conclusive proofs

---

## Page 6777

based on reason within it, because they have come to think that the Quran provides evidence only through reports. They therefore consider it necessary first to establish prophethood and the truthfulness of the reports through reason, before relying on the reports of someone whose truthfulness has been rationally established.

Other matters cannot be known by anyone other than the prophets except through their reports. Their reports alone constitute transmitted evidence. Examples include the details they have conveyed concerning divine matters, the angels, the Throne, Paradise and Hell, and the details of what is commanded and prohibited.

As for establishing the existence of the Creator, His oneness, knowledge, power, will, wisdom, mercy and similar matters, these are not known through rational proofs, even though the proofs and signs brought by the prophets are the most complete rational proofs. Yet knowledge of these matters is not confined to reports alone, although the prophets’ reports alone also yield certain knowledge. Thus, these matters are known through the rational proofs to which they directed people, and through their reports alone, once their truthfulness has been established by the proofs, signs and demonstrations attesting to it.

People have differed concerning “knowledge of the resurrection and of the moral goodness and badness of actions.” Most people maintain that these can be known through reason as well as revelation. Those who hold that moral goodness and badness can be known through reason are more numerous than those who hold that the resurrection can be known through reason. Abū al-Khattāb said: “This is the position of most jurists and theologians.” Others maintain that the resurrection and moral goodness and badness can be known only through reports alone. This is the position of al-Ash‘arī

---

## Page 6778

and his followers, as well as those among the adherents of the imams who agreed with them, such as al-Qādī Abū Ya‘lā, Abū al-Ma‘ālī al-Juwaynī, Abū al-Walīd al-Bājī and others. They all agree that some matters can be known through both reason and revelation, understood here as reports alone—for example, whether people’s actions are created by Allah or not, whether seeing Him is possible or impossible, and similar matters.

The books on the foundations of religion written by all the various groups are filled with arguments based on revealed reports. Al-Rāzī, however, challenged this in al-Matālib al-‘Āliya, saying: “Reliance on revelation as evidence is conditional upon its not being opposed by a conclusive rational proof. If a rational proof opposes it, that proof must take precedence.” He said: “Knowing that no opposing rational proof exists is impossible.” He accepts as established through revelation only those matters which are necessarily known to have been reported by the Messenger, such as the resurrection.

It might be supposed that this was the approach of the leading authorities of his school who suspended judgment concerning the threats of punishment, such as al-Ash‘arī, al-Qādī Abū Bakr and others. This is not so. They suspended judgment only concerning reports containing threats of punishment, because, in their view, general wording does not yield certainty, or because they do not recognize linguistic forms that inherently denote generality, and because they considered the evidence to be conflicting. Otherwise, they affirm Allah’s attributes known through transmitted reports, such as the Face and the Hand, on the basis of revelation and reports alone. Al-Ash‘arī’s position on this did not vary, and it is also the position of the leading authorities among his followers.

Abū al-Ma‘ālī and his followers, however, do not affirm the attributes known through transmitted reports. Rather, some of them deny these attributes, while others suspend judgment concerning them, such as al-Rāzī and al-Āmidī. It may therefore be said: al-Ash‘arī’s position can be inferred from what these scholars say

---

## Page 6779

by saying: they are not known to have relied on any revealed proof in matters of fundamental doctrine. Yet it may be replied that they argue for the resurrection on the basis of the Quran and hadith. It was al-Rāzī, however, who approached it through the necessary knowledge that the Messenger had taught it.

In fact, all conclusive proofs yield necessary knowledge, and revealed evidence consisting of transmitted reports yields necessary knowledge of what the Messenger reported. Some matters, however, have numerous supporting proofs—as with information conveyed by mass-transmitted reports—and yield necessary knowledge without any particular proof being identified. One may also identify the proofs and reason from them. A fuller discussion of this belongs elsewhere.

The point here is that knowledge concerning Allah and religion, whether transmitted or rational, should be taken from the Messenger, and that what he brought should be made the foundation. This is because conclusive, demonstrative proofs establish that everything he said is true, both in general and in detail. The proofs of prophethood generally establish this in broad terms, while the particular rational proofs found in the Quran and hadith establish it in detail.

Furthermore, the prophets and messengers were sent precisely to make this known. They therefore know it better than anyone else, are best qualified to uphold it, and have the strongest claim to being correct concerning it. Moreover, anyone who has put their statements and those of others to the test has found the truth to be on their side

---

## Page 6780

and error on the side of their opponents, as al-Rāzī himself acknowledges. Although he was among the fiercest critics of revealed evidence—even introducing the claim, which had no other known prominent proponent, that it does not yield certainty—he nevertheless says:

“I have reflected on the methods of speculative theology and the approaches of philosophy, but have not found them to heal the ailing or quench the thirsty. I have found the most direct path to be that of the Quran. Concerning affirmation, I read:

‘To Him ascends good speech.’

‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.’

And concerning negation, I read:

‘There is nothing like Him.’

‘They do not encompass Him in knowledge.’”

He said: “Anyone who has experienced what I have experienced will know what I know.”

Moreover, anyone who examines what is found among the groups that have not held fast to the prophets’ teaching, guidance, and reports will find them all bewildered, astray, beset by doubt and misgivings, or ignorant and unaware of their own ignorance. They fall within one or the other of the two likenesses in the Quran:

“As for those who disbelieve, their deeds are like a mirage on a level plain, which a thirsty man mistakes for water, until, when he reaches it, he finds nothing there, but finds Allah there, who settles his account in full. And Allah is swift in reckoning.”

“Or like layers of darkness in a deep sea, covered by a wave, above which is another wave, above which are clouds—layers of darkness, one above another. When one holds out his hand, he can scarcely see it. Whoever Allah does not grant light has no light.”

---

## Page 6781

Section

The misguided who have divided their religion and become sects are, as Mujāhid said, “the people of religious innovations and specious arguments.” They cling to what constitutes innovation in religion and is ambiguous to reason. As Imam Ahmad said of them:

“They differ over the Book, oppose the Book, and agree on opposing the Book. They argue from ambiguous statements and lead people astray through the confusion they create for them.”

The divided groups among the misguided establish for themselves a religion and religious foundations devised from their own opinions. They then measure the Quran and hadith against these. If the texts agree with their doctrines, they cite them as corroboration, not as their basis. If the texts contradict them, they sometimes distort words from their proper meanings and interpret the texts contrary to their true meaning—this is what their leaders do. At other times, they turn away from the texts and say, “We entrust their meaning to Allah”—this is what their ordinary followers do.

Inwardly, both groups rely on something other than what the Messenger brought. They treat their innovated doctrines as definitive, requiring that they be followed and their implications believed. Anyone who disagrees is, in their view, either an unbeliever or an ignorant person who does not understand this field and has no knowledge of rational argument or foundational principles

---

## Page 6782

and they classify the words of Allah and His Messenger that contradict their doctrines as ambiguous passages whose meaning is known only to Allah, or only to those firmly grounded in knowledge. In their view, those firmly grounded in knowledge are those who agree with their position. These people are more astray than those who cling to verses of the Book that they find ambiguous and abandon its clear passages, such as the Christians, the Khawārij, and others. For these latter took ambiguous passages from Allah’s words and treated them as definitive, while treating the clear passages as ambiguous.

As for the former—such as the Jahmiyyah who deny Allah’s attributes, those among the Muʿtazilah and others who agree with them, and the philosophers—they treat what they themselves have devised through their own opinions as the definitive foundation that must be followed, even if they have nothing from the prophets, the Book, or the Sunnah to support it. They classify what the prophets brought as ambiguous, even when it is explicit and its meaning may be self-evident.

For this reason, these people oppose the prophets more profoundly than all other adherents of religious innovation. Indeed, Yūsuf ibn Asbāt, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, and others, including a group of Ahmad’s followers, said:

“The Jahmiyyah who deny Allah’s attributes fall outside the seventy-two sects.”

They said: “The four principal sects are the Shīʿah, the Khawārij, the Murjiʾah, and the Qadariyyah.”

We have mentioned elsewhere that, concerning the ambiguous verses in Allah Most High’s words,

“In it are clear and definitive verses, which are the foundation of the Book, while others are ambiguous,”

there are two views:

---

## Page 6783

The first view is that these are particular verses that are ambiguous to everyone. The second—which is the correct view—is that ambiguity is relative: what is ambiguous to one person may not be ambiguous to another. There are, however, clear and definitive verses that are ambiguous to no one. Once the meaning of the ambiguous verses is understood, they cease to be ambiguous. Indeed, the entire discourse is perfected, as He says:

“Its verses have been perfected and then set out in detail.”

This parallels the saying:

“The lawful is clear and the unlawful is clear, and between them are doubtful matters which many people do not know.”

Likewise, they said:

“The cows all seem alike to us.”

Ahmad wrote a book, *Refutation of the Heretics and the Jahmiyyah*, concerning the ambiguous passages of the Quran about which they entertained doubts and which they interpreted incorrectly. He explained all those verses and condemned them for misinterpreting those ambiguous passages. Most of these are well-known verses whose interpretation scholars have discussed, such as the verses about which Nāfiʿ ibn al-Azraq questioned Ibn ʿAbbās.

Al-Hasan al-Basrī said: “Allah has not revealed a single verse without wishing that it be known what it was revealed about and what was meant by it.”

Those among the early generations who said that only Allah knows the interpretation of the ambiguous passages were also correct. By “interpretation,” they meant matters whose knowledge Allah has reserved for Himself, such as the time of the Hour and the appearance of its signs, the manner of His own Being, and what He has prepared in Paradise for His allies.

---

## Page 6784

Among the circumstances that occasioned the revelation of this verse was the Christians’ appeal to expressions they found ambiguous, such as His words “Indeed, We” and “We.” Scholars know that these refer to a single exalted One who has helpers; He did not mean that there are three gods. Those firmly grounded in knowledge know the interpretation of this—that is, its explanation. They distinguish between passages in which He says “Me” and those in which He says “Indeed, We”: the latter include the angels in the tasks on which He sends them, since they are His messengers. But being the God who is worshipped belongs to Him alone. Hence He does not say, “So worship Us alone,” or “Fear Us alone.” Rather, whenever He commands worship, piety, reverent fear, or reliance, He mentions Himself alone, using His distinctive name. When He mentions actions for which He sends the angels, He says:

“Indeed, We have granted you a clear victory.”

“So when We have recited it, follow its recitation.”

“We recount to you in truth some of the story of Moses and Pharaoh.”

There are other examples of this. Yet its interpretation—in the sense of the actual reality of the angels referred to, their attributes, and how the Lord sends them—is known only to Allah, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

The point here is that one must take what Allah and His Messenger have said as the foundation, reflect upon and understand its meaning, and recognize its proof and evidence, whether rational or transmitted through revelation. One must also recognize how the Quran provides both kinds of evidence. People’s statements, which may agree or disagree with it, should be treated as ambiguous and insufficiently explicit. Those who use such expressions should therefore be told: “These may mean such-and-such, or they may mean such-and-such.” If by them they mean what

---

## Page 6785

agrees with what the Messenger has reported, it is accepted; if they mean something contrary to it, it is rejected. Examples include the terms “composite,” “body,” “that which occupies space,” “substance,” “direction,” “accident,” and similar expressions, as well as “space” and the like. These expressions do not occur in the Quran and Sunnah with the meanings intended by those who employ this terminology. Nor do they carry these meanings in ordinary Arabic usage. Rather, these people alone use them to express meanings that others have not expressed in these terms. They then explain these meanings in other words and reject what the Quran establishes through rational and revealed proofs.

When clarification is sought and distinctions are drawn, truth becomes distinct from falsehood, and it becomes clear how their arguments should be addressed. Their arguments are pieced together from premises involving equivocal terms: they take an equivocal expression in one sense in one premise and in another sense in the other premise. In its verbal form, this appears to be a valid argument, but in meaning it is not.

It is like someone saying: “Suhayl (Canopus) is far from al-Thurayyā (the Pleiades); he cannot be joined to her or marry her.” Yet the poet who said:

“O you who give al-Thurayyā in marriage to Suhayl”

meant a woman named al-Thurayyā and a man named Suhayl. He then said:

“For Allah’s sake, how can the two ever meet?”

---

## Page 6786

“She is Syrian when she rises … and Suhayl, when he rises, is Yemeni.”

These are equivocal names: his wonder and objection arise from the apparent sense of the ambiguous wording. Their detailed arguments have been discussed at length elsewhere.

Those who deny the divine attributes based their position and their negations on the following argument: the world came into being because bodies came into being. They argue that bodies came into being because they are never free of newly occurring events and did not precede them; whatever is never free of such events and did not precede them must itself have come into being. Their position ultimately led them to that of Pharaoh, who denied the Creator and accused His Messenger Moses of lying when he said that Allah had spoken to him. This is the foundation of the doctrine of the Jahmiyyah, whom the early generations and the leading scholars unanimously condemned. It is also the foundation of the doctrine of the speculative theologians whom they unanimously condemned. Numerous works have been compiled recording the statements of the early generations and the leading scholars condemning the Jahmiyyah and these speculative theologians.

The early generations did not condemn speech as such, for every human being speaks. Nor did they condemn reasoning, reflection, or the disputation that Allah commanded His Messenger to engage in, nor reasoning based on what Allah and His Messenger have explained. Indeed, they did not condemn any discourse that was true. Rather, they condemned false discourse: that which contradicts the Quran and Sunnah and also contradicts reason. Thus, the discourse condemned by the early generations was false discourse, which conflicts with both revelation and reason.

---

## Page 6787

Yet many people fail to see the falsity of this reasoning. Some believe it accords with revelation and reason, even believing that Abraham, Allah’s intimate friend, used it in his argument. Some of these people make it the foundation of religion, holding that faith cannot be attained—or cannot be complete—without it. But anyone familiar with what the Messenger brought and the way of the Companions knows beyond doubt that neither the Messenger nor the Companions followed this approach. Those who recognize this therefore know that it is an innovation. Many of them, however, do not recognize that it is unsound. They still consider it rationally valid, but believe that it is lengthy, makes knowledge more remote, or is a frightening and dangerous path whose followers are at risk. They thus criticize it as one might criticize a long or frightening road, while believing that it leads to knowledge and is sound in itself.

Those with expertise who thoroughly understand it, however, know that it is invalid according to both reason and revelation, and that it is not a path leading to knowledge. Rather, it leads those who believe it sound only to ignorance and error, while leading those who recognize its contradictions to perplexity and doubt. This is why even its most accomplished adherents end in perplexity and doubt: its true implication is that everything that exists has come into being, having been preceded by nonexistence, and that nothing eternal exists.

This is an obstinate denial of the obvious. For existence is plainly witnessed, and what exists has either come into being or is eternal. Whatever has come into being necessarily requires something eternal. Thus, the existence of something eternal is established in either case.

---

## Page 6788

Likewise, the approach introduced into this method by Ibn Sīnā and his followers—arguing from the contingent to the necessary—is even more unsound, as has been explained at length elsewhere. Its real implication is that everything that exists is contingent and that nothing exists independently, even though they have made this a means of establishing a being necessary in itself, just as the others make their method a means of establishing an eternal being. Both arguments contradict the existence of the eternal and the necessary. Neither establishes an eternal being or one necessary in itself, even though the existence of an eternal being necessary in itself is known with self-evident certainty.

This is why the most accomplished among them came to hold that the necessary, eternal being is the world itself, saying, “It is Allah.” They denied that the world has a Lord distinct from it, since the existence of an eternal being necessary in itself must be acknowledged on any account. Pharaoh and others like him who denied the Maker did not deny this observable existence.

The substance of those people’s position thus entails that no eternal or necessary being exists. Yet they do not recognize that this follows from their position; rather, they think they have established proof of an eternal being necessary in itself. But they describe Him in terms that make His existence impossible, saying, “He is neither within the world nor outside it; He is neither an attribute nor possessed of attributes; nor can He be pointed to,” along with other negative attributes that entail His nonexistence. Such descriptions repel reason and people’s innate dispositions, and are recognized as descriptions of what does not and cannot exist, not of what exists. Thus their argument, in reality,

---

## Page 6789

entails that there is no eternal or necessary being. Yet they thought they had established an eternal, necessary being, when what they had established was impossible. Thus they had established neither an eternal nor a necessary being.

Then others among their Jahmī adherents saw this as wilful denial of the obvious. Since an eternal, necessary being must be affirmed, they said, “It is this world.” The early Jahmiyyah used to say, “He Himself is in every place,” whereas these others said, “He is identical with existing things, and the eternal, necessary being is none other than the originated, contingent being.” Indwelling was what the Jahmiyyah openly professed to people, so the early generations and the imams came to know it and refuted it. The reality of their position, however, is negation: that He is neither within the world nor outside it. But the imams had not heard this and knew it to be their position only through their concealed teachings.

For this reason, the imams reported that the Jahmiyyah held Him to be in every place, and also reported that they described Him through negative attributes. It became widely known that the Jahmiyyah described Him through negations, to the point that Abū Tammām said:

Jahmī in its attributes, yet adorned
With the beauties of all things.

They did not intend to deny the eternal, necessary being, for no rational person, whether Muslim or unbeliever, intends this. It contradicts what everyone knows through self-evident reason. For if all existing things are supposed to have come into being from nonexistence, it follows that all existing things came into being by themselves. Yet it is self-evidently known to reason that whatever comes into being

---

## Page 6790

does not come into being by itself. For this reason, the Most High says:

“Or were they created by nothing, or are they the creators?”

The words “Or were they created by nothing” have been interpreted as meaning: without a Lord who created them. Other interpretations are: without any material, or without any ultimate outcome or recompense. The first meaning is certainly intended, for anything created from some material or for some purpose must have a creator. Innate human nature recognizes more clearly that whatever is brought into being must have someone who brings it into being than that everything brought into being must have material from which it was created and a purpose for which it was created. Many rational people have disputed both of the latter claims, whereas no group has disputed the first by saying that this world came into being without anyone bringing it into being.

Rather, some groups have said that it is eternal in itself and necessary in itself, with no maker. But the claim that it originated and came into being by itself, without a maker, is not known to have been held by any recognized group; it is only attributed to unidentified people. Such a claim, and others like it, are made by people whose reason has become impaired, leading them into sophistry. Sophistry may affect particular individuals and concern certain matters. But an entire people being sophists in every matter is inconceivable. Thus, no people are known to have maintained that the world came into being without anyone bringing it into being.

When these people came to believe that everything possessing attributes, or anything in which there subsists an attribute or

---

## Page 6791

an act performed at its will, is brought into being and contingent, they were compelled to maintain that everything that exists has a beginning. For the Creator—exalted be His majesty—possesses attributes and voluntary acts that subsist in Him: He speaks by His will and power, and creates whatever He creates by His will and power. Yet these people believed that He was devoid of these attributes, because they considered the following proposition sound: whatever has attributes and temporal events subsisting in it must itself have a beginning, since it is never free of temporal events, and whatever is never free of temporal events must itself have a beginning. If it has a beginning, it must have a beginningless originator. They believed that they had thereby established the Lord’s existence, and that He is an essence stripped of attributes whose existence is unqualified: He can neither be pointed to nor identified as a particular being.

They say, “He can neither be pointed to nor identified as a particular being.” Yet what they have affirmed has no reality outside the mind; it exists only in thought. Thus, what they affirmed and believed to be the Maker of the world exists only in minds, not in concrete reality. Their position therefore amounted to denying the Maker.

Then others who shared the fundamental premise of their doctrine came along and said, “This unqualified existence, stripped of attributes, is the existence that pervades all existing things.” They thus maintained that He indwells everything.

Others among them said, “He is the existence of everything.” Some distinguished between existence and subsistence; others distinguished between individuation and being unqualified. Some held that His relation to the world is like that of matter to form, while others held that He is in the world like butter in

---

## Page 6792

milk, and like olive oil and sesame oil in sesame seeds and olives. These views have been discussed at length elsewhere.

“The point here” is that the fundamental premise that led them astray was their assertion that anything in which attributes, acts, voluntary activities, or temporal events subsist must have a beginning. They then said that a body is never free of temporal events, and sought to establish this through various arguments. Some said that it is never free of the four states: motion, rest, conjunction, and separation. Others mentioned only motion and rest. Still others said that it is never free of accidents, that all accidents have a beginning, and that they do not persist for two successive moments. This is al-Āmidī’s approach, which he claimed most adherents of the Ash'arī school relied upon. Al-Rāzī relied on the argument from motion and rest.

These arguments, together with everything they adduced to establish that bodies have a beginning and are contingent, have been discussed at length. In that discussion, we cited their own statements demonstrating the invalidity of all these arguments. They themselves exposed the invalidity of everything adduced to establish that bodies have a beginning and are contingent, refuting these arguments one by one through their own discussions, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

As for the Hishāmiyyah, the Karrāmiyyah, and others who maintain that He is a beginningless body, they shared the fundamental premise of this doctrine. However, they did not maintain that every body has a beginning, nor

---

## Page 6793

did they maintain that a body is never free of temporal events, since, in their view, the Beginningless One was a beginningless body free of temporal events. It has been said that the first person in Islam to assert that the Beginningless One is a body was Hishām ibn al-Hakam, just as the first to openly deny in Islam that He is a body was al-Jahm ibn Safwān.

The statements of the early generations and the imams condemning the Jahmiyyah are numerous and well known, for the malady of denying the divine attributes is worse than that of corporealism. The early generations specifically condemned those who likened Allah to creation. Thus, Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal—may Allah be pleased with him—Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, and others said: “Those who liken Allah to creation are those who say: ‘Sight like my sight, a hand like my hand, and a foot like my foot.’”

Ibn Kullāb and his followers affirmed the attributes that are not connected with His will and power, but denied those that are connected with His will and power. They argued: “These attributes have a beginning. If temporal events subsisted in Him, He would have a beginning, because whatever can receive something cannot be devoid of both it and its opposite. Thus, if He could receive any of these temporal events, He could not be devoid of both it and its opposite. He would therefore never be free of temporal events and would consequently have a beginning.”

“Muhammad ibn Karrām” lived after Ibn Kullāb, in the time of Muslim ibn al-Hajjāj. He affirmed that Allah possesses attributes dependent on His choice and speaks by His will and power. In his view, however, it was impossible that He should have been speaking by His will and power from eternity, because a beginningless series of temporal events was impossible. He therefore did not adopt the position of the early generations that He has always spoken whenever He wills. Rather, he said that He began to speak by His will and power, just as He began to act by His will and power, after not having done so.

According to the well-known account of his position, he and his followers said:

---

## Page 6794

He cannot become free of the temporal events that subsist in Him, nor cease to possess them. For if temporal events subsisted in Him and then ceased, He would be capable of their coming into being and ceasing to be. If He were capable of this, He could never be free of it; and whatever is never free of temporal events has a beginning. What their premises allow is merely that temporal events may subsist in Him, just as He may perform and originate them. This does not entail that they have always accompanied Him, just as it does not entail that He has always been bringing them about. In their view, coming into being is distinct from bringing into being. Accordingly, they regard the Quran as having come into being, but not as having been brought into being: what is brought into being requires an act of origination, whereas coming into being does not.

When they say, “He was free of them in pre-eternity and was at rest,” they do not mean that a temporal event subsisted in Him. Rather, they say that rest is a privative state, as the philosophers also maintain, whereas motion is a positively existing state. This contrasts with the position of those among the Mu'tazilah and the Ash'ariyyah who maintain that rest, like motion, is a positively existing state.

Thus, once a temporal event occurs in Him, that event cannot cease to exist, for a temporal event can cease to exist only through an act of origination subsisting in Him, and this is impossible. They maintain that it is impossible for a body to cease to exist. In their view, the acts of bringing creatures into being and annihilating them subsist in the Creator. Thus, the temporal events that subsist in them subsist in Him. Were He to annihilate them, both origination and annihilation would subsist in Him. He would therefore be capable of having a temporal event arise within Him and of that event ceasing to exist. Whatever is such can never be free of origination and annihilation, and thus can never be free of temporal events; whatever is never free of them has a beginning. This is so because whatever can receive something cannot be devoid of both it and its opposite, as the Kullābiyyah maintained.

The Mu'tazilah, however, say:

---

## Page 6795

“Rest is the opposite of motion, so whatever can receive either cannot be devoid of both.”

These others say:

“Rest is not a positively existing opposite; rather, it is a privative state. What positively exists is origination and annihilation. Were He capable of having origination and annihilation subsist in Him, He would be capable of having positively existing opposites subsist in Him; and whatever can receive something cannot be devoid of both it and its opposite.”

When their opponents sought to refute their position, their principal argument was to demonstrate the inconsistencies in their statements, as Abū al-Ma'ālī and his followers did. Al-Āmidī likewise identified numerous inconsistencies in their position, which have been discussed elsewhere. At most, the arguments of these two demonstrate their inconsistency, not the soundness of their opponents’ position.

There is also a large group who say that temporal events subsist in Him and cease to exist, and that He spoke to Moses with a voice which subsequently ceased to exist. This is the position of the leading scholars of the Sunnah and hadith, among the early generations and others. I believe the Karrāmiyyah hold two views on this. Otherwise, the claim that the voice with which He spoke to Moses ceases to exist is of the same kind as the claim that it is beginningless. The latter is maintained by some theologians, hadith scholars, and jurists among the Sālimiyyah and others, including some Hanbalīs, Shāfi'īs, and Mālikīs. They say that He spoke to Moses with a voice that Moses heard, and that this voice is beginningless.

The falsity of this view is immediately evident to reason. The same applies to the view that He spoke to him with a voice that came into being, and that this voice remains forever, as do all the other temporal events that subsist in Him. These are views whose falsity is self-evident.

---

## Page 6796

What led these groups into such views was the principle they received from the Jahmiyyah: whatever is never devoid of temporal events is itself temporally originated. This is false according to both reason and revelation. This corrupt principle contradicts reason and revelation, and through it the eternalist philosophers gained the upper hand over them. Thus, they neither defended Islam nor defeated its enemy. Rather, they opposed the early generations and the leading scholars, contradicted reason and revelation, and gave their enemies among the philosophers, eternalists, and heretics power over themselves and the Muslims—all because of their error concerning this principle, which they made the foundation of their religion. Had they held fast to what the Messenger brought, they would have agreed with both transmitted revelation and sound reason, and their foundation would have stood firm. But they neglected the foundations and were therefore denied attainment; the foundations lie in following what the Messenger brought.

They introduced principles which they imagined to be firm foundations, yet their condition was as Allah depicted in the two parables of the building and the tree. Concerning the believers and the hypocrites, He said:

“Is one who founded his building upon fear of Allah and His good pleasure better, or one who founded his building on the edge of a crumbling bank, so that it collapsed with him into the Fire of Hell? Allah does not guide the wrongdoing people.”

And He said:

“Allah presents a parable: a good word is like a good tree, whose root is firm and whose branches reach into the sky.”

“It yields its fruit at all times by its Lord’s permission. And Allah presents parables for people so that they may take heed.”

“And the parable of a bad word is that of a bad tree, uprooted from the surface of the earth, having no stability.”

“Allah keeps those who believe steadfast with the firm word in this worldly life and in the Hereafter. And Allah leads the wrongdoers astray, and Allah does whatever He wills.”

The term “foundations” is derived

---

## Page 6797

from the roots of a tree and the foundations of a building. Hence, a foundation is defined as that upon which something else is built, or from which something else branches out.

The firm foundations are those of the prophets, as it has been said:

O you who set out in the morning seeking knowledge—
All a servant’s knowledge is subordinate to the Messenger’s knowledge.
You seek the branch to establish a ruling,
Yet neglect the very root of all foundations.

May Allah guide us and all our fellow believers to His straight path: the path of those whom Allah has blessed—the prophets, the steadfastly truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous. What excellent companions they are!

What lies within the hearts is built upon these foundations and branches out from them. Allah has given parables of the good word within the believers’ hearts and the evil word within the disbelievers’ hearts. And (a word is a categorical proposition and a comprehensive belief. Our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was granted words that open and conclude discourse, and concise expressions encompassing many meanings. He was thus sent with comprehensive knowledge, and knowledge of things first and last, in its most complete form.

The good word within the believers’ hearts—the belief founded upon faith and the oneness of Allah—is like a good tree whose root is firm and whose branches reach into the sky. Thus, the very foundation of faith is firmly established in the believer’s heart, just as the root of the good tree is firmly established, while its branches reach into the sky:

“To Him ascends good speech, and righteous deeds

---

## Page 6798

raise it up.”

Allah, glorified is He, likens the good word—that is, the declaration of His oneness—to a good tree whose root is firm and whose branches reach into the sky. He thereby explains that the good word has a firm root in the believer’s heart and branches reaching high, and that it is firmly established in a steadfast heart. As He says:

“Allah keeps those who believe firm with the firm word, in this worldly life and in the Hereafter.”

The believer possesses certainty and tranquillity. Faith is firmly established and settled in his heart, and he himself remains steadfast and settled in faith, never turning away from it. The evil word, however, is:

“Like an evil tree uprooted from the surface of the earth.”

It has been torn out by its roots, just as something is cut away and uprooted from the surface of the earth:

“Having no stability.”

It has neither a place in which to settle nor stability in any place. For qarār can mean a place of settlement, as Allah Most High says:

“And wretched is that resting place.”

And He says:

“He made the earth a place of settlement for you.”

It is also said, “So-and-so has no qarār,” meaning no stability. The word in the verse has been interpreted in both senses. Thus, the belief of one who upholds falsehood is not firmly established in his heart, nor is he himself steadfast or settled in it. As Allah Most High says in the other parable:

“As for the foam, it passes away, cast aside; but what benefits people remains in the earth.”

Although he may hold that belief for a time, it fails him at the moment of truth. He is like someone who associates partners with Allah: at the moment of truth, whatever he used to invoke besides Allah vanishes from him. Likewise, the vain deeds in which a person believes fail him at the moment of truth and do not benefit him. Rather, they are like the evil tree uprooted from the surface of the earth, having no

---

## Page 6799

stability. Whoever possesses a good word with a firm root has a branch reaching into heaven that connects him to Allah. For, as He, glorified is He, says:

“To Him ascend good words, and righteous deeds raise them.”

Whoever lacks a firm root is denied attainment because he has neglected the foundations. This is why you find that those given to religious innovations and doubts reach no praiseworthy end. As Allah Most High says:

“To Him belongs the true supplication. Those whom they invoke besides Him do not respond to them at all. They are only like someone stretching out his hands towards water for it to reach his mouth, but it will never reach it. The supplication of the disbelievers is nothing but futile wandering.”

Allah, glorified is He, sent the messengers and revealed the Scriptures so that He alone would be worshipped, without any partner, and worshipped only as He commanded through His messengers. The foundation of worshipping Him is knowing Him through His descriptions of Himself in His Book and His messengers’ descriptions of Him. Thus, the way of the early generations was to describe Allah as He described Himself and as His messengers described Him, without distortion or negation, and without specifying how His attributes are or likening them to those of creation. Those who deny any of this have not esteemed Allah as He deserves, known Him as He deserves to be known, described Him as He deserves to be described, or worshipped Him as He deserves to be worshipped.

Allah, glorified is He, has mentioned this statement:

“They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves,”

in three places, to affirm His own greatness and the attributes that are His due; to affirm His oneness and that none but Him deserves worship; and to affirm what He revealed to

---

## Page 6800

His messengers. He says in Surah al-Zumar:

“They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves, though the entire earth will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection”—to the end of the verse.

He says in Surah al-Hajj:

“Weak are both the seeker and the sought.”

“They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves.”

And He says in Surah al-An‘ām:

“They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves when they said, ‘Allah has revealed nothing to any human being.’”

In all three passages, He condemns those disbelievers who have not esteemed Him as He deserves. This indicates that the believer must esteem Allah as He deserves, just as he must fear Him as He should be feared and strive in His cause as he ought to strive. Allah Most High says:

“Strive in Allah’s cause as you ought to strive.”

And He says:

“Fear Allah as He should be feared.”

The verbal noun here is attached to its object, while its agent is understood. That is: strive for Him as He has commanded you to strive; fear Him as He has commanded you to fear Him; and esteem Him in the manner He has explained to you and commanded you to observe. So believe the Messenger in what he has reported, and obey him in what he has made obligatory and commanded.

As for what lies beyond human capacity, no one is blamed for failing to do it. ‘Ā’ishah said: “So make due allowance for a young girl who is eager for amusement.”

The verse also indicates His immense greatness, especially His words:

“They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves, though the entire earth will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens will be rolled up in His right hand.”

In Ibn Abī Talhah’s commentary, Ibn ‘Abbās is reported to have said: “Whoever believes that Allah has power over all things has esteemed Allah as He deserves.”

---

## Page 6801

It is established in the two Sahīh collections, in a hadith narrated by Ibn Mas‘ūd, that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) recited this verse when some Jews told him that Allah holds the heavens on one finger, the earths on one finger, the mountains on one finger, the trees and soil on one finger, and the rest of creation on one finger. The Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) laughed in amazement and in affirmation of the rabbi’s statement, and recited this verse.

Ibn ‘Abbās reported:

“A Jewish man passed by the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) and said: ‘O Abū al-Qāsim, what do you say when Allah places heaven on this one, the earth on this one, the mountains and water on this one, and the rest of creation on this one?’

“Then Allah Most High revealed:

‘They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves, though the entire earth will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens will be rolled up in His right hand.’”

This was narrated by Imam Ahmad ibn Hanbal and al-Tirmidhī through Abū al-Duhā, from Ibn ‘Abbās. Al-Tirmidhī classified it as singular, good, and authentic.

This implies that His greatness exceeds what that rabbi described, for the verse conveys it more emphatically. Thus, in the two Sahīh collections, Abū Hurayrah reports that the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Allah will grasp the earth on the Day of Resurrection and roll up heaven in His right hand. Then He will say: ‘I am the King! Where are the kings of the earth?’”

Also in the two Sahīh collections, Ibn ‘Umar reported that the Messenger of Allah (peace and blessings be upon him) said:

“Allah will roll up the heavens on the Day of Resurrection, then take them in His right hand. Then He will say: ‘Where are the kings? Where are the tyrants?

---

## Page 6802

Where are the arrogant?’” Muslim narrated a fuller version, mentioning that He takes the earth in His other hand.

Ibn Abī Hātim narrated: My father narrated to us; ‘Amr ibn Rāfi‘ narrated to us; Ya‘qūb ibn ‘Abd Allāh narrated to us, from Ja‘far, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, who said:

“The Jews spoke about the attributes of the Lord, blessed and exalted is He, saying things they neither knew nor had seen. Then Allah revealed to His Prophet:

‘They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves, though the entire earth will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection, and the heavens will be rolled up in His right hand. Glory be to Him! Exalted is He above what they associate with Him!’

“He thus deemed the description they gave of Him to be an act of associating partners with Him.”

He also narrated: My father narrated to us; Abū Nu‘aym narrated to us; al-Hakam—meaning Abū Mu‘ādh—narrated to us, from al-Hasan, who said:

“The Jews set about examining the creation of the heavens, the earth, and the angels. When they had finished, they began to estimate His greatness. Then Allah Most High revealed to His Prophet:

‘They have not esteemed Allah as He deserves.’”

This indicates that He is greater than they described Him to be, and that they did not esteem Him as He deserves.

As for His words, “above what they associate with Him”: anyone who makes a created being equal to the Creator in any respect—loving it as he loves the Creator, or describing it in the same terms as the Creator—is associating partners with Allah. He has equated Allah with a created being in some respect and thus set up an equal to his Lord. The Lord Most High has no equal, no peer, and none like Him. And whoever

---

## Page 6803

likens Him to the nonexistent and the impossible is worse than these people, for he both negates His attributes and likens Him to others. One who negates His attributes is worse than one who associates partners with Him. Allah has repeated Pharaoh’s story in several places in the Quran because people need to learn from it. Pharaoh attained such dominion and made such claims to lordship, divinity, and supremacy as none of those who negate Allah’s attributes ever matched. His ultimate fate was as Allah Most High has described.

Allah has no attribute in which anyone else is His equal. Thus, neither reasoning by analogy nor categorical reasoning that treats its individual instances as equal may be applied to Him, for that constitutes associating partners with Him by equating Him with creation. Rather, a fortiori reasoning is to be used. To Him, glorified is He, belongs the highest description in the heavens and the earth. He is therefore more deserving than anyone else of attributes of perfection, and more deserving than anyone else of being declared free of attributes of imperfection. These matters have been discussed at length elsewhere. It has also been explained that those who make Him absolute existence and existence qualified by negation, or an abstract essence, have likened Him to the most deficient mental concepts and placed Him below things that exist in external reality. Those who negate His attributes and sought to establish that the world came into being by establishing that bodies came into being have not thereby established the coming into being of anything, as has been explained in its proper place.

They then made denying that the Lord is a body their chief basis for declaring Him free of imperfections. Whoever follows this approach has not thereby established Allah’s freedom from any imperfection whatsoever. For

---

## Page 6804

there is no attribute that he denies on the grounds that it entails corporeality and is an attribute of bodies without an argument analogous to his own being raised against what he affirms.

If he affirms some attributes, he is told: “What is said about this attribute that you deny is just like what is said about those you affirm. If one entails corporeality and is false, the other is likewise. If you say, ‘I affirm this in a manner befitting the Lord,’ you are told: ‘The same applies to this other attribute.’ If you say, ‘I affirm it while denying corporeality,’ the reply is: ‘The same applies here. You cannot use this to distinguish between two equivalent cases.’”

If he affirms the names but denies the attributes, as the Muʿtazilah do, then the same argument he makes concerning the names is put to him concerning the attributes. If he affirms One who is living, knowing, and powerful, although he knows of no one described in these terms except a body, then affirming that He possesses knowledge and power—as stated in the Quran and the Sunnah—is no different.

If he affirms neither the names nor the attributes, as is the case with the thoroughgoing Jahmiyyah and the heretics, he is told: “You must nevertheless affirm an existent that subsists in itself, although you know of nothing like this except a body.”

If he says, “I do not designate Him by any name, either affirmatively or negatively,” he is told: “Your silence does not negate realities. There is no middle ground between negation and affirmation: He is either real, actual, and existent, or unreal and nonexistent.”

---

## Page 6805

Moreover, if you do not know Him, you are ignorant, so do not speak. If you do know Him, you must distinguish Him from everything else by what is unique to Him, saying, for example: “the Lord of the worlds,” “the Eternal, the Beginningless,” or “the One who exists in His own right,” and so forth. You have thereby affirmed a living existent who subsists in Himself, and have affirmed that He acts, although you know of nothing answering this description except a body.

If it is supposed that he denies Him altogether, he is told: “What exists before us is observable. If it is eternal, beginningless, and exists in its own right, then this may establish an eternal, beginningless body existing in its own right—the very thing you sought to escape. If it is created and made, then it has a Creator who created it, and He must be eternal and beginningless. Thus, in either case, an eternal, beginningless existent who subsists in Himself has been established.”

This is discussed at length elsewhere. Here we have drawn attention to the following point: anyone who bases his declaration that the Lord is free of shortcomings and defects on the denial of corporeality cannot, by this argument, establish that He is free of any defect whatsoever. The same applies to anyone whose principal argument is the denial of composition.

Anyone who reflects on what they have written in their books will see that they have offered no proof of His existence. They have neither established His existence and affirmed what rightly belongs to Him, nor declared Him free of imperfection and denied of Him what cannot apply to Him. For their proof of His existence consists in establishing that bodies have a beginning, yet they have offered no proof of that. Their negation likewise rests on this, and they are inconsistent in it, even if

---

## Page 6806

they had established proof that He is not a body. How much more so, then, when they have offered no proof of this and have contradicted themselves!

This makes it clear to you that whoever departs from the Quran and Sunnah possesses no knowledge, whether rational or transmitted, especially concerning this supreme object of inquiry. Yet they may hold sound beliefs that they have come to know through their innate rational disposition and what they have heard of the Quran and the religion of the Muslims. Their hearts thus affirm what they affirm and deny what they deny on the basis of this innate disposition, perfected by the revealed Law. Nevertheless, they have followed these innovated methods, which contain no knowledge whatsoever. What can be gained from their discourse, however, is that some of them refute the position of another proponent of falsehood and expose his inconsistency.

Thus, when they discussed false doctrines concerning the Lord, they proceeded to refute them on the grounds that they ascribed corporeality to Him, as al-Qādī Abū Bakr did in Hidāyat al-mustarshidīn (Guidance for Those Seeking Right Direction), and as others did. They thereby failed to establish an argument against those proponents of falsehood. They also rejected much of what the Jews say on the grounds that it ascribed corporeality to Him. Yet the Jews were present with the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in Medina, and they would sometimes mention certain attributes to him, as in the hadith of the rabbi. Allah censured the Jews for such statements as “Allah is poor” and “His hand is shackled,” among other things. Yet the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, never said that they ascribed corporeality to Allah, or that the Torah contained such an ascription. He neither reproached them for this nor refuted these false statements on the grounds that they ascribed corporeality to Him, as some of those who deny the divine attributes have done.

---

## Page 6807

It is thus clear that this method conflicts with revelation and reason, with what Allah sent His Messenger with, and with the innate disposition upon which He created His servants. Its adherents are like those who said:

“If only we had listened or reasoned, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze.”

Elsewhere, we have demonstrated the falsity of al-Rāzī’s claim that the argument from necessity and contingency is among the greatest methods. We have shown that this argument is unsound, that it yields no knowledge, and that its proponents have established no proof for the existence of the Necessary Existent. We have also shown that the argument from perfection is superior to it and has been relied upon by rational people, both ancient and modern. Indeed, toward the end of his life, he acknowledged that he had reflected upon the methods of speculative theology and the approaches of philosophy, but found that they neither healed the sick nor quenched thirst, and that he found the Quran’s method to be the most direct.

No one before Ibn Sīnā had pursued the argument from necessity and contingency. He derived it from the discourse of the speculative theologians, who classified what exists as either temporally originated or eternal. He instead classified it as either necessary or contingent, so that he could maintain that the celestial sphere was contingent despite being eternal. In doing so, he contradicted the general body of rational thinkers, both his predecessors and others. He also contradicted himself, since in his writings on logic he had stated, as his predecessors had, that whatever is contingent must be temporally originated. This has been discussed at length elsewhere.

Moreover, those who pursued this method were ultimately led by it to Pharaoh’s position, for

---

## Page 6808

Pharaoh denied the Creator and accused Moses of lying when he said that Allah had spoken to him. These people’s position likewise leads ultimately to denying the Creator; and even if they affirm His existence, they say that He does not speak and has never called anyone or spoken privately to him. Their principal basis for denying His very being is their denial of corporeality, while their basis for denying His speech and His speaking to Moses is that temporally originated events cannot subsist in Him. Thus, their position leaves neither a Lord nor a messenger. In reality, it contradicts the testimony that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. A messenger conveys the message of the One who sent him, and that message is His speech with which He sent him. If He does not speak, there can be no message.

For this reason, the prophets agreed that Allah speaks. Whoever does not affirm that He speaks by His will and power, with speech that subsists in His essence, has not affirmed that He speaks. Among those who negate His attributes, some say that speech is an attribute of action, meaning that it is created and separate from Him. Others say that it is an attribute of His essence, meaning that, like life, it subsists in His essence, but that He does not speak by His will and power. Each group is correct in refuting the other’s falsehood.

The evidence establishes that speech is an attribute both of His essence and of His action, subsisting in the Lord’s essence, and that the Lord speaks by His will and power. All the evidence advanced by those who say that it is an attribute of action establishes only that He speaks by His power and will—and this is true. The evidence advanced by those who say that it is an attribute of His essence establishes only that His speech subsists in His essence—and this is true. As for whoever affirms one of the two

---

## Page 6809

—such as one who says that His speech is created, or that He does not speak by His will and power—such people have not, in reality, affirmed that He speaks, nor have they affirmed any speech for Him.

For this reason, they make unintelligible statements. One says, “It is a single meaning subsisting in His essence.” Another says, “It consists of letters, or letters and sounds, eternal and beginningless, inseparable from His essence.” Yet another says, “It is created and separate from Him.”

Thus, when the falsity of their position became apparent to some of their followers, who had not grasped the precise nature of these three positions, they became bewildered and suspended judgment. They said, “We affirm what Muslims generally hold: that the Quran is the speech of Allah. As for whether it is created, consists of letters and sound, or is a meaning subsisting in His essence, we assert none of these things.”

It is well known that guidance in these fundamentals and knowledge of the truth concerning them—which means knowing what the Messenger brought, in agreement with sound reason—are more beneficial and more important than much of the knowledge they discuss. This is especially so since hearts naturally seek to know the truth concerning these matters, and because of the disagreements people have witnessed about them.

These people mention this suspension of judgment in their statements of creed and their writings on the foundations of religion. I have seen some of them, among the leading scholars and religious authorities in Egypt and Syria, who have written works on the foundations of religion. When discussing “the question of the Quran”—whether it is created or eternal, whether it consists of letters and sounds or is a meaning subsisting in His essence—they forbade these assertions and said, “One must say what has been said by

---

## Page 6810

all Muslims: that the Quran is the speech of Allah, and refrain from these assertions.”

These people suspended judgment out of bewilderment and doubt, despite their desire for knowledge, guidance, and religion. They are among those most eager to know the truth concerning this and other matters, but they knew only these three positions: those of the Mu'tazilah, the Kullābiyyah, and the Sālimiyyah. Each group exposes the falsity of the others’ position, and each position contains errors that make it unacceptable. Knowing no position beyond these, they settled for simple ignorance, preferring it to ignorance compounded by false belief.

The causes of this lay in their agreement with these groups on the fundamental principle of their teaching and religion: arguing that bodies and the world came into being by the method of the practitioners of innovated speculative theology—as did those eminent scholars and religious authorities whom I have mentioned—and arguing for their contingency on the grounds that they are composite, as did the other shaykh. The latter uses this method to deny that the Necessary Existent is a body, while the former denies that He is a body by that other method. Yet the most accomplished thinkers, who were better acquainted with these methods and more proficient in reasoning and argumentation through them and through other methods, have recognized their invalidity, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Allah, glorified be He, has stated that He

“sent His Messenger with guidance and the religion of truth to make it prevail over all other religions.”

He has also stated

“that He grants victory in the life of this world to His messengers and those who believe.”

Allah, glorified be He, recompenses a person in a manner corresponding to his deeds: recompense is of the same kind as the deed. Whoever opposes the messengers is therefore punished in a manner corresponding to his sin. Thus, if he has impugned them

---

## Page 6811

and described their words as ignorance and a departure from knowledge and reason, his own reason and knowledge are afflicted, and his ignorance is exposed as a punishment.

Whoever says that they deliberately lied, Allah exposes his lies; and whoever says that they are ignorant, Allah exposes his ignorance. Thus, when Pharaoh, Hāmān, and Korah called Moses a sorcerer and a liar, Allah related their words, saying:

“We certainly sent Moses with Our signs and clear authority”

“to Pharaoh, Hāmān, and Korah, but they said, ‘A sorcerer and a liar!’”

Pharaoh sought to have him killed and began finding fault with him, as in his words:

“Pharaoh said, ‘Let me kill Moses, and let him call upon his Lord! I fear that he may change your religion or spread corruption in the land.’”

He also said:

“Am I not better than this lowly man, who can hardly express himself?”

Allah destroyed Pharaoh, exposed his lies and fabrications against Allah and His messengers, utterly humiliated him, and rendered him incapable of speaking to any purpose: he could articulate no argument.

The Pharaoh of this community, Abū Jahl, used to be called Abū al-Hakam, but the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, named him Abū Jahl, meaning “father of ignorance.” He was indeed just as the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had named him: a father of ignorance, through which he brought ruin upon himself and his followers in this world and the Hereafter.

Those who said that the Messenger was cut off, meaning that he would die and his memory would cease, were punished by being cut off themselves, as the Most High said:

“Indeed, the one who hates you is the one cut off.”

No one hates the Messenger without Allah cutting him off—even the adherents of religious innovation who oppose his Sunnah. Abū Bakr ibn ‘Ayyāsh was told, “There are people in the mosque who sit to address others and expound religious innovations.”

---

## Page 6812

He replied:

“Whoever sits to address people will find people sitting to listen to him. But the people of the Sunnah endure, and their memory endures, whereas the people of religious innovation die, and their memory dies with them.”

These Jahmiyyah, who deny the divine attributes, resemble Pharaoh: they agreed with him in his denial and said that Allah is not above the heavens and that Allah did not speak directly to Moses, as Pharaoh said:

“Pharaoh said, ‘Hāmān, build me a lofty tower so that I may reach the pathways—’”

“‘the pathways of the heavens—and look upon the God of Moses, though I certainly think him a liar.’”

Pharaoh denied the Lord. Had Moses not told him that his Lord was above the world, he would not have said:

“‘And look upon the God of Moses, though I certainly think him a liar.’”

The Most High said:

“Thus Pharaoh’s evil deeds were made appealing to him, and he was barred from the path. Pharaoh’s plotting led only to ruin.”

The Most High also said:

“Pharaoh said, ‘O chiefs, I know of no god for you other than myself. So bake clay bricks for me, Hāmān, and build me a lofty tower so that I may look upon the God of Moses, though I certainly think he is a liar.’”

“He and his soldiers behaved arrogantly in the land without right, and thought that they would not be returned to Us.”

“So We seized him and his soldiers and cast them into the sea. See, then, what was the end of the wrongdoers!”

“We made them leaders inviting others to the Fire, and on the Day of Resurrection they will not be helped.”

“We caused a curse to follow them in this world, and on the Day of Resurrection they will be among the despised.”

And when Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was taken up to his Lord and his Lord prescribed the prayers for him

---

## Page 6813

—five in all—he related that he returned to Moses, and that Moses told him: “Return to your Lord and ask Him to lighten the burden upon your community,” as is related through numerous independent chains in the hadiths of the Ascension. Moses thus affirmed Muhammad’s statement that his Lord is above, whereas Pharaoh denied Moses’ statement that his Lord is above. Those who affirm this follow Moses and Muhammad, while those who deny it agree with Pharaoh.

A number of scholars of rational inquiry have relied upon this argument, including Abū al-Hasan al-Ash‘arī in his book al-Ibānah. He presented several rational and scriptural proofs that Allah is above the world, and said at its beginning:

“If someone says, ‘You have rejected the positions of the Jahmiyyah, the Qadariyyah, the Khawārij, the Rāfidah, the Mu‘tazilah and the Murji’ah. Tell us, then, what position you hold and what religion you profess.’

He is told: The position we hold and the religion we profess consist in adhering to the Book of our Lord, the Sunnah of our Prophet, and what has been transmitted from the Companions, the Successors and the imams of the Muslims. We affirm what Abū ‘Abd Allāh Ahmad ibn Hanbal ibn Muhammad ibn Hanbal affirmed, and shun whatever contradicts his position. For he is the consummate imam and virtuous leader through whom Allah made the truth manifest, clarified the paths, and suppressed the innovations of the innovators, the deviations of the deviant and the doubts of the doubters. May Allah have mercy upon him, a foremost imam

---

## Page 6814

and an eminent teacher, and upon all the imams of the Muslims.”

He then set out the creed in outline, discussing Allah’s being above the Throne, seeing Allah, the question of the Quran, and similar matters. This is discussed at length elsewhere.

“The point here” is that those who deny the divine attributes, or some of them, do not base this denial on what the Messenger brought, since what the Messenger brought contains affirmation, not negation. Rather, they rely on what they suppose to be rational proofs and use these to oppose what the Messenger brought. Their position amounts to saying that the Messenger provided nothing on this subject to which one could turn, whether from revelation or reason. According to them, he neither conveyed a report that clarified the truth nor presented rational proofs that established the correct position on this matter. Other matters are different: they acknowledge that the Messenger presented rational proofs in the Quran for the existence of the Lord and the truthfulness of the Messenger.

They may also say that he spoke of the Hereafter. Yet they denied the attributes when they saw that the negation they advanced had not been mentioned by the Messenger: he neither conveyed it nor presented any rational proof for it. Rather, he mentioned only affirmation—which, on their account, is not actually true—thus leaving people in need of reinterpretation or consigning the matter to Allah. So, when they alleged that what the Messenger brought contained neither scriptural nor rational evidence—neither a report clarifying the truth nor

---

## Page 6815

a proof establishing it—Allah punished them in a manner corresponding to their sins. Thus, what they say on this subject falls outside both reason and revelation, despite their claim that it rests on demonstrative rational proofs. When someone knowledgeable examines it, he finds it to consist of satanic specious arguments, akin to those of the sophists and heretics who undermine both reason and revelation.

Their opposition to revelation is plain to everyone. Those who revere and follow them merely suppose that they have mastered rational inquiry. Yet anyone who examines the matter thoroughly finds their condition reflected in the words of the inhabitants of the Fire:

“If only we had listened or reasoned, we would not be among the inhabitants of the Blaze.”

And as Allah Most High says:

“As for those who disbelieve, their deeds are like a mirage in a lowland which a thirsty person mistakes for water, until, when he reaches it, he finds nothing there. But he finds Allah there, who pays him his account in full. And Allah is swift in reckoning.”

“Or like darkness in a deep sea, covered by waves, above which are waves, above which are clouds—layers of darkness, one above another. If he stretches out his hand, he can hardly see it. Whoever Allah does not grant light has no light.”

Since their position amounted to saying that the Quran and hadith contain neither scriptural nor rational evidence on this subject, Allah deprived them of knowledge of both scriptural and rational proofs concerning it. Thus they became among the most misguided of all creatures, despite claiming to be more knowledgeable than the Companions, the Successors, and the imams of the Muslims. Indeed, they may even claim to be more knowledgeable than the prophets. This is a legacy inherited from Pharaoh and his accursed followers.

---

## Page 6816

It has also been said that the first person known to have openly advocated in Islam the denial of Allah’s attributes implicit in Pharaoh’s statement was al-Ja'd ibn Dirham. Khālid ibn 'Abd Allāh al-Qasrī offered him as a sacrifice, saying:

“O people, offer your sacrifices; may Allah accept them. I am sacrificing al-Ja'd ibn Dirham, for he claimed that Allah did not take Abraham as a close friend and did not actually speak to Moses. Exalted is Allah far above what al-Ja'd says!”

Then he stepped down and slaughtered him. Muslim scholars, including al-Hasan al-Basrī and others, commended him for what he had done. It was through his association with this al-Ja'd that Marwān ibn Muhammad, the last Umayyad caliph, was called al-Ja'dī. That association brought him misfortune, culminating in the dynasty’s fall. For when innovations contrary to the religion of the messengers become manifest, Allah exacts retribution from those who oppose the messengers and vindicates His messengers.

Thus, when the esotericist heretics emerged and took control of Syria and other lands, hypocrisy and heresy spread there. These were the concealed reality of their movement, and they constituted the true substance of Pharaoh’s position: “denial of the Creator and denial of His worship.” The best of what they outwardly professed was Rāfidism, so the best among them and the closest to Islam were the Rāfidīs. Through them, Rāfidism and heresy spread, so that those residing in Syria, such as the extremist Banū Hamdān and others like them, were Shiites, as were the Banū Buwayh in the East.

Ibn Sīnā and his household were adherents of their movement. He said: “It was because of this that I took up philosophy.” Their rise began when al-Muqtadir assumed power before he had come of age. This marked the beginning of the disintegration of the Abbasid state. For this reason, the Umayyad ruler in al-Andalus was then given the title Commander of the Faithful, whereas previously he had not been called

---

## Page 6817

by this title and used to say: “The Muslims cannot have two caliphs.” But when al-Muqtadir assumed power, he said, “This is a boy whose rule is not valid,” and so he adopted this title.

The heretical descendants of 'Ubayd Allāh al-Qaddāh also bore this title. Inwardly, however, they were godless heretics and hypocrites, and their claimed lineage was as false as their religion. This was unlike the Umayyad and Abbasid rulers: both had authentic lineages, and they were Muslims like other Muslim caliphs.

When hypocrisy, religious innovations, and wickedness contrary to the Messenger’s religion became widespread, their enemies were given power over them. The Christian Byzantines repeatedly advanced into Syria and al-Jazīra, taking the Syrian frontier strongholds one after another, until they captured Jerusalem late in the fourth century. Some time later, they besieged Damascus. The people of Syria were in a most desperate plight, caught between the Christian unbelievers and the hypocritical heretics, until Nūr al-Dīn, the martyr, assumed power. He undertook his efforts to uphold Islam, establish its ascendancy, and wage jihad against its enemies.

Then the Banū 'Ubayd, the rulers of Egypt, sought his assistance against the Christians, and he came to their aid. [Many events followed until Egypt was taken from the Banū 'Ubayd. It was captured by Salāh al-Dīn Yūsuf ibn Sādī (1), who had the sermon delivered there in the name of the Abbasids. From that time onward, Islam gained ascendancy in Egypt, after it had remained for a hundred years in the hands of hypocrites who had apostatized from Islam] (*).

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 111, footnote):
> Thus it appears, but this is a scribal error; the correct reading is “Shādī,” for Salāh al-Dīn was the son of Ayyūb ibn Shādī.
>
