This is because Moses’ mission was not universal: a prophet would be sent specifically to his own people, whereas Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent to all humanity. Indeed, he was sent to both humankind and jinn, in inward as well as outward matters. Thus, no one may depart from obedience to him and from following him, either inwardly or

---

## Page 6906

outwardly, whether one belongs to the spiritual elite or the common people.

Some of these people rank certain friends of Allah above the prophets, and may count al-Khidr among them. This contradicts the consensus of the exemplary masters of the spiritual path, let alone the other leading religious authorities and Muslim scholars. Indeed, when al-Hakīm al-Tirmidhī stated in his book Khatm al-Awliyā’ (The Seal of the Friends of Allah) that among the last friends of Allah there would be someone superior to the Companions—and perhaps even hinted at something similar concerning the prophets—the Muslims rose against him, denounced his claim, and expelled him from the town because of it. There is no doubt that his statements on this matter were unsound and false.

This was the starting point of the misguidance of those who followed him in this matter. Eventually, numerous people each claimed to be the seal of the friends of Allah, such as Ibn ‘Arabī, the author of al-Fusūs, Sa‘d al-Dīn ibn Hamawayh, and others. Some people also began claiming that among later generations were those whose knowledge of Allah surpassed that of Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, the Emigrants, and the Helpers. Such assertions, which would take too long to enumerate, are false according to the Quran, the Sunnah, and scholarly consensus.

Indeed, many groups ultimately came to witness the reality of divine decree in creation, and imagined that whoever witnessed it was no longer subject to commands and prohibitions, or to the promise of reward and the threat of punishment. This is the religion of the polytheists who said:

“If Allah had willed, neither we nor our forefathers would have associated partners with Him, nor would we have forbidden anything.”

---

## Page 6907

And these people are worse than the Qadarī Muʿtazilites, who acknowledge commands and prohibitions, the promise of reward and the threat of punishment, but deny divine decree. The latter resemble the Magians, whereas the former resemble the polytheists who reject the prophets and revealed laws. They are thus among the worst of people. These matters have been discussed at length elsewhere.

“The point here” is that the outward must have an inward reality that gives it substance, confirms it, and accords with it. Whoever observes the outward forms of religion without inward belief is a hypocrite, while whoever claims an inward reality that contradicts its outward forms is a disbeliever and a hypocrite. Rather, the inward reality of religion gives substance to its outward forms, confirms them, and accords with them; its outward forms likewise accord with its inward reality, confirm it, and give it substance. Just as a human being must have both a soul and a body that are in harmony, a person’s religion must have outward and inward aspects that are in harmony. The inward aspect pertains to a person’s inner self, and the outward aspect to their outward self.

The Quran is full of statements concerning the inward and outward aspects of religion, and the inward is the foundation of the outward. As Abū Hurayrah said:

“The heart is king, and the bodily members are its troops. If the king is good, its troops are good; if the king is corrupt, its troops are corrupt.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, in the body there is a piece of flesh: if it is sound, the rest of the body is sound because of it; if it is corrupt, the rest of the body is corrupt because of it. Indeed, it is the heart.”

It is also reported in the Musnad that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Islam is outwardly manifest, while faith is in the heart.”

And Allah Most High said:

“Those are the ones in whose hearts He has inscribed

---

## Page 6908

faith and strengthened them with a spirit from Him.”

Allah Most High also said:

“It is He who sent down tranquillity into the hearts of the believers so that they might grow still further in faith.”

And He said:

“Whomever Allah wills to guide, He opens his breast to Islam; and whomever He wills to lead astray, He makes his breast tight and constricted, as though he were ascending into the sky.”

And He said:

“Allah has sent down the finest speech: a Book consistent in its parts and reiterating its teachings. The skins of those who fear their Lord shiver at it; then their skins and hearts soften at the remembrance of Allah.”

And He said:

“The believers are only those whose hearts tremble when Allah is mentioned, whose faith increases when His verses are recited to them, and who put their trust in their Lord.”

And He said:

“Those who believe and whose hearts find reassurance in the remembrance of Allah—surely, in the remembrance of Allah do hearts find reassurance.”

There are many similar passages in the Quran.

Concerning the disbelievers, He said:

“Those are the ones whose hearts Allah does not will to purify.”

And He said:

“Allah has set a seal upon their hearts and their hearing, and over their eyes is a covering.”

There are other similar passages.

We therefore ask Allah the Magnificent, by His grace and generosity, to set right our inward and outward selves and to guide us to what He loves and is pleased with in all our affairs. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless our master Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace.

---

## Page 6909

Shaykh al-Islām Abū al-Abbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī al-Dimashqī also said:

All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless our master Muhammad and his family, and grant them peace.

Section

Allah Most High says:

“We never sent any messenger or prophet before you but that, when he recited, Satan cast something into his recitation.”

Through to His words:

“So that He may make what Satan casts a trial for those whose hearts are diseased and those whose hearts are hardened. Indeed, the wrongdoers are in profound opposition.”

“And so that those who have been given knowledge may know that it is the truth from your Lord, believe in it, and their hearts humbly submit to it. Indeed, Allah guides those who believe to a straight path.”

Allah has divided hearts into three types: hardened hearts, diseased hearts, and believing, humbly submissive hearts. This is because they are either dry

---

## Page 6910

and rigid, not softening to the truth in acknowledgment and submission, or they are not dry and rigid.

The “first” is the hardened heart: rigid and dry, like a stone that takes no impression. Faith is not inscribed within it, nor is knowledge imprinted upon it, for that requires a soft, receptive place. The “second” must be one of two kinds: either the truth is firmly established within it and does not depart, owing to its strength together with its tenderness, or its tenderness is accompanied by weakness and laxity. The latter is the diseased heart, while the former is both strong and tender.

The heart resembles the limbs of the body—the hand, for example. A hand may be rigid and dry, unable to bend or grasp, or grasping only with violence: this resembles the hardened heart. Or it may be weak and diseased, rendered incapable by its weakness and disease: this resembles the diseased heart. Or it may grasp with strength and suppleness: this resembles the knowledgeable, merciful heart. Through mercy, it is free of hardness; through knowledge, it is free of disease, for this disease arises from doubts and misconceptions. Hence, those unlike these are described as possessing knowledge, faith, and humble submission.

His words:

“And so that those who have been given knowledge may know that it is the truth from your Lord, believe in it, and their hearts humbly submit to it.”

provide evidence that knowledge leads to faith—not that the people of knowledge

---

## Page 6911

have risen above the level of faith—as some speculative theologians imagine. Rather, they possess both knowledge and faith, as Allah Most High says:

“But those among them firmly grounded in knowledge, and the believers, believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you.”

And He says:

“Those who were given knowledge and faith will say, ‘You have indeed remained, according to Allah’s decree…’”

Thus, His words:

“Those firmly grounded in knowledge say, ‘We believe in it; all of it is from our Lord,’”

parallel this verse. Here He states that those given knowledge know that it is the truth from their Lord, while there He states that they say concerning the ambiguous passages: “We believe in it; all of it is from our Lord.” Both passages concern matters that give rise to doubt and confusion for others. There, the discussion concerns ambiguous passages; here, it concerns what Satan casts forth, which Allah annuls, after which Allah makes His verses firm. Here He contrasts what is firmly established with what Allah has annulled of what Satan cast forth.

For this reason, some early Quran commentators said that the “definitive” is the abrogating, and the “ambiguous” is the abrogated. They meant—and Allah knows best—His words:

“Allah annuls what Satan casts forth; then Allah makes His verses firm.”

Abrogation here means removing what Satan has cast forth, not repealing what Allah has legislated.

I have pointed out the reason for this in a later passage, namely: Allah sometimes contrasts the definitive with the ambiguous, and at other times with the abrogated. In the broad terminology of the early generations, “the abrogated” includes any text whose apparent meaning is set aside because of stronger countervailing evidence, such as restricting the scope of a general expression or qualifying an unrestricted one. Such a text is ambiguous because it admits of two meanings

---

## Page 6912

and this includes expressions requiring clarification, for these too are ambiguous. Making them definitive means ruling out any unintended meaning that might be imagined in them. The same applies to a text whose ruling has been repealed, for all of this involves annulling what Satan casts into the interpretation of the Quran.

For this reason, they used to ask: “Do you know the abrogating from the abrogated?” Once the abrogating is known, the definitive is known. Accordingly, it is valid to speak of “the definitive and the abrogated,” just as one speaks of “the definitive and the ambiguous.”

His subsequent words,

“Then Allah makes His verses firm,”

establish that all the verses are made firm, both their definitive and their ambiguous passages, as He says:

“Alif Lām Rā.”

“A Book whose verses have been made firm, then set out in detail.”

And He says:

“These are the verses of the firmly established Book,”

according to one of the two interpretations.

Elsewhere, He divides the verses into two categories, definitive and ambiguous, as He says:

“In it are definitive verses, which are the foundation of the Book, and others that are ambiguous.”

These ambiguous passages are among what the Most Merciful revealed, not among what Satan cast forth and Allah annulled. Thus, in the Quran, the definitive is sometimes contrasted with the ambiguous, both being among Allah’s verses; at other times, it is contrasted with what Allah annulled of what Satan cast forth.

Some people contrast it with whatever Allah has abrogated, without qualification, even saying: “This verse is definitive, not abrogated.” They regard what is abrogated as not definitive, even though Allah originally revealed it, following the apparent sense of His words:

“Allah annuls,”

“Then Allah makes His verses firm.”

---

## Page 6913

These, then, are three meanings contrasted with the definitive that should be borne in mind. In summary, “making firm” sometimes concerns revelation itself, in which case it is contrasted with what Satan casts forth. The definitive is what has been revealed from Allah and made firm by Him: He has distinguished it from anything with which it might be confused and separated from it whatever does not belong to it. For making something firm involves separation, distinction, differentiation, and delimitation, through which its identity is established and it is perfected. Hence its meaning includes prevention, just as delimitation does; prevention is part of its meaning, not its entire meaning.

At other times, “making firm” concerns keeping the revelation in force, according to those who contrast it with abrogation in the sense of repealing what has been prescribed. This is a technical usage. Alternatively—and this more closely accords with the usage of the early generations—it may be said that they called every removal “abrogation,” whether the removal of a ruling or of an apparent meaning.

What Satan casts into a person’s recitation may affect the very words of the one conveying it, what he hears, or his understanding, as Allah says:

“He sends down water from the sky, and the valleys flow according to their capacity”—to the end of the verse.

It is well known that when someone hears a text whose ruling, or one of whose implications, has been set aside, Satan casts into that recitation the suggestion that he should follow what has been abrogated. Allah then makes His verses firm through the abrogating text, by which the ruling is repealed and the intended meaning clarified.

On this understanding, it is valid to say: “The ambiguous is the abrogated,” in this respect. And Allah knows best.

At other times, “making firm” concerns interpretation and meaning: it consists in distinguishing the reality

---

## Page 6914

intended from all others, so that it is not confused with anything else. In contrast to the definitive verses are the ambiguous verses, which resemble both one thing and another and thus admit either meaning. Ahmad ibn Hanbal said: “The definitive is that in which there is no discrepancy; the ambiguous is that which appears in one form in one passage and in another form elsewhere.”

Allah did not say of the ambiguous verses that no one knows their explanation and meaning except Him. Rather, He said:

“But no one knows its ta’wīl (interpretation) except Allah.”

This decisively settles the dispute between those who disagree on this matter, for Allah has stated that no one knows its ta’wīl except Him. Pausing at this point in the recitation is supported by much evidence and is the position held by the Companions of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, the majority of the Successors, and the overwhelming majority of the Muslim community.

Yet He did not deny their knowledge of its meaning and explanation. Rather, He said:

“A blessed Book which We have revealed to you, that they might reflect upon its verses.”

This includes both the definitive and the ambiguous verses. Something whose meaning cannot be understood cannot be reflected upon. He also said:

“Do they not reflect upon the Quran?”

He did not exclude any part of it by forbidding reflection upon it. Allah and His Messenger condemned only those who pursue the ambiguous, seeking discord and seeking its ta’wīl. As for those who reflect upon both the definitive and the ambiguous, as Allah has commanded, seeking to understand them and know their meaning, Allah has not condemned them. Rather, He has commanded this and commended it.

This is illustrated, with regard to ta’wīl, by the report that among the Jews who were in Medina during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, such as Huyayy ibn Akhtab and others, there were those who

---

## Page 6915

sought to interpret the letters of the alphabet at the beginnings of the surahs as indicating how long this community would endure. Some in later times followed the same approach, agreeing with the Sabean astrologers. They claimed that it would endure for 693 years, this being the numerical value of those letters in the abjad system after repeated letters are excluded. This is a form of ta’wīl concerning the events of the Last Day foretold by the Quran.

It is also reported that some of the Christians who came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with the delegation from Najrān interpreted “Indeed, We” and “We” as meaning that there are three gods, because the pronoun is plural. This is a ta’wīl concerning belief in Allah. Thus, the former offered a ta’wīl concerning the Last Day, while the latter offered a ta’wīl concerning Allah.

It is well known that “Indeed, We” and “We” are among the ambiguous expressions. They may refer to one individual accompanied by others of the same kind, or to one individual accompanied by his helpers, even if they are not of the same kind. They may also refer to one individual magnifying himself, who stands in the place of someone accompanied by others because of the multiplicity of his names, each name taking the place of a named entity. Such an expression is therefore ambiguous: its wording is one, but its meanings are diverse.

“Equivocal terms” are among the ambiguous expressions, as are some “univocal terms.” Quranic commentators call these “multiple senses and parallel usages,” and have compiled “Books of Multiple Senses and Parallel Usages.” Multiple senses concern equivocal terms, while parallel usages concern univocal terms. Some of our companions who have written on this subject have supposed that the multiple senses

---

## Page 6916

and parallel usages both concern equivocal terms: they are parallel with regard to wording and have multiple senses with regard to meaning. Yet the matter is not as he claims. Rather, anyone who examines their words carefully will find that they explicitly support what we have said.

Those in whose hearts is deviation abandon the unambiguous passages, in which there is no uncertainty, such as:

“Your God is one God.”

“Indeed, I am Allah. There is no deity except Me, so worship Me.”

“Allah has not taken a son, nor is there any deity alongside Him.”

“He has not taken a son and has no partner in His dominion.”

“He neither begets nor is born.”

“Nor is anyone comparable to Him.”

Instead, they follow the ambiguous passages, seeking to cause discord—to lead people astray by applying them where they do not belong—and seeking their ta’wīl, meaning the reality of what they speak of.

This is because “speech is of two kinds”: non-declarative speech, which includes commands, and declarative speech. The ta’wīl of a command is the very act commanded. Thus, some of the early generations said that the Sunnah is the ta’wīl of the command.

‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say when bowing and prostrating: ‘Glory be to You, O Allah, and praise be to You. O Allah, forgive me.’ He was thereby putting the Quran into practice.”

She meant His words:

“Then glorify your Lord with praise and seek His forgiveness. Indeed, He is ever Accepting of repentance.”

As for declarative speech, its ta’wīl is the very thing reported, when it comes to pass; its ta’wīl is not the understanding of its meaning.

---

## Page 6917

The term “ta’wīl” occurs in several places in the Quran with this meaning. Allah Most High says:

“We have certainly brought them a Book which We have explained in detail with knowledge, as guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

“Do they await anything but its fulfilment? On the Day its fulfilment comes, those who had forgotten it before will say, ‘The messengers of our Lord certainly came with the truth.’”

He thus states that He has explained the Book in detail: this means clarifying it and distinguishing its meanings so that no confusion remains. He then says, “Do they await”—that is, wait for—“anything but its fulfilment? On the Day its fulfilment comes,” and so on to the end of the verse.

This refers precisely to the coming to pass of what the Quran foretells concerning the Resurrection and its signs: the Beast, Gog and Magog, the sun rising from the west, the coming of your Lord and the angels, rank upon rank, and what the Hereafter contains of records of deeds, scales, Paradise, Hell, the various forms of bliss and punishment, and other things. At that time they will say:

“The messengers of our Lord certainly came with the truth. Have we any intercessors who will intercede for us? Or could we be sent back, so that we might act differently from the way we used to act?”

None but Allah knows the timing, extent, and nature of these matters foretold by the Quran. For Allah says:

“No soul knows what delights have been kept hidden for them.”

And He says:

“I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and no human heart has ever conceived.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said: “Nothing of what is in Paradise exists in this world except the names.” Allah has told us that Paradise contains wine, milk, water, silk, gold, silver, and other things, and we know with certainty that

---

## Page 6918

the reality of those things is not identical to that of their worldly counterparts. Rather, there is a tremendous difference between them despite their resemblance, as in His words:

“And they will be given it in a similar form.”

According to one of the two interpretations, it resembles what is in this world without being identical to it. Thus, the names of those realities resemble the names of these realities, just as the realities themselves resemble one another in certain respects. When those names are used in addressing us, we understand them through what the two have in common. Yet those realities have a distinctive nature that we cannot apprehend in this world. We have no means of apprehending it, since we have experienced neither those realities themselves nor anything resembling them in every respect. Those realities, as they truly are, constitute the fulfilment of what Allah has told us.

This also refutes the Jews, Christians, and Sabians, whether philosophers or others, who deny that there is eating, drinking, clothing, or sexual intercourse in Paradise, and reject the existence of what the Quran describes. Someone who enters Islam but acts hypocritically toward the believers interprets these descriptions as parables intended to convey spiritual bliss, if he belongs to the Sabian philosophers who deny bodily resurrection. If he belongs to the hypocrites of the two faiths who affirm bodily resurrection, he interprets them as conveying the bliss of Paradise consisting of spiritual enjoyment, pleasing sounds, and fragrant scents. Thus, every misguided person distorts words from their proper meanings to conform to what he believes to be real.

In doing so, he also follows what is ambiguous: the names resemble one another, and the things named resemble one another, yet their differences are greater than their similarities. Thus these people follow this

---

## Page 6919

ambiguity, “seeking discord” through the specious arguments they advance to show that these realities cannot exist in Paradise, and “seeking its interpretation” so as to reduce it to what they know and are familiar with in this world.

Allah Most High says:

“And none knows its fulfilment except Allah.”

For Allah has said concerning those realities:

“No soul knows what joy has been kept hidden for them.”

Neither an angel brought near nor a prophet sent knows it.

In His words, “And none knows its fulfilment,” the pronoun may refer either to the Book or to what is ambiguous. If it refers to the Book, as in His expressions “of it” and “of it”—“They follow what is ambiguous in it, seeking discord and seeking its interpretation”—this is valid. For, regarding all the verses of the Book, both clear and ambiguous, that tell of the unseen in which we have been commanded to believe, Allah alone knows the true nature of that unseen and when it will occur.

This may be supported by the fact that Allah attributes fulfilment to the entire Book, while stating that it has been explained in detail, in His words:

“We have certainly brought them a Book which We have explained in detail with knowledge, as guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

“Are they waiting for anything but its fulfilment? On the Day its fulfilment comes…”

Thus, He attributes the coming fulfilment to the Book that has been explained in detail.

We have already explained that Allah alone knows the time, extent, kind, and true nature of that fulfilment. We know only some of its attributes, within the limits of our knowledge, since we have nothing comparable to it in our experience. Likewise, He says:

“Rather, they have denied that which they have not encompassed in knowledge and whose fulfilment has not yet come to them.”

---

## Page 6920

And if fulfilment pertains to the whole Book and means what we have described, the difficulty is removed, and this becomes comparable to His words:

“They ask you about the Hour: when will it arrive? Say, ‘Knowledge of it rests only with my Lord. None but He will reveal it at its appointed time. It weighs heavily upon the heavens and the earth.’”

Up to His words:

“Knowledge of it rests only with Allah.”

Likewise, He says:

“People ask you about the Hour. Say, ‘Knowledge of it rests only with Allah.’ And what will make you know? Perhaps the Hour is near.”

He thus states that knowledge of it rests only with Allah. This means knowledge of its precise time and true nature; otherwise, we already know those attributes of it that He has told us about. Knowledge of the Book’s fulfilment is therefore like knowledge of the Hour, and the Hour is itself part of its fulfilment. This is clear and evident. That knowledge of the Hour rests with Allah does not preclude our knowing what we know of its attributes and circumstances, or our explaining the texts that describe its circumstances. The same applies here.

If, however, the pronoun refers to what is ambiguous, as many people maintain, this is because the promised rewards and threatened punishments described in the reports are ambiguous, unlike commands and prohibitions. Hence the words found in transmitted reports:

“Acting upon what is clear in it and believing in what is ambiguous in it.”

For the purpose of a report is that it should be believed. The promised rewards and threatened punishments described in such reports contain the ambiguity we have mentioned, unlike commands and prohibitions.

Hence some scholars have said that “the ambiguous” comprises parables, promises and warnings, while “the clear” comprises commands and prohibitions. The latter are distinct and not confused with anything else: they concern things we do, which we know through actual experience, and things we refrain from doing, which we must necessarily be able to conceive.

---

## Page 6921

Another occurrence of the term ta’wīl (fulfilment) in the Quran is in His words, Exalted is He:

“Rather, they have denied what they have not fully comprehended and whose fulfilment has not yet come to them.”

The pronoun refers to the Quran, or to “what they have not fully comprehended,” which itself refers to the Quran. He, Exalted is He, says:

“This Quran could not have been fabricated by anyone other than Allah. Rather, it is a confirmation of what came before it and a detailed exposition of the Book, in which there is no doubt, from the Lord of all worlds.

“Or do they say, ‘He fabricated it’? Say, ‘Then produce a surah like it, and call upon whomever you can besides Allah, if you are truthful.’

“Rather, they have denied what they have not fully comprehended and whose fulfilment has not yet come to them. Those before them likewise denied; so see what was the fate of the wrongdoers.

“Among them are those who believe in it, and among them are those who do not believe in it. Your Lord knows best those who spread corruption.”

He, Glorified is He, thus states that this Quran could not have been fabricated by anyone other than Allah. This construction indicates the impossibility of what is negated, as in His words:

“Your Lord would not destroy towns unjustly,”

and His words:

“Allah would not punish them while you were among them.”

For created beings are incapable of producing anything like it. He challenged them and demanded this of them when He said:

“Or do they say, ‘He fabricated it’? Say, ‘Then produce a surah like it, and call upon whomever you can besides Allah, if you are truthful.’”

This challenge exposes the inability of all created beings.

He, Exalted is He, says:

“Rather, it is a confirmation of what came before it,”

meaning that it confirms what came before it;

“and a detailed exposition of the Book,”

meaning that it explains the Book in detail. He thus states that it confirms what came before it and is a detailed exposition of the Book. “Book” is a generic term. He challenged those who said, “He fabricated it,” and showed that they themselves are

---

## Page 6922

the fabricators. He says:

“Rather, they have denied what they have not fully comprehended and whose fulfilment has not yet come to them.”

That is, they denied the Quran, which they had not fully comprehended and whose fulfilment had not yet come to them. He thus distinguishes between fully comprehending it and the coming of its fulfilment. It is therefore clear that people of knowledge and faith can fully comprehend it even though its fulfilment has not yet come to them, and that fully comprehending the Quran is not the same as its fulfilment coming to pass. Fully comprehending it means knowing the meanings of its words completely, whereas the coming of its fulfilment is the actual occurrence of what it relates. He distinguishes between knowing the account and knowing what it recounts: knowing the account means knowing the explanation of the Quran, while knowing what it recounts means knowing its fulfilment.

“The key point here” is that the meaning of an account has a mental representation that exists within the knower—in the human mind, for example—while that meaning also has an established reality outside the mind. The wording initially signifies the meaning in the mind, then serves as an intermediary for it or signifies the external reality. Its fulfilment is thus the external reality, whereas knowing its explanation and meaning is knowing the mental representation.

This is what we explained earlier: Allah revealed the Quran so that it might be known, understood, comprehended, contemplated and reflected upon—both its clear and its ambiguous passages—even if its fulfilment is not known.

This is further illustrated by what Allah says about the disbelievers:

“When you recite the Quran, We place between you and those who do not believe in the Hereafter a concealed barrier.”

“And We place coverings over their hearts lest they understand it, and deafness in their ears. And when you mention your Lord in

---

## Page 6923

the Quran alone, they turn their backs in aversion.”

Thus, in condemnation of the polytheists, He states that when the Quran is recited to them, a concealed barrier is placed between their sight and the Messenger, coverings are placed over their hearts lest they understand it, and deafness in their ears. If the people of knowledge and faith had coverings over their hearts preventing them from understanding some of it, they would share this condition with them.

His words,

“lest they understand it,”

refer to the Quran in its entirety. It is therefore clear that Allah loves for it to be understood. Hence al-Hasan al-Basrī said:

“Allah has not revealed a verse without loving that people should know what it was revealed about and what He meant by it.”

No exception was made for ambiguous passages or any others.

Mujāhid said:

“I went through the Quran with Ibn ‘Abbās several times, from beginning to end, stopping at every verse and asking him about it.”

Here, then, is Ibn ‘Abbās, the great scholar of this community—one of those who said, “None knows its interpretation except Allah”—answering Mujāhid about every verse in the Quran. This is what led Mujāhid and those who agreed with him, such as Ibn Qutaybah, to place the recitational pause after His words,

“and those firmly grounded in knowledge,”

thus including those firmly grounded in knowledge among those who know its interpretation. For Mujāhid had learned from Ibn ‘Abbās the explanation of the entire Quran and the clarification of its meanings, so he thought that this was the interpretation whose knowledge was denied to anyone other than Allah. The underlying reason is that the term “ta’wīl” (interpretation) is used in different senses: what Allah meant by it in

---

## Page 6924

the Quran, its usage among groups of the early generations, and its technical usage among groups of later scholars. Because the term ta’wīl has these different senses, everyone who understood it according to his own usage assumed that this was the meaning intended in the Quran.

Mujāhid was a leading authority on Quranic explanation. Al-Thawrī said:

“If an explanation comes to you from Mujāhid, that is sufficient for you.”

As for ta’wīl, that is another matter.

This is made clear by the fact that none of the Companions or Successors refused to explain a verse of Allah’s Book or said, “This belongs to the ambiguous passages whose meaning is unknown.” Nor did any of the early generations of this community or the imams followed by others ever say:

“The Quran contains verses whose meaning neither the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor the people of knowledge and faith collectively know or understand.”

They may, however, deny that particular people know some of it, and this is beyond doubt. It was only later scholars from the various groups who formulated this issue because of discussions concerning the verses on Allah’s attributes, the verses on divine decree, and other matters.

They gave it such formulations as:

“May the Quran contain that whose meaning is unknown?”

and:

“that whose wording we have been required to recite as worship without understanding.”

Some groups allowed this possibility, clinging to an apparent sense of this verse and arguing that Allah tests His servants with whatever He wills. Other groups rejected it in order to use that rejection to arrive at their corrupt interpretations, which amount to displacing words from

---

## Page 6925

their proper places.

Error predominates in both groups. The former fall short in understanding the Quran, resembling those of whom it was said:

“Among them are unlettered people who know the Scripture only through mere recitation.”

The latter transgress, resembling those who displace words from their proper places.

Some later scholars framed the issue using an offensive label, saying:

“Allah cannot speak without intending any meaning, contrary to the Hashwiyyah.”

Yet no Muslim has ever said that Allah speaks words devoid of meaning. The dispute is only whether He speaks words whose meaning is not understood. There is a vast difference between denying that the speaker intends a meaning and denying that the person addressed understands it.

He then advanced an argument inconsistent with his own principles, saying:

“This would be purposeless, and purposelessness is impossible for Allah.”

Yet he holds that nothing Allah does is in any way reprehensible and that He may do anything. Nor can he say, “Purposelessness is an imperfection and is therefore excluded from Him,” because the dispute concerns letters, which, in his view, are created and belong to the category of acts. According to him, an act may possess any quality whatsoever. Thus, his position rests on neither authentic transmitted evidence nor clear reason.

The source of discord between the two groups, and the point at which their minds become bewildered, is that those claiming to provide ta’wīl erred both in asserting that the scholars know the ta’wīl and in claiming that ta’wīl means their own interpretation, which is a displacement of words from their proper places. For the former, owing to their knowledge

---

## Page 6926

of the Quran and the Sunnah, their sound reason, and their knowledge of the speech of the early generations and the Arabs, knew with certainty that the ta’wīl claimed by these people was not the meaning of the Quran. For these people displaced words from their proper places, falling into varying degrees: Qarmatians and Bātinīs, who reinterpret both reports and commands; Sabian philosophers, who reinterpret most reports concerning Allah and the Last Day, even those concerning most of the circumstances of the prophets; and Jahmīs and Mu‘tazilīs, who reinterpret some of what has been revealed concerning the Last Day and the verses concerning divine decree, and who reinterpret the verses concerning Allah’s attributes. Some later Ash‘arīs agreed with them regarding what has been revealed concerning certain attributes, and some regarding certain matters of the Last Day. Others from various groups within the Muslim community, though predominantly adherents of the Sunnah, may also reinterpret passages in ways that displace words from their proper places.

Those who claimed knowledge of ta’wīl—including a group among the early generations and Ahl al-Sunnah, and most speculative theologians and adherents of innovation—also saw that the revealed texts indicated that the meanings of the Quran could be known. They considered it a mark of incapacity, a defect, and something reprehensible for Allah to address His servants with words that they read and recite but do not understand. They were correct in the transmitted and rational evidence they adduced. Yet they erred concerning the meaning of the ta’wīl that Allah negated and the ta’wīl that they affirmed. The innovators among them used this as a stepping stone to displacing words from their proper places. The former were thus closer to silence and safety, albeit with a measure of ignorance, while the latter engaged in more discussion and disputation, but with fabrications against Allah and saying about Him what they do not

---

## Page 6927

know, and deviation concerning His names and signs. Such, then, is the situation.

The confusion arises from the different meanings of the term ta’wīl. In the usage of later jurists, speculative theologians, hadith scholars, Sufis, and others, “ta’wīl” means diverting an expression from its more likely meaning to a less likely meaning on the strength of accompanying evidence. This is the ta’wīl they discuss in the principles of jurisprudence and in disputed questions.

When one of them says, “This hadith, or this text, is subject to ta’wīl,” or, “It is to be understood in such-and-such a sense,” another replies, “This is a form of ta’wīl, and ta’wīl requires evidence.” The person advancing such an interpretation has two obligations: to show that the wording admits the meaning he claims, and to establish the evidence that requires departing from the apparent meaning in favour of that meaning.

This is the ta’wīl over which they dispute in matters concerning Allah’s attributes: one writes to refute or condemn ta’wīl; another says, “The verses concerning the attributes must not be reinterpreted”; another says, “Rather, they must be reinterpreted”; and a third says, “Rather, ta’wīl is permissible: it is employed when doing so serves a benefit and omitted when refraining serves a benefit,” or, “It is appropriate for scholars, but not for others.” There are other such statements and disputes.

As for “ta’wīl” in the usage of the early generations, it has two meanings. The first is explaining speech and clarifying its meaning, whether this agrees with its apparent meaning or differs from it. Thus, in their usage, ta’wīl and tafsīr (exegesis) are close in meaning or synonymous.

---

## Page 6928

And this—Allah knows best—is what Mujāhid meant when he said that the scholars know its ta’wīl. Muhammad ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī says in his Quran commentary, “Discussion of the ta’wīl of His words, such-and-such,” and, “The scholars of ta’wīl differed concerning this verse,” and similar expressions. What he means is explanation.

The “second meaning” in the usage of the early generations—which is the third sense of ta’wīl overall—is the very thing intended by the speech. If the speech is a request, its ta’wīl is the requested action itself; if it is a statement, its ta’wīl is the very thing being stated. There is a considerable difference between this meaning and the preceding one. In the preceding sense, ta’wīl belongs to the realm of knowledge and discourse, like explanation, commentary, and clarification. It exists in the mind and on the tongue, having mental, verbal, and written existence. In this sense, however, ta’wīl consists of the actual things that exist in external reality, whether past or future.

Thus, when someone says, “The sun has risen,” the ta’wīl of this is its actual rising. Here, “ta’wīl” belongs to the realm of concrete, external existence. The ta’wīl of speech, then, consists of the realities that exist externally, with their actual attributes, affairs, and conditions. Those realities cannot be known as they truly are through speech and reports alone, unless the listener has already formed a conception of them, or of something comparable to them, through something other than speech and reports. Nevertheless, he comes to know of their attributes and conditions to the extent that the speaker has conveyed them to him: whether by drawing

---

## Page 6929

analogies, by offering an approximation, by referring to what they share with other things, or by other means. This third sense and established usage is that of the language in which the Quran was revealed. We have already explained this.

Examples include Jacob’s words to Joseph, peace be upon him:

“Thus will your Lord choose you, teach you the interpretation of dreams, and perfect His favour upon you.”

And His words:

“Two young men entered the prison with him. One of them said, ‘I saw myself pressing wine.’ The other said, ‘I saw myself carrying bread on my head, from which birds were eating. Tell us its interpretation, for we see that you are among those who do good.’”

“He said, ‘No food provided for you will come to you without my telling you its interpretation before it comes to you.’”

And the words of the chiefs:

“Confused dreams! We have no knowledge of the interpretation of dreams.”

“The one of the two who had been saved, remembering after a time, said, ‘I will tell you its interpretation, so send me.’”

And Joseph’s words when his family came to him in Egypt:

“He took his parents into his embrace and said, ‘Enter Egypt, Allah willing, in safety.’”

“He raised his parents onto the throne, and they fell down before him in prostration. He said, ‘My father, this is the fulfilment of my earlier dream. My Lord has made it come true.’”

Thus, the ta’wīl of dreams seen in sleep is the very reality they signify and in which they find their fulfilment, as Joseph said:

“This is the fulfilment of my earlier dream.”

The one who knows their ta’wīl is the one who tells of that reality. As Joseph said:

“No food provided for you will come to you”—meaning in a dream—“without my telling you its interpretation before it comes to you”—meaning before its fulfilment comes to you.

---

## Page 6930

Allah Most High also says:

“If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger, if you believe in Allah and the Last Day. That is better and best in outcome.”

They explained this as meaning: best in its consequences and final outcome. Here, ta’wīl is the outcome of their action—referring matters to the Book and the Sunnah. In Sūrat Yūsuf, ta’wīl concerns accounts of dreams; in al-A‘rāf and Yūnus, it concerns the Quran, as it does in Sūrat Āl ‘Imrān.

In the account of Moses and the learned man, Allah Most High says:

“He said, ‘This is the parting between you and me. I will tell you the explanation of what you could not bear patiently.’”

The passage continues to His words:

“‘I did not do it of my own accord. That is the explanation of what you could not bear patiently.’”

Here, ta’wīl concerns the actions performed by the learned man: damaging the ship without its owner’s permission, killing the boy, and restoring the wall. It is thus the ta’wīl of an action, not of a statement. This is because ta’wīl is the verbal noun of awwalahu–yu’awwiluhu, just as tahwīl is the verbal noun of hawwala, and ta‘wīl that of ‘awwala. Awwala–yu’awwilu is the transitive form of āla–ya’ūlu–awlan, like hāla–yahūlu–hawlan.

The expression āla–ya’ūlu means to return or revert to something. From this comes ma’āl, meaning that to which something ultimately returns. Related to it through “greater derivation” is maw’il, for the latter comes from wa’ala, whereas the former comes from awila.

Maw’il means a place of return. Allah Most High says:

“They will find no refuge apart from Him.”

Among the words related to it through “lesser derivation” is āl, for a person’s āl are those who

---

## Page 6931

return to him. Hence, this term is used only of someone of great standing: the person to whom the āl are attributed must be greater than they are, someone to whom they may fittingly return—as with the āl of Abraham, the āl of Lot, and the āl of Pharaoh. This is unlike ahl (family).

Awwal (first) follows the af‘al pattern, for its feminine is ūlā, as in Jumādā al-Ūlā. In Sūrat al-Qasas, Allah says:

“To Him belongs all praise in this life and the Hereafter.”

Some maintain that it follows the faw‘al pattern and give its feminine as awwala. However, this requires supporting evidence from Arab usage. Indeed, its restricted declension indicates that it follows af‘al, not faw‘al, since words on the faw‘al pattern, such as kawthar and jawhar, are fully declined.

That which precedes is called awwal—and Allah knows best—because what follows it returns to it and is built upon it. It is thus the foundation and basis of what follows. The form is an elative, not an ordinary descriptive adjective: it is like akbar and kubrā (greater or greatest), and asghar and sughrā (smaller or smallest), rather than ahmar and hamrā’ (red).

Hence, people say, “I came to him the day before yesterday.” Allah says:

“A mosque founded on righteousness from the very first day.”

“And I am the first of the Muslims.”

“And do not be the first to disbelieve in it.”

When someone is described as “the first of these people,” he is the one who surpasses them in precedence. Each one refers back to whoever preceded him and relies upon him, while all of them ultimately refer back to this earliest predecessor. Whoever takes the lead in an action and is then followed as an example by those after him becomes the predecessor to whom they all refer back. The first thus has the qualities of leadership and being followed.

The word “awwal” suggests returning and reverting, yet “awwal” also suggests beginning and that with which a beginning is made—the opposite of that which returns. This is because something is first only in relation to what follows it, for one says:

---

## Page 6932

“the first of the Muslims” and “the first day.” The sense of returning and reverting belongs to the second term of the possessive construction, not the first.

When we say “the āl of so-and-so,” however, the return is to the first term. For the elative form denotes pre-eminence in being that to which others ultimately return and refer. Its indication of pre-eminence shows that it denotes a destination and point of reference, not something that itself returns and refers elsewhere. There is no pre-eminence in something’s returning and referring to another; rather, pre-eminence lies in its being that to which others return and refer. Since the form is an elative, it indicates pre-eminence in being a destination and point of reference. Absolute pre-eminence in this respect requires that it be what precedes and comes at the beginning. And Allah knows best.

Thus, the ta’wīl of speech is that to which the speaker directs it, that to which the speech leads, or that to which the speaker takes it to refer. A verbal noun on the taf‘īl pattern may accompany a verb of a different pattern, as in His words:

“And devote yourself to Him with complete devotion.”

Here the wording is wa-tabattal ilayhi tabtīlan. It is therefore permissible to say ta’awwala al-kalāmu ilā hādhā al-ma‘nā ta’wīlan (“the speech led to this meaning”), ta’awwaltu al-kalāma ta’wīlan (“I interpreted the speech”), and awwaltu al-kalāma ta’wīlan (“I interpreted the speech”).

The verbal noun stands in place of an adjective. A verbal noun may function adjectivally with an active meaning, as in ‘adl (a just person), sawm (one who fasts), and fitr (one who is not fasting). It may also carry a passive meaning, as with darb (“striking”) in “a dirham struck by the emir,” and khalq (“creation”) in “This is Allah’s creation.”

Ta’wīl, then, is that to which speech is directed, that to which it leads, or that to which it itself comes. Speech returns and reverts, comes to rest, leads, and is directed only to its reality

---

## Page 6933

which is precisely what is intended by it. This is as one of the early scholars explained His words:

“Every announcement has its appointed fulfillment.”

He said: “A reality.” For if the speech is a report, it ultimately leads and refers back to the reality being reported. Otherwise, it would have no reality, outcome, or point of reference, but would instead be false. If it is a request, it ultimately leads and refers back to the reality being sought, even if its object does not exist and has not been realized. Whenever a report is a promise or a threat, it ultimately leads to the reality sought and awaited. Thus, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited this verse:

“Say: He has the power to send punishment upon you from above you or from beneath your feet, or to divide you into factions.”

He said: “It will indeed come to pass, but its fulfillment has not yet come.”

It is also reported that ʿAbd Allāh said: “Five have already come to pass: the onslaught, the inevitable punishment, the smoke, the moon, and the Romans.”

Section

As for including Allah’s names and attributes, or some of them, among the ambiguous passages whose ta’wīl is known only to Allah—or believing that these constitute the ambiguous passages whose ta’wīl Allah has reserved to Himself—each of these two views is held by groups among our companions and others. Although they are correct in much of what they say and have escaped innovations into which others have fallen, this matter must be discussed from two perspectives.

First: To anyone who says, “This belongs to the ambiguous passages, and its meaning cannot be understood,” we say: As for the evidence that this is false, I know of no one among the early generations of this community or its leading scholars—neither Ahmad ibn Hanbal nor anyone else—who classified this among the ambiguous passages

---

## Page 6934

covered by this verse, denied that anyone could know its meaning, or treated Allah’s names and attributes as unintelligible foreign speech. Nor did they say that Allah sends down speech whose meaning no one understands. Rather, their statements had sound meanings.

Concerning the hadiths about the divine attributes, they said: “Let them stand as transmitted.” They forbade, rejected, and refuted the reinterpretations of the Jahmiyyah, which strip the texts of the meanings they convey. The statements of Ahmad and the imams before him clearly show that they rejected the Jahmiyyah’s reinterpretations, affirmed the texts according to the meanings they convey, and understood some of what they indicate, just as they understood the other texts concerning promises, warnings, merits, and other matters.

Ahmad also said, “Let them stand as transmitted,” concerning hadiths other than those about the divine attributes, including hadiths containing warnings, such as the Prophet’s saying:

“Whoever cheats us is not one of us.”

He also said this concerning hadiths about merits. What he meant was that the wording of a hadith must not be distorted from its proper sense, as is done by those who distort it and whose distortion is called “interpretation” in later terminology. Thus, what these later figures call interpretation is, in the imams’ judgment, false distortion.

Likewise, Ahmad explicitly stated in his book Refutation of the Heretics and the Jahmiyyah that they clung to the ambiguous passages of the Quran. Ahmad discussed those ambiguous passages and explained their meaning and interpretation in a manner contrary to the Jahmiyyah’s reinterpretation, following the path of the imams before him. This establishes the imams’ agreement that they know the meaning of these ambiguous passages and that their explanation and interpretation must not be withheld. Rather, by the imams’ agreement, they are to be explained and interpreted without distorting them from their proper sense or deviating concerning Allah’s names and verses.

---

## Page 6935

What further clarifies the confusion that has arisen here is that Ahl al-Sunnah agree on rejecting the reinterpretations of the Jahmiyyah and other deviant groups who depart from the truth. “Rejected interpretation” means diverting speech from its apparent meaning to a meaning contrary to it. If it were said that this is the interpretation mentioned in the verse, and that none but Allah knows it, this would concede to the Jahmiyyah that the verse has an interpretation contrary to what it indicates, but that none but Allah knows it. This is not the position of the early generations and the imams. Rather, their position is to deny and reject these reinterpretations, not to suspend judgment about them. In their view, reading the verse or hadith is itself its explanation. Such texts are to be left as transmitted, conveying their meanings, without distortion or deviation.

The evidence that this is not ambiguous speech whose meaning cannot be known is as follows:

There is no doubt that Allah has named Himself in the Quran with names such as the Most Merciful, the Most Loving, the Almighty, the Compeller, the All-Knowing, the All-Powerful, the Most Compassionate, and others like these. He has described Himself with attributes such as those set forth in Surah al-Ikhlās, the Verse of the Throne, the opening of Surah al-Hadīd, and the closing of Surah al-Hashr, and in His words:

“Indeed, Allah knows all things,”

and is

“Powerful over all things.”

He says that He

“loves those who are mindful of Him,”

“those who act justly,”

and “those who do good,”

and that He is pleased with those who believe and do righteous deeds.

“So when they angered Us, We took vengeance upon them.”

“That is because they followed what angered Allah.”

“But Allah disliked their going forth.”

“The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.”

“Then He rose over the Throne. He knows what enters the earth and what emerges from it, what descends from heaven and what ascends into it. And He is with you wherever you are.”

“And He is the One who

---

## Page 6936

is God in heaven and God on earth; and He is the All-Wise, the All-Knowing.”

“To Him ascends good speech, and righteous deeds raise it.”

“Indeed, I am with you both; I hear and I see.”

“And He is Allah in the heavens and on earth.”

“What prevented you from prostrating to what I created with My two Hands?”

“Rather, both His Hands are outstretched; He spends however He wills.”

“And the Face of your Lord, full of majesty and honour, will remain.”

“Desiring His Face.”

“So that you might be brought up under My Eye.”

And other passages like these.

Anyone who claims that these are ambiguous texts whose meaning cannot be known should be asked: Do you say this about all the names and attributes with which Allah has described Himself, or only some of them?

If you say that this applies to all of them, that is manifest obstinacy and a denial of what is necessarily known in Islam—indeed, outright disbelief. For we understand a meaning from His words:

“Indeed, Allah knows all things.”

And we understand from His words:

“Indeed, Allah is Powerful over all things,”

a meaning different from the first. We understand a meaning from His words:

“My mercy encompasses all things.”

And we understand a meaning from His words:

“Indeed, Allah is Almighty, capable of vengeance.”

Muslim children—indeed, every rational person—understand this. I have encountered someone from the Maghrib who embraced innovation and denial—despite his affiliation with hadith, corrupt philosophy had influenced him—who says: “We call Allah the Most Merciful, the All-Knowing, and the All-Powerful merely as proper names, without understanding from them any meaning indicating anything whatsoever. Likewise, concerning His words, ‘They encompass nothing of His knowledge,’ we utter this expression without saying that He possesses knowledge.” And this

---

## Page 6937

extremism concerning outward meanings is akin to the Qarmatians’ extremism concerning hidden meanings, though the former is more barren and the latter more unbelieving.

This obstinate disputant should then be asked: “Do these names indicate the God who is worshipped and a reality that exists, or not?”

If he says no, he is an outright denier of God, and I know of no Muslim who says this.

If he says yes, he should be asked: “Why, then, do you understand that they indicate the Lord Himself, yet fail to understand that they indicate the meanings they contain, such as mercy and knowledge, when both are equally indicated?”

He must then say: “Yes, because the existence of attributes is rationally impossible, since it entails composition or a beginning in time, unlike the divine essence.”

He should then be addressed as the second group is addressed, as we shall explain: those who acknowledge that some of the meanings of these names and attributes can be understood, but not others.

He should be asked: “What is the difference between what you affirm and what you deny, or what you refrain from either affirming or denying?” The distinction must rest either on revelation—because one of the two texts conveys its meaning conclusively or apparently, unlike the other—or on reason, because one of the two meanings may or must be affirmed, whereas the other may not. Both grounds, however, are invalid in most cases.

As for the “first,” the Quran’s indication that He is Most Merciful, Compassionate, Loving, All-Hearing, All-Seeing, Most High and Magnificent is like its indication that He is All-Knowing and All-Powerful. There is no difference between them in terms of the text. Likewise, its mention of His mercy, love and highness is like its mention of His will and intent.

---

## Page 6938

As for the “second,” whoever affirms some attributes while denying others should be asked: “Why, for example, have you denied the reality of His mercy and love and reduced them to His will?”

If he says, “Because mercy, as understood in our case, is a tenderness that cannot be attributed to Allah,” he should be told: “Likewise, will, as understood in our case, is an inclination that cannot be attributed to Allah.”

If he says, “His will is not of the same kind as the will of His creatures,” he should be told: “Neither is His mercy of the same kind as the mercy of His creatures, and the same applies to His love.”

If he says—and this is what his position really amounts to—“I did not establish will and the other attributes through revelation. Rather, I established knowledge, power and will through reason, as well as hearing, sight and speech according to one of the two approaches, because action indicates power, precision indicates knowledge, and particularization indicates will,” he should be told that there are three answers:

(First: The bestowal of blessings, beneficence and the removal of harm also indicate mercy, just as particularization indicates will. Bringing near, drawing close, and the kinds of particularization that proceed only from one who loves indicate love. Or, particularization in general indicates will. Particularization through the bestowal of blessings, however, is a specific kind of particularization, and particularization through bringing near and choosing is a specific kind of bringing near. The same reasoning he followed to establish will applies to matters such as these.

Second: He should be told: Grant that reason does not indicate this; it can nevertheless deny it only through the same reasoning by which it would deny will. Revelation is an independent proof in its own right; indeed,

---

## Page 6939

it affords greater assurance in these difficult matters, and its evidence is more conclusive. Why, then, have you denied what it indicates, or suspended judgment, and reduced all these attributes to will, when the scriptural texts make no such distinction? Whatever argument he offers can be countered with a similar argument against his affirmation of will as something additional to the act itself.

Third: He should be told: If a Jahmī says to you, “Will means nothing but the absence of coercion, or the act itself and the command to perform it,” and claims that affirming a will entails an unacceptable consequence if it is held to be beginningless, and another if it is held to have come into being—here the Muʿtazilah fell into confusion. They do not affirm a beginningless will, since they regard a beginningless attribute as impossible; nor do they affirm that a new attribute arises in Him, since most of them regard temporal occurrences inhering in Him as impossible, despite their own inconsistencies.

They therefore split into two groups:

The Baghdad school, whose innovations concerning the divine attributes and divine decree were more extreme, denied the reality of will. Al-Jāhiz said that it means nothing but the absence of coercion. Al-Kaʿbī said that it means only the act itself when it relates to His own action, and only the command itself when it relates to His servants’ obedience.

The Basran school, including Abū ʿAlī and Abū Hāshim, said: “A will comes into being without inhering in a subject; thus there is no will.” They thereby committed themselves to something coming into being without being willed, and to an attribute subsisting without a subject,

---

## Page 6940

and both are self-evidently unsound to all rational people. His reply would be that affirming the attributes his opponent declares impossible is not impossible at all, and that both scripture and reason attest to them. If his opponent then begins disputing what scripture or reason indicates, he brands him a sophist or a Qarmatian. Yet precisely the same applies to mercy and love, for his opponents contest whether revelation and reason provide conclusive evidence for them.

His opponents should then be asked:

“By what have you established that He is All-Knowing and All-Powerful?”

Whatever evidence from revelation and reason they use to establish this also establishes will. Whatever specious objections they raise against it can be met with similar objections against His being All-Knowing and All-Powerful. When the discussion reaches the affirmation of real qualities and the claim that these entail temporal origination, or composition and dependence, the answer is what we have established elsewhere: this entails neither temporal origination nor any composition necessitating dependence upon something other than Him.

They can also be confronted with the false objections by which those who negate the divine attributes deny the divine essence itself. They must acknowledge the existence of the Lord, the Creator, which is known through the innate disposition with which people are created, necessary rational knowledge, conclusive rational proofs, the agreement of nations, and other evidence. They can then be challenged to establish an existence of the kind familiar to us, or an existence whose modality they know. They will inevitably have to resort to affirming something whose reality does not resemble other realities. Thus, what is said concerning all the other names and attributes by which He has described Himself follows the same principle as what is said concerning His very Self—glorified and exalted is He.

---

## Page 6941

And the “crux of this discussion” is that most of those who deny some things indicated by the Quran and Sunnah while affirming others necessarily affirm something because there is a warrant for affirming it and no impediment, and deny something because an impediment exists or a warrant is absent. Or they suspend judgment when, in their view, there is neither a warrant nor an impediment. It should therefore be shown to such a person that a warrant exists for what he denies, just as it exists for what he affirms—either in every respect or in a respect that requires its affirmation. If the warrant is valid in the latter case, it is likewise valid in the former; otherwise, rejecting that warrant is no different from rejecting this one.

As for the impediment, it should be shown that the impediment he imagines in what he denies is of the same kind as the impediment he imagines in what he affirms. If that impediment—the alleged impossibility—is present on both suppositions, he does not escape the objection by affirming one and denying the other. If it is valid, it rules out both; if it is invalid, it rules out neither. He must therefore treat both alike in affirmation and denial. Since denial is untenable, affirmation is the only remaining course.

This is the crux of the argument that binds anyone who affirms anything. And everyone must either affirm something or be obliged to affirm it. This shows you, in general terms, that the consequences he claims require denial are unfounded imaginings, even without knowing in detail why they are unsound. As for the detailed argument, it establishes the invalidity of the impediment and the existence of the warrant, as has been explained on several occasions.

---

## Page 6942

If someone who affirms those attributes which in us are accidents, such as life, knowledge, and power, but does not affirm those which in us are parts, such as the hand and foot, says, “These are components and parts that entail composition and corporeality,” he is told:

“Those, too, are accidents that entail corporeality and conceptual composition, just as, in your view, these entail composition perceptible to the senses.”

If he affirms those in a manner that does not make them accidents, or holds that calling them accidents does not preclude their affirmation, he is told:

“Then affirm these, too, in a manner that does not amount to composition and parts, or hold that calling them composition and parts does not preclude their affirmation.”

If it is said, “These can only be conceived of as components,” he is told, “Those can only be conceived of as accidents.”

If he says, “An accident is something that does not endure, whereas the Lord’s attributes endure,” the reply is:

“A part is something that can be separated from the whole, and that is impossible with respect to Allah. The separation of the eternal attributes from Allah Most High is absolutely impossible, whereas a created being’s accidents and parts may be separated from it.”

If he says, “That amounts to corporeality, and corporeality is ruled out,” the reply is: “This, too, amounts to corporeality, and corporeality is ruled out.”

---

## Page 6943

If he says, “I can conceive of an attribute that is not an accident, subsisting in an entity that does not occupy space, even though it has no counterpart in the observable world,” he is told:

“Then conceive of an attribute which, in us, is a part, subsisting in an entity that does not occupy space, even though it has no counterpart in the observable world. For denying that this is conceivable amounts to denying that the other is conceivable. Even if there is a certain difference between them, it has no bearing on the point at issue.”

This is why the Jahmiyyah, who negate the divine attributes, deny them all. Yet that, too, entails denying the divine Essence. Those among their counterparts who affirm these attributes known through revelation explicitly describe them as attributes subsisting in Him, like knowledge and power. This, too, is neither the intelligible meaning of the revealed text nor what reason indicates. It is only necessity that has driven them into these straits.

The root of this is that they introduced expressions found neither in the Book nor in the Sunnah—broad, undifferentiated expressions such as “occupying space,” “bounded,” “body,” “composite,” and the like. They denied what these expressions signify and made that denial a premise accepted among themselves, regarding it as established by a certain form of reasoning. They were led into that reasoning by an approach they adopted to establish the temporal origination of the world through the temporal origination of accidents, or to establish the contingency of a body through its composition from parts. They were therefore compelled to extend the argument for temporal origination and contingency to everything falling within its scope, since a conclusive proof cannot be set aside in favour of a stronger opposing argument.

They then found that this created difficulties for them, both in relation to the revealed texts and, from another angle, in relation to reason. Thus they split into factions. Some give precedence to the first argument and reject whatever opposes it: these are the Muʿtazilah. Others give precedence to the second argument and reject the first

---

## Page 6944

as did Hishām ibn al-Hakam, the Rāfidī. For it has been said that discussion of whether to deny or affirm that Allah is a body first arose in the time of Hishām ibn al-Hakam and Abū al-Hudhayl al-ʿAllāf. Abū al-Hudhayl and other early Muʿtazilah denied that He is a body because of the reasoning they followed, whereas Hishām opposed them and affirmed that He is a body on the basis of the reasoning they had adopted. The former considered its affirmation impossible, while he considered its denial impossible. At other times, they reconcile the revealed texts with their reasoning in a manner whose impossibility and contradiction are apparent.

I know of no champion of speculative theology or philosophy who departs from the Book and the Sunnah without inevitably contradicting himself: he declares impossible the like of what he has deemed necessary, and deems necessary the like of what he has declared impossible. This is because their discourse comes from other than Allah. Allah Most High has said:

“Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would have found in it much inconsistency.”

The correct position is that of the imams of guidance: to describe Allah as He has described Himself, or as His Messenger has described Him, without going beyond the Quran and hadith, and to follow in this the path of the righteous predecessors, the people of knowledge and faith. The meanings understood from the Book and the Sunnah must not be rejected on the strength of specious objections, for that would amount to distorting words from their proper places. Nor should one turn away from those meanings and thus become like those who, when reminded of their Lord’s signs, respond to them with deafness and blindness. Nor should one abandon reflection upon the Quran and thus become like those who know nothing of the Scripture except wishful notions.

This, then, is the first of the two arguments: denying that these passages belong to those whose meaning is ambiguous.

---

## Page 6945

The second argument:

If it is said that these passages are ambiguous, or that some of them are—as it is reported that some of the imams described certain passages cited by the Jahmiyyah as ambiguous—the response is this: the Quran’s statement that none but Allah knows its interpretation refers either to the ambiguous passages or to the entire Book, as explained earlier. Denying knowledge of its interpretation does not mean denying knowledge of its meaning, as we have already explained concerning the Resurrection and its events.

This argument is strong if Ibn Ishāq’s report concerning the delegation of Najrān is authentic: that they argued against the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, by citing the expressions “Indeed, We,” “We,” and the like. It is also supported by the established fact that the Quran contains ambiguous passages—that is, passages admitting two meanings. Some matters concerning the divine attributes fall into this category, just as matters concerning the Resurrection do—and all the more so. For the dissimilarity between Allah and His creation is far greater than that between what is promised in Paradise and what exists in this world.

The essential point of the answer, however, is what we stated at the outset: denying knowledge of the interpretation does not mean denying knowledge of the meaning. We further substantiate this by noting that Allah, glorified is He, says:

“We have certainly presented people with every kind of example in this Quran, so that they may take heed—an Arabic Quran, free from any crookedness, so that they may be mindful of Allah.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Alif Lām Rā. These are the verses of the clear Book. Indeed, We have sent it down as an Arabic Quran so that you may understand.”

Thus, He states that He sent it down for them to understand, and that He sought for them to take heed.

He also says:

“These examples We present to people so that they may reflect.”

---

## Page 6946

Thus He urged them to ponder it, comprehend it, understand it, take heed through it, and reflect upon it, without excluding any part of it. Indeed, several passages explicitly establish that this applies to the whole Quran, such as His words:

“Do they not ponder the Quran, or are there locks upon their hearts?”

And His words:

“Do they not ponder the Quran? Had it been from anyone other than Allah, they would surely have found many contradictions in it.”

Clearly, ruling out contradictions within it requires pondering it in its entirety. Pondering only part of it does not warrant concluding that the unexamined portions contain nothing that contradicts what one has examined.

When ‘Alī, may Allah be pleased with him, was asked, “Did the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, leave anything with you?” he replied:

“No, by Him who split the seed and created the soul, except an understanding of His Book that Allah grants a servant, and what is in this document.”

He thus indicated that understanding of the Quran varies within the Muslim community. Understanding is more specific than knowledge and judgment. Allah Most High says:

“We gave Solomon understanding of the matter, and to each We gave judgment and knowledge.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Many a transmitter understands better than a hearer.”

He also said:

“Convey from me, even if only a single verse.”

Furthermore, the early generations—the Companions, the Successors, and the rest of the Muslim community—discussed all the passages of the Quran, including the verses concerning the divine attributes and others. They explained them in accordance with what they signify and make clear, and narrated many hadiths from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that accord with the Quran. The leading Companions were foremost in this regard, such as ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd, who used to say:

“If I knew of anyone more knowledgeable about Allah’s Book than myself whom I could reach by camel, I would go to him.”

And ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās, for whom the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed

---

## Page 6947

and who was the great scholar of the Muslim community and the interpreter of the Quran—these two, together with their students, were among the Companions and Successors who most firmly affirmed the divine attributes and most extensively transmitted reports concerning them from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Anyone acquainted with hadith and Quranic commentary knows this. Among the Successors, none were more distinguished than the students of these two eminent men. Indeed, the third alongside them, with regard to the foremost Successors, is of their kind or close to them. Comparable to their stature is that of the students of Zayd ibn Thābit. Yet, despite their distinction, his students did not study exclusively under him; they also learned from others, such as ‘Umar, Ibn ‘Umar, and Ibn ‘Abbās.

If the meanings of these verses were to be denied or left unspoken, the godly scholars among the Companions, those versed in the Book and the Sunnah, would not have been the ones who spoke most extensively about them. Moreover, the Companions reported that they learned the explanation of the Quran from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, along with its recitation. Not one of them ever reported that he declined to explain any verse.

Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī said:

“Those who taught us the Quran—‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd, and others—told us that whenever they learned ten verses from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they would not proceed beyond them until they had learned the knowledge and practice they contained. They said, ‘Thus we learned the Quran, knowledge, and practice together.’”

Likewise, when the leading scholars were asked about any of these matters, they did not deny its meaning. Rather, they affirmed the meaning while disclaiming knowledge of how, as in the statement of Mālik ibn Anas when he was asked about His words,

---

## Page 6948

exalted is He:

“The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.”

“How did He rise over it?” He replied: “Rising over is known; how is unknown. Believing in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation.” Rabī‘ah had said the same before him. People have accepted this statement, and no one among Ahl al-Sunnah rejects it. He made clear that rising over is known, just as everything else Allah has told us is known. Its manner, however, is unknown, and it is not permissible to ask about it: one must not ask, “How did He rise over it?” Mālik did not say that there is no manner; rather, he said that the manner is unknown.

There is disagreement about this among our associates and other scholars of Ahl al-Sunnah. Most of them, however, say: “His manner of being cannot be conceived by the mind, nor can His essential nature be expressed in words.” Some say: “He has neither a manner of being nor an essential nature.”

If it is said that his statement, “Rising over is known,” means that the occurrence of this wording in the Quran is known—as some of our associates have maintained, regarding knowledge of these meanings as part of the interpretation whose knowledge Allah has reserved to Himself—the reply is:

This is a weak explanation, for it merely states what is already known. The questioner already knew that this occurs in the Quran and had recited the verse.

Moreover, he did not say, “The mention of rising over in the Quran,” or, “Allah’s declaration that He rose over.” Rather, he said:

---

## Page 6949

“Rising over is known.” He thus described the individual term as known, not the entire sentence.
