Moreover, he said: “How is unknown.” Had he meant that, he would have said, “The meaning of rising over is unknown,” “The explanation of rising over is unknown,” or “What rising over signifies is not known.” He denied only knowledge of how He rose over, not knowledge of rising over itself.

The same applies to everything with which Allah has described Himself. If someone asked, concerning His words:

“Indeed, I am with you both, hearing and seeing,”

“How does He hear, and how does He see?” we would reply: “Hearing and seeing are known; how is unknown.” If someone asked, “How did He speak directly to Moses?” we would reply: “Speaking is known; how is unknown.”

Furthermore, those among our associates and other scholars of Ahl al-Sunnah who have said this acknowledge that Allah is truly above the Throne, and that He Himself is above the Throne itself. They do not deny the meaning of rising over, nor do they consider it among the ambiguous passages whose meaning is entirely unknown. Moreover, the early generations agree on explaining it in accordance with the position of Ahl al-Sunnah.

Some of them said: “He rose above the Throne,” and “He was high above the Throne.” Others used different expressions. These explanations are reliably transmitted from the early generations; al-Bukhārī mentioned some of them in his Sahīh, towards the end of the Book of the Refutation of the Jahmiyyah. As for the interpretations that distort the meaning, such as “He took possession,” and the like, these are innovated interpretations introduced when the Jahmiyyah emerged.

---

## Page 6950

Moreover, it is established that pursuing ambiguous passages is not confined to passages concerning the divine attributes. Indeed, Sahīh al-Bukhārī records that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Aisha:

“O Aisha, when you see those who pursue what is ambiguous in it, those are the ones Allah has named, so beware of them.”

This is general in scope. The account of Sabīgh ibn ‘Asal and Umar ibn al-Khattāb is among the best-known cases. News had reached Umar that Sabīgh was asking about the ambiguous passages of the Quran. When Umar eventually met him, Sabīgh asked him about:

“By the winds that scatter far and wide.”

Umar said, “What is your name?” He replied, “Allah’s servant, Sabīgh.” Umar said, “And I am Allah’s servant, Umar,” and beat him severely.

Whenever someone persistently questioned Ibn Abbas about a matter of this kind, he would say, “How much you need to be treated as Umar treated Sabīgh!” This was because they saw that the questioner’s purpose was to stir up discord, not to seek guidance and understanding. As the Prophet, blessings and peace be upon him, said:

“When you see those who pursue what is ambiguous in it.”

And as Allah Most High said:

“As for those in whose hearts is deviation, they pursue what is ambiguous in it, seeking discord.”

They therefore punished them for this corrupt intention, as in the case of someone who sets verses of the Quran against one another. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade this, saying:

“Do not set parts of Allah’s Book against one another.”

For that casts doubt into their hearts. Along with seeking discord, they sought its interpretation, which none but Allah knows. Their purpose was therefore blameworthy, and what they sought was unattainable—as with the trick questions that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade.

---

## Page 6951

Another illustration of the distinction between “meaning” and “interpretation” is that Sabīgh asked Umar about “those that scatter,” which is not a passage concerning the divine attributes. The Companions discussed its explanation. For example, when Ibn al-Kawwā’ asked Ali ibn Abī Tālib about it, Ali disliked his question because of the intention he perceived behind it. Ali’s subjects, however, were defiant toward him; he did not command the obedience Umar had enjoyed, which would have enabled him to discipline the questioner.

“Those that scatter,” “those that carry,” “those that glide,” and “those that apportion” contain ambiguity, because the wording could refer to winds, clouds, stars, angels, or other things: it does not name the things being described. The interpretation known only to Allah concerns the particular winds, their measure and characteristics, and when they blow; the particular clouds, the rain they carry, and when the rain falls. The same applies to “those that glide” and “those that apportion.” This is known only to Allah.

The same applies to His words “Indeed, We” and “We,” and similar designations of Allah that convey a plural sense—expressions such as the Christians pursued. Their referent is known: Allah, glorified is He. Yet a plural designation indicates a multiplicity of meanings, just as multiple names do, such as the All-Knowing, the All-Powerful, the All-Hearing, and the All-Seeing. The One named is one, while the meanings of the names are multiple. The same is true of a designation expressed in the plural.

As for the interpretation whose knowledge Allah has reserved to Himself, it is the reality of His Essence and attributes, as Mālik said: “The manner is unknown.” Thus, if they ask, “What is the true nature of His knowledge, power, hearing, and sight?” the answer is: “This is the interpretation known only to Allah.”

---

## Page 6952

How fitting it is to understand interpretation as referring to the Quran as a whole.

If it is objected, “But the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prayed for Ibn Abbās, ‘O Allah, grant him understanding of the religion and teach him interpretation,’” the answer is: He can know the interpretation of commands and prohibitions. The definite article here refers to the interpretation in question; the Prophet did not say, “the interpretation of the entire Quran.” The interpretation whose knowledge is denied to people concerns reports whose underlying reality is known only to Allah. The interpretation that can be known concerns matters whose interpretation people know. This is like His words:

“Do they await anything but its fulfillment? The Day its fulfillment comes…”

And His words:

“Rather, they have denied what they do not encompass in knowledge and whose fulfillment has not yet come to them.”

What is meant is the interpretation of reports concerning the future, for that is what is “awaited,” what “comes,” and what “has not yet come to them.” As for the interpretation of commands and prohibitions, that pertains to what is commanded. The interpretation of reports about Allah and those who lived in the past—if included under the term “interpretation”—is not something to be awaited.

Allah, glorified is He, knows best, and success comes through Him.

---

## Page 6953

And the shaykh, imam, erudite scholar, exemplary guide, man of spiritual insight, jurist, hadith master, ascetic, worshipper, spiritual wayfarer, devout servant, mufti to the various groups, pillar of the Sharia, scholar of his age, peerless figure of his time, interpreter of the Quran, heir to the prophets, last of the jurists qualified for independent legal reasoning, Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-Abbās Ahmad ibn Abd al-Halīm ibn Abd al-Salām ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī—may Allah envelop him in His mercy—said:

Section: Oaths in the Quran

He, glorified is He, swears by certain things to affirm others. He swears only by His holy Self, characterized by His attributes, or by His signs, which necessarily point to His essence and attributes. His swearing by certain created things indicates that they are among His mighty signs.

---

## Page 6954

An oath, then, may affirm a declarative statement—which is most often the case—as in His saying, exalted is He:

“So by the Lord of heaven and earth, it is indeed true.”

Or it may affirm a non-declarative utterance, as in His saying, exalted is He:

“So by your Lord, We will surely question them all about what they used to do.”

Such an oath may, however, be intended to establish the truth of what is sworn to, in which case it is declarative; or it may be intended simply as an oath.

The purpose of an oath is to emphasize and establish what is sworn to. Its subject must therefore be something for which such emphasis and confirmation are appropriate, such as unseen or hidden matters whose reality is affirmed by an oath. As for visible, manifest things, such as the sun, the moon, night, day, heaven and earth, these are things He swears by, not things He swears to affirm. Whatever the Lord, mighty and majestic, swears to affirm is among His signs, and may therefore itself be sworn by; the converse does not hold.

He, glorified is He, sometimes states the clause expressing what the oath affirms—as is most often the case—and sometimes omits it, just as the result clause after “if” is often omitted. Examples include His saying, exalted is He:

“If you knew with certain knowledge”;

and His sayings:

“And if there were a Quran by which mountains could be moved”;

“If only you could see when the angels take the disbelievers in death”;

“If only you could see when they are seized by terror, with no escape”;

“If only you could see when they are made to stand before the Fire”;

“If only you could see when they are made to stand before their Lord.”

Omitting the result clause in such cases is among the finest forms of expression, for what is meant is that, if you were to see it

## Page 6955

you would see a tremendous horror; explicitly stating the result clause therefore adds nothing to what is indicated . . . (1)

the inviolable—and this too draws attention to a point. If He swears by it when it contains what is lawful, then it is all the more worthy of veneration when it contains what is inviolable. Likewise, if residence is intended, it is the negative sense, so the meaning is the same. [He has sworn by “the fig and the olive” and “this secure city.” The clause expressing what the oath affirms is stated in His saying, exalted is He:

“We have certainly created man in hardship.”

This means struggling with the affairs of this world and the Hereafter.] (*)

Such struggle entails strength on the part of the one engaged in it, together with much activity and resourcefulness. He therefore says, exalted is He:

“Does he think that no one will ever have power over him?”

“He says, ‘I have squandered vast wealth.’”

“Does he think that no one has seen him?”

This person is of the same kind as those nations, and of the same kind as the one who said:

“My wealth has availed me nothing. My authority has vanished from me.”

He possesses strength with which to contend with difficulties, yet He destroyed every one of them. Does he nevertheless think that no one will have power over him and requite him for his deeds? Does he think that no one has seen the wealth he squandered and thus knows what he has done?

Requital is accomplished through power and knowledge; indeed, everything is accomplished through them. His declaration, exalted is He, that He is powerful and knowing carries a warning and a threat. For if He has power, requital is possible; and if He has knowledge, requital is possible: He assesses what a person has done with justice. Anyone who lacks power and knowledge cannot requite, for someone who has no power over a person cannot

---

## Page 6956

requite him. Someone who has power but does not see what the person has done would be unjust and transgressive if he requited him without knowledge. He must therefore know what that person has done. This is why a judge needs witnesses, and kings need administrative officials to inform them of the amounts of wealth and other matters, so that they may act with knowledge. . . . (1)

He states that He created man in hardship:

“Does he think that no one will ever have power over him?”

The particle lan negates a future occurrence. He is saying: Does he think that no one will have power over him in the future? This is why that man who feared his Lord and instructed his family to burn him and scatter his ashes knew that requital depended on power. He said:

“If Allah has power over me, He will surely punish me as He has punished no one in all the worlds.”

He, glorified is He, warns by mentioning His power because what He has power to do is connected with that power, just as He warns by mentioning His knowledge because requital takes place in conjunction with it. This occurs in His saying, exalted is He:

“Say, ‘He has power to send punishment upon you from above you or from beneath your feet.’”

When this was revealed, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “I seek refuge in Your Face; I seek refuge in Your Face.”

“Or to divide you into factions and make you taste one another’s violence.”

He said: “These two are less severe.”

This is because He mentioned His power and the kind of thing He has power to do, just as someone might say: “Where can you flee from me? I have the power to seize you.”

The same applies to knowledge through seeing, as in His saying here:

“Does he think that no one has seen him?”

## Page 6957

and His saying, exalted is He, concerning the one who forbids a servant when he prays:

“Does he not know that Allah sees?”

And His saying, exalted is He:

“Say, ‘Work, for Allah will see your deeds, as will His Messenger and the believers.’”

And His saying:

“Or do they think that We do not hear their secrets and private conversations? Indeed We do, and Our messengers are with them, recording.”

And His saying, exalted is He:

“Everything they have done is in the records.”

“And everything small and great is written down.”

There are other similar passages.

Mentioning that He sees deeds, knows them and keeps account of them entails a warning of requital for them. It is like someone saying, “I know what you have done, and reports of everything you have done have reached me,” and similar expressions. The purpose is not to speak merely of power or knowledge in isolation, but of power and knowledge associated with requital. Once knowledge and power are present, requital is possible and depends only on the will of the one who requites; nothing else is then needed. One must therefore seek salvation by asking His forgiveness, repenting to Him and doing good deeds that erase bad deeds.

Section

When He, glorified and exalted is He, swore by “those ranged in ranks,” “those that scatter” and “those sent forth,” He stated what the oath affirmed. He said, exalted is He:

“Indeed, your God is One.”

And He said, exalted is He:

“Indeed, what you are promised is true.”

“And Judgment will surely come to pass.”

And He said, exalted is He:

“Indeed, what you are promised will surely come to pass.”

He did not state what the oath affirmed in al-Nāzi'āt; for those ranged in ranks are the angels, and He did not swear to establish their existence, just as He did not swear to establish His own existence;

---

## Page 6958

since the nations acknowledged those ranged in ranks, and knowledge of Him was manifest among them, requiring no oath. His oneness, however, was otherwise, for, as He, exalted is He, said:

“Most of them do not believe in Allah without associating others with Him.”

Likewise, the nations generally acknowledge the angels. Allah mentioned that the people of Noah, ‘Ād, Thamūd and Pharaoh knew of the angels despite their associating partners with Him and rejecting the messengers.

The people of Noah said:

“This is only a man like yourselves who wishes to assert his superiority over you. Had Allah willed, He would have sent down angels.”

He, exalted is He, said:

“I have warned you of a thunderbolt like the thunderbolt that struck ‘Ād and Thamūd.”

“When the messengers came to them from before them and from behind them, saying, ‘Worship none but Allah,’ they said, ‘Had our Lord willed, He would have sent down angels.’”

Pharaoh said:

“Or am I not better than this one who is insignificant and can hardly express himself?”

“Then why have bracelets of gold not been bestowed upon him, or angels come with him in attendance?”

The same applies to the Arab polytheists. He, exalted is He, said:

“They say, ‘Why has an angel not been sent down to him?’ Had We sent down an angel, the matter would have been decided, and they would then have been granted no respite.”

He, exalted is He, said:

“They say, ‘What sort of messenger is this who eats food and walks in the markets? Why has an angel not been sent down to him to be a warner alongside him?’”

He, exalted is He, said of the nations in general:

“Nothing prevented people from believing when guidance came to them except that they said, ‘Has Allah sent a human being as a messenger?’”

“Say, ‘Had there been angels on earth, walking about at ease, We would have sent down to them from heaven an angel as a messenger.’”

---

## Page 6959

Thus, these nations, which rejected the messengers and associated partners with their Lord, acknowledged Allah and His angels. How much more, then, would others do so? It is therefore clear that acknowledgment of the Lord and His angels is widespread among the nations. For this reason, He did not swear an oath to affirm it; rather, He swore to affirm His oneness, because most of them associate partners with Him.

Likewise, “those that scatter,” “those that bear,” and “those that sail” are things people witness, while “those who apportion affairs” are the angels. Thus, what He swore by did not itself include what He was affirming by the oath, so He stated what the oath affirmed. He, exalted is He, said:

“What you are promised is surely true, and judgment will surely come to pass.”

As for “those sent forth”—whether they are the angels descending with revelation, with the oath affirming recompense in the Hereafter, or the winds, or both—they too are known. As for “those who wrench out forcefully,” they are the angels who take souls. This entails recompense, which is among the greatest matters affirmed by these oaths.

He, exalted is He, said:

“Say, ‘The Angel of Death, who has been entrusted with you, will take your souls; then to your Lord you will be returned.’”

And He, exalted is He, said:

“Our messengers take his soul, and they never neglect their duty.”

“Then they are returned to Allah, their true Master.”

… (1) He, and none but He helps one worship Him. This certainty gives rise to seeking His help and placing one’s trust in Him,

> (1) An omission in the original.

---

## Page 6960

and this is certainty concerning what has been decreed but has not yet occurred, for seeking help and placing one’s trust in Allah concern only the future. As for what has already occurred, the response is patience, submission, and contentment, as in the hadith narrated by ‘Ammār ibn Yāsir, may Allah be pleased with him, and traced back to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“I ask You for contentment after Your decree has come to pass.”

Saying, “There is no might nor power except through Allah,” brings His help. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prescribed it in response to the call to prayer. When the caller says, “Come to prayer,” the respondent says, “There is no might nor power except through Allah.” When he says, “Come to success,” the respondent says, “There is no might nor power except through Allah.”

The believer said to his companion:

“Why did you not say, when you entered your garden, ‘What Allah wills comes to pass; there is no power except through Allah’?”

For this reason, whoever fears the evil eye affecting something is instructed to say this. The expression “What Allah wills” means “What Allah wills comes to pass.” Thus, one does not consider oneself secure, but rather believes in divine decree and says, “There is no power except through Allah.”

In the hadith narrated by Abū Mūsā al-Ash‘arī, may Allah be pleased with him, and recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“It is a treasure from among the treasures of Paradise.”

A “treasure” is wealth already amassed that needs no gathering. This is because these words express reliance upon Allah Most High and utter need of Him. It is well known that nothing occurs except by Allah’s will and power, and that nothing comes from created beings except what Allah brings about in them. Thus, when the heart ceases seeking

---

## Page 6961

help from them and seeks it from Allah, it has sought it from the Creator of that help, who alone can provide it.

Allah Most High says:

“Whatever mercy Allah grants to people, none can withhold; and whatever He withholds, none can release thereafter.”

And He says:

“If Allah touches you with harm, none can remove it except Him; and if He intends good for you, none can repel His bounty.”

And He says:

“If He touches you with good, He has power over all things.”

And He says:

“Say, ‘Have you considered those you invoke besides Allah? If Allah intended harm for me, could they remove His harm? Or if He intended mercy for me, could they withhold His mercy?’”

The man mentioned in Surah Yā-Sīn said:

“Shall I take gods besides Him? If the Most Merciful intends harm for me, their intercession will avail me nothing, nor can they save me. I would then be in manifest error.”

For this reason, Allah commands people in numerous passages to place their trust in Him alone. A report states:

“Whoever wishes to be the strongest of people, let him place his trust in Allah; and whoever wishes to be the richest of people, let him have greater confidence in what is in Allah’s hand than in what is in his own.”

Allah Most High says:

“Place your trust in the Ever-Living, who does not die, and glorify Him with praise. Sufficient is He as One fully aware of His servants’ sins.”

Allah Most High has commanded that He be worshipped and trusted. He says:

“So worship Him and place your trust in Him.”

And He says:

“Say, ‘He is my Lord; there is no god but Him. In Him I have placed my trust, and to Him I turn in repentance.’”

Moses said:

“O my people, if you have believed in Allah, then place your trust in Him, if you are Muslims.”

---

## Page 6962

And Shuayb said:

“My success is only through Allah. In Him I have placed my trust, and to Him I turn in repentance.”

The believers said:

“Our Lord, in You we have placed our trust, to You we turn in repentance, and to You is the final return.”

Allah Most High says:

“Remember the name of your Lord and devote yourself wholeheartedly to Him. He is the Lord of the East and the West; there is no god but Him, so take Him as your Trustee.”

And He says:

“Whoever fears Allah, He will make a way out for him and provide for him from where he does not expect. Whoever places his trust in Allah, He is sufficient for him. Allah will surely accomplish His purpose. Allah has set a measure for everything.”

In this regard, people fall into four groups:

One group neither worships Him nor places its trust in Him. These are the worst of creation.

Another group seeks to worship Him by doing what He has commanded and refraining from what He has forbidden, but they have not fully realized trust in Him and dependence on His help. They are therefore unable to accomplish much of what they seek and lose heart in many afflictions.

Among these are those who deny divine decree and regard themselves as the originators of their own actions. In reality, they neither seek His help nor ask Him to reform their hearts, set them straight, or guide them. These people are forsaken, just as the Muslim community regards them.

Others affirm divine decree in both word and belief, yet this belief has not taken hold of their hearts in knowledge and practice as has their intention to perform purification and prayer. These too are weak and helpless.

---

## Page 6963

Another group focuses on divine power and will, and on the fact that Allah Most High is the One who gives and withholds, lowers and raises. Their predominant concern is therefore to turn to Him in this respect, seeking His help and expressing their need of Him to obtain what they desire. Such a person may acquire a measure of authority and power, outward or inward, overcome his enemy—even kill him—and achieve his aims. Yet no good end awaits them, for the good end belongs to piety; rather, their fate in the Hereafter is evil. We are not speaking here of disbelievers and wrongdoers who turn away from Allah, for these fall within the first category: those who neither worship Him nor seek His help.

Rather, we are speaking of people who turn towards Allah with worshipful devotion and possess some measure of fear, remembrance and asceticism. Yet their turning towards Him is governed predominantly by their own desires, spiritual experiences and ecstasies, and by what they find pleasing and desirable, rather than by the commands of the revealed Law. They fall into several groups:

Among them are those who turn away from observing the prescribed acts of worship, while receiving from devils disclosures of hidden matters or powers of influence. Many of these people die outside Islam. Others perform the outward acts of worship prescribed by the revealed Law, such as prayer, fasting and pilgrimage, and refrain from forbidden things. Yet in the deeds of the heart they do not adhere to the commands of the revealed Law; instead, they pursue what they themselves love and desire. Allah Most High says:

“To all We give aid—to these and to those

---

## Page 6964

from your Lord’s bounty.” He, glorified be He, grants authority and wealth to the righteous and the wicked alike. One of these people may therefore be granted powers of influence, whether to subdue his enemy or support his ally, just as kings are granted such powers. He may also be granted some disclosure of hidden matters, either through information conveyed to him by some jinn—whether or not he knows that they are jinn—or by other means.

One of these people may say, “I receive from Allah, while others receive from Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” His own state in this regard and what sets him apart lead him to see that the powers of influence and disclosures granted to him come through a path other than that of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. In this he speaks the truth. Yet these things are, in reality, a source of ruin for him.

For if someone acts without the command of the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and takes what the Messenger has not permitted him; appoints and dismisses, gives and withholds without the Messenger’s command; kills and strikes without his command; honours and humiliates without his command; and receives an inward address containing commands and prohibitions, believing that Allah has commanded and forbidden him without the Messenger’s mediation—then this entire state of his comes from Satan. It is Satan who commands and forbids him: Satan commands him, and he acts, imagining that he is acting by Allah’s command.

By my life, he does act by Allah’s command governing creation and destiny, through the intermediary of Satan’s command, as Allah Most High says concerning sorcerers:

“They cannot harm anyone through it except by Allah’s permission.”

Likewise, the believer acts by Allah’s command governing creation and destiny, but through the intermediary of the command of the Messenger, who conveys it to him from Allah, Mighty and Majestic.

---

## Page 6965

Thus, for him, what is lawful is what Allah and His Messenger have made lawful; what is forbidden is what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden; and religion is what Allah and His Messenger have prescribed. The other person, by contrast, does not look to the Messenger for the commands and prohibitions he receives inwardly, or for what he does and commands others to do.

This type is common among shaykhs concerned with the heart and spiritual states whose knowledge of the Quran and Sunnah and adherence to the Messenger are weak. They are dominated by what they find in their hearts and the commands they receive inwardly, whether these agree with the Messenger or contradict him.

They vary in this according to their closeness to the Messenger or their distance from him. Many have strayed so far from him that they see fit to help the unbelievers fight the Muslims, believing that Allah, glorified be He, has commanded them to do so. They also believe that Ahl al-Suffa (the People of the Portico) did this. Some hold that the Messenger was not sent to them and their like, but only to ordinary people. Others believe that the Messenger was subordinate to Ahl al-Suffa, while they had no need of him. There are other such types, who have become numerous in these times.

---

## Page 6966

All these people claim knowledge of spiritual reality, saying: “Spiritual reality is one thing, and the revealed Law is another.” They share two characteristics: they possess powers and spiritual unveilings beyond those of ordinary people, and they refuse to weigh these against the Quran and Sunnah or submit them to the Messenger’s judgment.

They are like kings who govern their kingdoms without following the commands of Allah and His Messenger. Yet none of these kings says, “Allah commanded me to do this,” or “I am an ally of Allah,” or “I receive spiritual support from Allah independently of the Messenger,” or “The messengers were not sent to people like me.” Kings merely pursue their own ends without making them a religion. These people, however, turn their own ends—which involve some of the gravest injustice and corruption, indeed even unbelief—into a religion practised by those whom they regard as Allah’s allies.

This is because these things come to them through a kind of asceticism and worship. Yet it is not the asceticism and worship with which Allah sent His Messenger. Rather, it resembles the condition of the People of the Book and the polytheists, such as the devotees of India, the Christians, and others like them. Thus their resemblance to the devotees among the polytheists and the People of the Book becomes apparent. Indeed, someone who had seen the devotees of India and then saw the ecstatic devotees of the house of al-Rifā'ī found their presence in Muslim lands objectionable. He said: “These are exactly like the pagan devotees of India.”

And higher than

---

## Page 6967

these are those who resemble Christian devotees and monks in many matters outside the law of Islam. Since they practised an innovated religion akin to that of the polytheists and the People of the Book, they believed what those people believe: that this is a sound religion, that it brings one closer to Allah, and that its adherents are Allah’s allies. For all groups of scholars and devotees among the followers of every religion believe (1).
