---

## Page 6968

Shaykh al-Islām said:

In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful.

My Lord, make this easy and help me by Your mercy.

All praise is due to Allah. We seek His help and forgiveness, and we seek refuge in Allah from the evil within ourselves and from our evil deeds. Whoever Allah guides, none can lead astray, and whoever He leads astray, none can guide. I bear witness that none has the right to be worshipped except Allah alone, without partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May Allah bless him and grant him peace.

To proceed: One of the brothers asked me to write him an introduction setting out general principles that would help him understand the Quran, its interpretation and its meanings; distinguish truth from the various kinds of falsehood in both transmitted reports and reasoned explanations concerning it; and identify the decisive evidence by which to judge between differing views. For books of Quranic commentary are filled with both the worthless and the valuable, blatant falsehood and manifest truth. Knowledge consists either of an authenticated report from one protected from error or of a statement supported by established evidence,

---

## Page 6969

and anything beyond these is either spurious and rejected or left undecided, its soundness or falsity unknown.

The Muslim community urgently needs to understand the Quran, which is Allah’s firm rope, the wise reminder, and the straight path. Through it, desires do not lead astray and tongues do not become confused. It never wears out through repeated recitation, its wonders never cease, and scholars never have their fill of it. Whoever speaks according to it speaks the truth; whoever acts upon it is rewarded; whoever judges by it judges justly; and whoever calls to it is guided to a straight path. Any tyrant who abandons it, Allah will crush; and whoever seeks guidance elsewhere, Allah will lead astray.

Allah Most High says:

“Then if guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray nor fall into misery. But whoever turns away from My remembrance will have a life of hardship, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection. He will say, ‘My Lord, why have You raised me blind when I used to see?’ He will say, ‘Just as Our signs came to you and you forgot them, so today you will be forgotten.’”

Allah Most High also says:

“There has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book, through which Allah guides those who seek His pleasure to the ways of peace, brings them out of darkness into light by His permission, and guides them to a straight path.”

Allah Most High also says:

“Alif Lām Rā. This is a Book We have sent down to you so that you may bring people out of darkness into light, by their Lord’s permission, to the path of the Almighty, the Praiseworthy—Allah, to whom belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth.”

Allah Most High also says:

“And thus We have revealed to you an inspiration by Our command. You did not know what the Book was, nor faith, but We have made it a light by which We guide whomever We will among

---

## Page 6970

Our servants. And indeed, you guide to a straight path—the path of Allah, to whom belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on earth. Truly, all matters return to Allah.”

I have written this brief introduction as Allah Most High has enabled me, setting down what my heart dictated. Allah is the Guide to the path of right guidance.

Section

It must be understood that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, explained the meanings of the Quran to his Companions just as he taught them its wording. Allah Most High’s words,

“So that you may explain to people what has been sent down to them,”

embrace both.

Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī said: “Those who taught us the Quran, such as ‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd, and others, told us that when they learned ten verses from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they would not go beyond them until they had learned the knowledge and practice they contained. They said, ‘Thus we learned the Quran, knowledge, and practice together.’”

For this reason, they would spend a considerable time memorizing a surah. Anas said: “When a man had read al-Baqarah and Āl ‘Imrān, he rose in our esteem.” Ibn ‘Umar spent several years memorizing al-Baqarah—eight years, it is said—as Mālik related.

This was because Allah Most High said:

“This is a blessed Book which We have sent down to you so that they may reflect upon

---

## Page 6971

its verses.”

He also said:

“Then do they not reflect upon the Quran?”

And He said:

“Then have they not reflected upon the Word?”

Reflecting upon speech is impossible without understanding its meanings. Likewise, Allah Most High said:

“Indeed, We have sent it down as an Arabic Quran so that you may understand.”

Grasping speech necessarily entails understanding it. It is well known that the purpose of all speech is to understand its meanings, not merely its wording; this applies all the more to the Quran. Moreover, ordinary practice makes it inconceivable that people would read a book in a field of knowledge, such as medicine or arithmetic, without seeking an explanation of it. How much more so with the speech of Allah, which safeguards them and upon which their salvation, happiness, and well-being in both their religious and worldly affairs depend?

For this reason, disagreement among the Companions over the interpretation of the Quran was very rare. Although it was more common among the Successors than among the Companions, it was still slight compared with that of later generations. The more virtuous the age, the greater its unity, harmony, knowledge, and clarity of explanation.

Some of the Successors received the entire explanation of the Quran from the Companions. As Mujāhid said: “I went through the Quran with Ibn ‘Abbās, stopping him at each verse and asking him about it.” For this reason, al-Thawrī said: “If an interpretation comes to you from Mujāhid, that is sufficient for you.” Thus al-Shāfi‘ī, al-Bukhārī, and other scholars rely on his interpretation. Likewise, Imam Ahmad and others who compiled works of Quranic interpretation cite chains of transmission from Mujāhid more frequently than from anyone else.

The point is that the Successors received Quranic interpretation from the Companions just as they received from them knowledge of

---

## Page 6972

the Sunnah, although they sometimes addressed matters of interpretation through deduction and inference, just as they addressed certain matters of the Sunnah through deduction and inference.

Section

Disagreement among the early generations over Quranic interpretation was slight, and their disagreements over legal rulings were more numerous than their disagreements over interpretation. Most disagreements authentically reported from them involve variation rather than contradiction. This is of two kinds:

The first is when each expresses the intended meaning in different words, indicating a different aspect of the same referent. This resembles co-referential names, which fall between synonyms and terms with distinct referents—as with the names given to a sword: “the keen blade” and “the Indian blade.” Such are the Most Beautiful Names of Allah, the names of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the names of the Quran. All of Allah’s names refer to the same One, so invoking Him by one of His Most Beautiful Names does not contradict invoking Him by another. Rather, it is as the Most High says:

“Say, ‘Call upon Allah or call upon the Most Merciful. Whichever name you call Him by, His are the Most Beautiful Names.’”

Each of His names denotes the Essence so named and the attribute that

---

## Page 6973

the name contains.

Thus, the All-Knowing denotes the Essence and knowledge; the All-Powerful denotes the Essence and power; and the Most Merciful denotes the Essence and mercy. Whoever denies that His names signify His attributes, while claiming to uphold the apparent meaning, speaks in a manner akin to the extremist Qarmatian esotericists, who say: “One must say neither that He is living nor that He is not living.” Rather, they deny both contradictory alternatives concerning Him. These Qarmatian esotericists do not reject a name that merely identifies, as pronouns do; they reject the positive attributes contained in His Most Beautiful Names. Whoever agrees with their objective thus agrees with the extremist esotericists in this respect, despite claiming strict adherence to the apparent meaning. This is not the place to elaborate on that. The point is simply that each of His names denotes His Essence and the attributes it contains.

It also denotes, by necessary implication, the attribute contained in another name. The same applies to the names of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, such as Muhammad, Ahmad, al-Māhī, al-Hāshir, and al-ʿĀqib; and likewise to the names of the Quran, such as the Quran, the Criterion, the Guidance, the Healing, the Clear Exposition, the Book, and others of this kind.

If the questioner’s purpose is to identify what is named, we may refer to it by any name, provided that the referent of that name is known. The name may be a proper name or a description. For example, someone might ask about His words:

“And whoever turns away from My remembrance”

“What is meant by His remembrance?” He may be told, for example: “It is the Quran,” or: “It is the Books He has revealed.” For “remembrance” is a verbal noun, and a verbal noun is sometimes grammatically linked to the one performing the action and sometimes to its object.

---

## Page 6974

Thus, when “remembrance of Allah” is used in the second sense, it means the words by which He is remembered, such as a servant’s saying: “Glory be to Allah; praise be to Allah; there is no god but Allah; Allah is the Greatest.” When used in the first sense, it means what He Himself mentions—that is, His speech. This is what is meant in His words:

“And whoever turns away from My remembrance”

For He says before this:

“Then if guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will neither go astray nor suffer.”

His guidance is the remembrance He has revealed. After this, He says:

“He will say, ‘My Lord, why have You raised me blind when I used to see?’”

“He will say, ‘Thus did Our signs come to you, but you forgot them.’”

The point is to determine whether “remembrance” means His revealed speech or the servant’s remembrance of Him. Whether “My remembrance” is explained as “My Book,” “My speech,” “My guidance,” or something similar, the referent is the same.

If, however, the questioner wishes to know the particular attribute conveyed by a name, something more is needed than merely identifying its referent. Someone might, for example, ask about al-Quddūs, al-Salām, and al-Mu’min while already knowing that these names refer to Allah. What he wishes to understand, however, is what it means for Him to be Quddūs, Salām, Mu’min, and so forth.

Once this is understood, the early generations often referred to the same entity using an expression that identifies it, even though that expression conveys an attribute not conveyed by another name. This is like someone saying that Ahmad is al-Hāshir, al-Māhī, and al-‘Āqib, and that al-Quddūs is the All-Forgiving

---

## Page 6975

and the Most Merciful—that is, the referent is one and the same, not that one attribute is identical to another. Clearly, this is not a disagreement involving contradiction, as some people suppose. An example is their interpretation of the straight path.

Some said it is “the Quran,” meaning following it, because of the Prophet’s words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) in the hadith of ‘Alī recorded by al-Tirmidhī and transmitted by Abū Nu‘aym through several chains:

“It is Allah’s firm rope, the reminder full of wisdom, and the straight path.”

Others said it is “Islam,” because of his words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) in the hadith of al-Nawwās ibn Sam‘ān recorded by al-Tirmidhī and others:

“Allah sets forth a parable: a straight path, with two walls on either side. In the walls are open doors, with curtains hanging over them. A caller calls from above the path, and another caller calls at its beginning.” He said: “The straight path is Islam; the two walls are Allah’s limits; the open doors are the things Allah has forbidden; the caller at the beginning of the path is Allah’s Book; and the caller above the path is Allah’s admonisher in the heart of every believer.”

These two interpretations agree, for the religion of Islam consists in following the Quran. Each, however, draws attention to a different characteristic, just as the word “path” conveys a third characteristic. The same applies to those who said it is “the Sunnah and the community,” those who said it is “the way of servitude to Allah,” those who said it is “obedience to Allah and His Messenger” (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), and similar interpretations. All of these, then, were referring

---

## Page 6976

to one and the same thing, but each described it by one of its attributes.

The Second Category

Each interpreter mentions some of the kinds encompassed by a general term, by way of illustration and to draw the listener’s attention to that kind—not as a definition exactly coextensive with what is being defined. This is like a non-Arabic speaker asking what the word “bread” denotes, then being shown a loaf and told, “This.” The indication is to this kind of thing, not to that particular loaf alone.

An example is what has been transmitted concerning His words:

“Then We gave the Book as an inheritance to those We chose from among Our servants. Among them are those who wrong themselves, those who take a middle course, and those who are foremost in good deeds.”

It is well known that “those who wrong themselves” includes those who neglect obligatory duties and commit forbidden acts. “Those who take a middle course” includes those who fulfil obligatory duties and refrain from forbidden acts. “Those who are foremost” includes those who advance ahead, drawing near to Allah through good deeds in addition to obligatory duties. Those who take a middle course are the people of the right:

“And the foremost will be the foremost.”

“Those are the ones brought near.”

Each interpreter then applies this to a particular kind of obedience. For example, one says that the foremost is someone who prays at the beginning of the prescribed time; the one who takes a middle course prays within that time; and the one who wrongs himself delays the afternoon prayer until the sun turns yellow.

Another says that Allah mentioned the foremost, the one who takes a middle course, and the wrongdoer at the end of Sūrat al-Baqarah. He mentioned the benevolent person who gives charity, the wrongdoer who consumes usury, and the just person who engages in trade. In matters of wealth, people are either benevolent, just, or unjust. Thus, the foremost is the benevolent person who performs

---

## Page 6977

recommended deeds as well as obligatory ones; the wrongdoer is someone who consumes usury or withholds zakat; and the one who takes a middle course pays the obligatory zakat and does not consume usury. There are other statements of this kind. Each statement mentions a particular kind encompassed by the verse, to show the listener that the verse includes it and, through it, draw attention to similar cases. Explaining by example can be easier than giving an abstract definition.

A sound mind recognizes the general kind, just as it does when someone points to a loaf and says, “This is bread.” Their statement, “This verse was revealed concerning such-and-such,” often belongs to this category, especially when a particular person is mentioned. Examples include the occasions of revelation mentioned in Quranic commentary: their statements that the verse concerning zihār was revealed concerning the wife of Aws ibn al-Sāmit; that the verse concerning mutual imprecation was revealed concerning ‘Uwaymir al-‘Ajlānī or Hilāl ibn Umayyah; and that the verse concerning kalālah was revealed concerning Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh.

They also say that His words:

“And judge between them by what Allah has revealed”

were revealed concerning Banū Qurayzah and Banū al-Nadīr; that His words:

“Whoever turns his back to them on that day”

were revealed concerning the Battle of Badr; and that His words:

“When death approaches any of you, let there be witnesses among you”

were revealed concerning the case of Tamīm al-Dārī and ‘Adī ibn Badā’. There is also Abū Ayyūb’s statement that His words:

“Do not cast yourselves into destruction with your own hands”

“were revealed concerning us, the Ansār”—and the rest of the hadith. There are many similar examples in which they mention that a passage was revealed concerning a group of polytheists in Mecca, a group from the People of the Book—Jews or Christians—or a group of believers.

---

## Page 6978

Those who said this did not mean that the verse’s ruling applied exclusively to those particular individuals and no one else, for no Muslim or rational person would say such a thing. Although scholars have disputed whether general wording revealed on a particular occasion is restricted to that occasion, no Muslim scholar has held that the general statements of the Quran and Sunnah apply exclusively to the particular individual concerned. The most that is argued is that they are restricted to the category to which that individual belongs, thus encompassing others like him, rather than having the full general scope of their wording.

If a verse revealed on a particular occasion contains a command or prohibition, it applies both to that individual and to others in the same position. Likewise, if it contains a statement of praise or blame, it encompasses both that individual and others in the same position.

Knowing the “occasion of revelation” helps one understand the verse, for knowledge of the cause leads to knowledge of its effect. Hence, the sounder of the two juristic opinions is that, when an oath-taker’s intention is unknown, one refers to the reason for his oath and what prompted and provoked it. Their statement, “This verse was revealed concerning such-and-such,” sometimes means that this was the occasion of its revelation, and sometimes means that it falls within the verse’s scope, even if it was not the occasion of revelation—just as one might say, “This verse refers to such-and-such.”

---

## Page 6979

Scholars have also disagreed over a Companion’s statement, “This verse was revealed concerning such-and-such”: should it be treated as a report attributed to the Prophet, as when he mentions the occasion for which it was revealed, or as the Companion’s own interpretation, which is not attributed to the Prophet? Al-Bukhārī includes it among reports attributed to the Prophet, whereas others do not. Most musnad collections, such as the Musnad of Ahmad and others, follow this latter convention. By contrast, when a Companion mentions an event immediately after which the verse was revealed, they all include such an account among reports attributed to the Prophet.

Once this is understood, one scholar’s statement, “It was revealed concerning such-and-such,” does not contradict another’s statement, “It was revealed concerning such-and-such,” provided that the wording encompasses both, as we explained concerning interpretation by example. If one mentions an occasion for which the verse was revealed and another mentions a different occasion, both accounts may be true: the verse may have been revealed after all those events, or it may have been revealed twice—once on one occasion and once on the other.

The two kinds of diversity in interpretation that we have mentioned—arising sometimes from the variety of names and attributes, and sometimes from mentioning particular types and subdivisions of what a term denotes, by way of illustration—account for most of the interpretations transmitted from the early generations of this Ummah that are thought to conflict.

Some disagreements transmitted from them concern wording that admits either of two meanings: either because a word has distinct meanings, such as (qaswarah), which can mean an archer or a lion, and ('as'asa), which can refer to the night’s coming on or its passing away

---

## Page 6980

or because it has a single shared meaning in its basic usage, but what is intended is one of two types or one of two things, as with the pronouns in His words:

“Then he drew near and came down,
until he was two bow-lengths away or nearer.”

And likewise His words:

“By the dawn,
and ten nights,
and the even and the odd,”

and similar expressions.

In such cases, all the meanings mentioned by the early generations may be intended, or this may not be possible. The former possibility may arise because the verse was revealed twice, with one meaning intended on one occasion and another on the other; or because a word with distinct meanings may be intended to convey both meanings—as most jurists, including the Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs and Hanbalīs, and many speculative theologians have allowed; or because the term has a shared meaning and therefore applies generally unless there is a reason to restrict its scope. When both interpretations of this kind are valid, it belongs to the second category.

Among the statements transmitted from them that some people regard as disagreements are explanations expressed in words that are close in meaning but not synonymous. True synonymy is uncommon in language; in the wording of the Quran, it is either rare or nonexistent. Seldom can a single word be explained by another single word that conveys its entire meaning. Rather, the explanation only approximates its meaning. This is one of the reasons for the Quran’s inimitability. Thus, if someone explains His words:

“On the Day the sky will sway to and fro,”

by saying that mawr (swaying) means movement, this is only an approximation, for mawr is light, rapid movement.

---

## Page 6981

Likewise, if someone explains “revelation” as “communication,” or “We revealed to you” as “We sent down to you,” or “We conveyed Our decree to the Children of Israel” as “We informed them,” and so forth, all these are approximations, not precise explanations. Revelation is swift, hidden communication, while conveying a decree to them is more specific than merely informing them, for it involves sending down to them and revealing to them.

The Arabs incorporate the meaning of one verb into another and give it the same prepositional construction. Hence the error of those who regard some prepositions as interchangeable, as when they explain His words:

“He has certainly wronged you by demanding your ewe in addition to his ewes,”

as meaning “along with his ewes”; and:

“Who will be my helpers unto Allah?”

as meaning “with Allah,” and similar examples. The precise explanation is what the grammarians of Basra described as incorporating an additional meaning: demanding the ewe includes the meaning of gathering it together with his ewes and adding it to them. Likewise, in His words:

“They almost tempted you away from what We revealed to you,”

the verb incorporates the meanings of making you deviate and turning you away. Similarly, in His words:

“We helped him against the people who denied Our signs,”

the verb incorporates the meanings of saving and delivering him. Likewise, in His words:

“From which the servants of Allah drink,”

the verb incorporates the meaning of having their thirst quenched by it. There are many similar examples.

Anyone who explains lā rayba as lā shakka (“there is no doubt”) is only approximating the meaning. Rayb carries a sense of agitation and movement, as in his saying:

“Leave what makes you uneasy for what does not make you uneasy.”

And the hadith relates:

“He passed by a gazelle curled up asleep and said, ‘Let no one disturb it.’”

Just as certainty carries a sense of stillness and reassurance, its opposite, rayb, carries a sense of agitation and movement. Even if shakk is said to entail this meaning, the word itself does not express it.

---

## Page 6982

Likewise, if “that Book” is explained as “this Quran,” this is only an approximation. Although the referent is the same, pointing to something as present differs from pointing to it as distant and absent. The word “Book” conveys its being written and gathered together, whereas “Quran” conveys its being recited, made manifest, and brought into the open. Such distinctions are found in the Quran.

Thus, when one of the early scholars explains “to be given over” as “to be confined,” while another says “to be held in pledge,” or gives a similar explanation, this is not a contradictory disagreement. Someone confined may or may not be held in pledge, but these explanations merely approximate the meaning, as discussed above. Collecting the explanations of the early generations in such cases is extremely beneficial: taken together, their expressions convey the intended meaning more fully than one or two expressions alone. Nevertheless, genuine disagreement does occur among them, just as it does concerning legal rulings.

We know that most of what people at large need to know concerning disputed matters is well established—indeed, transmitted so widely as to yield certainty among laypeople or scholars. Examples include the number of prayers, the number of bowings in them, their times, the obligatory zakat dues and their thresholds, the identification of the month of Ramadan, circumambulation, standing at Arafat, stoning the pillars, the prescribed times and places, and other such matters.

The Companions’ disagreement concerning inheritance involving a grandfather and siblings, the case known as al-musharraka, and similar questions does not cast doubt on the great majority of inheritance rulings. Rather, what people generally need concerns the direct line of descent—parents and children—collateral relatives, namely brothers and sisters, and their womenfolk, such as wives. For Allah revealed three detailed verses concerning inheritance.

---

## Page 6983

in the first of which He mentioned ascendants and descendants; in the second, those outside the direct line who inherit prescribed shares, such as spouses and maternal siblings; and in the third, those outside the direct line who inherit as residuary heirs, namely full siblings or paternal half-siblings. Cases involving both a grandfather and siblings are rare; hence no such case arose in Islam until after the death of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Disagreement may arise because the evidence is obscure or has been overlooked, because someone has not heard it, because a text has been misunderstood, or because someone believes that stronger countervailing evidence exists. The purpose here is to outline the subject, not to discuss its details.

Section

Disagreement in Quranic interpretation is of “two types”: one rests solely on transmitted reports, while the other can be understood through other means. For knowledge rests either on reliable transmission or on soundly established inference. Transmitted reports come either from one protected from error or from someone not protected from error. The point is that transmitted material in general—whether from one protected from error or from someone else, this being the first type—includes reports whose soundness or weakness can be determined and others for which this cannot be determined.

This “second category of transmitted material”—that whose truth we have no way of establishing with certainty—consists mostly of matters of no benefit. Discussing them is therefore superfluous.

---

## Page 6984

As for what Muslims need to know, Allah has provided evidence establishing the truth concerning it. Examples of matters that offer no benefit and for which there is no evidence establishing the correct view include their disagreements about the colour of the dog belonging to the Companions of the Cave, which part of the cow Moses struck with, the dimensions of Noah’s ark and the wood from which it was made, the name of the boy whom al-Khidr killed, and similar matters.

Such matters can be known only through transmitted reports. Whatever has been authentically transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace—such as the fact that Moses’ companion was named al-Khidr—is therefore known. Whatever has not been transmitted in this way, but instead comes from the People of the Book—such as reports transmitted by Kaʿb, Wahb, Muhammad ibn Ishāq, and others who relate material from the People of the Book—may neither be affirmed nor denied without evidence. As authentically reported, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the People of the Book tell you something, neither believe them nor disbelieve them, for they may tell you something true and you reject it, or they may tell you something false and you believe it.”

The same applies to reports transmitted from some of the Successors, even when a Successor does not mention having taken the report from the People of the Book. Whenever the Successors disagree, the statements of some do not constitute authoritative evidence against those of others.

Authentically transmitted reports from some of the Companions concerning these matters inspire greater confidence than reports from some of the Successors. This is because there is a stronger possibility that the Companion heard it from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or from someone who heard it from him, and because the Companions transmitted less from the People of the Book than the Successors did. Moreover, given the firm conviction of

---

## Page 6985

the Companion regarding what he relates, how can it be said that he took it from the People of the Book, when the Companions were forbidden to believe them? The point is that disagreements of this kind—where the correct view cannot be determined and recounting the various opinions serves no benefit—are like knowing reports whose authenticity is unsupported by evidence, and similar matters.

As for “the first category,” in which the correct account can be established, this is possible in matters that people need to know, and all praise belongs to Allah. Works of Quranic exegesis, hadith, and accounts of military campaigns frequently contain reports attributed to our Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and to other prophets, may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them, which authentic transmission refutes. Indeed, this occurs both in matters based on transmission and in matters that may be known through other means. The point is that Allah has provided evidence by which to distinguish authentic reports from others in matters needed for religion.

It is well known that most reports transmitted in Quranic exegesis resemble those transmitted in accounts of military campaigns and great battles. For this reason, Imam Ahmad said: “Three subjects have no chains of transmission: Quranic exegesis, accounts of great battles, and accounts of military campaigns.” Another version reads, “They have no basis,” meaning no chains of transmission. This is because most of their reports have incomplete chains, such as the accounts of military campaigns related by ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr, al-Shaʿbī, al-Zuhrī, Mūsā ibn ʿUqba, Ibn Ishāq, and those who came after them, such as Yahyā ibn Saʿīd al-Umawī, al-Walīd ibn Muslim, al-Wāqidī, and others like them.

The people most knowledgeable about the military campaigns are the people of Medina, followed by the people of Syria, then the people of Iraq. The people of Medina are the most knowledgeable about them because these campaigns took place

---

## Page 6986

among them. The people of Syria were engaged in military expeditions and jihad, and thus possessed knowledge of jihad and military affairs that others did not. For this reason, scholars held Abū Ishāq al-Fazārī’s book on this subject in high esteem and considered al-Awzā'ī more knowledgeable in this field than other scholars of the major centres.

As for “Quranic exegesis,” those most knowledgeable about it are the people of Mecca, for they were the students of Ibn 'Abbās, such as Mujāhid, 'Atā ibn Abī Rabāh, 'Ikrima—the freedman of Ibn 'Abbās—and his other students, including Tāwūs, Abū al-Sha'thā', Sa'īd ibn Jubayr, and others like them. The same applies to the people of Kufa, including the students of Ibn Mas'ūd; in certain aspects of this field, they distinguished themselves above others. Among the scholars of Medina versed in Quranic exegesis was Zayd ibn Aslam, from whom Mālik learned exegesis, as did Zayd’s son, 'Abd al-Rahmān. 'Abd Allāh ibn Wahb, in turn, learned it from 'Abd al-Rahmān.

“Reports with incomplete chains,” when transmitted through multiple routes free of deliberate collusion or unintentional coincidence, are certainly authentic. A transmitted account is either truthful, corresponding to what is reported, or false, its narrator having either deliberately lied or made a mistake. Once deliberate falsehood and error are ruled out, it is undoubtedly true. Thus, when a report comes through two or more sources, and it is known that the narrators have not colluded over their differing accounts, and that agreement of this kind cannot occur by coincidence

---

## Page 6987

without deliberate intent, it is known to be authentic. For example, someone recounts an event and gives details of what was said and done. Another person, known not to have colluded with the first, then recounts the same details. It is thereby known with certainty that the event truly occurred in its broad outlines. For if both had given a false account, whether deliberately or mistakenly, they would not ordinarily have supplied matching details of a kind that, in the normal course of events, two people cannot agree upon without colluding.

One person may happen to compose a verse and another compose an identical one, or one may tell a lie and another tell the same lie. But if someone composes a long poem covering various themes, with a fixed rhyme and rhyme consonant, ordinary experience does not admit that someone else would independently compose a poem identical in wording and meaning despite its considerable length. Rather, ordinary experience establishes that he took it from the first poet. Likewise, if someone relates a lengthy account covering various matters and another relates an identical account, either he colluded with the first narrator, took the account from him, or the account is true.

Through this method, the truth of most transmitted accounts reaching us through multiple distinct channels in this manner can be established, even if no individual channel is sufficient on its own, either because its chain is incomplete or because its transmitter is weak. However, this method does not establish precise wording or fine details that cannot be known in this way. Thus, there is no need for a transmission route that establishes such precise wording and fine details.

Accordingly, the Battle of Badr and the fact that it preceded Uhud are established by mass transmission. Indeed, it is known with certainty that Hamza, 'Alī, and 'Ubayda went out to confront 'Utba, Shayba, and al-Walīd

---

## Page 6988

and that ‘Alī killed al-Walīd and Hamza killed his opponent, although there is uncertainty as to whether Hamza’s opponent was ‘Utba or Shayba.

This principle should be understood, for it is useful in establishing the certainty of many transmitted accounts concerning hadith, Quranic commentary, the Prophet’s military campaigns, people’s words and deeds, and other matters. Thus, when a hadith to which this principle applies is transmitted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, through two channels, and it is known that one did not derive it from the other, its truth can be established with certainty. This is especially so when its transmitters are known not to lie deliberately, and the only concern is that one of them might forget or make a mistake.

Anyone familiar with the Companions—such as Ibn Mas‘ūd, Ubayy ibn Ka‘b, Ibn ‘Umar, Jābir, Abū Sa‘īd, Abū Hurayra, and others—knows with certainty that none of them would deliberately lie about the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, let alone those superior to them. This is just as a man knows, through long, close acquaintance with someone whose character he has tested, that he is not the sort to steal people’s property, commit highway robbery, bear false witness, or do similar things.

The same applies to the Successors in Medina, Mecca, Syria, and Basra. Anyone familiar with the likes of Abū Sālih al-Sammān, al-A‘raj, Sulaymān ibn Yasār, Zayd ibn Aslam, and their peers knows with certainty that they were not people who would deliberately lie in transmitting hadith

---

## Page 6989

let alone those superior to them, such as Muhammad ibn Sīrīn, al-Qāsim ibn Muhammad, Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab, ‘Ubayda al-Salmānī, ‘Alqama, al-Aswad, or others like them. The only concern regarding any one of them is the possibility of error, for error and forgetfulness frequently affect human beings.

Among the masters of hadith are those whom people have known to be exceptionally free of such failings, as they knew of al-Sha‘bī, al-Zuhrī, ‘Urwa, Qatāda, al-Thawrī, and their peers—especially al-Zuhrī in his time and al-Thawrī in his. Indeed, one might say: “No error is known on the part of Ibn Shihāb al-Zuhrī, despite the abundance of his hadith and the vast extent of his memorization.”

“The point” is that when a lengthy hadith is transmitted, for example, through two independent channels without collusion, it cannot be the product of error, just as it cannot be a lie. An error cannot account for an entire lengthy narrative containing varied details; it can only affect some part of it. Thus, if one person relates a lengthy narrative containing varied details, and another relates it just as the first did, without collusion, it is impossible for the whole of it to be erroneous, just as it is impossible for the whole of it to be fabricated without collusion.

For this reason, errors in such accounts occur only in some details of the events related. An example is the hadith concerning the Prophet’s purchase of a camel from Jābir, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Anyone who examines its chains of transmission knows with certainty that the hadith is authentic, even though its transmitters differed over the price. Al-Bukhārī explained this in his Sahīh.

Indeed, the great majority of the reports in al-Bukhārī and Muslim can be affirmed with certainty to have been spoken by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, because most of them are of this kind, and because the scholars have received them with acceptance and affirmed their truth;

---

## Page 6990

and the Muslim community does not agree upon error. If a hadith were in fact false, yet the community believed and accepted it, they would have reached a consensus affirming something that was actually false. This would constitute consensus upon error, which is impossible. Even if, without consensus, we admit the possibility of error or falsehood in a report, this is comparable to admitting—before learning of a consensus concerning a conclusion established through an apparent textual meaning or a probabilistic analogy—that the underlying truth might differ from what we believe. Once they reach a consensus on the ruling, however, we affirm with certainty that it is established both outwardly and in reality.

For this reason, the majority of scholars across all schools hold that “a solitary report,” when received with acceptance by the Muslim community, whether by affirming its truth or acting upon it, yields certain knowledge. This is the position stated by authors on the principles of jurisprudence among the followers of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad, apart from a small group of later scholars who followed a faction of speculative theologians in denying it. Yet many, or most, speculative theologians agree on this with the jurists, hadith scholars, and early generations. It is also the position of most Ash‘arīs, such as Abū Ishāq and Ibn Fūrak.

As for Ibn al-Bāqillānī, he rejected this position and was followed by such figures as Abū al-Ma‘ālī, Abū Hāmid, Ibn ‘Aqīl, Ibn al-Jawzī, Ibn al-Khatīb, al-Āmidī, and others like them. The former position is the one stated by Shaykh Abū Hāmid, Abū al-Tayyib, Abū Ishāq, and other leading Shāfi‘ī scholars like them. It is likewise the position stated by al-Qādī ‘Abd al-Wahhāb and other Mālikī scholars like him.

---

## Page 6991

and it is the position stated by Abū Ya‘lā, Abū al-Khattāb, Abū al-Hasan ibn al-Zāghūnī, and other Hanbalī scholars like them. It is also the position stated by Shams al-Dīn al-Sarakhsī and other Hanafī scholars like him. If consensus affirming the truth of a report establishes its certainty, then what counts here is the consensus of scholars knowledgeable in hadith, just as, in consensus concerning rulings, what counts is the consensus of scholars knowledgeable in commands, prohibitions, and permissions.

“The point here” is that multiple routes of transmission, where mutual awareness or agreement is ordinarily ruled out, yield certain knowledge of the substance of what is transmitted. This is particularly useful in studying the circumstances of transmitters. In such cases, benefit can be derived from reports transmitted by someone whose reliability is unknown, someone with a poor memory, or through a disconnected chain, and the like.

For this reason, scholars would record such hadiths and say, “What is unsuitable for other purposes may be suitable for corroboration and comparison.” Ahmad said, “I may record a man’s hadith in order to compare it with other reports.” He cited as an example ‘Abd Allāh ibn Lahī‘a, the judge of Egypt. He was among the most prolific transmitters of hadith and among the best of people. However, because his books were burned, errors entered his later narrations. His reports thus came to be used for comparison and corroboration. His narrations are frequently paired with those of al-Layth ibn Sa‘d, who was an authoritative, thoroughly reliable leading scholar.

Just as scholars use the hadiths of someone with a poor memory for corroboration and comparison, they also judge certain reports of a trustworthy, truthful transmitter with accurate retention to be weak when evidence shows them that he made mistakes in those reports. They call this “the study of hidden defects in hadith.”

---

## Page 6992

and it is among their most distinguished disciplines. A hadith may be narrated by a trustworthy transmitter with accurate retention who nevertheless makes an error in it. His error may be detected through clear evidence. For example, scholars established that “the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, married Maymūnah while not in a state of ritual consecration, and that he prayed two units of prayer inside the Kaaba.” They therefore regarded Ibn ‘Abbās’s reports that he married her while in a state of ritual consecration and that he did not pray inside the Kaaba as instances of error.

Likewise, they established that “he performed four ‘umrahs,” and recognized Ibn ‘Umar’s statement that “he performed ‘umrah in Rajab” as an error. They established that he combined ‘umrah and Hajj during the Farewell Pilgrimage while in safety, and that ‘Uthmān’s statement to ‘Alī, “We were afraid at the time,” was an error. They also recognized as an error the wording found in some transmission routes in al-Bukhārī: “The Fire will not be filled until Allah creates other beings for it.” There are many such examples.

People fall into two extremes in this matter. At one extreme are speculative theologians and others like them who are far removed from knowledge of hadith and its specialists. They cannot distinguish authentic reports from weak ones, so they doubt the authenticity of certain hadiths, or whether their authenticity can be established with certainty, even though specialists in hadith know them to be authentic with certainty.

At the other extreme are some who claim to follow hadith and act upon it. Whenever they find a wording in a hadith narrated by a trustworthy transmitter, or encounter a hadith whose chain of transmission appears authentic, they seek to place it on the same footing as reports whose authenticity the scholars have decisively established. Then, if it conflicts with an established authentic report, they resort to strained and unconvincing interpretations, or use it as evidence for their position on scholarly questions, even though hadith specialists recognize such a report as an error.

---

## Page 6993

Just as there is evidence by which a hadith is known to be true—and this may be established with certainty—there is also evidence by which it is known to be false, and its falsehood established with certainty. For example, we know with certainty that reports transmitted by fabricators among the people of innovation and those who exaggerate merits are false, such as the hadith about the day of ‘Āshūrā’ and similar reports claiming that whoever prays two units of prayer will receive a reward equal to that of so many prophets.

Quranic commentaries contain a substantial number of these fabricated reports. One example is the hadith transmitted by al-Tha‘labī, al-Wāhidī, and al-Zamakhsharī concerning the merits of the Quran’s surahs, one surah at a time. Scholars agree that it is fabricated. Al-Tha‘labī himself possessed goodness and religious devotion, but he was an indiscriminate collector, transmitting whatever he found in books of Quranic commentary, whether authentic, weak, or fabricated. His companion al-Wāhidī was more knowledgeable than him in Arabic, but further from soundness and from following the early generations. Al-Baghawī’s commentary is an abridgment of al-Tha‘labī’s, but he kept his commentary free of fabricated hadiths and innovated opinions.

Fabricated reports abound in books of Quranic commentary. Examples include the many hadiths explicitly advocating reciting the basmalah (“In the name of Allah”) aloud, and the lengthy hadith about ‘Alī giving his ring in charity while praying, which scholars agree is fabricated. Other examples include the report interpreting His words,

“And for every people there is a guide,”

as referring to ‘Alī, and His words,

“And that a mindful ear might retain it,”

as meaning, “Your ear, O ‘Alī.”

---

## Page 6994

Section

As for the second of the two bases of disagreement—what is known through inference rather than transmission—most errors in this area arise from two sources. These emerged after the Quranic interpretations of the Companions, the Successors, and those who followed them in righteousness. Commentaries that contain exclusively their statements scarcely contain anything of these two kinds. Examples include the commentaries of ‘Abd al-Razzāq, Wakī‘, ‘Abd ibn Humayd, ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Ibrāhīm Duhaym, Imam Ahmad, Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh, Baqī ibn Makhlad, Abū Bakr ibn al-Mundhir, Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah, Sunayd, Ibn Jarīr, Ibn Abī Hātim, Abū Sa‘īd al-Ashajj, Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mājah, and Ibn Mardawayh.

“The first” consists of people who adopted certain meanings and then sought to make the wording of the Quran conform to them.

“The second” consists of people who interpreted the Quran solely according to meanings that an Arabic speaker could conceivably intend by his words, without considering the One who spoke the Quran, the one to whom it was revealed, or those addressed by it.

“The former” attended to the meaning they had adopted without considering what the wording of the Quran itself signifies and makes clear.

---

## Page 6995

And “the latter” considered only the wording and what, in their view, an Arabic speaker might intend by it, without considering what befits the Speaker or the context. They often err in judging whether the wording can bear that meaning in Arabic, just as the former do. Likewise, the former often err in judging the soundness of the meaning by which they interpret the Quran, just as the latter do. Nevertheless, the former begin by considering the meaning, while the latter begin by considering the wording.

The former fall into “two categories”: sometimes they strip the Quran’s wording of the meaning it signifies and is intended to convey; at other times, they impose upon it a meaning that it neither signifies nor is intended to convey. In either case, the meaning they seek to deny or affirm may be false, so their error concerns both the evidence and the conclusion. Or it may be true, so their error concerns the evidence, not the conclusion.

Just as this has occurred in interpreting the Quran, it has also occurred in interpreting hadith. Those who have erred in both the evidence and the conclusion—such as various groups among the people of innovation—adopted a doctrine contrary to the truth upheld by the balanced community, whose members do not collectively agree upon misguidance, such as the Muslim community’s early generations and its imams. They then turned to the Quran and interpreted it according to their own views. Sometimes they cite verses as evidence for their doctrine, although those verses provide no such evidence; at other times, they interpret what contradicts their doctrine in ways that distort the words away from

---

## Page 6996

their proper places. Among these are the sects of the Khawārij, the Rāfidah, the Jahmiyyah, the Muʿtazilah, the Qadariyyah, the Murjiʾah, and others.

Take the Muʿtazilah, for example. They are among those most engaged in speculative theology and disputation, and they have composed Quran commentaries based on the principles of their school. These include the commentary of ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn Kaysān al-Asamm, the teacher of Ibrāhīm ibn Ismāʿīl ibn ʿUlayyah, who used to debate al-Shāfiʿī; the work of Abū ʿAlī al-Jubbāʾī; the extensive commentaries of Judge ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn Ahmad al-Hamdānī and ʿAlī ibn ʿĪsā al-Rummānī; and al-Kashshāf by Abū al-Qāsim al-Zamakhsharī. These men and others like them embraced the doctrines of the Muʿtazilah.

The Muʿtazilah have “five” principles, which they themselves call: divine oneness, justice, the intermediate position between the two positions, the fulfilment of the threat of punishment, and enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong.

Their “divine oneness” is that of the Jahmiyyah, which entails denying the divine attributes and other matters. They say that Allah cannot be seen, that the Quran is created, that He is not above the world, and that no knowledge, power, life, hearing, sight, speech, will, or any other attribute subsists in Him.

As for their “justice,” part of what it entails is that Allah has not willed everything that exists, nor created it all, and that He does not have power over everything. Rather, they hold that the actions of His servants were not

---

## Page 6997

created by Allah, whether good or evil, and that He has willed only what He has commanded in His law; everything else occurs without His will. Later Shia scholars, such as al-Mufīd, Abū Ja'far al-Tūsī, and others like them, agreed with them on this. This Abū Ja'far has a Quran commentary that follows this approach, but combines it with the doctrines of the Twelver Imāmiyyah. Among the Mu'tazilah, however, none professes those doctrines or denies the caliphates of Abū Bakr, 'Umar, 'Uthmān, and 'Alī.

Among the principles that the Mu'tazilah share with the Khawārij is “the fulfilment of the threat of punishment in the Hereafter”: Allah will accept no intercession for those guilty of major sins, nor bring any of them out of the Fire. Undoubtedly, groups among the Murji'ah, the Karrāmiyyah, the Kullābiyyah, and their followers have refuted them. At times they argued well, and at other times poorly, until they came to occupy opposite extremes, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

The point is that people such as these adopted a doctrine and then construed the words of the Quran to fit it. They have no predecessors among the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, or the leading scholars of the Muslims, either in their doctrines or in their interpretations. The falsity of every one of their erroneous interpretations is evident in many ways. This can be established from two directions: through knowledge of the falsity of their doctrine, and through knowledge of the falsity of the interpretation they have given the Quran, whether as evidence for their doctrine or as a response to their opponents. Some of these people write elegantly and eloquently, subtly inserting religious innovations into

---

## Page 6998

their discourse without most people realizing it, as with the author of al-Kashshāf and others like him. He thus manages to pass off as much as Allah wills of their false interpretations on many who do not themselves subscribe to falsehood. I have seen scholars, Quran commentators and others, include in their writings and discourse interpretations of theirs that accord with principles they know or believe to be false, without recognizing the connection.

Then, because of these people’s extremism and misguidance, the Imāmī Rāfidah, followed by the philosophers, the Qarmatians, and others, ventured into interpretations even more extreme. Matters grew still worse among the philosophers, the Qarmatians, and the Rāfidah: they interpreted the Quran in ways that never cease to astonish a knowledgeable person. Examples of the Rāfidah’s interpretations include their claims that:

“May the hands of Abu Lahab perish” refers to Abū Bakr and ʿUmar;

“If you associate others with Allah, your deeds will surely come to nothing” means associating Abū Bakr with ʿAlī in the caliphate;

“Allah commands you to slaughter a cow” refers to ʿĀ’ishah;

“Fight the leaders of disbelief” refers to Talhah and al-Zubayr;

“He released the two seas” refers to ʿAlī and Fātimah;

“Pearls and coral” refers to al-Hasan and al-Husayn;

“We have recorded everything in a clear register” means in ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib;

“What are they asking one another about?” “About the momentous news” refers to ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib;

“Your only allies are Allah, His Messenger, and those who believe, who establish prayer and give zakah while bowing” refers to ʿAlī. They cite the report that he gave his ring in charity during prayer—a report that the scholars unanimously agree is fabricated.

Likewise, they claim that His words, “Upon them are blessings from their Lord, and mercy,” were revealed concerning ʿAlī when he was bereaved by Hamzah’s death.

---

## Page 6999

And somewhat similar to this, in certain respects, are the interpretations many commentators give of passages such as His words:

“Those who are patient, truthful, devoutly obedient, charitable, and who seek forgiveness before dawn.”

They claim that “those who are patient” means the Messenger of Allah; “the truthful,” Abū Bakr; “the devoutly obedient,” ʿUmar; “the charitable,” ʿUthmān; and “those who seek forgiveness,” ʿAlī. Likewise, concerning His words:

“Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, and those with him”—Abū Bakr;
“are firm against the disbelievers”—ʿUmar;
“merciful among themselves”—ʿUthmān;
“you see them bowing and prostrating”—ʿAlī.

Even more astonishing is the claim some of them make:

“By the fig”—Abū Bakr;
“and the olive”—ʿUmar;
“and Mount Sinai”—ʿUthmān;
“and this secure city”—ʿAlī.

These and similar absurdities sometimes involve interpreting words as meaning something they cannot possibly signify, for these expressions do not denote those individuals. In the words of the Most High:

“And those with him are firm against the disbelievers, merciful among themselves; you see them bowing and prostrating,”

all these are descriptions of those who are with him. This is what grammarians call successive predicates. The point here is that they are all attributes of a single subject: those who are with him. It is not permissible to take each description as referring to a separate individual.

At other times, such interpretations involve restricting unqualified, general wording to a single individual, as in the claim that His words:

“Your only allies are Allah, His Messenger, and those who believe,”

refer to ʿAlī alone; or the claim some make that His words:

“And the one who brought the truth and believed in it,”

refer to Abū Bakr alone; or that His words:

“Those of you who spent and fought before the conquest are not equal,”

refer to Abū Bakr

---

## Page 7000

alone, and similar claims.

The Quran commentaries of Ibn ʿAtiyya and others like him follow the Sunnah and the way of the community more closely, and are freer of religious innovation, than al-Zamakhsharī’s commentary. Had he faithfully presented the statements of the early generations preserved in commentaries transmitting their interpretations, that would have been better and more fitting. He frequently quotes the commentary of Muhammad ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī, which is among the most distinguished and highly esteemed commentaries. Yet he then leaves out what Ibn Jarīr transmitted from the early generations, not reporting it at all, and instead presents what he claims is the view of “the discerning scholars.” By these he means a group of speculative theologians who established their principles through methods of the same kind as those by which the Muʿtazilah established theirs, although they are closer to the Sunnah than the Muʿtazilah. Nevertheless, everyone must be given their due, and it must be recognized that this is a form of interpretation driven by doctrinal affiliation.

For if the Companions, the Successors, and the leading scholars hold a view concerning the interpretation of a verse, and others then interpret it differently because of a doctrine they have adopted—a doctrine not held by the Companions or those who followed them in righteousness—then they share with the Muʿtazilah and other adherents of innovation in this practice.

In short, whoever departs from the views and interpretations of the Companions and the Successors in favour of something contrary to them is mistaken in doing so; indeed, he is an innovator in that respect, even if he is exercising independent scholarly judgment and his error is forgiven. The aim here is to explain the means of acquiring knowledge, the evidence on which it rests, and the ways of arriving at what is correct.

---

## Page 7001

And we know that the Companions, the Successors, and their followers read the Quran, and that they were more knowledgeable about its interpretation and meanings, just as they were more knowledgeable about the truth with which Allah sent His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Whoever contradicts their views and interprets the Quran contrary to their interpretation has therefore erred both in the evidence and in the conclusion drawn from it. It is well known that everyone who contradicts their views puts forward some specious argument, whether rational or based on transmitted texts, as explained at length elsewhere.

“The purpose here” is to draw attention to the source of disagreement over Quranic interpretation. Among its greatest causes are the false religious innovations that led their adherents to distort words from their proper places, explain the words of Allah and His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in ways other than those intended, and interpret them contrary to their true meaning. Among the foundations for understanding this are knowing the view they opposed and recognizing that it is the truth; knowing that the interpretation of the early generations differs from theirs; and knowing that their interpretation is newly introduced and innovated. One must then understand, through detailed examination, why their interpretation is invalid, using the evidence Allah has provided to make the truth clear.

Later authors who wrote explanations and commentaries on hadith likewise fell into errors of the same kind as those found in their explanations and commentaries on the Quran.

As for those who err in the evidence but not in the conclusion, they include many Sufis, preachers, jurists, and others who interpret the Quran in terms of meanings that are sound, but which the Quran does not indicate. Examples include much of what Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī mentioned in Haqā’iq

---

## Page 7002

al-Tafsīr. If, however, some of the meanings they mention are false, this falls into the first category: error in both the evidence and the conclusion drawn from it, since the meaning they intend is unsound.

Section

If someone asks, “What are the best methods of interpreting the Quran?” the answer is:

The soundest method is to interpret the Quran through the Quran. What is stated generally in one place is explained in another, and what is presented briefly in one place is elaborated elsewhere. If this proves beyond you, then turn to the Sunnah, for it explains and clarifies the Quran.

Indeed, Imam Abū ‘Abd Allāh Muhammad ibn Idrīs al-Shāfi‘ī said: “Every ruling given by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was based on his understanding of the Quran.” Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, We have sent down to you the Book in truth so that you may judge between people by what Allah has shown you. And do not be an advocate for the treacherous.”

And He, Most High, says:

“And We have sent down to you the Reminder so that you may explain to people what has been sent down to them, and so that they may reflect.”

And He, Most High, says:

“We have sent down the Book to you only so that you may clarify for them that over which they have differed, and as guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

For this reason, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, I have been given the Quran and its like along with it.”

He meant the Sunnah.

---

## Page 7003

And the Sunnah too is sent down to him through revelation, just as the Quran is sent down, though it is not recited as the Quran is recited. Imam al-Shāfi‘ī and other imams have supported this with abundant evidence, which this is not the place to discuss.

The point is that you should seek the interpretation of the Quran in the Quran itself; if you do not find it there, then seek it in the Sunnah. Thus, when the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sent Mu‘ādh to Yemen, he asked him:

“On what basis will you judge?”

He replied, “By the Book of Allah.”

He asked, “And if you do not find it there?”

He replied, “By the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah.”

He asked, “And if you do not find it there?”

He replied, “I will exercise my own judgment.”

He related that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then struck him on the chest and said:

“Praise be to Allah, who has guided the envoy of the Messenger of Allah to what pleases the Messenger of Allah.”

This hadith is found in the Musnad and Sunan collections with a good chain of transmission.

When we find no interpretation in either the Quran or the Sunnah, we turn to the statements of the Companions. They knew better about this because they directly witnessed the Quran’s revelation and experienced circumstances unique to them, and because of their thorough understanding, sound knowledge, and righteous deeds. This applies especially to their scholars and leading figures, such as the four imams—the Rightly Guided Caliphs—and the rightly guided leaders, such as ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd.

Imam Abū Ja‘far Muhammad ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī said: Abū Kurayb narrated to us, saying: Jābir ibn Nūh informed us: al-A‘mash informed us, from Abū al-Duhā, from Masrūq, who reported that ‘Abd Allāh—meaning Ibn Mas‘ūd—said:

“By Him besides whom there is no god, no verse of the Book of Allah was revealed without my knowing

---

## Page 7004

concerning whom it was revealed and where it was revealed. If I knew where to find anyone more knowledgeable about the Book of Allah than I, whom I could reach on a mount, I would go to him.”

Al-A‘mash also reported from Abū Wā’il, from Ibn Mas‘ūd, who said:

“When one of us learned ten verses, he would not move beyond them until he understood their meanings and how to act upon them.”

Among them was the erudite scholar of boundless knowledge, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās, the paternal cousin of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and the interpreter of the Quran. This was through the blessing of the supplication that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, made for him when he said:

“O Allah, grant him understanding of the religion and teach him the interpretation of the Quran.”

Ibn Jarīr said: Muhammad ibn Bashshār narrated to us: Wakī‘ informed us: Sufyān informed us, from al-A‘mash, from Muslim, from Masrūq, who said: ‘Abd Allāh—meaning Ibn Mas‘ūd—said:

“What an excellent interpreter of the Quran Ibn ‘Abbās is!”

He then related it from Yahyā ibn Dāwūd, from Ishāq al-Azraq, from Sufyān, from al-A‘mash, from Muslim ibn Subayh, Abū al-Duhā, from Masrūq, from Ibn Mas‘ūd, who said:

“What an excellent interpreter of the Quran Ibn ‘Abbās is!”

He then related it likewise from Bundār, from Ja‘far ibn ‘Awn, from al-A‘mash, through the same chain. This is therefore an authentic chain of transmission establishing that Ibn Mas‘ūd said these words about Ibn ‘Abbās. According to the correct view, Ibn Mas‘ūd died in 33 AH, and Ibn ‘Abbās lived for thirty-six years after him. What, then, do you think of the knowledge he acquired after Ibn Mas‘ūd’s death?

Al-A‘mash reported from Abū Wā’il: ‘Alī appointed ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abbās to oversee the pilgrimage season in his stead. He addressed the people and recited Surah al-Baqarah in his sermon—and in

---

## Page 7005

another narration, Surah al-Nūr—and explained it so eloquently that, had the Byzantines, the Turks, and the Daylamites heard his explanation, they would have embraced Islam.

For this reason, most of what Ismā‘īl ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Suddī al-Kabīr relates in his commentary comes from these two men: Ibn Mas‘ūd and Ibn ‘Abbās. Sometimes, however, he transmits from them accounts they relate from the People of the Book, which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted when he said:

“Convey from me, even if only a single verse. Relate accounts from the Children of Israel; there is no harm in doing so. Whoever deliberately attributes a lie to me, let him take his seat in the Fire.”

Al-Bukhārī narrated this from ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr.

Thus, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr acquired two camel-loads of books belonging to the People of the Book on the day of the Battle of al-Yarmūk. He would relate accounts from them, understanding this hadith to permit that. These Israelite reports, however, are cited for corroboration, not to be accepted as established truths. They fall into three categories:

The first consists of reports whose truth we know because the evidence we possess confirms them. These are true.

The second consists of reports whose falsehood we know because they contradict the evidence we possess.

The third consists of reports concerning which nothing has been stated, belonging to neither of the preceding categories. We neither believe nor reject them, and relating them is permissible for the reason already mentioned.

---

## Page 7006

and most of these reports offer no benefit in matters of religion. This is why the scholars among the People of the Book frequently disagree over such matters, and disagreements consequently arise among Quran commentators. They discuss, for example, the names of the Companions of the Cave, the colour of their dog, their number, the kind of wood Moses’ staff was made from, the names of the birds Allah brought back to life for Abraham, the particular part of the cow with which the slain man was struck, and the kind of tree from which Allah spoke to Moses. These and other matters Allah has left unspecified in the Quran bring those bound by religious obligations no benefit, either in their worldly affairs or in their religion, through knowing their precise details. Nevertheless, relating their differing views on these matters is permissible, as the Most High says:

“They will say, ‘They were three, their dog being the fourth.’ Others will say, ‘They were five, their dog being the sixth’—guessing at the unseen. Others will say, ‘They were seven, their dog being the eighth.’ Say, ‘My Lord knows best their number. Only a few know them.’ So do not dispute about them beyond what is plainly known, and do not consult any of those people about them.”

This noble verse contains guidance on proper conduct in this matter and teaches how such questions should be approached. The Most High reports three views they held, discredits the first two, and remains silent about the third, thereby indicating its correctness. Had it been false, He would have refuted it just as He refuted the other two. He then indicates that knowing their number serves no useful purpose. In such cases, one should say:

“Say, ‘My Lord knows best their number.’”

For only a few people know this: those to whom Allah has made it known. Hence He says:

“So do not dispute about them beyond what is plainly known.”

That is, do not weary yourself over what serves no useful purpose, and do not ask them about it, for they

---

## Page 7007

know nothing about it beyond conjecture about the unseen.

The best way to present a disagreement, then, is to include all the views on the matter, identify the correct ones, refute the false ones, and explain the disagreement’s significance and implications. This prevents fruitless disputes from becoming prolonged and distracting people from more important matters.

Anyone who recounts a disagreement without including all the views held on the matter gives an incomplete account, since the correct view may be among those omitted. Likewise, an account that merely presents the disagreement without identifying the correct view is incomplete. Whoever knowingly endorses an incorrect view has deliberately lied; whoever does so out of ignorance has erred. Similarly, anyone who raises a dispute over something of no benefit, or recounts numerous views whose wording differs but whose substance amounts to only one or two positions, has wasted time and made a false show of abundance. He is like someone wearing two garments of falsehood. Allah alone grants success in reaching what is right.

Section

If you do not find the explanation in the Quran or the Sunnah, nor in reports from the Companions, many of the leading scholars have turned in such cases to the statements of the Successors, “such as Mujāhid ibn Jabr.” He was outstanding in Quranic interpretation, as Muhammad ibn Ishāq said: “We were told by

---

## Page 7008

Abān ibn Sālih, reporting from Mujāhid, who said: ‘I recited the Quran before Ibn ‘Abbās three times, from beginning to end, stopping him at every verse and asking him about it.’”

Through the same chain leading to al-Tirmidhī, he said: Al-Husayn ibn Mahdī al-Basrī narrated to us: ‘Abd al-Razzāq narrated to us, from Ma‘mar, from Qatādah, who said: “There is not a verse in the Quran concerning which I have not heard something.” Through the same chain leading to him, he said: Ibn Abī ‘Umar narrated to us: Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah narrated to us, from al-A‘mash, who said: Mujāhid said: “Had I read the Quran according to Ibn Mas‘ūd’s reading, I would not have needed to ask Ibn ‘Abbās about much of the Quran that I asked him about.”

Ibn Jarīr said: Abū Kurayb narrated to us, saying: Talq ibn Ghannām narrated to us, from ‘Uthmān al-Makkī, from Ibn Abī Mulaykah, who said: “I saw Mujāhid asking Ibn ‘Abbās about the interpretation of the Quran, with his writing tablets beside him. Ibn ‘Abbās would tell him, ‘Write,’ until he had asked him about the interpretation of the entire Quran.” This is why Sufyān al-Thawrī used to say: “If an interpretation reaches you from Mujāhid, that is sufficient for you.”

Others include Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, ‘Ikrimah—the freedman of Ibn ‘Abbās—‘Atā’ ibn Abī Rabāh, al-Hasan al-Basrī, Masrūq ibn al-Ajda‘, Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab, Abū al-‘Āliyah, al-Rabī‘ ibn Anas, Qatādah, al-Dahhāk ibn Muzāhim, and other Successors, their followers, and those who came after them. Their statements concerning a verse are cited, and their wording may differ. Someone without knowledge may mistake these differences for disagreement and therefore present them as distinct views, although that is not the case. Some express a thing by mentioning what it entails or something analogous to it, while others

---

## Page 7009

refer explicitly to the thing itself. In many instances, all convey the same meaning. Let the discerning reader be attentive to this. Allah is the Guide.

Shu‘bah ibn al-Hajjāj and others said: “The statements of the Successors are not binding authority in subsidiary matters of law, so how can they be binding authority in Quranic interpretation?” This means that their statements are not binding upon others who disagree with them, and this is correct. If, however, they reach a consensus on something, there is no doubt that it constitutes binding authority. If they disagree, the view of some is not binding upon others, nor upon those who come after them. In such cases, reference must be made to the language of the Quran, the Sunnah, general Arabic usage, or the Companions’ statements on the matter.

[As for interpreting the Quran on the basis of mere personal opinion, this is forbidden. Mu’ammal narrated to us: Sufyān narrated to us: ‘Abd al-A‘lā narrated to us, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever speaks about the Quran without knowledge, let him take his seat in the Fire.”

Wakī‘ narrated to us: Sufyān narrated to us, from ‘Abd al-A‘lā al-Tha‘labī, from Sa‘īd ibn Jubayr, from Ibn ‘Abbās, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever speaks about the Quran without knowledge, let him take his seat in the Fire.”

Through the same chain leading to al-Tirmidhī, he said: ‘Abd ibn Humayd narrated to us: Hassān ibn Hilāl narrated to me, saying: Suhayl, the brother of Hazm al-Quta‘ī, narrated to us, saying: Abū ‘Imrān al-Jawnī narrated to us, from Jundub, who said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever speaks about the Quran on the basis of his own opinion, even if he is correct, has nevertheless erred.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “This is a singular hadith, and

---

## Page 7010

some hadith scholars have criticized Suhayl ibn Abī Hazm.”] (*).

It has likewise been reported that some scholars among the Prophet’s Companions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and others strongly warned against interpreting the Quran without knowledge. As for the reports that Mujāhid, Qatādah, and other scholars interpreted the Quran, we should not suppose that they spoke about it and interpreted it without knowledge or merely on their own authority. Indeed, reports from them support what we have said: they did not speak on their own authority without knowledge.

Whoever speaks about the Quran on the basis of his own opinion has undertaken something of which he has no knowledge and followed a course other than the one he was commanded to follow. Even if he arrived at the correct meaning, he would still have erred, because he did not approach the matter properly. He is like someone who judges between people in ignorance: he is destined for the Fire even if his judgment happens to be correct, though his offense is less grave than that of someone whose judgment is wrong. And Allah knows best.

Likewise, Allah Most High called those who accuse others of unlawful sexual intercourse liars, saying:

“Since they have not produced the witnesses, it is they who are the liars in the sight of Allah.”

Thus, the accuser is a liar even if the person he accused actually committed unlawful intercourse, because he reported something he was not permitted to report and ventured into a matter of which he had no knowledge. And Allah knows best.

For this reason, a number of the early generations scrupulously refrained from interpreting what they had no knowledge of. Thus, Shu‘bah narrated from Sulaymān, from ‘Abd Allāh ibn Murrah, from Abū Ma‘mar, who said:

Abū Bakr al-Siddīq said: “What earth would bear me, and what sky would shelter me, if I were to say about the Book of Allah what I do not know?”

Abū ‘Ubayd al-Qāsim ibn Sallām said: Mahmūd ibn Yazīd narrated to us

He then followed it with this hadith, using the same chain back to al-Tirmidhī, saying “wa-bihi” (and through it)—meaning through the preceding chain. Alternatively, “wa-bihi ilā” may be a scribal corruption of “wa-rawā” (and he narrated). This is the more likely possibility, and Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7011

from al-‘Awwām ibn Hawshab, from Ibrāhīm al-Taymī, who reported that Abū Bakr al-Siddīq was asked about His words:

“and fruit and pasture.”

He said: “What sky would shelter me, and what earth would bear me, if I were to say about the Book of Allah what I do not know?” Its chain of transmission is interrupted.

Abū ‘Ubayd also said: Yazīd narrated to us, from Humayd, from Anas, that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb recited from the pulpit:

“and fruit and pasture.”

He said: “We know what fruit is, but what is meant by pasture?” Then he reconsidered and said: “This is indeed overburdening yourself, ‘Umar!”

‘Abd ibn Humayd said: Sulaymān ibn Harb narrated to us, saying: Hammād ibn Zayd narrated to us, from Thābit, from Anas, who said: We were with ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, whose shirt had four patches on its back. He recited:

“and fruit and pasture.”

He said: “What is meant by pasture?” Then he said: “This is indeed overburdening yourself. What harm is there in not knowing it?”

All of this is to be understood as meaning that the two of them—may Allah be pleased with them—only sought to ascertain the precise nature of pasture. Otherwise, that it is vegetation growing from the earth is plainly evident and cannot be unknown, for He, the Most High—exalted is He—says:

“We caused grain to grow therein,”
“and grapes and fresh fodder,”
“and olives and palm trees,”
“and gardens of dense growth.”

Ibn Jarīr said: Ya‘qūb ibn Ibrāhīm narrated to us, saying: Ibn ‘Ulayyah narrated to us, from Ayyūb, from Ibn Abī Mulaykah, that Ibn ‘Abbās was asked about a verse which some of you would have offered an explanation of had you been asked about it, yet he declined to explain it. Its chain of transmission is sound.

Abū ‘Ubayd said: Ismā‘īl ibn Ibrāhīm narrated to us, from Ayyūb, from Ibn Abī Mulaykah, who said: A man asked Ibn ‘Abbās about:

“a day whose measure is a thousand years.”

He said to him:

---

## Page 7012

“Then what about ‘a day whose measure is fifty thousand years’?” Ibn ‘Abbās asked.

The man replied: “I only asked you so that you would explain it to me.” Ibn ‘Abbās said: “They are two days that Allah has mentioned in His Book. Allah knows best about them.” He was unwilling to say anything about the Book of Allah without knowledge.
