Ibn Jarīr said: Ya‘qūb—meaning Ibn Ibrāhīm—narrated to me, saying: Ibn ‘Ulayyah narrated to us, from Mahdī ibn Maymūn, from al-Walīd ibn Muslim, who said: Talq ibn Habīb came to Jundub ibn ‘Abd Allāh and asked him about a verse of the Quran. He replied: “I solemnly charge you, if you are a Muslim, to get up and leave me”—or he said: “not to sit with me.”

Mālik reported from Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd, from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab, that whenever he was asked to explain a verse of the Quran, he would say: “We do not say anything about the Quran.” Al-Layth reported from Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd, from Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab, that he would speak only about what was known of the Quran’s meaning.

Shu‘bah reported from ‘Amr ibn Murrah, who said: A man asked Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab about a verse of the Quran. He replied: “Do not ask me about the Quran. Ask the one who claims that nothing in it is hidden from him”—meaning ‘Ikrimah.

Ibn Shawdhab said: Yazīd ibn Abī Yazīd narrated to me, saying: We used to ask Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab about what was lawful and unlawful, and he was the most knowledgeable of people. Yet whenever we asked him to explain a verse of the Quran, he remained silent, as though he had not heard.

Ibn Jarīr said: Ahmad ibn ‘Abdah al-Dabbī narrated to me, saying: Hammād ibn Zayd narrated to us, saying: ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn ‘Umar narrated to us, saying: “I knew the jurists of Medina, and they certainly

---

## Page 7013

regarded speaking about Quranic interpretation as a grave matter. Among them were Sālim ibn ‘Abd Allāh, al-Qāsim ibn Muhammad, Sa‘īd ibn al-Musayyab, and Nāfi‘.”

Abū ‘Ubayd said: ‘Abd Allāh ibn Sālih narrated to us, from al-Layth, from Hishām ibn ‘Urwah, who said: “I never heard my father interpret a single verse from the Book of Allah.”

Ayyūb, Ibn ‘Awn, and Hishām al-Dastawā’ī reported from Muhammad ibn Sīrīn, who said: I asked ‘Ubaydah al-Salmānī about a verse of the Quran. He replied: “Those who knew what the Quran was revealed concerning have passed away. So fear Allah and adhere to what is right.”

Abū ‘Ubayd said: Mu‘ādh narrated to us, from Ibn ‘Awn, from ‘Ubayd Allāh ibn Muslim ibn Yasār, from his father, who said: “When you report something from Allah, pause until you have considered what precedes it and what follows it.”

Hushaym narrated to us, from Mughīrah, from Ibrāhīm, who said: “Our companions were wary of Quranic interpretation and stood in awe of it.”

Shu‘bah reported from ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī al-Safar, who said: Al-Sha‘bī said: “By Allah, there is not a single verse about which I have not asked. Yet interpreting it means reporting on Allah’s authority.”

Abū ‘Ubayd said: Hushaym narrated to us, saying: ‘Umar ibn Abī Zā’idah informed us, from al-Sha‘bī, from Masrūq, who said: “Beware of Quranic interpretation, for it is nothing less than reporting on Allah’s authority.”

These authentic reports, and similar reports from the leading scholars of the early generations, are to be understood as reflecting their scrupulous avoidance of speaking about Quranic interpretation without knowledge. As for someone who speaks about what he knows on linguistic and religious grounds, there is no blame on him. Thus, statements interpreting the Quran have been transmitted from these scholars and others, and there is no contradiction: they spoke about what they knew and remained silent about what they did not know. This is everyone’s duty, for just as one must remain silent about what

---

## Page 7014

one does not know, one must likewise speak about what one knows when asked, for Allah Most High says:

“You must make it clear to the people and not conceal it.”

And the hadith transmitted through several chains states:

“Whoever is asked about knowledge and conceals it will be bridled on the Day of Resurrection with a bridle of fire.”

Ibn Jarīr said: Muhammad ibn Bashshār narrated to us: Mu’ammal narrated to us: Sufyān narrated to us, from Abū al-Zinād, who said: Ibn ‘Abbās said: “Quranic interpretation is of four kinds: a kind the Arabs understand through their own language; a kind no one is excused for not knowing; a kind known to the scholars; and a kind known only to Allah.”

And Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

---

## Page 7015

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—also said:

Section

When Allah sent Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with His Book—which is guidance, healing, and light—and made it the finest discourse and the finest of narratives, and the straight path for those devoted to understanding and reflection, recitation and remembrance, and listening and spiritual states, those who held fast to it in knowledge, spiritual state, recitation, and listening, inwardly and outwardly, were truly Muslims—particularly the community of Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Then, when some of those concerned with discourse and the Quran’s wording deviated towards other discourse, and some of those concerned with listening and vocal expression turned to listening to something else, this deviation appeared among four related groups:

One group abandoned learning from it, examining it, and reflecting upon it, turning instead to other discourse: that of the Sabians or the Jews, or discourse derived from it, akin to it, or of a similar kind. These are the deviant practitioners of speculative theology.

Opposite them are people who rendered its letters correctly, memorized it, and recited it, without comprehending it or

---

## Page 7016

understanding its meanings, or knowing which doctrines agree with it or contradict it, and how it explains their questions and proofs. These are those among Quran reciters, hadith scholars, and others who confine themselves to outward forms. These two groups resemble a student of jurisprudence who does not know hadith, or a student of hadith who does not understand its jurisprudence. Likewise, there is the speculative theologian who does not reflect upon the Quran, or the reciter who cannot distinguish, through the Quran, the various kinds of true and false discourse. These, then, are two groups concerned with knowledge.

The “third” group consists of people who abandoned listening to it with their hearts, delighting in it, responding inwardly to its stirring influence, savoring its sweetness, and experiencing its flavor. Instead, they turned to listening to other sounds: poetry or musical entertainments drawn from the sounds of the Sabians or Christians, or things derived from these, akin to them, or of a similar kind. These are the deviant Sufis and those who profess spiritual poverty.

Opposite them are people who recite it aloud and listen to its recitation without being moved by it, experiencing spiritual emotion through it, or tasting its truths and meanings. These are those concerned with outward forms among worshippers, those who perform voluntary devotions, and those devoted to Quran recitation.

Thus, these two types are the person of spiritual states whose state is stirred by sounds, but whose stirring and spiritual state do not arise from the sound of the Quran; and the person of doctrinal discourse who distinguishes between statements and examines them, but whose examination and discourse do not derive from the Quran. Opposite these two are the person of outward worship, whose listening to the Quran and recitation of it remain outward acts; and the person of outward knowledge, who memorizes the Quran’s wording or explains it in terms of its unfamiliar vocabulary, grammatical analysis, circumstances of revelation, and similar matters.

These, then, are the four categories: those who stopped at the outward aspects of prescribed knowledge and practice, and those who delved into the inward aspects of knowledge

---

## Page 7017

and practice, but in forms that were not prescribed. Neglect and transgression arose among these groups. This is why mutual hostility developed between them: the former accuse the latter of religious innovation and misguidance, and rightly so; the latter accuse the former of ignorance and incapacity, and rightly so.

Yet some of the former may possess much prescribed knowledge and practice, just as some of the latter may possess much inward knowledge and a latent spiritual state. Thus, al-Hasan al-Basrī narrated, in one of his reports omitting the Companion from the chain, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Knowledge is of two kinds: knowledge in the heart and knowledge on the tongue. Knowledge in the heart is beneficial knowledge, while knowledge on the tongue is Allah’s proof against His servants.”

Yahyā ibn Sa‘īd al-Taymī, Abū Hayyān, said, as related by al-Khallāl in his Compendium on the authority of al-Thawrī:

“Scholars are of three kinds: one who knows Allah but does not know Allah’s commands; one who knows Allah’s commands but does not know Allah; and one who knows both Allah and His commands.”

---

## Page 7018

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section

As for his question about “taking the Quran according to its apparent meaning”: if he believes in what Allah has said of Himself and what His Messenger has said of Him, without distorting its meaning or specifying its modality, then he has followed the path of the believers.

The expression “apparent meaning” has come to carry more than one meaning in the usage of later scholars. If, by taking it according to its apparent meaning, he means attributing to Allah characteristics peculiar to created beings, thereby likening Allah to His creation, then he is misguided. Rather, one must affirm with certainty that nothing is like Allah, whether in His essence, His attributes, or His actions.

Ibn ‘Abbās said:

“Nothing in this world shares anything with what is in Paradise except names.”

He meant that the realities of the gold, silk, wine, and milk that Allah has promised in Paradise differ from the realities of these things as they exist in this world. Allah Most High is therefore far further removed from resemblance to His creatures, to a degree His servants cannot comprehend

---

## Page 7019

—His reality is unlike that of any of them.

If, however, by taking it according to its apparent meaning he means its apparent meaning as understood by the early generations of the Muslim community—neither distorting words from their proper places, nor deviating concerning the names of Allah Most High, nor reading the Quran and hadith in a manner contrary to the interpretation of those early generations and Ahl al-Sunnah, but rather understanding them in accordance with what the texts entail, what the evidence of the Quran and Sunnah jointly establishes, and what the early generations unanimously agreed upon—then he is correct in doing so, and this is the truth.

This is a summary whose details cannot be elaborated here. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7020

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about the saying of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Whoever interprets the Quran according to his own opinion, let him take his seat in the Fire.”

“If the commentators’ differing interpretations of a single verse are based on personal opinion, how can one escape this punishment? If they are not based on personal opinion, how did these differences arise, when two contradictory positions cannot both be true? Please give us your ruling.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—replied:

It should be understood that differences among Quran commentators and others are of two kinds.

The first involves neither opposition nor contradiction; rather, both views may be true. Such differences are simply variations, or differences in the attributes described or the expressions used. Most of the differences reliably reported from the early commentators—the Companions and the Successors—are of this kind. When Allah, glorified be He, mentions a term in the Quran, as in His saying:

“Guide us to the straight path,”

each commentator describes the straight path in terms that indicate some of its attributes, and all these descriptions are true. This is comparable to the names by which Allah, His Messenger, and His Book are known: each name indicates an attribute of the one it names. Thus, some commentators say that “the straight path” is the Book of Allah, or following the Book of Allah.

---

## Page 7021

and another says that “the straight path” is Islam, or the religion of Islam. Another says that “the straight path” is the Sunnah and the Muslim community. Another says that “the straight path” is the path of servitude to Allah, or the path of fear, hope, love, obeying commands and avoiding prohibitions; or following the Book and the Sunnah; or acting in obedience to Allah—or uses other such names and expressions.

Clearly, the referent is one and the same, even though its attributes vary and its names and descriptions are numerous. Thus, one may say: Muhammad is Ahmad, the Gatherer, the Effacer, the Last, the Seal of the Messengers, the Prophet of Mercy, and the Prophet of Battle.

Likewise, one may say: the Quran is the Criterion, the Light, the Healing, the Wise Reminder, and the Book whose verses have been perfected and then explained in detail. The same applies to Allah’s Most Beautiful Names:

“He is the First and the Last, the Most High and the Most Near, and He has knowledge of all things.”

He is the One

“who created and proportioned, who determined and guided, who brought forth pasture, then turned it into dark stubble.”

“He is Allah, besides whom there is no deity, Knower of the unseen and the seen. He is the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.”

“He is Allah, besides whom there is no deity, the Sovereign, the Most Holy, the Source of Peace, the Giver of Security, the Watcher over all, the Almighty, the Compeller, the Supremely Great.”

“He is Allah, the Creator, the Originator, the Fashioner,”

---

## Page 7022

and the like. He—glorified is He—is One, the Self-Sufficient. His Most Beautiful Names all denote His Essence, but each signifies attributes that another does not. Thus, they agree in denoting the Essence while differing in the attributes they signify. A name signifies the Essence together with a particular attribute as its full meaning, and either one individually as part of that meaning. Each name also signifies, by necessary implication, the attribute to which it points, for it denotes the Essence designated by all the attributes. Much exegesis and translation proceeds in this manner.

There is also “another category”: the commentator or translator explains a word’s meaning by identifying an instance or giving an example, rather than by providing an exhaustive definition. For example, a non-Arab might ask, “What does ‘bread’ mean?” and someone points him to a loaf. The intention is not to restrict the meaning to that particular loaf; rather, the gesture identifies this individual instance.

This is likewise the case when they are asked about His words:

“Among them are those who wrong themselves, among them are those who are moderate, and among them are those who are foremost in good deeds,”

or His words:

“Indeed, Allah is with those who fear Him and those who do good,”

or about the righteous, the wrongdoers, and similar general, comprehensive terms whose full meaning may be difficult or impossible for the listener or speaker to encompass, since he has no need to do so. The speaker therefore mentions types and individual instances sufficient to serve his purpose. These may also provide a basis for inferring analogous cases.

For the one who wrongs himself is someone who neglects what is commanded and does what is forbidden, while “the moderate one”

---

## Page 7023

is one who fulfils what is obligatory and avoids what is forbidden, while “the foremost” is one who fulfils both what is obligatory and what is recommended, and avoids both what is forbidden and what is disliked.

Thus, according to the questioner’s needs, the respondent may say: “The one who wrongs himself” is someone who misses the prayer, does not perform ablution thoroughly, does not fully perform the prayer’s essential elements, or the like. “The moderate one” is someone who prays at the prescribed time as commanded. “The one foremost in good deeds” is someone who performs the prayer with its obligatory and recommended acts, together with the recommended voluntary prayers. He may give similar explanations concerning zakah, fasting, Hajj, and all other obligations.

It has been reported that Ibn ʿAbbās, may Allah be pleased with them both, said:

“Quranic interpretation is of four kinds: interpretation that the Arabs understand through their own language; interpretation that no one is excused for not knowing; interpretation known to scholars; and interpretation known only to Allah. Whoever claims knowledge of the last is a liar.”

The Companions received both the wording and the meaning of the Quran from the Messenger, just as they received the Sunnah from him. Just as some people altered the Sunnah, some altered certain meanings of the Quran, since they could not alter its wording.

Furthermore, some meanings of the Quran may escape certain scholars, just as some of the Sunnah may escape them. This is one source of error among scholars exercising independent judgment. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7024

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

About a soldier who has copied out by hand the Sahīh collections of Muslim and al-Bukhārī and the Quran, intending to write down hadith and the glorious Quran. If he hears of paper or pens, he buys a thousand dirhams’ worth and says, “Allah willing, I shall fill all this paper with the Messenger’s hadiths and the Quran.” He entertains far-reaching hopes. Is he sinful or not? And which Quranic commentary is closest to the Book and the Sunnah: al-Zamakhsharī’s, al-Qurtubī’s, al-Baghawī’s, or someone else’s?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. He incurs no sin through his intention and his work of writing down the religious sciences. Indeed, writing down the Quran, authentic hadiths, and the available, established Quranic commentaries is among the greatest acts of worship and means of drawing near to Allah.

As for the Quranic commentaries in circulation, the soundest is the commentary of Muhammad ibn Jarīr al-Tabarī. He records the statements of the early generations with reliable chains of transmission. His commentary contains no religious innovation, nor does he transmit from suspect narrators such as Muqātil ibn Bukayr and al-Kalbī. There are many commentaries not transmitted with chains of narration, such as the commentaries of ʿAbd al-Razzāq, ʿAbd ibn Humayd, Wakīʿ, Ibn Abī Shaybah, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and Ishāq ibn Rāhawayh.

---

## Page 7025

As for the three commentaries asked about, al-Baghawī’s is the freest from religious innovation and weak hadiths. However, it is an abridgment of al-Tha‘labī’s commentary, from which he removed the fabricated hadiths and religious innovations, along with other material.

As for al-Wāhidī, he was al-Tha‘labī’s student and more knowledgeable than him about Arabic. Al-Tha‘labī, however, was relatively free of religious innovations, even though he mentioned them in imitation of others. His commentary and al-Wāhidī’s al-Basīt, al-Wasīt, and al-Wajīz contain valuable insights, but also much worthless material, including false reports and other things.

As for al-Zamakhsharī, his commentary is filled with religious innovation and follows the Mu‘tazilah’s approach: denying Allah’s attributes and the possibility of seeing Him, asserting that the Quran is created, and other Mu‘tazilite doctrines. He also denied that Allah wills all that comes into being and creates people’s actions.

Their five foundational principles are what they call divine oneness, justice, the intermediate position between the two positions, carrying out the threat of punishment, and enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong. Yet what they mean by “divine oneness” includes denying Allah’s attributes. This is why Ibn Tūmart called his followers al-Muwahhidūn (the affirmers of divine oneness). This is nothing but deviation concerning Allah’s names and signs.

---

## Page 7026

And what they mean by “justice” includes denying divine decree—that is, Allah’s creation of people’s actions, His willing of all that comes into being, and His power to act. Some of them deny His prior knowledge and prior recording of events; yet this is the position of their leading authorities. It is with these that al-Zamakhsharī aligns himself, for his doctrine is that of al-Mughīra ibn ‘Alī, Abū Hāshim, and their followers. The doctrine of Abū al-Husayn and the Mu‘tazilites who follow his approach has two forms: Masāyikhiyya and Khashabiyya.

As for the “intermediate position between the two positions,” they mean that a sinner cannot be called a believer in any respect, just as he cannot be called a disbeliever. They thus assign him a position between the two. By “carrying out the threat of punishment,” they mean that sinners within the Muslim community will remain in Hell forever, never leaving it through intercession or any other means, as the Khawārij also maintain. In their understanding, “enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong” includes the permissibility of rebelling against rulers and fighting them with the sword.

He filled his book with these principles, expressing them in language through which most people cannot discern either the principles themselves or his purposes in conveying them. In addition, it contains fabricated hadiths and relatively few reports from the Companions and their successors.

Al-Qurtubī’s commentary is far better than his, closer to the approach of those who follow the Quran and Sunnah, and further removed from religious innovations. Although each of these books inevitably contains matters open to criticism, one must judge them fairly

---

## Page 7027

and give each its due. “Ibn ‘Atiyya’s Commentary” is better than al-Zamakhsharī’s, more reliable in its transmission of reports and its analysis, and further removed from religious innovations, although it contains some of them. Indeed, it is far better; it may even be the strongest of these commentaries. Yet Ibn Jarīr’s commentary is sounder than all of these. There are very many other commentaries, such as those of Ibn al-Jawzī and al-Māwardī.

---

## Page 7028

He was also asked:

Regarding the Prophet’s saying (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), “The Quran was revealed in seven ahruf (forms),” what is meant by these seven? Do the readings attributed to Nāfi‘, ‘Āsim, and others represent the seven ahruf, or one of them? What caused the reciters to differ over readings accommodated by the script of the Quranic codex? Is it permissible to recite the noncanonical readings transmitted by al-A‘mash, Ibn Muhaysin, and others? If reciting them is permissible, is it also permissible to use them in prayer? Give us your ruling; may Allah reward you.

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. This is a “major question,” discussed by scholars of various disciplines: jurists, Quranic reciters, specialists in hadith, Quranic exegesis, speculative theology, and the explanation of unfamiliar vocabulary, among others. Indeed, entire works have been devoted to it. Among the most recent such works is that written by Shaykh Abū Muhammad ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Ismā‘īl ibn Ibrāhīm al-Shāfi‘ī, known as Abū Shāma, the author of the “Commentary on al-Shātibiyya.”

---

## Page 7029

As for presenting the various views and their evidence, and explaining the correct position in detail, this would require citing the relevant hadiths, their wording, and the other evidence at a length that exceeds the space available here and is unsuitable for an answer of this kind. We shall instead mention the essential points that clarify the intended answer.

We say:

There is no disagreement among recognized scholars that the “seven ahruf” in which the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said the Quran was revealed are not the “well-known readings of the seven reciters.” Rather, the first to compile the readings of these reciters was Imam Abū Bakr ibn Mujāhid, who was in Baghdad around 300 AH. He wished to collect the well-known readings of the two Holy Cities, the two Iraqi cities, and Syria, for it was from these five centers that prophetic knowledge spread: the Quran and its interpretation, hadith, jurisprudence concerning inward and outward actions, and all the other religious sciences. In undertaking this, he collected the readings of seven renowned leading reciters from these centers, so that their number would correspond to the number of ahruf in which the Quran was revealed—not because he or any other scholar believed that the seven readings were the seven ahruf, or that no readings other than those of these particular seven reciters could be used.

Hence one of the leading reciters said:

“Had Ibn Mujāhid not preceded me in selecting Hamza, I would have put Ya‘qūb al-Hadramī in his place, the imam of Basra’s congregational mosque and the leader of its Quranic reciters in his time, around 200 AH.”

---

## Page 7030

There is no disagreement among Muslims that the seven ahruf in which the Quran was revealed contain no contradictory or mutually opposed meanings. Their meanings may be identical or closely related, as ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd said:

“It is simply like one of you saying, ‘Come here,’ ‘Come along,’ and ‘Come.’”

One reading may also convey a meaning different from another, while both meanings are true. This is variation and diversity, not opposition and contradiction. Thus, in the hadith traced back to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) concerning this matter, he said:

“The Quran was revealed in seven ahruf. Whether you say ‘All-Forgiving, Most Merciful’ or ‘Almighty, All-Wise,’ Allah is indeed so—as long as you do not conclude a verse of mercy with a verse of punishment, or a verse of punishment with a verse of mercy.”

Examples occur in the well-known readings: “Rabbanā bā‘ad” (“Our Lord has made [our journeys] far apart”) and “bā‘id” (“make [them] far apart”); “unless the two fear that they will not maintain” and “unless it is feared that the two will not maintain”; “wa-in kāna makruhum li-tazūla” (“their scheming was not such as to make [the mountains] vanish”) and “wa-li-yazūla minhu al-jibālu” (“and so that the mountains would vanish thereby”); and “Rather, you marvel” and “Rather, I marvel”; and similar examples.

Some readings convey meanings that agree in one respect but differ in another, such as “they deceive” and “they seek to deceive”; “they lie” and “they deny”; “you have touched,” read as lamastum and lāmastum; and “until they become pure” and “until they purify themselves”; and similar examples.

All these readings whose meanings differ are true. Each stands in relation to another as one verse stands in relation to another. It is obligatory to believe in them all and to follow the meanings they convey, in both knowledge and practice. It is not permissible to abandon what one entails because of another, supposing that this constitutes

---

## Page 7031

a contradiction. Rather, as ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd (may Allah be pleased with him) said: “Whoever disbelieves in a single harf of it has disbelieved in all of it.”

As for cases in which the wording and meaning remain the same and only the manner of pronunciation varies—such as the treatment of hamzah, vowel lengthening, vowel inclination, transferring vowel sounds, distinct articulation, assimilation, reduced articulation of vowels, and pronouncing lām and rā lightly or emphatically, and similar matters known as the general principles of the readings—the absence of contradiction or opposition is even clearer and more evident here than in cases where the wording or meaning varies. These different ways of pronouncing an expression do not prevent it from remaining one and the same expression. Nor are they counted among cases involving different wordings with identical or different meanings, such as synonyms and the like.

For this reason, these variations have an even stronger claim to fall within a single harf of the seven ahruf in which the Quran was revealed than variations in wording or meaning, even when the latter conform to the consonantal outline of the Quranic manuscript—that is, where the difference lies in the consonantal dots or vowel marks.

Accordingly, the leading scholars of Islam among the early generations and the imams did not dispute that recitation throughout Muslim lands need not be confined to these particular readings. Rather, anyone for whom the reading of al-A‘mash, Hamzah’s teacher, or the reading of Ya‘qūb ibn Ishāq al-Hadramī and others like them is as firmly established as the readings of Hamzah and al-Kisā’ī may recite

---

## Page 7032

according to them, without any disagreement among the recognized scholars whose views count in matters of consensus and disagreement. Indeed, most leading scholars who were acquainted with Hamzah’s reading—such as Sufyān ibn ‘Uyaynah, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Bishr ibn al-Hārith, and others—preferred the readings of Abū Ja‘far ibn al-Qa‘qā‘ and Shaybah ibn Nisāh, both of Medina, and those of the Basran reciters, such as the teachers of Ya‘qūb ibn Ishāq and others, to the recitation of those who followed Hamzah and al-Kisā’ī. The leading scholars’ statements on this matter are well known among scholars.

For this reason, the leading scholars of Iraq for whom the ten or eleven readings were as firmly established as these seven compiled them in books and recited them both within and outside prayer. This was agreed upon among the scholars; none of them objected to it.

As for the censure of Ibn Shanabūdh mentioned by al-Qādī ‘Iyād and those who quoted him—Ibn Shanabūdh being the man who recited irregular readings in prayer during the fourth century AH and whose case became well known—it concerned only irregular readings that departed from the Quranic manuscript, as we shall explain.

None of the scholars objected to the ten readings. However, someone who does not know them, or for whom they have not been established—for example, someone living in a Muslim land in the Maghreb or elsewhere whom some of these readings have not reached—may not recite what he does not know, for

---

## Page 7033

recitation, as Zayd ibn Thābit said, is a transmitted practice which later generations receive from earlier ones. Likewise, the various opening supplications in prayer, forms of the call to prayer and the call to commence it, ways of performing the prayer in times of fear, and other practices established from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, are all good and prescribed for those who know them. Someone who knows one form but does not know another may not abandon what he knows for what he does not know. Nor may he condemn or oppose someone who knows forms of these practices that he himself does not know. As the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Do not differ, for those before you differed and perished.”

As for irregular readings that depart from the written form of the Uthmānic Quranic manuscript, examples include the reading of Ibn Mas‘ūd and Abū al-Dardā’, may Allah be pleased with them both:

“By the night when it covers, and the day when it appears, and the male and the female,”

as is established in the two Sahīh collections; ‘Abdullāh’s reading:

“Then fasting for three consecutive days”;

and his reading:

“It was but a single cry,”

and similar readings. If these are established from some of the Companions, is it permissible to recite them in prayer? Scholars hold two views on this, both well-known positions reported from Imam Ahmad, with two positions also reported from Mālik.

The first is that this is permissible, because the Companions and the Successors used to recite these readings in prayer. The second is that it is not permissible, which is the view of most scholars, because these

---

## Page 7034

readings have not been established through mass transmission from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him. Even if they are established, they were abrogated by the final review. For it is established in the authentic hadith collections:

“On the authority of ‘Ā’ishah and Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with them, Gabriel, peace be upon him, used to review the Quran with the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, once each year. In the year in which he passed away, he reviewed it with him twice. The final review is the reading of Zayd ibn Thābit and others.”

This is the reading that the Rightly Guided Caliphs—Abū Bakr, ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān and ‘Alī—ordered to be written in the Quranic manuscripts. Abū Bakr and ‘Umar had it written on sheets during Abū Bakr’s caliphate, entrusting Zayd ibn Thābit with writing it down. Then, during his caliphate, ‘Uthmān ordered it to be written in Quranic manuscripts, sent to the provincial centres, and adopted by everyone, with the agreement of the Companions, including ‘Alī and others.

This disagreement must be considered in light of the underlying issue raised by the questioner: do the seven readings constitute one of the seven modes, or not? The majority of the early scholars and leading authorities hold that they constitute one of the seven modes. Indeed, they say that ‘Uthmān’s Quranic manuscript represents one of the seven modes and incorporates the final review in which the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, recited the Quran to Gabriel. The well-known and widely transmitted hadiths and reports support this view.

Groups of jurists, Quran reciters and scholastic theologians, however, held that this manuscript encompasses all seven modes. This position was expounded by groups of scholastic theologians

---

## Page 7035

such as the judge Abū Bakr al-Bāqillānī and others, on the grounds that the Muslim community cannot neglect to transmit any of the seven modes. Yet they agreed to transmit this master ‘Uthmānic manuscript and abandon all others. ‘Uthmān ordered the Quran to be copied from the sheets on which Abū Bakr and ‘Umar had had it written. Then, after consulting the Companions, he sent a manuscript to each of the Muslim provincial centres and ordered that all other copies be abandoned.

These scholars said:
“It is not permissible for him to prohibit recitation in any of the seven modes.”

Those who defend the first position sometimes respond with the explanation given by Muhammad ibn Jarīr and others: reciting in the seven modes was not obligatory upon the Muslim community; rather, it was permitted as a concession, and they were free to choose whichever mode they wished. Similarly, no particular arrangement of the surahs was explicitly prescribed as obligatory upon them; rather, it was left to their independent judgment. Hence, the surahs in ‘Abd Allāh’s manuscript were arranged differently from those in Zayd’s manuscript, as were those in other manuscripts.

The order of the verses within the surahs, however, was revealed and explicitly prescribed. They therefore had no right to place one verse before another in the written text, as they had placed one surah before another. The order of the verses was explicitly commanded, whereas the order of the surahs was left to their independent judgment.

They said:
“The same applies to the seven modes. When the Companions saw that the Muslim community would become divided, disagree and fight one another unless they united upon a single mode, they agreed upon this

---

## Page 7036

and their agreement was permissible. They are protected from agreeing upon misguidance, and this involved neither neglecting an obligation nor committing anything forbidden.”

Some of these scholars hold that the concession to recite in the seven modes applied in the early days of Islam, because adhering to a single mode initially caused them hardship. Once their tongues had become accustomed to recitation, and agreeing upon a single mode became easy and more accommodating for them, they unanimously adopted the mode used in the final review of the Quran.

They say: “All other modes were abrogated.”

Their position accords with that of those who say: “The readings of Ubayy ibn Kaʿb, Ibn Masʿūd and others that differ from the written form of this manuscript were abrogated.”

As for anyone who claims that Ibn Masʿūd permitted paraphrasing the Quran in recitation, he has lied about him. Ibn Masʿūd merely said: “I have observed the reciters and found their recitations to be close to one another. It is simply like one of you saying, ‘Come forward,’ ‘Come here,’ or ‘Come.’ So recite as you have learned”—or words to that effect.

Those who permit reciting readings authentically transmitted from the Companions that depart from this manuscript say that this is permissible because they belong to the seven modes in which the Quran was revealed. Those who do not permit it have three grounds for their position: sometimes one of them says that it is not among the modes

---

## Page 7037

known as the seven modes. At other times, he says that it belongs to the abrogated modes; or that it is among those the Companions unanimously agreed to set aside; or that it has not reached us through transmission sufficient to establish its status as Quran. This is the distinction between the earlier and later scholars.

For this reason, there is a “third view” on this issue, preferred by my grandfather, Abū al-Barakāt: if someone uses these readings for the obligatory recitation—which is al-Fātihah, when one is able to recite it—his prayer is invalid, because he is not certain that he has fulfilled the obligatory recitation, since such transmission does not establish its status as Quran. If, however, he uses them for recitation that is not obligatory, his prayer is not invalidated, because he is not certain that he has done anything that invalidates the prayer: it may belong to the seven modes in which the Quran was revealed.

This view rests on a “principle”: if something has not been established as belonging to the seven modes, must we categorically conclude that it does not belong to them? The majority of scholars hold that no such categorical conclusion is required, since this is not a matter concerning which we are required to have certain knowledge, whether affirming or denying it.

A group of speculative theologians, however, held that it must be categorically denied. Some of them—such as al-Qādī Abū Bakr—even declared categorically that al-Shāfiʿī and others who affirmed that the basmalah (the opening invocation in Allah’s name) is a verse of the Quran outside Sūrat al-Naml were mistaken. They claimed that anything whose status as Quran is subject to scholarly judgment must be categorically denied that status. The correct view, however, is

---

## Page 7038

to affirm categorically that these scholars were mistaken and that the basmalah is a verse of Allah’s Book wherever the Companions wrote it in the Quran manuscript. They wrote nothing in it except the Quran and kept it free of anything extraneous, such as markers for groups of five or ten verses and surah names. Nevertheless, it is not said to belong to the surah that follows it, just as it does not belong to the surah that precedes it. Rather, as its placement indicates, it is a verse that Allah revealed at the beginning of each surah, even though it is not part of that surah. This is the most balanced of the three views on this question.

Whether its status is categorically denied or affirmed, this does not prevent the question from being a matter open to scholarly judgment, in which neither those who deny it nor those who affirm it are to be declared unbelievers or transgressors. Indeed, one may say, as a group of scholars have said, that both views are true: it is a verse of the Quran in some readings—those of the reciters who separate two surahs with it—and is not a verse in other readings—those of the reciters who join the surahs without separating them with it.

As for the questioner’s words, “What caused the reciters to differ over readings accommodated by the script of the Quran manuscript?” this comes down to transmission and the Arabic language, since the Lawgiver permitted them to recite in all these ways. No one may adopt a reading merely on the basis of personal opinion; rather, Quranic recitation is an established practice to be followed. When they agree to follow the Quran written in the authoritative master manuscript, but some recite with a yāʾ and others with a tāʾ, neither reading departs from the manuscript. And among the things that

---

## Page 7039

make this clear is the fact that they agree on a yāʾ or a tāʾ in some passages and differ in others. Thus, they agree in one passage on the reading of the Most High’s words,

“And Allah is not unaware of what they do,”

but differ in two other passages. We have explained that two readings are like two verses: additional readings are like additional verses. However, when the written form is the same and the wording admits both readings, this makes for a more concise script.

The transmission of the Quran rests on memorization by heart, not on written copies. Thus, in an authentic hadith, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“My Lord said to me, ‘Rise among Quraysh and warn them.’ I said, ‘My Lord, they will then crush my head’—that is, smash it. He said, ‘I will test you and test others through you, and reveal to you a Book that water cannot wash away, which you will recite asleep and awake. Send forth an army, and I shall send twice as many. With those who obey you, fight those who disobey you. Spend, and I shall spend on you.’”

He thus stated that preserving His Book does not depend on a written sheet that water can wash away. Rather, it can be recited in every circumstance, as stated in the description of the Prophet’s community: “Their Gospels are in their hearts.” This contrasts with the People of the Book, who preserve their scripture only in books and can recite it in its entirety only by reading it, not from memory.

An authentic report also establishes that a number of the Companions memorized the entire Quran during the lifetime of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, including the four men from among the Helpers and ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr. Thus, what we have explained makes it clear that the readings attributed to

---

## Page 7040

Nāfi‘ and ‘Āsim are not the seven forms in which the Quran was revealed. On this, the scholars of the early and later generations agree. Likewise, these seven readings do not comprise the entirety of any single one of the seven forms in which the Quran was revealed, according to the consensus of recognized scholars. Rather, the readings established from leading reciters—such as al-A‘mash, Ya‘qūb, Khalaf, Abū Ja‘far Yazīd ibn al-Qa‘qā‘, Shaybah ibn Nisāh, and others—have the same standing as the readings established from these seven, for anyone to whom their transmission has been established just as that of the seven has been. This, too, was undisputed among the leading authorities followed in jurisprudence, Quranic recitation, and other disciplines.

The disagreement among later generations concerned only the authoritative ‘Uthmānic codex, upon which the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, those who followed them in righteousness, and the Muslim community after them agreed. Does this codex—with the seven readings it accommodates, the additional readings completing the ten, and others—represent one of the seven forms in which the Quran was revealed, or does it encompass all seven? There are two well-known positions. The first is the position of the leading early authorities and scholars; the second is held by groups of speculative theologians, Quranic reciters, and others.

They agree that the seven forms do not differ from one another in a way that makes their meanings mutually opposed or contradictory. Rather, they confirm one another, just as the verses confirm one another.

---

## Page 7041

The reason for the diversity of readings accommodated by the written text of the codex is that the Lawgiver authorized and permitted this for them. Its basis is the Sunnah and adherence to it, not personal opinion and innovation.

If these readings are said to constitute the seven forms, this is evident. It applies even more clearly if they are said to constitute one of the seven forms. For if they were permitted to recite it in seven forms, each satisfying and sufficient, despite differences in their written outlines, then such permission is all the more appropriate when the written outline is the same and only the pronunciation differs.

This was one reason why, when the codices were first written, they left them without vowel marks or distinguishing dots: the written outline could thus accommodate either reading, such as tā’ or yā’, or the a- or u-vowel, while they preserved both precisely through oral recitation. A single written outline indicating both transmitted pronunciations—heard and recited—is comparable to a single expression indicating both transmitted meanings—grasped by reason and understood.

The Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, received from him everything of the Quran that Allah had commanded him to convey to them, both its wording and its meaning. Thus Abū ‘Abd al-Rahmān al-Sulamī said—he was the one who narrated from ‘Uthmān, may Allah be pleased with him, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The best of you are those who learn the Quran and teach it.”

Al-Bukhārī recorded this in his Sahīh, and al-Sulamī taught Quranic recitation for forty years. He said:

“Those who taught us to recite—‘Uthmān ibn ‘Affān, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd, and others—told us that whenever they learned ten verses from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, they would not proceed beyond them until

---

## Page 7042

they had learned the knowledge and practice contained in them.” They said: “Thus we learned the Quran, knowledge, and practice together.”

For this reason, his statement,

“The best of you are those who learn the Quran and teach it,”

includes teaching both its wording and its meanings. Indeed, learning its meanings is the primary purpose of teaching its wording, and it is this that increases faith. As Jundub ibn ‘Abd Allāh, ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Umar, and others said:

“We learned faith, then we learned the Quran, and our faith increased. You, however, learn the Quran and then learn faith.”

In the two Sahīh collections, Hudhayfah said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, told us of two things. I have witnessed one and am awaiting the other. He told us that ‘trustworthiness descended into the very roots of people’s hearts, and the Quran was revealed.’”

He related the hadith in full, but this sheet does not have room to recount it. The point is simply to emphasize that all of this was among what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, conveyed to the people. His Companions conveyed to us from him both faith and the Quran—its wording and its meanings. This was among what Allah revealed to him, as the Most High said:

“And thus We have revealed to you a spirit by Our command. You did not know what the Book was, nor faith, but We have made it a light by which We guide whom We will of Our servants.”

It is permissible, both in prayer and outside it, to recite according to established readings that conform to the written outline of the codex, as these readings have been established. In that case, they are not anomalous. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7043

He was also asked about “combining the seven readings”:

Is this a sunnah or an innovation? Were they combined during the lifetime of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace? Does someone who combines them receive greater reward than someone who recites according to a single transmitted reading?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Learning and memorizing the readings is itself an established sunnah, transmitted from one generation to the next. Thus, learning a reading that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used, approved their reciting, or permitted them to recite and which they accepted, is a sunnah. Someone who knows and has memorized the readings has an advantage over someone who lacks this knowledge and knows only one reading.

As for combining them in prayer or in recitation, this is a reprehensible innovation. Combining them for memorization and study, however, is a practice that certain groups of Quran reciters adopted through their own scholarly judgment. As for the Companions … (1).

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 7044

Shaykh al-Islām said:

Section: Dividing the Quran into Portions for Recitation, How Long It Should Take to Recite It, and the Prescribed Extent of Fasting and Night Prayer

ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr, may Allah be pleased with them both, reported:

“My father married me to a woman of noble lineage. He would regularly visit his daughter-in-law and ask her about her husband. She would say, ‘What a fine man! He has neither shared my bed nor approached me intimately since I came to him.’

“When this continued for a long time, my father mentioned it to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said, ‘Bring him to meet me.’ I subsequently met him, and he asked, ‘How do you fast?’

“I replied, ‘Every day.’

“He asked, ‘When—or how—do you complete the Quran?’

“I replied, ‘Every night.’

“He said, ‘Fast three days each month, and recite the Quran once each month.’

“I replied, ‘I can manage more than that.’

“He said, ‘Fast three days each week.’

“I replied, ‘I can manage more than that.’

“He said, ‘Do not fast for two days, then fast for one day.’

“I replied, ‘I can manage more than that.’

“He said, ‘Observe the best form of fasting, the fast of David: fasting one day and not fasting the next. And recite the Quran once every seven nights.’”

He said, “If only I had accepted the concession granted by the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace!” This was because he had grown old and weak.

He would therefore recite to

---

## Page 7045

some of his household one-seventh of the Quran during the day. He would go over his intended recitation in the daytime so that it would be easier for him at night. When he wished to regain his strength, he would refrain from fasting for several days, keeping count of them, and then fast an equal number of days, unwilling to abandon any practice he had maintained when he parted from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

Some narrators reported three days, and others five, but most reported seven.

Another wording reads:

“‘Recite the Quran in a month.’ I said, ‘I have the strength for more.’ He said, ‘Then recite it in seven days, and do not exceed that.’”

Al-Bukhārī narrated the report in full, and this is his wording. Muslim narrated a similar report, and the other wording is likewise found in his narration.

Another version reads:

“‘Have I not been told that you fast every day and recite the Quran every night?’ I replied, ‘Yes, O Prophet of Allah.’”

It also includes:

“He said, ‘Recite the Quran once every month.’ I said, ‘O Prophet of Allah, I can manage more than that.’ He said, ‘Then recite it once every ten days.’ I said, ‘O Prophet of Allah, I can manage more than that.’ He said, ‘Then recite it in seven days, and do not exceed that.’

“I insisted on a stricter practice, so a stricter practice was imposed on me. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to me, ‘You do not know; perhaps you will live a long life.’ And I came to experience what the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had foretold.”

ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr also reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Recite the Quran once every three days.”

Narrated by Ahmad and Abū Dāwūd. I say: Al-Bukhārī drew attention to this version, noting that some narrators said, “In three days.” This accords with the report from Saʿd ibn al-Mundhir al-Ansārī, who said:

“‘O Messenger of Allah, should I recite the Quran in three days?’ He replied, ‘Yes.’”

He continued to recite it in that time until he died.

---

## Page 7046

Narrated by Ahmad through Ibn Lahīʿah.

He also mentioned that some narrators said, “In five days,” while most reported seven. Thus, in their judgment, the sound version of the hadith of ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ultimately set seven days for him, having initially instructed him to recite it in a month. He therefore set the range between a month and a week. It has also been reported that he initially instructed him to recite it in forty days. This represents the more lenient end of the range, corresponding to three days at the more strenuous end.

As for the version which states:

“Whoever recites the Quran in fewer than three days does not understand it,”

this does not contradict the version specifying seven days. It is not an instruction addressed to ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr, nor does it state that the Prophet made regularly reciting it in three days a prescribed sunnah. It merely states that whoever recites it in fewer than three days does not understand it. Its implication arises from the specified number, and this is a valid implication: the opposite judgment applies to someone who recites it in three days or more. Such opposition can consist in a difference even in only some respects.

Thus, if someone who occasionally recites it in three days may understand it, the intended meaning of the hadith is fulfilled. If doing so occasionally is prescribed for some people, it does not follow that maintaining this practice is recommended. Accordingly, no Companion during the Prophet’s lifetime is known to have maintained this practice—that is, to have regularly recited the Quran in fewer than seven days. For this reason, Imam Ahmad, may Allah have mercy on him, used to recite it once every seven days.

---

## Page 7047

The point of this chapter is that, if the recommended division of the Quran into portions allows for its completion in between one week and one month—although a range of three to forty days has also been reported—the Companions divided it into portions consisting of complete surahs, rather than subdividing individual surahs. Thus, Aws ibn Hudhayfah related:

“We came to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, as part of the delegation of Thaqīf. The Ahlāf stayed with al-Mughīrah ibn Shuʿbah, while the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, lodged Banū Mālik in a tent belonging to him. Every night after the night prayer, he would come and speak to us, standing for so long that he would shift his weight from one foot to the other. He spoke mostly of what he had suffered at the hands of his own people, Quraysh. He would then say:

“‘Things were not the same: in Mecca we were weak and humiliated, but after we went to Medina, the fortunes of war alternated between us and them: sometimes we prevailed over them, and sometimes they prevailed over us.’

“One night, he came later than his usual time. We said, ‘You have kept us waiting tonight.’ He replied, ‘My allotted portion of the Quran demanded my attention, and I did not wish to come until I had completed it.’”

Aws said: “I asked the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ‘How do you divide the Quran into portions?’ They replied, ‘Three, five, seven, nine, eleven, thirteen, and the Mufassal as a single portion.’”

This was narrated by Abū Dāwūd, whose wording is given here, as well as by Ahmad and Ibn Mājah. In one version narrated by Imam Ahmad, they said:

“We divide it into portions of three surahs, five surahs, seven surahs, nine surahs, eleven, thirteen, and the Mufassal portion, from (Qāf) to the end.”

It was also narrated by al-Tabarānī

---

## Page 7048

in his Muʿjam:

“We asked the Companions of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ‘How did the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, divide the Quran into portions?’ They replied, ‘The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, divided it into portions of three surahs and five surahs…’”—then he related the remainder of the hadith.

This hadith agrees in meaning with the hadith of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr: they regarded completing its recitation in seven days as the Sunnah practice. Hence they divided it into seven portions, not three or five. It also indicates that they divided it by surahs. This is established through mass transmission. For it is known that dividing the Quran by letter count into twenty-eight, thirty, or sixty portions—the divisions whose sections and portions begin in the middle of a surah, a narrative, or the like—dates from the time of al-Hajjāj and thereafter. It is reported that al-Hajjāj ordered this. The practice spread from Iraq, while the people of Medina were unfamiliar with it.

If division by letter count was introduced in Iraq in the time of al-Hajjāj, then it is clear that the Companions, before that, during the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and afterward, had another way of dividing the Quran into portions. Sometimes they measured their recitation by verses, saying, “Fifty verses,” or “Sixty verses,” and sometimes by surahs. Yet no one has narrated or mentioned that they divided it into seven portions by verse count. Division by surahs is therefore the only remaining possibility.

If it is said: “But the ordering of the Quran’s surahs is not an obligation expressly prescribed

---

## Page 7049

but is instead left to people’s discretion. Hence the copies of the Quran belonging to the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, differed in their arrangement. For this reason, two positions are reported from Imam Ahmad concerning whether reciting the surahs out of order is disliked. According to the first, it is disliked because it departs from the agreed-upon arrangement of the ʿUthmānic Quran. According to the second, it is not disliked, as when children are taught to recite, since it is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited al-Baqarah, then al-Nisā’, then Āl ʿImrān.”

The reply is: There is no doubt that reciting one surah after another necessarily follows an order. At most, there may be different arrangements, just as the Quran was revealed in different forms. On this understanding, the division into portions would follow the arrangement adopted. It is also possible for this division into portions to apply to any arrangement, since the hadith does not specify the surahs.

This practice of the Companions is the best, for several reasons:

First: These newly introduced divisions invariably involve stopping within a passage that is connected to what follows. They even entail stopping at a conjoined passage rather than the passage to which it is conjoined, so that the reader begins the second day with a conjoined passage, such as Allah’s words:

“And married women, except those whom your right hands possess,”

and His words:

“And whoever among you devoutly obeys Allah and His Messenger,”

and similar passages. They also involve stopping partway through a narrative—even in the midst of an exchange between two speakers—so that one begins

---

## Page 7050

the following day with the reply, as in Allah’s words:

“He said, ‘Did I not tell you that you would never be able to bear patiently with me?’”

Such pauses are not permissible even within a single sitting when the two parts are separated by a lengthy interruption involving unrelated matter. Hence, if a coordinate phrase, exception, condition, or the like is appended to a statement after such an interruption, it is not valid, by agreement of the scholars. Likewise, if acceptance is separated from the offer by such an interruption between the speakers, it is not valid, without dispute. Whoever attributes a contrary view to Ahmad is mistaken, just as whoever attributes a contrary view to Ibn ‘Abbās concerning the former issue is mistaken.

What is reported from Ahmad concerns a situation in which both contracting parties are absent, or one is absent and the other present: one conveys the offer to the other, who accepts it during the session in which it is communicated to him. This is permissible, unlike the case in which both are present. What the Quran relates, however, is the speech of two people present beside one another. How, then, can separating their words in this way be permissible without any need? This differs from dividing a passage when teaching someone to recite because the learner has not memorized it, or for a similar reason.

Second: The usual practice of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and his Companions was to recite a surah such as Qāf or the like during prayer. Likewise, ‘Umar, may Allah be pleased with him, used to recite Yūnus, Yūsuf, and al-Nahl.

“When he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recited the surah of the Believers in the dawn prayer, a coughing fit overtook him, so he bowed partway through it. He also said: ‘I begin the prayer intending to prolong it, but then I hear a child crying and shorten it, knowing the distress this causes his mother.’”

---

## Page 7051

As for reciting the concluding or middle portions of surahs, this was not their usual practice. Hence, caution is exercised in declaring it disliked. There is a well-known disagreement over this within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere. Among the most balanced views is that making a habit of it is disliked, whereas doing it occasionally is not, so as not to depart from the established Sunnah and the practice of the early generations—the Companions and Successors.

That being so, it is clear that this method of dividing the Quran into portions and sections departs from the Sunnah more markedly than reciting the concluding or middle portion of a surah in prayer. In any case, there is no doubt that dividing it into sections and portions in keeping with their usual manner of recitation is better. The point is that assigning recitation portions by complete surahs is preferable to assigning them by subdivisions.

Third: The newly introduced division into sections cannot achieve an equal number of letters in each section. This is because spoken and written letters differ in what they include and omit: each has more than the other in certain respects, and the letters also differ in some respects. This may be explained through several points:

First: The alifs that are elided in connected recitation are retained in writing. In pronunciation, they are sounded when starting afresh but omitted when joining words. If the person counting includes them, his count fails to match the reciter’s connected recitation, which is their usual state. If he excludes them, his count fails to match both the reciter’s pronunciation when starting afresh and the written text.

---

## Page 7052

Second: A doubled letter represents two letters in pronunciation, the first of which is unvowelled. This is evident from direct observation and is universally agreed upon. The two are identical in sound. In writing, however, they may appear as a single letter, as in iyyāka (You alone) and iyyāka (You alone). Alternatively, they may appear as two different letters, as in al-Rahmān al-Rahīm (the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful), ihdinā al-sirāta al-mustaqīm (Guide us to the straight path), sirāta alladhīna an‘amta ‘alayhim (the path of those upon whom You have bestowed favor), hīna’idhin (at that time), and qad sami‘a (has certainly heard).

If the person counting follows pronunciation, assimilation occurs only in connected recitation, not when words are pronounced separately. He must therefore treat the first letter as being of the same kind as the second, yet this differs from the letter repeated there. If he follows the written text, the inconsistency is greater still: he must count this sometimes as one letter and sometimes as two different letters. Although this accords with how the words are spelled out, their pronunciation differs.

Third: Breaking pronunciation down into its constituent letters is akin to the scansion employed by specialists in poetic meter. Written letters, however, differ from this in many respects. People ordinarily spell out letters as they are written, not as they are pronounced, and there is a substantial difference between the two.

Fourth: Letters may be pronounced in measured recitation or otherwise, and the duration of vowel prolongations and vocal sounds is not uniform among reciters. One recitation portion may contain more long-vowel letters than another. Equality in pronunciation therefore cannot be maintained, while considering the written text alone serves no purpose, since it does not ensure equal recitation time.

---

## Page 7053

If dividing the Quran into portions by counting letters is only approximate rather than exact, then it is comparable to dividing it by surahs, which is also approximate: some sevenths may contain more letters than others. Dividing it by surahs, however, offers great benefits absent from division by letter count: reciting connected passages together, beginning where Allah began each surah, ending where He ended it, and conveying each surah’s intended meaning in full. It also avoids the drawbacks of division by letter count, some of which have already been noted. In these respects, it is therefore preferable.

It is well known that the duration of worship varies according to the benefits involved. Thus, in both obligatory and voluntary prayers, it is sometimes recommended to prolong the standing and at other times to shorten it, according to considerations recognized by Islamic law. It is not prescribed that its duration should be equal every day. This shows that outward equality in the amounts of bodily worship is of no consequence when a recognized benefit is involved. Nor does equality in amount necessarily entail equality in merit.

Indeed, it is established through several reports in the authentic hadith collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said that “Say, ‘He is Allah, One’” is equivalent to one-third of the Quran. It is also established in an authentic report that nothing like the Opening of the Book has been revealed in the Torah, the Gospel, or the Quran. Another authentic report establishes that the Verse of the Throne is the greatest verse in the Quran. There are other examples of this kind.

Thus, if on the first day the reciter reads al-Baqarah, Āl ‘Imrān, and al-Nisā’

---

## Page 7054

in full, and on the second day through to the end of Barā’ah, and on the third day through to the end of al-Naml, that is preferable to reading on the first day as far as His words, “far-reaching,” and on the second day as far as His words, “Indeed, We do not allow the reward of the reformers to be lost.”

Accordingly, if he completes the Quran every month, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, initially instructed ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Amr to do, following a method analogous to the Companions’ division, he should make a separate portion of any surah approximately one-thirtieth of the Quran in length, or longer by about half that amount, or slightly shorter, such as Āl ‘Imrān, al-Nisā’, al-Mā’idah, al-An‘ām, and al-A‘rāf.

As for al-Baqarah, one might say that he should make it a single portion, even though it is the length of two and one-third portions. However, the more fitting approach is to divide it into two portions out of necessity, since the portions must be reasonably similar in length: a portion may equal a standard thirtieth of the Quran or exceed it by less than half. But when it is twice that length and more, this amounts to doubling it and adding still more.

On this basis, there would be seven portions through to the end of al-A‘rāf, with al-Anfāl forming one portion and Barā’ah another. This is preferable to making them a single portion, since that would place approximately one-third of the Quran in eight portions, whereas our preferred arrangement places approximately one-third in nine. This is closer to an even division. The Companions’ division required the first portion to be longer.

The second set of ten portions, extending through to the end of al-‘Ankabūt, would consist of two surahs each. As for Yūnus and Hūd, they too may form two portions, or a single portion, since they are the first of

---

## Page 7055

the surahs beginning with “Alif Lām Rā.” On this basis, the first third would consist of portions of one surah each, and the second of two surahs each. The former arrangement, however, comes closer to placing approximately the first third within the first ten portions, since exceeding a third by one surah is closer than exceeding it by two. It would also give ten portions consisting of one surah each, which more closely resembles the Companions’ practice.

Yūsuf and al-Raʿd form one portion; likewise Ibrāhīm and al-Hijr; al-Nahl and Subhān; al-Kahf and Maryam; Tā Hā and al-Anbiyā’; al-Hajj and al-Mu’minūn; al-Nūr and al-Furqān; and the surahs beginning with “Tā Sīn”—al-Shuʿarā’, al-Naml and al-Qasas. The surahs beginning with “Alif Lām Mīm”—al-ʿAnkabūt, al-Rūm, Luqmān and al-Sajdah—form one portion. Al-Ahzāb, Saba’ and Fātir form one portion; Yā Sīn, al-Sāffāt and Sād form one portion; al-Zumar, Ghāfir and Hā Mīm al-Sajdah form one portion; and the remaining five surahs of the Hā Mīm group form one portion.

The first third more closely reflects the similarity between the openings of the surahs, while the second more closely matches the length of a portion determined by letter-count, and this is preferable. Then “al-Qitāl,” “al-Fath,” “al-Hujurāt,” “Qāf” and “al-Dhāriyāt” form one portion, followed by the four familiar portions.

This is a suitable division into portions, resembling that of the Companions, may Allah be pleased with them, while remaining close to the division based on letter-count. Eleven surahs are allotted a portion apiece, since al-Baqarah counts as two surahs. This gives eleven surahs, the allocation for eleven nights. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7056

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A group gathers to complete a recitation of the Quran, following the readings of ʿĀsim and Abū ʿAmr. Upon reaching Sūrat al-Duhā, they recite neither the tahlīl (“There is no god but Allah”) nor the takbīr (“Allah is greatest”) through to the end of the recitation. Is their practice preferable or not? Is the hadith concerning the tahlīl and takbīr authentically established through mass transmission or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, when they follow a reading other than that of Ibn Kathīr, omitting these declarations is preferable; indeed, it is the prescribed practice established by the Sunnah. These leading Quran reciters did not recite the takbīr either at the beginnings or at the ends of the surahs.

If someone may say, “Ibn Kathīr transmitted the takbīr from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,” then another may equally say, “These reciters transmitted its omission from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.” For it is inconceivable that those who transmitted the majority’s reading—more numerous than those who transmitted Ibn Kathīr’s reading—should have omitted from it something that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, had commanded them to recite. Transmitters whose reports attain the level of mass transmission cannot be presumed to conceal something that there is ample motivation and reason to transmit. Thus, whoever considers it possible that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, taught the great majority of Quran reciters to recite an additional takbīr, but that they disobeyed the command of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 7057

and neglected what he had commanded them to do, deserves a severe punishment that will deter him and others like him from such conduct.

The basmalah (“In the name of Allah, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful”) is an even stronger example. Some Quran reciters separate the surahs with it, while others do not, even though it is written in the copies of the Quran. Those who follow the reading of a reciter who omits the basmalah likewise omit it. Their fellow reciters who do recite it do not therefore condemn them for omitting it. How, then, can someone following the reading of the majority be condemned for omitting the takbīr? The takbīr is not written in the copies of the Quran, and Muslims unanimously agree that it is not part of the Quran. Anyone who believes that the takbīr is part of the Quran must be called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is to be put to death.

The basmalah is different: according to al-Shāfiʿī’s school, it is part of the Quran wherever it is written. This is also Ahmad’s position, explicitly reported from him on more than one occasion, and the position of Abū Hanīfah according to the discerning scholars among his followers and other leading scholars. However, the position of Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad, and others is that the basmalah is part of the Quran wherever it is written, but is not part of the surah. Mālik’s position is that it is not part of the Quran except in Sūrat al-Naml. This is also one position within the schools of Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad.

Nevertheless, the disagreement over it is a matter open to scholarly interpretation. Thus, whoever says, “It is part of the Quran wherever it is written,” or says, “It is not part of the Quran except in Sūrat

---

## Page 7058

al-Naml,” has expressed a view that falls within the legitimate scope of scholarly interpretation.

As for the takbīr, anyone who says that it is part of the Quran is misguided by agreement of the leading scholars. He must be called upon to repent; if he does not repent, he is to be put to death. How, then, can anyone be condemned for omitting it? Whoever brands someone who omits the takbīr an innovator, someone who contravenes the Sunnah, or a sinner is closer to disbelief than to Islam and must be punished. Indeed, if he persists in this after the proof has been made clear, he must be put to death.

Even if it were assumed that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, instructed some of those whom he taught to recite the Quran to say the takbīr, this would at most indicate that it is permissible or recommended. Had it been obligatory, the majority of Quran reciters would not have omitted it, nor would the leading Muslim scholars have agreed that it is not obligatory. No leading scholar of the religion has reported that the takbīr is obligatory. The most that someone who follows Ibn Kathīr’s reading can say is that it is recommended.

This differs from the basmalah, whose recitation is obligatory according to those who consider it part of the Quran. Nevertheless, Quran reciters allow its omission by those who do not hold that it should separate the surahs. How, then, could it be impermissible to omit the takbīr for someone whose reading does not include it? As for the claims some Quran reciters make of mass transmission concerning particular details, this is not the place to discuss them in detail.

---

## Page 7059

He was asked:

“Someone attributes the following statement to Imam Mālik: ‘Whoever writes out a copy of the Quran without following the written form of the ʿUthmānic codex has sinned’—or he said, ‘has disbelieved.’ Is this authentic? Most copies of the Quran today do not conform to the ʿUthmānic codex. Is it permissible to write a copy that differs from the ʿUthmānic codex, provided that one neither alters any wording nor changes any meaning?”

He replied:

This report attributing to Mālik the declaration that whoever does this is a disbeliever is falsely ascribed to him, whether it refers to the script or the wording. Mālik used to say that each member of the consultative council had a codex whose written form differed from that of ʿUthmān’s codex. Their standing is far too exalted for such a statement to be made about them. They were ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib, al-Zubayr, Talhah, Saʿd and ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf, along with ʿUthmān.

Furthermore, concerning someone who recites according to one of their readings that falls outside the ʿUthmānic codex, two views are reported from Mālik and Ahmad. Most scholars accept as evidence those readings authentically transmitted from them. How, then, could someone who does this be deemed a disbeliever?

---

## Page 7060

As for following the style of handwriting by writing it in Kufic script, no Muslim holds this to be obligatory. Likewise, following its use of the letters wāw and alif is a fine way of reproducing the wording in the Companions’ written form.

As for declaring someone a disbeliever for writing the words of the Quran in the script he ordinarily uses, I know of no one who has held that doing so makes him a disbeliever. However, following their script is better. This is what has been reported from Mālik and others. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 7061

He was asked about some people who recited the Quran with errors. Someone objected to their recitation, whereupon one of them said: “Every error earns ten good deeds?”

He replied:
“Praise be to Allah. If they are able to correct their recitation, they must do so. If they are unable, there is no blame on them for reciting to the best of their ability.”

---

## Page 7062

He was also asked about a man who recites the Quran for fear of forgetting it and in hope of reward: “Is he rewarded for reciting it to review it and guard against forgetting it, or not? A man regarded as knowledgeable has stated that someone who recites it for review, fearing that he might forget it, receives no reward. Is his statement correct?”

He replied:

On the contrary, if he recites the Quran for the sake of Allah Most High, he is rewarded in every case, even if his intention in reciting is to avoid forgetting it. Forgetting the Quran is a sin. If, through his recitation, he intends to fulfil his obligation to keep the Quran memorized and to avoid the prohibited neglect that leads to forgetting it, then he intends to obey Allah. How, then, could he receive no reward?

It is reported in the two Sahih collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Keep reviewing the Quran, for it escapes from men’s hearts more readily than livestock escape their tethers.”

He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The sins of my community were shown to me, and among its evil deeds I saw a man whom Allah grants a verse of the Quran, but who then neglects it until he forgets it.”

And in

---

## Page 7063

Sahīh Muslim, it is reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No people gather in one of the houses of Allah, reciting the Book of Allah and studying it together, without mercy enveloping them, tranquillity descending upon them, the angels surrounding them, and Allah mentioning them among those who are with Him. Whoever is held back by his deeds will not be advanced by his lineage.”

And Allah knows best.
